PREFACE 

Ignorance is no excuse in law, yet there is an almost universally prevailing idea that law is a subject too difficult to be studied by the laity, or taught in our schools. 

The knowledge of law, the most beautiful of all sciences-the science of what is right and wrong, and the reason why it is so-when better understood should make better citizens.

To the mind of the author there is a missing link in the chain of our educational system, in that there is no work accessible to practical men or women whereby they may familiarize themselves with: The laws under which we live; the manner in which these laws are administered; and the ordinary terms of legal phraseology. 

Our statutes and decisions which furnish the lawyer with legal information are very voluminous and are couched in technical terms, worded and prepared, not with an idea of being easily understood, but with the view of passing the critical test of the courts and of skilled attorneys, the element of simplicity scarcely being considered.

From these books none but the trained mind--that of the professional lawyer-is able to receive knowledge or dig out the desired information.

It is, therefore, not strange that the best informed, even many of the teachers of our schools, are embarrassingly ignorant on this vital and important subject. Com-

mercial Law and Civil Government may have supplied fragments of this knowledge, but as to the practical workings and administration of our laws we are at sea without rudder or compass. 

When is a debt barred by limitation? What is the

Statute of Frauds? The Laborer's Lien? The Land lord's Lien-when it attaches and how preserved? What is Pleading? What is Argument? What is a Demurrer? 

What is a Distress Warrant? A Writ of Attachment? 

A Writ of Sequestration? An Injunction? A Writ of

Mandamus? The Law of Descent and Distribution? 

What is Community Property? The Wife's Separate Property? What is the County Court? What is the Dis trict Court? And what is the jurisdiction of each? What is an Executor? An Administrator?

These are simple questions and you meet them almost daily, yet cultured persons will, perhaps, not be able to answer them; they have had no opportunity to learn. To fill such a want is the purpose of this work, that parents, teachers, and school boards may have the pleasure of having this work taught to the children as a part of their necessary education, and that this condensed statement of the law may find a place in the library of every home. 

T. WHITFIELD DAVIDSON.

PART ONE INTRODUCTORY

CHAPTER I LAW 

Sec. 1. Definition.-Law might be defined as a rule, or code of rules, prescribing what is right, and forbidding what is wrong.

Laws are prescribed by the supreme power of the 

State or Nation.

Sec. 2. The Supreme Power.-In a despotic king dom the will of the ruler is the supreme power. In the 

United States it is the will of the people, written by their chosen representatives.

Sec. 3. The Constitution of the United States.-As the basic law of the Nation is the United States Constitution, so the Constitution of Texas is the basis of all Texas laws. A law contrary to the Constitution is void. 

Sec. 4. An Example.-The Constitution says: No ex post facto law shall be enacted. An ex post facto law is one that makes an act a crime, when it was not so at the time it was committed. So, if John Doe should kill a quail in December, and in the following January a law should be passed making it an offense to kill quail, John would not be guilty of any offense because it was not against the law at the time the act was committed, and the Legislature has no power to make it such. 1

Sec. 5. The Constitution of Texas.-Our State Con stitution was framed in 1876, by a convention of delegates sent by the people, and this Constitution can not be amended, except by a vote of the people. 

The lawmakers are limited in their powers by the terms of the Constitution: As for example, the Legislature can enact no law depriving a man of the right of trial by jury.¹ It cannot levy a state tax upon the people at a higher rate than 35 cents on the $100.00 valuation of property.2

Sec. 6. Common Law.-In a large measure, the unwritten law in use by the English-speaking people has been handed down by long-established custom and practice. It embodies a remedy for grievances in most instances where no statute has been enacted to cover the case. It is found in the written opinions of the higher courts, as given by them in determining suits that have been appealed for their decision. It is also found in the textbooks, as in Blackstone's Commentaries and like works, and is known as the Common Law of England. 

Sec. 7. An Example.-An illustration is shown in the case of John Doe wilfully or negligently killing the horse of Richard Roe. Richard can sue John for the value of his horse, regardless of whether there be any statute covering the case or not. So, if you suffer an injury because of the negligence or carelessness of another, that person is liable to you in damages to recompense your injury. 

1Art. 1, Sec. 15, Cons. of Texas. 

2This does not include tax to pay public debt or for the support of public schools. Art. 8, Sec. 9, Cons. of Texas.

LAW 



Sec. 8. Statutory Law.-The Acts of Congress em brace the Federal Law. The Acts of the State Legislature are known as the Statutes of Texas. The Enactments of a City Council are known as City Ordinances. 

Sec. 9. Civil Statutes.-All that part of the written law dealing with property and the regulations of the rights of persons is generally referred to as civil law.¹ 

The right to acquire and own land; suits relating to same; the manner of descent and distribution; and like subjects are embraced within the Civil Statutes. 

Sec. 10. Criminal Statutes.-The Penal Code and 

Code of Criminal Procedure are designed to define and punish crime. Murder is a crime, a capital offense, punishable by death, or by confinement in the penitentiary. 

Theft, a felony, is a crime punishable by confinement in the penitentiary. Abusive language is an offense, a misdemeanor, punishable by fine. 

1Civil law was also the term applied to the Ecclesiastical, or Church Courts. In past ages, certain persons were entitled to trials before Church Courts instead of law courts. The law governing such trials was then known as the Civil, or Roman Law, and when the term is so used it has no connection with our own Civil Statutes.

CHAPTER THE NECESSITY OF LAW Sec. 11. Society Requires Order.-Law may be sub ject to abuses, and there are perhaps many imperfections, but to live without it is as unthinkable as it is impossible. 

If all law should be abolished and chaos, anarchy and lawlessness should reign, the best people, as a matter of self-protection, would band themselves together, and prescribe rules to secure concert of action among themselves for the control of the dangerous and lawless. These rules would, when order was restored, be the law of the land. They would be added to and altered from time to time, as the occasion or need should arise, until we would again reach our present code of laws. 

Sec. 12. Protection of the Law.-The strong would oppress the weak, no one would be secure in person or property, and an era of robbery and piracy would spread terror and destruction to every home but for the protecting strong arm of the law. A people will have good or bad laws in proportion to their intelligence and patriotism.

Sec. 13. Law to the Uncivilized. The barbarian knows nothing of a free government and does not, therefore, seek or desire it. He is ruled by the more powerful member of his tribe. The will of the chief is the law of 4

THE NECESsity of Law 

5 the tribe and may vary according to his whims. The blessing of liberty and equality under the law is a thing. unknown to his tribesmen.

Sec. 14. The Reign of Intelligence.-The enlight ened man feels and knows that he is born free and equal before the law and that he is, by birth, as much a king or ruler as any other man in the realm. Knowing that he will be dominated by no one, he will not rule and dominate his neighbor and equal.

By the vote of each and all, representatives are selected to make rules best adapted to the needs of the governed, such as will be fair and just to all men alike.

CHAPTER  Sec. 15. History of Law.-The history of law, like the history of man, dates back beyond the art of writing. 

Law at first, crude in its forms and application, developed and improved with the advancement of the people and their rulers.

The earlier conception of law seems to have been that one man was born to rule and the other to obey. The king could do no wrong, but possessed a divine right to rule his subjects, and such ideas are even recognized at the present time in some countries.

This idea of government perhaps attained its height during the Feudal Ages-a period of about one thousand years, dating from the fall of the Roman Empire in 476 to the discovery of America by Columbus in 1492. 

Sec. 16. The Earlier Races.-Mankind did not originally have fixed habitations, but roved from place to place in companies consisting of large families and kindred, constituting a tribe or clan. These would often be a nucleus of what would later become a nation. The wealth of these people consisted largely of their flocks, which would, in some instances, assume great proportions. Such was the case with Abraham and Lot, uncle and nephew, whose substance became so great they could not dwell

together.¹ Lot turned to the verdant plains and grassy meadows of Sodom and Gomorrah, while Abraham sought pasturage in the hills of Canaan. 

From Lot sprang the nations of Moab and Ammon.2 

From Abraham the Children of Israel and the House of Judah and the Arabs, in part-all these people being kindred races. 

Sec. 17. Pastoral Peoples.-Such were the days of the patriarchs, and such was the origin of every race. 

With these nomadic people, land was not property any longer than when occupied by the tribes in their temporary dwellings, or tents, so when other fields of pasturage were sought, the land vacated was subject to the occupancy of the next wandering band that chanced to claim it.

In this simple life the wants of man and the laws that regulated them were few. As between the tribes, might made right, but within the tribes the Patriarch (Father), or his appointed successor, dispensed in a paternal but harsh manner the orders for the government of his clan. 

Sec. 18. Agriculture.-These wandering bands often became pleased with their location in some fertile valley and, Jacob like, would dig a well and begin to make some permanent improvement, and the permanency of the settlement would lead to the cultivation of the soil, the planting of vineyards, and the growth of fruits. Cities sprang up and property rights now appeared in more ways than when the people dealt alone in flocks and herds. 

1Genesis 13: 7 to 10. 

2Genesis 19:37.

New laws and regulations came to meet the needs of the changed condition. And with the coming of the art of writing, these regulations, in many cases, were written down for the observance of the people. 

Sec. 19. Early Lawgivers.-Moses was one of the first to reduce to writing a code of laws which were preserved and known to modern civilization in its formation. 

This he did to establish government for the nation of Israel. These laws, with their basis, the Ten Commandments, are found in Exodus, in the writings of Moses and are regarded with reverence by the Jews, Christians and Mohammedans.

Because of its sacred teachings, the Mosaic Law has been handed down to us carefully preserved. It was not only the law of a great people, but it embodied a beautiful code of morals for the individual. 

Sec. 20. Lycurgus.-In southern Greece beyond the

Isthmus of Corinth in the small State of Sparta, about 900 years before Christ, lived a man who traveled and studied the laws of all nations, and selected what he considered good. Like Moses, he was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians. He prepared a code of laws for his country and bound the people by a solemn oath to observe them until his return. Then he went into voluntary exile never to return.

Some of the peculiarities of these laws were: That gold and silver were not money, but all the money was made of iron; that a child was not permitted to live unless it was a healthy child; that children were taught to endure great hardships, and that they belonged to their country rather than themselves.

The capital of the Spartans was not walled in as were other cities of that time. "Strong is the city walled with a wall of men" was their motto. Every man was a soldier and must conquer or die with his face to his foe. 

The central idea of these laws was that the individual existed for the law and not the law for the individual, as with us.

Sec. 21. Solon.-Athens was the great center of democratic ideas of the ancient world. One of the principal founders and dispensers of enlightenment and liberty was 

Solon, who lived B. C. 594.

He changed the laws so as to lift the burden of government from the poor and place it upon those more able to bear it. He extended the right of suffrage to the masses, where before it had been restricted to the few. 

Here it was, perhaps for the first time, that the standard of man was gauged more by merit, worth and ability than by reason of aristocratic birth. So, it has been ever true, in a land where effort receives its just rewards, the achievements of her sons shine forth in the most brilliant lustre.

Athens gave to the world a long list of statesmen, orators, philosophers, poets and sculptors, that has never been equaled by any country of the past, and that will hardly be equaled or surpassed by any country of the present or future. And perhaps not until the adoption of the Declaration of Independence and the Constitution of the American Colonies, did any other people enjoy so much freedom. 

Sec. 22. Republican Rome.-On the banks of the

Tiber, on the Peninsula of Italy, about the year 509 B. C., there existed, among warring tribes, a petty kingdom

whose people, because of a great outrage on the part of their king, Tarquin, rose in revolt, abolished the kingly office and declared that henceforth there should be no king in Rome. They then went forth into the darkness, without the lamp of experience, and began the preparation of a code of laws to govern what later became the greatest and most powerful nation upon the earth. A delegation was sent to Greece and to other countries to study the laws in force with a view of adopting and applying them at home. After much labor and travel they evolved a code known as the Twelve Tables, which was caused to be published and proclaimed as the Law of the Republic, and which was set up in public places that the people might read.

Under the wholesome influences of a free government, the little republic overcame her neighbors and swayed her scepter over all of Italy, over the Alps, and across the seas, until Roman citizenship extended itself from the deserts of Arabia to Berwick on the Tweed, from York to Jerusalem.

In a true democracy every sovereign voter is supposed to possess a power equal to that of his fellow citizen. When this is not true it lacks that much of being a free government. So, any condition that tends to enlarge the power and influence of one citizen and diminish that of another, tends to sap the vitality and undermine the foundation of republican institutions. Vast aggregations of wealth lend the possessors power, and make others dependent. Large bodies of soldiers, trained to fight rather than think, give their commanders more power than the plain citizens.

Concentrated wealth and military power became so predominant that the plain, wealth-producing citizen

was unable to stem the tide of the baleful influence, and the forum of the people became the throne of the Caesars. 

The Republic was no more. 

Sec. 23. Imperial Rome.-A single strong hand is often more capable of dealing with powerful interests and corrupt influences than the great mass of people constituting a democracy. So the Roman nation, in the hands of the Caesars, took a new lease on life. Many of the laws of the old republic, in so far as they applied to the individual citizen, were permitted to stand, always subject more or less to the will of the particular emperor then on the throne.

Rival aspirants would rise, seeking the royal purple, and the people were dragged into bloody civil wars in which they had no interest. The successful rival would celebrate, perhaps, by a wholesale slaughter of his enemies. At one time the throne was put up and sold to the highest bidder and it was bought by Didius Julianus,' he paying therefor the sum of $12,000,000.00, $1,000.00 to each soldier of the Praetorian Guard. He wore the purple, however, only sixty-five days, when he was overthrown by an indignant and more righteous contestant, Septimius Servius. 

During this internal strife and corruption the laws were, nevertheless, administered (under the better emperors) with some degree of justice in respect to the individual citizens, and the decisions of the courts were recorded and preserved and handed down to unborn nations. 

Sec. 24. Papal Rome.-A corrupt government can not receive the patriotic support of its people, and with- 

1Gibbon's Rome, Vol. 1, p. 138.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law out such it cannot withstand the assaults of its enemies. 

So in the year of 476 A. D. Rome, as a nation, went down forever before the invading hordes of semi-barbarians, who came down in great numbers from the country now constituting Germany.

During the early days of the Empire, Christ appeared in Judea, then under Roman control. After His crucifixion the Christian religion spread rapidly. The 

Apostles (Paul, Peter and others), vigorously teaching and preaching the doctrine, came to Rome itself, so that before the fall of the Empire it became the established religion of the country.

When Rome was overrun by the heathen barbarians the Christian priests, or ministers, began vigorously and successfully to teach and convert them to the Christian religion. And as these priests were the most scholarly men of the country', they did most all the teaching, kept the records, such as there were, and brought Roman culture, habits and laws into the courts of the heathen conqueror. Not only so, but the religious service was conducted in Latin, the language of the Romans. The Bible was written only in Latin and the few books were in Latin.

By the time of the Crusades, about the year 1000, the Christian religion was recognized and fanatically² adhered to in the important nations of Europe. The head of the Church, the Pope, as the supposed successor of St. 

Peter, resided at Rome. 

1During the Middle Ages it was not thought proper for a king or lord to waste his time in study. Not being able to read or write, he kept a clerk to sign his name and write his letters.

The rulers of the country being densely illiterate, knowing nothing of religion more than a smattering gathered from the priest, often went forth sword in hand to do violence for the Prince of Peace. 1

The rulers of the Christian nations frequently made pilgrimages to Rome. The Pope was looked to as having great power and influence over the kings and rulers of the different nations, and by the expression of a preference could usually turn victory to the side of his choice. 

It was deemed very essential to obtain the Pope's consent before engaging in war. Thus, the power of the 

Church was great in the land. 

Many dignitaries claimed the right of having their grievances adjudicated in the Church courts, instead of the State courts. And as the law and procedure in these courts was handed down from Rome, it partook of the old Roman laws. The law thus administered in England was called the canonical or civil law. And in this way some of the old Roman law has come down to us. 

Spain was a Latin nation, Mexico its colony. Texas was, at first, a part of Mexico, so the Texas government had another opportunity to absorb some of this Civil¹ or 

Roman Law. 

Sec. 25. Feudalism.-At the fall of the Roman Em pire the various provinces, Italy, France, Spain and England, were overrun and conquered by great hordes of Germanic tribes from the shores of the Baltic Sea, and from beyond the Rhine. The clans were each under its own earl or lord, and were united under the leadership of one great overlord or king, and when harmony prevailed, this constituted a strong military organization. 

War and pillage being the principal occupation, as well as entertainment of the noble gentry, a military government was needed and desired. 

1The term, Civil Law, as here used, should not be confused with the term as now ordinarily used in our practice as distinguished from Criminal Law.

The land would be parceled out into dukedoms, earldoms, and smaller shires. The lord would thereafter take the name of his principality, thus: Normandy would be given to William and he and his successors would henceforth be known as the Dukes of Normandy. The earldom of Essex would be alloted to Alfred and he would be Lord Essex, or Earl of Essex. The whole land was divided among the favorites of the king, each promising to aid the king in his wars and bring his vassals out armed, organized and equipped for battle. A single lord could often raise a company of several thousand soldiers. 

These lords then again parceled the territory out to knights, and finally to the ordinary yeomen, who tilled the soil for the benefit of their overlords. 

The castle of the lord combined in itself a fortress, a prison, and a residence surrounded by walls and defended by battlements and subterranean passages. Dark and nameless crimes were often concealed in one apartment while gay festivities were enacted in another. It also had its bright side, rich cellars of fine wine, gaily caparisoned horses, beautiful women, brave and handsome men. 

When knighthood was in flower, valiant and chivalrous deeds were performed that will ever animate the mind and fill the reader with enthusiastic admiration. 

Sec. 26. Chivalry.-To gallantly defend the ladies, and valiantly fight for the king, was chivalry, and to be chivalrous was the life duty of a knight. 

The word "chivalry" in our language signifies politeness, gentility and nobility of heart. Unhappily, it was not incumbent upon the knight to be gallant, or even considerate, toward any but the ladies and gentlemen-

the noblemen, their wives and daughters. To the ordinary merchant, farmer or artisan, his noble deeds and gallant conduct did not extend. They were subject to abuse and ill treatment, and for taking the life of one, the lord would not be called to account. Their wives and daughters were often subjected to even more brutal and uncivil treatment, from which there was no appeal-no one from whom redress could be had. To suffer in silence because of his unfortunate birth was the yeoman's lot. 

Before the invention of firearms, the lord and his knights rode forth to battle covered in steel armor, helmet, breastplate and shield. The yeoman came on foot, without armor, and fought with a bow and arrow, and bore about the same relation to the mounted knight as a wooden craft to a modern battleship.

The yeoman held his home subject to the lord; the lord, his title and estate subject to the king. 

The lords would often, by alliances and intermarriages, control vast estates, and would war among themselves, and not infrequently with the king. 

The title could only be held by one man at a time, as it was deemed essential by the rulers that the estates be not subdivided into petty estates; hence, it usually descended to the eldest son, to the exclusion of the other children.

Sec. 27. The Dark Ages.-The days of Feudalism are sometimes referred to as the "Dark Ages." There was no very great respect for law and order, no great desire for education and learning. To study or labor was not deemed proper employment for a gentleman. Nevertheless, the usages and practices of the period have left their stamp upon our laws of the present age. The op-

pression of the vassal has been removed by the elevation of all men to the standard of gentlemen, at least, to an opportunity to be such.

The people of this country are not ruled by "Divine Right of the King," but by consent of the governed. These improvements in the condition of man have been for centuries on the way and accomplished little by little. Here and there great strides would be made in the direction of freedom, and these stand out as mileposts in the long march of humanity for equal rights and better conditions, among which we will notice: The Signing of the Magna 

Charta, Discovery of America, and the American Revolution.

Sec. 28. The Magna Charta.-Alfred the Great, 

King of England, A. D. 871, arranged and collected many good laws to govern the people and was greatly beloved by his subjects because of his love of justice. But after his death many changes in rulers occurred until, in the decisive battle of Hastings, 1066, William, Duke of Normandy, came over the channel from North France and, with a French army, forcibly took possession of the Crown of England. He was now William the Conqueror, King of England, Duke of Normandy, Brittany, etc.

With him and his Norman lords there came a new era for the people and government of England. Feudalism was now inaugurated in its most vicious and tyrannical form. Most of the old Saxon or English thanes were executed or exiled and had their estates confiscated and foreign French or Norman lords were installed over the peasants, whose condition was no better than slavery. 

One of the successors of William, Henry I, partially restored some of the old laws of Alfred, but these were

HISTORY ANd Development of Law 

17 only observed for a season. The English kings were too busily engaged in foreign wars to care for the welfare of their people, who were heavily taxed and oppressed to raise means to carry on wars in which they were not interested.

Several generations had passed and the once Norman lords were now, in fact, English; when England was oppressed, so were they. Finally, in the year 1215, the nobles assembled an army, met King John in a field at Runnymede and presented him with a document to sign, The Magna Charta, meaning the Great Charter. He was intensely angry and refused, but the people were back of the nobles. The King was without support, so he came in like a spoiled child and signed, but resolved in his heart not to obey the Charter. It guaranteed to the people the right of habeas corpus, the trial by a jury of their peers, or equals, and it provided that no freeman should be disseized of his land, or deprived of his liberties, outlawed or exiled, except by the lawful judgment of his peers.

By these wholesome provisions a man could not be deprived of his property or personal liberty, save by a lawful trial before a jury of the people. 

By the habeas corpus, the king could not imprison a man indefinitely without giving him a trial, and the accused had a right to be tried by a jury if he chose, rather than to submit his case to the king's judges. 

The original of this important document, now over 800 years old, is still in existence.¹ King John and many of his successors tried for many years to evade and escape the provisions of the Charter, but each time the people were successful in bringing them back to its provisions. 

1Original copies may now be seen at the British Museum.

Thus, this great document, dear to all English speaking people, became the foundation of free government. The powers of the king have grown less; those of the people have correspondingly advanced.

This Charter was drawn up with the idea of protecting the nobles against the king, but, as they were written laws, they tend to relieve the lower classes also, and the commons have risen higher until the law now protects all. But it is within the past century, after the American and French Revolutions, that the laws finally applied to all alike. 

Sec. 29. The American Revolution.-Columbus discovered America in 1492, shortly after the art of printing had come into use and in the same era when Martin Luther, and others, started teaching and preaching that people should act, think and read on religious subjects for themselves. Magellan had sailed around the world and proven that it was round. Every one now began to consider that perhaps the old way of doing things was not the right way. Thinking and reasoning are never wholesome to the powers of darkness and tyranny. Men began to think of, and demand, their lawful rights, and particularly religious freedom, the privilege of worshiping God according to the dictates of their own consciences. 

Sec. 30. Freedom of Thought.-America offered a home for the persecuted; a cradle of liberty it was. The gentlemen, the adventurers, the victims of Cromwell, all came to Virginia.

The persecuted Puritan came to New England. 

The Quaker came to Pennsylvania. 

The Catholic came to Maryland.

HISTORY AND Development of Law 

19 

The Huguenot came to South Carolina.

The imprisoned poor found an asylum in Georgia. 

In a wild and savage country they learned to think for, and rely upon, themselves. No armored knights gave protection against the savage enemy, or came to oppress or exact toll.

To believe as one pleased, to worship God as one would, to have no lord, to have no king, was something new under the sun.

All countries had a religion established by law. Any man who questioned the correctness of its tenets or doctrines was deemed a heretic and imprisoned or (as was often the case) burned at the stake.

The Colonies were not of the same religion and, when united in a nation, did not adopt the religion of the 

Episcopalian, Puritan, Catholic, or Quaker, but they held that every man should worship God according to the dictates of his own conscience, without the interference of the law; something then enjoyed nowhere else. 

In 1776, there were thirteen English Colonies along the Atlantic Coast, extending from New Hampshire to Georgia. Several of the Colonies were over a hundred years old. Generation after generation had come and gone, and had breathed the air of free America, and had not known the heel of oppression or bowed beneath the yoke of the feudal baron. The broad ocean behind, the wilderness in front, the unrestrained, wild, roving savage, and the very air he breathed, taught the young American self-reliance and love of freedom.

The old Mother Countries, beyond the seas, were still ruled by their nobles and bishops. For a thousand years

and more their imperious and haughty rule no one dared question. 

As an arrogant, bold governor will collide with a free, self-reliant people, so did England and her American Colonies collide. England sought to impose burdens upon the Colonies, without their consent; she made laws to govern them, in the making of which the Americans were allowed no vote. These laws were unpleasant and distasteful. The Colonies were growing stronger, and began not only to protest against, but to disobey and defy laws so enacted and imposed. 

England, incensed at what appeared to her as the acts of disobedient children, resorted to arms; Lexington was fought; the Continental Congress met; on the 4th of July, 1776, the Declaration of Independence was proclaimed; Washington was made Commander-in-Chief of the Army, and the War of the American Revolution. was on in earnest. The world knows the result. America was free and, as a source of liberty, henceforth enlightens the world. A wave of freedom spread through 

France, and on to the limits of the civilized world. And thus did the Fathers serve all humanity. 

Sec. 31. The Constitution.-These people, now free, left upon their own resources, with no guiding hand, save Patrick Henry's Lamp of Experience, must now agree upon and formulate some plan of action as a basis. for the laws by which they will be governed. So, on the 

4th day of May, 1787, the Constitutional Convention

General Washington was made chairman, and 

Benjamin Franklin, James Madison and many other great minds of that day and age were present and participated in the deliberations and in the preparation of

HISTORY ANd Development of Law 

21 

that world-renowned document, the Constitution of the United States of America, to which was shortly added, at the instance of the great Jefferson, the Bill of Rights, making the first Ten Amendments.

The Constitution is the basic law of the land. It prescribes how, and within what limitations, laws shall be made, and provides a Congress to make them. It prescribes the means by which the law shall be enforced. It provides a way for the interpretation and construction of the same. It reserves to the State Governments their rights and to the people their rights-rights beyond which no law can go.

CHAPTER HOW LAWS ARE MADE Sec. 32. How Laws Are Made.-The laws of the 

Federal Government are made by Acts of Congress and are for the entire nation.

The laws of the State of Texas are formulated and enacted in a very similar manner to those of the nation, and apply to the people of this state only. 

The Legislature bears the same relation to the state that Congress bears to the nation.

The state, like the nation, has a Constitution which creates the Legislature, and limits its powers. 

The Legislature is composed of two bodies, sitting in separate chambers, called the House and Senate. The 

Senate is called the Upper House, and is composed of 31 members. It is presided over by the Lieutenant Governor. The House of Representatives is composed of 

150 members and is presided over by one of its own number, chosen by vote of the House, and called the Speaker. He simply presides over the deliberations of the body, and rarely speaks, and then not until he has called some other member to the chair. 

Sec. 33. Committees.-The presiding officer of each branch of the Legislature appoints a number of standing committees, and when a subject is brought before the 22

How LAWS ARE MADE 

23 body it is ordinarily referred to its proper committee for an investigation and a report before it is permitted to take up the time of the entire assembly. 

Sec. 34. Bills, or Proposed Laws.-When a member desires to have a proposition enacted into law he has it drawn up in proper form and introduced. It is then referred to a proper committee, and those favoring the bill go before this committee and show how and why it would be a good law. If the committee is pleased with the bill, it is reported back to the House favorably; if the committee does not approve the bill, it is reported unfavorably. Should the committee disagree, a majority report and a minority report are made.

When the bill has been reported it is called up for passage. The presiding officer orders the clerk to call the roll, and each member votes "aye" or "no," as he thinks best. If carried, the bill is then carefully copied by the engrossing clerk and a copy of it sent over to the other branch of the Legislature, where it is again voted upon, and if passed it is again carefully copied by the enrolling clerk, and sent to the Governor for his approval. If he approves, it becomes a law; or if he neglects for ten days to sign it, it becomes a law. If he vetoes the bill it is dead, unless it be again taken up, and passed by a twothirds vote of each body. 

Sec. 35. The Caption.-Each bill, or proposed law, must have a caption, stating in a few words a summary of the contents. And no bill must contain more than one subject, else the law is unconstitutional and void. 

The object of this restriction is to prevent laws being secretly or stealthily passed without due consideration. 

Some lengthy bill of but little general interest might con-

tain some short provision on another subject of very dangerous character.

The general public, and many of the members, can hardly have time to read all the bills introduced. By reading the caption, and knowing that there can be no other subject than the one named, the reader can readily tell if he is interested in the proposed law. 

Sec. 36. The Enacting Clause.-Next after the cap tion is the enacting clause: "Be it enacted by the Legislature of the State of Texas." The caption and enacting clause do not become any part of the law, as finally copied in the Statutes.

PART TWO CIVIL PROCEDURE OR THE ADMINISTRATION OF LAW

CHAPTER  

The law is administered by courts created for that purpose. These courts have no power to change the law, but must apply it to all alike, regardless of the individual ideas of the judges as to whether the law is a good one or a bad one. 

Sec. 37. The courts are the vehicles of the law, the arbiters of the people, and are constituted as follows: 

The Justice's Court.-Presided over by the Justice of the Peace with jurisdiction over cases where the amount involved does not exceed $200.00.

The County Court.-Presided over by the County 

Judge with jurisdiction over cases involving from $200.00 to $1,000.00.

The District Court.-Presided over by a District 

Judge, elected for four years, with jurisdiction over amounts in controversy from $500.00 up. Also over all land litigation and divorce matters. 

The Court of Appeals.-There are now eleven of these courts located in as many districts: At Galveston, Austin, Beaumont, Dallas, San Antonio, Fort Worth, Texarkana, Amarillo, Waco, Eastland and El Paso, re- 27

spectively. These courts are presided over by three judges, each elected by the people. They are not courts of original jurisdiction, but try civil cases appealed from the County and District Courts. The hearings are not upon evidence adduced there, but upon a written record sent up from the lower court. 

The Supreme Court of Texas.-Located at Austin and the court of last resort in the State. It is composed of three judges elected alternately by the people of the whole State. A party dissatisfied with the decisions of the Court of Appeals, may apply here for a writ of error to have the case reviewed.

Commission of Appeals.-The Legislature has provided for six auxiliary judges who are appointed by the Supreme Court, who aid the court in its work. 

The Court of Criminal Appeals.-Sits at Austin and is composed of three judges elected alternately by the people.

In addition to the foregoing are The Federal Courts, which try suits of $3,000.00 and over, arising between citizens of different states, and all other matters over which the National Government has supervision. These will be treated in a separate chapter. 

There are also in the cities, Corporation Courts. The jurisdiction of these courts is ordinarily about the same as the Justice's Court in criminal cases.

Sec. 38. The Justice's Court.-The Justice's Court is the smallest and most inferior court in point of power. A county is divided into a number of precincts; these are numbered 1, 2, 3, etc. The reader is, no doubt, acquainted with the Justice of the Peace in his home precinct. Any

suit for $200.00 or less must be brought in this court. If the value of the property in controversy be more than $200.00, the suit cannot be brought in the Justice's Court, but must be brought in the County Court, or in a District Court having jurisdiction of County Court cases. 

Sec. 39. Venue.-Definition: The term venue in law applies to the precinct or county wherein suit may be brought. The general rule is: That the defendant-the man sued-has a right to have the case brought and tried in the precinct and county where he lives. There are a number of exceptions to this rule: One is, where the parties have, by contract, agreed to pay a note, or perform some act at a place other than the residence of the defendant. Example: If John Doe gives Richard Roe his note, payable in Dallas, Texas, instead of his own county seat, then suit may be brought on that note in Dallas,

Texas, or at the home of the defendant, as the holder of the note may prefer or elect.

Sec. 40. Citation.-When the plaintiff determines to institute suit his first step in the Justice's Court is to have issued out of said court, a citation to the defendant stating briefly the contention of the plaintiff, the ground of the suit, and the relief desired and prayed for.

This citation must be signed by the Justice of the Peace, and must be delivered by the sheriff or constable to the defendant, at least ten (10) full days before the day of trial, which must be at a regular term of the court. 

The ten days must be exclusive of the day of court and the day of service; that is, ten days must intervene between these periods.

The constable, or sheriff, delivers a copy of this citation to the defendant, or to each of the defendants, if

there be more than one, and returns the original copy to the Justice of the Peace, showing, by his return written thereon, the day on which he received the citation, and the date upon which, and the party or parties upon whom, it was served.

Before the institution of a suit, or before going into trial, the officers of the court, or the defendant, may require the plaintiff to file a good and sufficient cost bond, conditioned that he will pay all costs of suit if unsuccessful. 

Sec. 41. Trial.-Upon the regular day for court, which, in the Justice's Court, is once each month, the particular day being fixed by the County Commissioners' Court, the case is called for trial by the Justice of the Peace.

The plaintiff is called upon for an announcement, and if he announces ready for trial the defendant is called upon, and if both parties announce ready, the case proceeds to trial. If one of the parties should be sick and unable to attend, the case would be continued to some future date. Similarly, if some important witness is absent, the party desiring to call or use him may be entitled to a continuance by asking therefor, if he has shown diligence in trying to procure the attendance of this absent witness. 

Sec. 42. Jury. The parties may, if they desire, try the case before the Justice of the Peace, but either party may, upon application, have a jury drawn and impanelled to try the case.

The jury should be composed of men who can read and write, and who are not biased or prejudiced for or

against either party, and who are not related to either party within the third degree. For example: not closer than second cousin.

These jurors are summoned by the sheriff or constable, and they should not have any fixed opinion about the rights of the parties, but must try the case according to the law and the evidence that they have received during the trial, and not upon what they have heard on the outside of the courtroom by hearsay or otherwise.

In the Justice Court six men constitute a jury. In selecting these six men, a list of twelve qualified jurors is given to each party, and from this list each has the right to strike three names, and the six remaining men constitute the jury. They are then sworn to render a verdict according to the law and evidence. 

Sec. 43. Rule. After the jury has been impanelled and sworn, the plaintiff, who has the burden of proof, calls his witnesses, and if either the plaintiff or defendant desires, he may invoke what is called the "rule," by which is meant that all the witnesses, on both sides, may be sworn before the trial begins, and sent out of the courtroom and not permitted to hear the testimony of the other witnesses. The plaintiff then calls his witness, and has him testify to such facts as may be within his knowledge, that will establish, or tend to establish, the facts set up by the plaintiff in his pleading previously stated in the citation.

When the plaintiff's witnesses have testified, the defendant then calls his witnesses and disproves or attempts to disprove the case of the plaintiff, or shows some other fact, or facts, exempting him from the demand, or demands, of the plaintiff.

After the defendant has closed his testimony, the plaintiff may introduce other testimony in rebuttal. 

Sec. 44. Argument.-After the evidence is all in, the parties, or their attorneys, will proceed to argue the case before the court, or the jury, as the case may be, showing why the facts and law are such that the plaintiff should recover. The defendant follows with his argument, and the plaintiff has the right to conclude. At the conclusion of the argument the jury retire to consider their verdict.

Sec. 45. Verdict.-When the jury have retired into privacy they elect one of their number as foreman, and after discussing the evidence among themselves, they ballot as to whether they should find for the plaintiff or the defendant. When they have all agreed they return into court with a written verdict, signed by the foreman, stating for whom they have found. If they do not agree they report, after mature deliberation, and are discharged, and the case is tried before a new jury, at some subsequent date, usually at the next term of court. 

Sec. 46. Judgment.-When the verdict is returned, the party in whose favor it is rendered, writes upon the records of the court a judgment, which is signed by the Justice of the Peace, reciting the verdict of the jury, and ordering that the plaintiff recover the amount thereof; or, if the verdict be for the defendant, the judgment so recites, and directs the plaintiff to pay the costs. 

Sec. 47. New Trial.-The losing party, or either party who is dissatisfied with the results of the trial, may, within two days, file a motion for a new trial, and if good grounds be set up in this motion, the court may

order another trial of the case, as though it had never been tried in the first instance. 

Sec. 48. Appeal.-If the amount in controversy, for which the suit was brought, be more than $20.00, either party who is dissatisfied with the final results, may take an appeal to the County Court by filing an appeal bond, signed by two or more good and sufficient sureties, conditioned that in case he be not successful on appeal the appellant will pay the judgment of the lower court, together with all cost of suit and cost of appeal. 

If the party is unable to make an appeal bond, he may file his affidavit so stating, and the appeal will be perfected thereby.

CHAPTER  (Continued) Sec. 49. Suits in County and District Courts.-The 

County Court is one presided over by a County Judge, sitting in the courthouse at the county seat. 

The exclusive jurisdiction of this court in civil cases is from $200.00 to $500.00. It has concurrent jurisdiction with the District Court in sums ranging from $500.00 to $1,000.00. The District Court has jurisdiction of any sum over and above $500.00, regardless of the amount, and in all cases where title to land is involved. 

The venue of suits in these courts, as in the Justice's Court, is fixed by the residence of the defendant, unless it comes within one of the several exceptions. In cases of divorce in the District Court, for instance, suit must be brought in the county where the plaintiff resides, and has resided for six months.

Sec. 50. Pleadings.-In the Justice's Court pleadings are oral, but in the County and District Courts the pleadings are in writing, and are filed in the following order: 34

When the plaintiff determines to institute suit he prepares what is styled The Plaintiff's Original Petition. 

In this paper he sets out his grievance and shows why he is entitled to the relief at the hands of the court. He concludes his petition with a request to the court for the relief desired. This latter portion is called the Prayer. 

Upon the filing of this petition with the clerk of the court, the plaintiff also prepares a citation directed to the defendant or defendants, giving him or them notice of the suit, as in Justices' Courts. The clerk has copies of this citation issued to the sheriff of the county or counties, where the defendants reside. 

When the citation is thus served and the defendant is apprised of the fact that he has been sued, it becomes his duty, either by himself, or through his lawyer, before the case is called on appearance day of court, to file Defendant's Original Answer, in writing, setting up the facts upon which he will rely to defeat the plaintiff's cause of action.

If any new matter is set up in this answer, not covered by the plaintiff's petition, the latter then files another paper, which he styles Plaintiff's First Supplemental Petition, wherein he replies to any matter set up in the defendant's answer. 

Sec. 51. Demurrers.-In the defendant's answer to plaintiff's petition, he frequently files what is called a General Demurrer, as the first paragraph of his answer. 

By this he says to the court in substance, that if all that plaintiff pleads be true, still he has alleged no lawful reason why he should recover. This demurrer is supposed to be addressed to the Judge upon the bench, and not to the jury.

After the general demurrer comes the General Denial wherein defendant denies the things alleged in the pleading of the opposite party, and demands strict proof of the same.

Then follow in separate counts and paragraphs, specific averments and allegations relative to the plaintiff's rights, or the defendant's defense in the case. 

Sec. 52. Jury. In the County Court the jury is composed of six men, as in the Justice's Court, while in the District Court the jury is composed of twelve men who must be qualified and be free from bias or prejudice, as required in the Justice's Court.

A Jury Commission, composed of three men, is, at each term of the District Court, appointed by the Judge to select from the voters of the county a list of men to serve as jurors for the next term of the court. This list is sealed in an envelope and placed in the hands of the clerk, and not opened until the approach of the next court. The list is then turned over to the sheriff, who will proceed to summon the jurors for the particular week for which they are to serve. 

Sec. 53. Evidence.-After the jury have been impanelled and sworn and the pleadings read, the plaintiff proceeds to introduce his evidence.

There are a great many rules that limit the introduction of testimony. For instance, the best evidence by which a written instrument can be proved must always be offered. That is to say: That John Doe would not be permitted to testify as to the contents of a land deed, because the deed itself is the best evidence of what it contains. Another restriction or limitation is: That the testi-

mony offered must be from the personal knowledge of the witness; he will not generally be permitted to give hearsay testimony. Another rule is: That oral testimony cannot be introduced to vary the terms of a written instrument.

When incompetent testimony is offered it is the duty of the opposing party to object to its admission, and it thereupon becomes the duty of the court to rule upon its admissibility. 

After the evidence of both parties is concluded, the argument of the counsel is heard, the plaintiff opening and closing. 

Sec. 54. Charge.-In the Justice's Court, the jury is the judge of the law and the evidence, while in the County and District Courts, the jury is the judge of the evidence only, the law being received from the court in written instructions, called the Court's Charge. 

In this charge he tells the jury what the law is as applied to the contention of the parties, and instructs the jurors that if they believe from the evidence that certain facts exist, they will find for the plaintiff; but, if they believe that certain other facts exist, then to find for the defendant. Or the court may submit special issues, that is, a list of questions, to the jury, and upon the answers render judgment.

The court cannot charge upon the weight of the testimony, because it is the province of the jury to believe one witness in preference to two or three if they think that the circumstances warrant it, and that the one witness was correct in his statement rather than the others.

Sec. 55. Verdict and Judgment.-The jury elect one of their number foreman and the entire twelve must agree. This, however, is becoming to be regarded as an unjust or an unwise provision, and it is being urged by many of our best lawyers and jurists that two-thirds or three-fourths of the jury render a verdict, instead of having the concurrence of the entire twelve. 

When the verdict is returned it is the duty of the attorney, or attorneys, in whose favor the verdict is rendered, to prepare a judgment for the signature of the judge, reciting the verdict of the jury and the order of the court thereon. This judgment, if not appealed from, finally settles the matters between the parties. And if a sum of money be involved in the suit the judgment directs the payment of the money and orders the issuance of execution to enforce its collection by the sheriff. If the title to real estate be involved, the judgment settles the matter in controversy and may have the effect of a deed as passing title between the parties, such as removing a cloud from the title, for example. 

Sec. 56. Jury Service is one of the highest prerogatives of citizenship. It may not always be convenient, but no one should shirk this great duty to his fellowman and his state. Some one must be the arbiter of the grave and serious differences of the contesting litigants. Some good man or woman may be in danger of losing his or her hard earnings; some man's life or liberty may be in jeopardy; or some criminal may be seeking to evade the hands of retributive justice.

Good men and true men only should sit on the jury, sworn to do equal and exact justice. This oath should

not be borne lightly. A juror should learn to weigh carefully his feelings and the sentiment and emotions. that influence him in reaching his verdict, following as his guide the unbiased testimony, the law in the court's charge, and the Golden Rule and, in applying these, he should look as often to one side as to the other.

CHAPTER RECORD AND HISTORY OF A LAW SUIT Sec. 57. A Law Suit.-A suit at law arises where the parties fail to agree amongst themselves upon their just and lawful rights, and refer the matter to the decision of the court to be passed upon by the judge or the jury.

Sec. 58. A Suit in Justice Court.-The Justice's Court is, perhaps, the best known of all the courts to the student, because of the great number of these courts, and of the Justices of the Peace who preside over them, there being several in each county.

To illustrate the workings of a court the reader will here be carried through the details of a petty law suit, such as may be seen in any one of the hundreds of Justice's Courts in Texas.

The plaintiff, John Doe, lived in the village of Woodlawn, in Harrison County, Texas, and was the owner of a small bay horse, about nine years old, which he had raised, and to which he had become very much attached. 

On or about the 1st of August, in the year of 1929, this horse was missing from the pasture and, after making efforts to recover his horse, Mr. Doe was unable to obtain any trace of same. 40

RECORD AND HISTORY OF A Law SUIT 

41 About six months later, while at the county seat, in the city of Marshall, Mr. Doe discovered his horse in the service of another person, namely, Richard Roe. Upon inquiry, Mr. Roe claimed to have purchased the horse from a drover several months before. The drover being consulted, verified the contention of Mr. Roe that the horse had been brought to Marshall in a drove from 

Western Texas. 

John Doe, being firmly fixed in the opinion that it was his horse, determined to assert his right to it. He went to see a lawyer and, being advised of his rights, he called upon the Justice of the Peace, the Honorable S. F. 

Perry, a kindly gentleman, who had long experience in the office, who received him and listened to his statements of the case with interest and attention. There being apparently no way to adjust the matter except by suit, citation was issued.

Sec. 59. Citation.

You Are Hereby Commanded, That you summon Richard Roe to be and appear before me, a Justice of the Peace, in and for Harrison County, at the next regular term of the Justice's Court, for Precinct No. 3, of said County, to be begun and holden at my office, in Marshall, Texas, on the 27th day of December, A. D. 1929, at ten o'clock A. M., to answer the complaint of John Doe in a certain suit therein pending, in which John Doe is plaintiff, against Richard Roe, defendant, filed on the 1st day of December, 1929, and numbered on the Docket 

No. 960.

The nature of plaintiff's demand being in substance: A suit for possession of one bay horse, about nine years old, the property of the plaintiff, but now in possession of the said Richard Roe, and the said Richard Roe unlawfully and wilfully, and by force and violence withholds pos-

session of said horse from this plaintiff, and avers that he is the lawful owner of same, and for which plaintiff sues. The reasonable value of said horse being $90.00.

Herein Fail Not, and have you then and there before said Court this writ, with your return thereon, showing how you have executed the same. 

Given under my hand this the 1st day of December, A. D. 1929.

S. F. PERRY, Justice of the Peace, Precinct No. 3, Harrison County, Texas Sec. 60. Sheriff's Return.-The object of the citation is to notify the defendant of suit and command him to appear and defend it, or suffer the consequences of having judgment by default taken in his absence. And, in order that it may always appear to the court that the defendant has been duly served with citation, the sheriff, or constable, affixes to the back of same his written return as follows: 

RETURN 

Came to hand on this the 1st day of December, A. D. 1929, and executed on the 3rd day of December, A. D. 1929, by delivering to

Richard Roe, the within named defendant, in person a true copy of this citation.

Sec. 61. Court Day.-On the 27th day of December, while the Christmas festivities were yet in evidence, at 10 o'clock a. m., the justice began the calling of his docket, and the case of John Doe vs. Richard Roe being called for trial, the plaintiff, by attorney, announced that he was not ready for trial on account of the absence of the

RECORD AND HISTORY OF A LAW SUIT 

43 witness, Hugh Spencer, but at this moment Hugh Spencer came into court, and the plaintiff announced ready for trial. The defendant, being called for announcement, examined the list of his witnesses, and finding them present, also announced ready for trial.

Sec. 62. The Bond for Costs.-Before proceeding to trial, however, the defendant demanded that the plaintiff give bond for costs. The plaintiff, being a man of some means, is not disposed to make a pauper's affidavit of inability to pay cost, but files with the Justice of the Peace his cost bond as follows: 

Sec. 63. Cost Bond. 

No. 960

Harrison County, Texas.

That we, John Doe, as principal, and Bill Craver and Jim Jones, as sureties, do hereby bind ourselves jointly and severally to pay to the officers of said Court, and to whom it may concern, all costs that have accrued, or that may accrue, in the prosecution of the above numbered cause, wherein John Doe is plaintiff, and Richard Roe is defendant.

Sec. 64. The Jury.-The defendant, Richard Roe, announced that he desired to have the cause tried before a jury, whereupon Justice Perry informed him that it would be necessary for him to deposit $3.00 in cash with

the court to entitle him to a jury upon the trial of the case.

Three dollars being deposited by the defendant, the party desiring the jury, Constable Charles Youngblood was directed by Justice Perry to summon twelve or fourteen good and lawful men. After being absent a few moments the constable returned into court with a list of gentlemen who then appeared and answered to their names, and who were sworn to answer such questions touching their qualifications as might be propounded to them by either party in the case. 

All of the fourteen jurors thus summoned, having stated that they had no bias or prejudice in the matter and were not related to either of the parties, and had no fixed opinion in the case, but that they could try the same upon the law and evidence as given them during the trial, a duplicate list was prepared and copies given both to plaintiff and defendant, who, with their attorneys and friends, retired to a private place and there discussed those upon the list who were considered the most objectionable jurors for the particular case; the attorneys informing both plaintiff and defendant that they each had the right to scratch from the list the names of any three persons whom they did not desire as jurors. 

Each having marked the objectionable three jurors, the lists were returned to the Justice of the Peace. The names thus marked or scratched being eliminated, the six first remaining names upon the list were left to try the case, the rest being discharged.

These six were now sworn to render a verdict according to the law and evidence.

RECORD AND HISTORY OF A Law SUIT 

45 Sec. 65. Pleading.-Pleading is a clear and concise statement of the plaintiff's cause of action, or the defendant's defense. In the District and County Courts this pleading must be in writing and filed, but in the Justice's 

Court it is oral.

After the jury had been impanelled John Doe's attorney stood before them and stated that the horse in controversy was the property of Mr. Doe, and that Mr. 

Doe had raised the horse, and was then and there the lawful and rightful owner. That the same was now unlawfully in possession of Richard Roe, who refused to deliver over the same to Mr. Doe, but retained possession of the horse, which was plaintiff's property. 

Mr. Richard Roe's attorney then appeared before the jury and stated for his pleading in the case (and which he said his testimony would show) that the horse in controversy was not the property of plaintiff and never had been; that the defendant had purchased the same for a valuable consideration; and that it was a different horse, and not the same formerly owned by the plaintiff; and asked that defendant be allowed to remain in possession of the horse, and recover all costs of this suit from the plaintiff. 

Sec. 66. Evidence.-The witnesses in the case, for both plaintiff and defendant, were now notified to come around and be sworn, and each, holding up his right hand at the same time, was sworn "to tell the truth, and nothing but the truth, so help me God."

The plaintiff asked the Rule. Thereupon, Justice 

Perry notified the witnesses that the rule had been asked, by which was meant that they would not be permitted to sit in the courtroom, or listen to the testimony of any

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED LAw witness, or talk about the case to any one except to one of the lawyers in the presence of an officer. The constable then conducted the witness out of the courtroom and the taking of testimony began.

Plaintiff, John Doe, took the witness stand and testified that he had known the horse in question all its life; that same had been raised upon his place, and that he could most positively identify it as his pony, Dexter, by a small scar on the right fore leg, and a small spot of white hair on the left hind foot, and by the general appearance of the horse, as well as his movements and carriage; that it was most certainly the same horse. 

Defendant's attorney then cross-examined the plaintiff, John Doe, and asked him if it were not true that there were many horses of a very very similar appearance. To which Mr. Doe agreed. He then asked him if it were not possible for this horse, now in question, to be some other horse than the one that he had raised. To which he replied that it could not be, that he was positively certain of the identity of the horse. A great many other questions were asked, but Mr. Doe continued firm in his statement that it was the identical horse in question. 

The next witness called was Willie Wright, who testified that he was a near neighbor of Mr. Doe, and had frequently for the past several years seen the horse in controversy, and could identify him as the same horse. 

He also remained positive through his cross-examination and entire testimony that it was the identical horse called Dexter, and the property of Mr. Doe.

At the conclusion of Willie Wright's testimony, the plaintiff announced that he would rest, by which is meant, that he had introduced enough testimony to make

RECORD AND HISTORY OF A LAW SUIT 

47 a prima-facie case; that is, a case that would entitle him. to judgment if no other testimony be introduced. 

Defendant's Testimony.-The defendant, Richard

Roe, now called to the witness stand George Jones, who testified that he was a dealer in horses and live stock, and as such had sold this horse to the defendant, Richard Roe, a few months since. That he, himself, had purchased it, with a drove of other horses, upon a ranch in the western portion of Texas, where it was at that time being treated as one of the broken and gentle horses on a ranch.

Defendant, Richard Roe, also took the stand and testified that he had purchased this horse from George Jones some few months since, and paid for it the sum of one hundred dollars.

The defendant further called the witness Thomas Rice, who testified that he had worked upon the ranch in West Texas where George Jones purchased a drove of horses, and that he had seen the horse in controversy in service on this ranch.

Several other witnesses testified for the defendant to facts substantially the same as those stated by George Jones and Thomas Rice, after which the defendant closed his case.

Plaintiff then offered in rebuttal the testimony of Frank Green, who stated that he lived in the vicinity of the city of Marshall, and on or about the 2nd day of August, 1929, saw the horse in controversy in possession of some strangers, or drovers, who were traveling with him in a westerly direction; that he was a lover of live stock, and noticed this horse in particular and spoke of trading for him, but the party did not seem particularly

anxious to trade and directed his attention to other animals in the drove. 

The plaintiff also offered in rebuttal the testimony of Tom Marks, who stated that he lived, or was living, sometime last August, near the ranch where George Jones had purchased this particular drove of horses; that he saw the horse in controversy in possession of some traveling horse traders; and that he was purchased by the manager of the "96 ranch," the same ranch from which George Jones had purchased his drove of horses shortly before returning to Marshall. The defendant closed. 

Sec. 67. Argument.-At the conclusion of the testi mony the plaintiff's lawyer discussed the evidence to the jury, and endeavored to show them why it was John Doe's horse, and announced to them the law was that, although they might believe from the testimony that Richard Roe had actually purchased the horse, still, if the same had been strayed or stolen from the pasture of John Doe, and John Doe had never parted with the ownership, it was still his horse, and the jury should return a verdict for the plaintiff.

The defendant's lawyer, or counsel, insisted that the testimony was not sufficient-that it did not show, by a preponderance thereof, that it was the same horse. That inasmuch as plaintiff's testimony thus failed to show that it was the same horse, and as the defendant had paid his hard-earned money for same, the jury should return a verdict for the defendant.

The plaintiff's lawyer then said, in rejoinder or conclusion, that it was no greater hardship upon Richard Roe to lose his one hundred dollars than it was for John Doe to lose his horse; that the law of the land was that

RECORD AND HISTORY OF A LAW SUIT 

49 a man's property rightfully remains his until he willingly and lawfully parts with the title; that this horse was therefore the property of John Doe. 

Sec. 68. Verdict.-At the conclusion of the argument, the jury retired, in charge of the constable, away from the hearing of any person, where they might consider their verdict. Upon retiring, they elected Richard 

Sutton as foreman, and after some discussion they returned into court the following verdict: "We, the jury, find for the plaintiff, John Doe. "RICHARD SUTTON, Foreman."

Sec. 69. Judgment.-After the return of the verdict, the attorney for John Doe took the book from the Justice of the Peace, and wrote therein a judgment as follows: 

On this, the 27th day of December, 1929, came on to be heard the above numbered and entitled cause, and came the plaintiff and defendant in person and by attorneys into open court and announced ready for trial. Thereupon came a jury of six good and lawful men, who being duly sworn, proceeded to hear the pleading, evidence and argument of counsel, and after hearing same, retired to consider of their verdict, and returned into open court the following verdict, to-wit: "We, the jury, find for the plaintiff, John Doe. "RICHARD SUTTON, Foreman."

It is therefore ordered, adjudged and decreed by the court that the plaintiff, John Doe, do have and recover of and from the defendant, Richard Roe, the title and possession of the bay horse, about nine years old, now in controversy, together with all costs of this suit, for which let execution issue.

S. F. PERRY, Justice of the Peace, Precinct No. 3, Harrison County, Texas. 

Sec. 70. New Trial.-After the rendition of the verdict and the entering of the judgment in favor of the plaintiff, the defendant made a motion for a new trial, setting up several reasons therefor, among others that the

verdict was contrary to the weight of the evidence, and the ground of newly discovered testimony. The court being of the opinion that the newly discovered testimony was either immaterial, or that no diligence had been shown to discover it, overruled the motion for a new trial. 

The defendant neglected to appeal to the County Court. 

Sec. 71. Execution.-At the end of ten days the defendant, Richard Roe, having perfected no appeal to the

County Court and not having turned over the horse to the court or to the plaintiff, an execution was issued against the said defendant. This execution was a written. instrument, something like the citation, and directed the constable to seize the horse in question and deliver same to the custody of plaintiff, as provided in the judgment, and also of the goods, chattels and property of the defendant, Richard Roe, to make the sum of ten dollars as costs of this suit.

The constable, taking the execution, called upon the defendant, Richard Roe, who then agreed, without further resistance, to pay the cost and deliver the horse into his custody.

CHAPTER TITLE OF PERSONAL PROPERTY Sec. 72. Definition.-By the term Personal Property is ordinarily meant movable chattels and possessions of a person, such as money, notes, live stock, and all other belongings not so fastened or fixed upon the earth as to become real estate.

Sec. 73. Suits for Possession Of.—Where two or more people assert ownership in the same article or piece of property, as illustrated in the foregoing chapter, suits not infrequently occur for the right, title and possession. 

In such cases the principal question to be determined by the court is one of identity or superior right, or such other fact as will in law and equity entitle one person to possession as against the other.

Sec. 74. Mortgages and Foreclosures.--A mortgage is a written transfer of certain personal property for the purpose of securing a debt, conditioned that when the obligation shall be discharged the mortgage shall be of no further force or effect.

In many instances where one of the parties fails to pay the debt, or where the parties disagree as to when the obligation has actually been performed, a suit is 51

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law brought for the amount of the debt and for foreclosure of the mortgage lien.

In a suit of this kind the plaintiff sets out the nature of the debt, frequently attaching a copy of the note, bond, or other obligation to his pleading. He shows that the same is still due and unpaid, and that it is secured by mortgage on certain property, describing it and frequently attaching a copy of the mortgage. The petition concludes with a prayer for judgment at the hands of the court for the amount of the debt, and for a foreclosure of his mortgage lien upon the property described in his mortgage and that same be sold, as under execution. 

In this suit of foreclosure the defendant may set up, by sworn pleading, a failure of consideration, if the note is in the hands of the original party and has not been transferred, before maturity, to an innocent person. Or, he may set up some breach of the contract whereby the plaintiff did such things that made it impossible for him to meet his obligation, or that the obligation has been met and satisfied in some other manner. 

This variety of litigation is very frequent. It is the duty of the party receiving a mortgage to immediately file it in the office of the county clerk, paying therefor the filing fee of twenty-five cents. It is the duty of every person who contemplates taking a lien upon any personal property, for his own protection, to look upon the records. in the county clerk's office to determine if there be any other lien prior to his. It is no offense to mortgage the same property more than once, but it is an offense to sell or dispose of the property for the purpose of placing it beyond the reach of the creditors to whom it has been pledged.

TITLE OF PERSONAL PROPERTY 

53 If, upon examining the mortgage records in the county clerk's office, you find that this property is not mortgaged to any other person, and you take a mortgage upon the same, without any actual knowledge of any other lien in existence, then your lien becomes superior to every other lien that may be given against said property, although there may have been an unrecorded mortgage in existence at the time, of which you did not know. 

Sec. 75. Pledges.-Pledging is another means of securing a debt. In the case of live stock or large, cumbersome objects, a chattel mortgage is generally given and recorded as above indicated, but where the property pledged as collateral to secure the debt consists of notes and bonds, or valuable paper of any kind, or jewelry, it is frequently pawned or placed with the party making the loan, or holding the debt. In this case there is not such a necessity of recording a mortgage in the clerk's office, because the actual physical possession of the property is placed in the hands of the mortgagee, who holds it as collateral to secure the payment of his debt. 

In case of suit for the purpose of collecting this debt, after setting up the amount of his debt and the justice of the same, the plaintiff would further set up the fact that he held these articles of personal property, describing them, as a pledge to secure the payment of his debt, and ask that his lien upon same be foreclosed and that the same be sold by order of the court, and the proceeds applied in satisfaction of his debt.

The pledgee, or person holding this property, has no right whatever to appropriate it to his own personal use and benefit, because the property pledged is not his, and it is usually of greater value than the debt, and after the

debt was paid the owner would have a residue, or balance, going back to him.

The same principle applies in the law of Texas to mortgaged property. Upon failure to meet the debt the mortgagee has no lawful right to go and personally take possession of the property, and use it as his own without the consent of the mortgagor, and unless the mortgagor voluntarily agrees to deliver the property over to the mortgagee in satisfaction of the debt an action must be had in court to foreclose the mortgage, reducing it to a judgment, and applying the proceeds of the mortgaged property to the payment of the debt. 

Sec. 76. Lost Articles.-The finder of any lost article or of personal property does not thereby become the owner of same, but should seek to find the proper owner and to satisfy himself that the claimant is entitled to its lawful possession, and for this purpose the finder may resist any claimant in the courts and require him to prove his ownership to the property claimed. 

Sec. 77. Estrays.-When an animal, such as a horse or a cow, strays away from home and its accustomed range, it becomes what is known in law as an estray, and may be taken up by any person as such, but it is unlawful for him to take up this stray animal without posting notice at the courthouse door, and otherwise letting it be known that he has taken up the same, and is holding it, not as his own property, but for the owner. After this notice has been posted for twenty days, the party taking up such animal will have it appraised and sold in the following manner:

An affidavit and bond will be filed with the Justice. of the Peace, who will cause the animal to be appraised

TITLE OF Personal ProPERTY 

55 at its fair value by three disinterested persons. At the end of twelve months the estray will be sold at the courthouse on the 1st Monday in the month. The stock so sold must first have been advertised in the manner provided for by law. In case of sheep, goats, hogs or cattle, they may be sold on the premises at the end of six months by the takers-up, who will pay the cost of making the sale, keep 25 per cent and deposit 75 per cent, the balance of the purchase price of the estray at said sale, with the county treasurer, who will apply same to the jury fund of the county.

No animal should be taken up as an estray until it shall have run at large without an apparent owner. 

Sec. 78. Limitation.-An important item to be con sidered in all actions relative to personal property, is that of limitation. The statutes of the state have fixed periods of limitation wherein, if certain debts are not paid, they become barred and cannot be collected. So, if an open account against any one is not paid within two years after the time it becomes due, the same is barred by the Statute of Two Years Limitations, and cannot be collected by law if the defendant shall object to the payment and interpose the plea of limitation. 

All notes and written instruments evidencing a debt are barred within four years from due date and cannot be collected if the defendant shall plead the Statute of 

Four Years Limitation. 

In this way title to personal property may be acquired by the Statute of Limitation. 

If A claims possession of any personal property and holds, claims and uses it as his own, and claims notoriously and visibly to be the owner of it, appropriating the

same without the objection of the owner, he becomes the owner by the Statute of Limitation, within two years, and the owner cannot recover the property. But if the person in possession of such property shall admit, or in any way publish orally or in writing that he is not the owner of said property, or that B or C is the owner, this prevents him from setting up title by limitation, as the claim must be adverse and hostile to all the world.

CHAPTER REAL PROPERTY 

Sec. 79. Definition.-Real property is the foundation of all wealth, and the source of a very large percentage of litigation. It consists of a portion of the earth, ordinarily with marked and definite boundaries. It may be a vast tract of unimproved land consisting of millions of acres, or it may be a city lot of a few feet only, or even a lot in a cemetery.
