With the term "real estate" is included all the improvements thereon, such as buildings, fences and the timber.

Sec. 80. Suits Affecting Real Estate.-Among the more frequent actions brought in our courts affecting real property, might be mentioned Trespass to Try Title, Forcible Entry and Detainer, Partition, and Actions to 

Remove Cloud. 

Sec. 81. Trespass to Try Title.-Our statutes have limited almost every variety of action in the nature of an ejectment, or suits for possession, to the one heading of "Trespass to Try Title." This action may be maintained in all cases where one person trespasses, enters upon or sets up any character of claim to the real property of another. 57

Sec. 82. Pleading.-Plaintiff's petition filed in a suit of this character must allege that on a certain date, naming it, he was the owner, lawfully seized and possessed of the property. It must give a sufficient description of the property to locate or identify it. It must allege the name of the trespasser and should properly allege the nature and extent of the trespass and the amount of damage consequent thereto, where there is any damage. 

It must contain a prayer for relief from the court, asking for possession of the premises, and that the judgment vest the title in plaintiff. 

Sec. 83. Defendant's Pleading. In actions of this character, the pleading of the defendant is frequently one of "not guilty," which is a short and simple statement that he is not guilty of the wrongs charged against him in the plaintiff's petition. In addition to the plea of "not guilty," any other special pleas may be made. 

Sec. 84. Limitation.-Pleas of three, five and ten years limitations very often constitute an important part of the defendant's defense.

The Statute of Limitation was not enacted for the purpose of protecting trespassers and wrongdoers, or to serve the purpose of an artifice or trick to enable one man to steal the land of another, although it may be, and doubtless is, in some instances, used for such purpose. 

Sec. 85. Three Years Limitation.-The Statute of 

Three Years Limitation may be availed of by either plaintiff or defendant in showing a title to his land. 

Where there is only some slight defect in the consecutive chain of title, then it may be shown that the claimant has had actual, peaceable and adverse possession of the prop-

REAL PROPERTY 

59 erty, as required by the Statute, for a period of three which will cure the defects in his chain of title. years,

This section of the Statutes is not of as frequent service as are the Statutes of Five and Ten Years Limitations.

The Statute of Five Years Limitation provides that when any person shall have been in actual, peaceable and adverse possession of a piece of realty, claiming the same under deed or deeds, duly registered, and paying taxes thereon for a period of five years the same may vest in the claimant, or party in possession, a good and perfect title regardless of whether the deed under which he claims conveyed any title or not. 

This Statute is a very valuable protection and safeguard to many landholders in our State. A great many farmers and other people not schooled in the knowledge of the law, have heretofore been very careless in many instances about recording their land deeds, and also about whom they had to write them and take the acknowledgements and, as a result, there is a large proportion of the land titles in some sections of the State that have more or less defects, or missing links. 

By missing links is meant defective deeds, or absent deeds somewhere back between the present owner and the original patentee to whom the State patented or granted the land.

In all suits of trespass to try title it is necessary for the plaintiff to first show that he has title to the property. In order to do this he must introduce a patent or evidence that the state had parted with the title, and that the land had ceased to be a portion of the public domain. He must next introduce deeds or copies of

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law deeds from each person who held the property from the time that the land was patented down to the present owner.

This constitutes a chain of title, containing just as many links as there are deeds by which the property has been transferred, or wills by which it has been devised. 

In rare instances an abstract may show but two or three transfers; in others it may show forty or fifty, or even hundreds. Each of these deeds must be properly written; the property must be sufficiently described; and the signature of the grantor must be duly acknowledged. 

When all of this has been done, and the deeds have been duly recorded, or are in actual possession of the claimant, the party is said to have a good title from the sovereignty of the soil; that is, from the beginning, and the Statutes of Limitations are of no service to him. 

If any of these deeds, however, be missing, or if any of the acknowledgements be defective, or if the description of the property in any one of these deeds be so vague and indefinite as not to enable one to locate the property, then there is a missing link in the title, and in such case the Statute of Five Years Limitation is of great benefit and service to the claimant. 

In proving title by Five Years Limitation, it is not necessary that the same party should have been in actual possession for the entire period of time; he may have deed of possession of another party, who had lived on it for a portion of the five years, and he himself have finished out the five year period, but, in this case, it is necessary for the last claimant to hold under the former owner, or claimant, and have from him a written conveyance transferring the property.

REAL PROPERTY 

61 

Sec. 86. Ten Years Limitation.—Under the Statute of Ten Years Limitation it is not necessary even for the party to have paid taxes. Any person in actual possession of real property for a period of ten years, and not disturbed by an adverse suit, acquires title to all of the land that he so possesses, or has enclosed, and is entitled to as much as 160 acres, including his improvements, if there be that much in the tract, even though his improvements had not enclosed all the land claimed; and, if he has field notes, or some written memoranda of title, defining the tract that he lays claims to, he can claim all of the land described in this memoranda up to an amount not exceeding five thousand acres. 

To illustrate: If John Doe should deed to Richard

Roe a certain tract of real property, describing it by metes and bounds, that is, starting at some known corner and going from place to place, and coming back to beginning point, so as to enclose one thousand acres of land, more or less, and Richard Roe should hold this deed and assert ownership and exercise the proper control over this property, and actually possess a portion of it for ten years, he would have a good and perfect title to all of the land described in the deed, which would be true of any number of acres not exceeding five thousand. 

This Statute seems originally to have served a different purpose from the Statute of Five Years Limitation. The Five Years Statute is most largely beneficial in curing defective titles as above illustrated. Where the lawful owner is in possession of his property, but not in possession of the deeds sufficient to establish his claim of title thereto, the Five Years Statute comes to his relief and protection.

Under the Ten Years Statute, the claimant is not required to have a deed. It goes upon the theory that if one man will permit another to live upon land, improve and claim it as his own for ten years without molesting him, then the original owner should lose his right thereto, and the squatter should not be deprived of the land and improvements upon which he has made his home. Where he is permitted to reside unmolested for so long a period the presumption arises that he must have had some right to go upon the premises in the first instance, which the law has permitted to ripen into title, at least to all the property that he actually uses. 

Another feature of this Statute is that it is in a large measure analogous to the old preemption law, by which the State gave 160 acres of land to any married man who would settle upon it and make it his home. 

The Ten Years Statute says to the man who holds unimproved property: If you do not improve your property, and if you permit others to do so and do not molest them for ten years, you lose your right to the land. 

Sec. 87. Burden of Proof.-As before stated, in all cases of trespass to try title, the burden is upon the plaintiff, that is, he must recover upon the strength of his own title, and not upon the weakness of the defendant. 

He must be able to show title in himself by chain of transfers, or under the Statutes of Limitation. The first must be shown by written instruments or copies thereof from the Deed or Probate Records. Limitation, how ever, is shown by oral testimony whereby the party claiming under limitation must show by proper witnesses that he has actually used, occupied and enjoyed the land as required by law, for three, five or ten years. When

REAL PROPERTY 

63 the plaintiff is able to do this, it is then the defendant's turn to disprove or lose.

If the plaintiff is not able to show title from the record or by limitation, he loses his case, and the defendant is not required to put on any testimony whatever, but if the plaintiff is able to show title by the record or by limitation, the defendant must introduce such testimony as will, in the minds of the jury, or judge, as the case may be, overcome the testimony of the plaintiff and establish the rights of the defendant. 

Sec. 88. Damages. Where it appears that the defendant has no right to the property and is guilty of a trespass thereon, the court will, in the same trial, assess such damages against him as the proof may show that the plaintiff has suffered on account of the trespass. 

The plaintiff's petition, when filed, must have endorsed thereon these words: "This action is brought in trespass to try title, as well as for damages."

CHAPTER 10 PARTITION OF REAL ESTATE 

Sec. 89. Definition.-A partition suit is an action brought in a District Court whereby one or more of the joint owners of a tract of land petition the court to have the land divided among the several owners thereof. 

Sec. 90. Tenants in Common.-Tenants in common, cotenants and joint owners are terms applied to two or more people who, together, own a piece of property; as partners who had purchased it as a firm, or as brothers and sisters and other descendants who have inherited it from a common ancestor, or any other persons, who, by purchase, inheritance, or otherwise jointly own the same piece of property.

The proper course in such cases is for the several owners to divide the property in several tracts and make a partition deed each to the other. But, in many instances, the several owners do not agree upon the manner of partition, and in such cases a suit for partition must be brought, in which the court, after hearing the evidence, will decide the extent of the interest of the several parties and have the property partitioned accordingly. 

Sec. 91. Minors.-Another situation making it necessary in many instances to divide property in court by 64

PARTITION OF REAL ESTATE 

65 means of a friendly partition suit, is where there are minors owning some interest, who cannot, on account of their minority, sign a partition deed, but the court can set aside to them their interest in the property. 

Sec. 92. Partition-How Made. When the Court hears the evidence and decides the interest of the several claimants, a judgment to this effect is entered, in which it is determined whether the property is susceptible of being divided. If the court finds that it can be, three commissioners are appointed, who are instructed to divide the property and take with them a surveyor, if necessary, so as to cut the property into lots or blocks of equal value. By which is meant that each one of the parties shall receive property equal in value to his interest in the whole property, but not necessarily equal in acreage. As for example: One interest may contain ten acres with improvements and be as valuable as one hundred acres without improvements. Thus, where there were joint owners and the commissioners should decide the two tracts of land were of equal value, lots would be cast to determine which particular block one claimant should have, and which block the other should have. 

In case it should be made to appear to the court, either by proof in the first instance or by report of the commissioners, that the property was not susceptible of a fair and equitable division, then the court will order the property sold and the money paid into the hands of the clerk, who will divide it among those in interest. 

Contested suits for partition are sometimes the result of family disagreements, and should never be, any more than there should be contests over the will of a departed ancestor. But the desire of one heir, or one co-

tenant, to receive more than his just and reasonable proportion of the common property, often makes these suits necessary and sometimes bitterly contested. 

In all suits for partition it is necessary for the entire number of claimants to be in court, and they must appear either as plaintiffs or as defendants or be brought in before a partition can be had. 

If there be minors who are made defendants and who have no guardian, these minors must have appointed for them a guardian ad litem. This means a guardian for the particular suit, who is usually some attorney, to look after the minors' interests, and his fees are paid from the minors' property and taxed up as court costs.

CHAPTER II 

TO REMOVE CLOUD 

Sec. 93. Cloud on Title.-An action to remove cloud from the title is usually necessitated by some outside person setting up some claim to the property of plaintiff, and this claim becoming generally known, casts a cloud over plaintiff's title. Again, where some deed that purports to convey an interest in plaintiff's property has been made and placed upon record, or where plaintiff's property has been unlawfully sold by virtue of some execution or order of sale, an action is necessary to clear up the title. These and other like causes cast over the title to real estate a shadow that may be, and is, designated in law as a "cloud," and in order to clear up his title. and have it above question, the plaintiff brings an action in the District Court, whereby he sues the parties who set up claim or illegal judgment, and notifies them to appear in court and defend their claim, if any they have, to the property.

Upon hearing the facts the court decrees, if the evidence is sufficient, that cloud be removed and the plaintiff be quieted in the possession of his property. 67

Sec. 94. In Case of Contest.-In cases of contest, where the party sued to remove cloud answers back, setting up title, the suit then takes on all the proportions. of a case of trespass to try title, which has been outlined in Chapter 9.

CHAPTER 12 FORCIBLE ENTRY AND DETAINER Sec. 95. Forcible Entry.-Where one person, by force, enters upon the landed property of another, or makes entry without the other's consent, such conduct is deemed a Forcible Entry.

Sec. 96. Forcible Detainer.-Where a person re mains upon the property of another after the expiration of his rights thereon, his act is deemed one of Forcible Detainer.

An example of this kind would be where a renter, at the expiration of his lease, refuses to vacate the property.

Sec. 97. Suits for Possession.-The owner of the property upon which a forcible entry has been made, or in a case of forcible detainer, has the right to elect whether he will sue the party in the District Court of the county, in an action of trespass to try title, or whether he will bring a more speedy action in the Justice's Court of his precinct, which latter action is known in law as 

Forcible Entry and Detainer. 

Sec. 98. The Purpose of the Statutes.-Actions in the District Court of Trespass to Try Title are slow and 69

frequently consume a considerable period of time, often taking from one to three or four years. In these cases the right of possession only is adjudicated; the final right of title to the property is not determined. 

However, in the Justice's Court procedure of Forcible Entry and Detainer the case is different. The suit can be heard within six days after its institution, and the question of title is not and cannot be adjudicated, but the immediate right of possession is determined by the Justice of the Peace, or by the jury if one is desired. This enables the party to obtain speedy relief, and enables one to recover his rightful possession without long and tedious delays, and no doubt helps to keep down trouble and personal violence which might arise between the parties were there no other recourse than the long and tedious delays in an action of Trespass to Try Title.

Sec. 99. Appeals.-Either party, it is true, may appeal from the Justice's Court where the case must be tried, to the County Court, but in such cases he must give bond to compensate the other party for the delay consequent upon his appeal, in the event that the judgment of the Justice's Court is sustained. This is one of the few instances where the litigant must make bond to protect the other for damages consequent upon a delay that may arise, as these delays are in most all other suits looked upon and treated as a part of the ordinary loss incident to law suits and litigation. 

Sec. 100. Question Tried.—In the trial of a case of

Forcible Entry and Detainer, the fact that the landlord may, or may not, have a perfect title to his property cannot be questioned. If it appears that the plaintiff was in possession of the property and that another forcibly en-

FORCIBLE ENTRY AND DETAINER 

71 tered and took possession from him, this party must vacate, even though he may be setting up some claim to the title. 

Suits in Forcible Entry and Detainer settle the right of the parties to immediate possession, but do not bar either one from bringing an action, in the District Court, of Trespass to Try Title and thus have the matter finally adjudicated between them. The collection of rents may be enforced as an incident to these suits.

CHAPTER 13 PERSONAL INJURIES 

Sec. 101. Nature of Suit.-In order to maintain an action for personal injuries, two things must concur: 

First, the party must have been injured; second, this must have been wilfully done, or negligently caused, by another person.

Sec. 102. Wilful Injury.-Where one person shall intentionally and wilfully illtreat, maim, cripple, or otherwise injure another, except as a matter of self-defense, the person inflicting such injury is liable to be called upon in the courts to respond in damages according to the nature of the injury inflicted. 

These suits are not so frequent, because in the first place, a party who would violently and wilfully injure another is not often the possessor of sufficient worldly goods to warrant any effort to obtain a judgment against him. 

Sec. 103. Negligence.-Negligence is a failure to exercise that degree of ordinary care that a reasonably prudent man would exercise under the same circumstances. So, if A is hurt or sustains injuries on account of or by reason of B's negligence, or the negligence of 72

PERSONAL INJURIES 

73 B's agents or servants, A can maintain an action in the court against B to reimburse him in money for his mental and physical pain and suffering, his diminished capacity to earn money, his loss of time, his doctor's bills, and such other losses as he may have sustained.

Sec. 104. Justice and Fairness.-The justice and fairness of this variety of litigation is often brought into question. It is sometimes the case that a man with but very slight, or perhaps no apparent injury at all, or with an apparent but no real injury, frequently maintains a suit based on slight negligence against some person or corporation, and obtains a great sum of money in compensation for injuries and damages that have never been really sustained. Such instances necessarily bring reproach upon the law under which this class of litigation is maintained, but upon the whole it is one of the most wholesome, just and righteous laws known to our procedure.

It suffices the double purpose of causing the great public carriers and corporations, who have in their hands the lives of millions of passengers and employees, to realize that if a passenger, employee, or other person is hurt by reason of their negligence, they will be required to respond in damages for this injury, and it causes them to adopt safety devices and appliances for the protection of the public. It moves them to exercise care and precaution that would, in all probability, not otherwise be thought of or attempted.

Another benefit is the relief carried to the sufferer, his widow or orphan. The passenger, wage earner, or other victim of some great corporation's carelessness or negligence, may lose his limb or his life and be thereby

rendered helpless, and homes and families may be left penniless. In such cases, if this injury was caused by the negligence of another, the law steps in and directs that payment be meted out to compensate, as far as possible, the great loss and injury sustained and thereby alleviate suffering and mitigate the force of the blow. 

Sec. 105. Contributory Negligence.-Contributory negligence is that negligence whereby the injured party, by reason of his own carelessness or negligence, contributes to his own injury. In this case, he is not allowed to recover as he cannot justly be paid, at the cost of another, for his own negligence. This rule has been so modified by statute that it does not apply to men engaged in the extra hazardous work of operating trains. 

In a great many of the personal injury cases that are brought in our courts, contributory negligence constitutes one of the main grounds of defense, and the jury are told by the judge in his charge, that if they believe that the injured party contributed to his own injury by reason of his own carelessness and negligence, then he cannot recover damages from the defendant. 

Sec. 106. Extent of This Litigation.-This variety of litigation has grown into great proportions in the last half century. A very large percentage of it is against railroad companies, but much of it is of late directed against owners of trucks and motor vehicles. 

These actions, however, are not confined to corporations, but may be maintained against individuals and people in every vocation of life, by whose negligence others are injured. 

Sec. 107. Libel and Slander.-Not only may an action be maintained for injury to the person, but one may

PERSONAL INJURIES 

75 also be brought for injury to the reputation, as in the case where "A" publishes or circulates a false report upon the good name of "B," whereby "B" suffers the loss of social or business standing in the community. In such a case "B" could maintain an action against "A" for the injury thus sustained,

CHAPTER 14 INJURIES TO PROPERTY 

Sec. 108. In General.-In Chapter 13, we discussed the subject of personal injuries. We now propose the subject of injuries to property.

It has long been recognized as an established feature of our jurisprudence that one whose property has been damaged by the malicious or negligent act of another, is entitled to be reimbursed in money for the damage his property has sustained. This is a very righteous and just provision of our law, and is a part of the old common law that has come down to us through the ages, independent of any statutory provision.

Where one man intentionally or negligently trespasses upon the property of another and injures same, or in any manner depreciates its value, it is right that he should recompense the injured party, as the person who is responsible for the injury or who inflicts the injury, should be required to stand the loss rather than he who is innocent or careful in his actions and conduct.

Sec. 109. Injuries to Live Stock.-An example is shown in cases of injury to cattle, horses, or other live stock: If John Doe maliciously and intentionally maims, cripples, or kills the horse of Richard Roe, the law will 76

INJURIES TO PROPERTY 

77 require John Doe to pay to its owner the value of the horse so destroyed or injured, and if the act was maliciously done he may also be prosecuted by the State, but this prosecution for a penal offense by the State has no connection, and will in no manner interfere, with the action that Richard Roe is entitled to maintain to have himself reimbursed for his financial loss. He has the right to bring suit in the proper court and obtain a judgment against John Doe for the value of his horse or for the amount of the injury done to it. If the action be for less than $200.00, it should (as before stated) be brought in the Justice's Court; if for more than $200.00, in the 

County Court; if for more than $500.00, in the County or the District Court, as the plaintiff may elect; and if for more than $1000.00, in the District Court having jurisdiction.

This suit may be maintained in the county where the injury occurred or in the county where the said John 

Doe resides.

Sec. 110. Negligent Injury to Property. Suits of this variety more frequently occur on account of negligence than by reason of any malicious act. It is a rare case where any person owning property maliciously destroys the property of another. The guilty party is rarely possessed of any property against which a judgment would be of any value, hence the only recourse that is open in cases of malicious injury to property is criminal prosecution. In such a case, the State may have the guilty party imprisoned and fined, but the injured person will not be able to receive any recompense for his damaged property. 

Damages for negligent injury to property are very often charged to railroads for killing stock upon the

right of way. In cases where the stock killed are upon the track of a railroad that has not fenced its right of way, it is the duty of the railroad to pay for all of the stock killed by its engines and cars. But, where the railroad has seen fit to fence its right of way, then it is not liable for the stock killed, unless the accident was caused by some act of negligence on the part of the company. 

For example: If the stock gate was left open and horses, cattle, or other stock entered upon the right of way and were killed, the company is liable. Or, if the accident occurs at a crossing and the engineer in charge of the engine neglects to blow the whistle and sound the alarm, or to exercise care not to injure the stock, then, in this instance also the company is held liable. But where the track is fenced and the whistle is blown and due care is taken by the engineer and crew, the company is not liable for damage, as there is no negligence and the owner must sustain the loss. 

Sec. 111. Freight and Baggage.-A very prolific source of litigation by reason of loss or damage to property, is that against express and railway companies and other carriers of freight and baggage.

The carrier is not an insurer, in every case, of the property entrusted to his care. He must, however, exercise proper care and precaution to prevent loss and damage, and after goods have been entrusted to him for transportation and are lost by him in transit or are damaged, he must respond to the owner to the extent of the injury or value of the property lost, unless, of course, he can show that it was an act of Providence or some other unforeseen occurrence beyond his control.

INJURIES TO PROPERTY 

79 Frequently a shipment of freight and baggage goes a great distance over the lines of a great many companies, and if it arrives in a damaged condition the presumption is that the last company that handled the freight or baggage caused the injury, and this last company must then either show that the property was received by it from the connecting carrier in a damaged condition, or else be liable for the injury done.

On the contrary, however, in cases of lost shipments, the presumption is that the initial carrier lost the shipment and he must account for the goods and show that they were delivered to the next connecting carrier, or become responsible for their loss.

Parties desiring a speedy and prompt delivery of freight, especially of express, should notify the agent receiving same, of the necessity of the prompt delivery and the injury that he would sustain by reason of a delay, and unless this is done an express company can rarely be held liable for an ordinary delay in transportation. 

Sec. 112. Injuries to Realty.-A very large percentage of the litigations arising from injuries to property, are in a measure connected with, or incident to, the holding of real estate and appurtenances, such as cutting timber, burning grass and destroying crops. 

Sec. 113. Cutting Timber.-If any person shall enter upon the land of another without his consent, cut and destroy, or cut, carry away and appropriate any of the timber thereon, he commits a trespass and is liable in damages to the owner of said property. 

The measure of this damage is dependent upon the intent of the party taking and carrying away the timber. 

If John Doe cuts the timber of Richard Roe under a

mistaken belief of right, having exercised reasonable care in the matter, and believes the timber to be his own or to be from land that he owns, but is in error in this belief, then, in this event, he is liable for the value of the timber at its then market price upon the ground at the time it was cut. But if John Doe, without the exercise of any care to determine or ascertain whose timber it is, or if he intentionally undertakes to steal or appropriate the timber, knowing that it is not his, he is then indebted to Richard Roe, or the owner of the timber, for the timber in whatever state it may then be. That is to say, Richard Roe is entitled to his timber regardless of where it is, and how it is. If John Doe has made it into lumber of a very valuable variety, or even into matches, the owner is entitled to recover it or its moneyed value.

An illustration would be: If the timber at the stump in the forest was worth $5.00 per thousand, and was innocently taken and appropriated by mistake, the taker would have to pay $5.00 per thousand to the owner. But if it was negligently or wilfully taken and appropriated and made into lumber, which lumber was worth $50.00 per thousand, then the taker would have to turn the lumber over to the owner of the land or pay him $50.00 per thousand for the timber thus appropriated. 

Sec. 114. Burning Grass.-If one person, either negligently or intentionally, burns the grass upon the pasture of another, he is liable to the owner for the value of the pasturage so destroyed. The same is true of growing crops or of forest.

Sec. 115. Flooding Land.-Where a person or cor poration shall cause the overflow of another's land by

INJURIES TO PROPERTY 

81 means of dams, levees, or embankments of any kind, so as to pond the water or cause it to overflow ground that it would not have otherwise overflowed, such person or corporation causing such overflow would be liable in damages for the property destroyed, if any, and for the depreciated value of the land by reason of its being thus flooded. 

Sec. 116. Changing of Streams.-Water has its established course over which it flows in its effort finally to reach the sea. The purchaser of real estate has the right to take into consideration the flow of the water and the location of the streams when he acquires title to property.

Any person who by ditching or any other artificial means diverts water from its established channel, and thereby deprives the adjoining landholder of the right to its enjoyment, becomes liable to the landholder for the injury that he has inflicted upon him, and the court will, in many instances, not only award damages, but will grant a writ of injunction restraining the actions of the party in diverting the course of the water. 

The same principle also applies in cases where the water is diverted from its established course and caused to run in a new channel over land that was not formerly subject to overflow.

Sec. 117. Changing the Grade of Property.-An other injury that property is sometimes made to sustain is where a railroad, in building its track, raises a high embankment, or makes a big cut in such close proximity to the home or improvements of the owner as to injure the usefulness or appearance of the property; or where a city, in establishing a grade upon a street, lowers the

street or fills it in in such a way as to make the street less accessible to the property of the owner than it formerly was. In such cases, the city or railway company may be required to recompense the owner for the depreciation of his property.

In some instances, however, and especially with reference to street grades, the property is very much enhanced in value by reason of the highly improved condition of the street after having been graded and paved. 

The owner of the abutting property may be temporarily inconvenienced but the value of his property, upon the whole, is very much enhanced. In such a case damages should not be awarded, as the benefits are allowed and permitted to offset the damage.

The above examples, of course, are not intended to include all of the injuries to property, but are sufficient to illustrate the principle.

CHAPTER 15 DIVORCE 

Sec. 118. Definition.-A divorce is a decree of the 

District Court annulling, for a sufficient and lawful cause, the marriage relation between the husband and wife.

Sec. 119. Grounds for Divorce.-The Statutes have defined four principal grounds upon which a decree of divorce may be had:

Ist. Abandonment for a space of three years, with the intention of abandonment. 

2nd. Excessive, cruel and abusive treatment by one towards the other, so as to render their living together insupportable.

This ground for divorce may include many acts and things under the general head of cruel and abusive treatment: A dominating, overbearing disposition, a high and ungovernable temper, neglect and want of support, physical violence and fear of bodily injury, are all defined within the limits, and make up the element, of cruel and abusive treatment. 

3rd. Unfaithfulness on the part of either spouse towards the other. 83

4th. Conviction of a felony and imprisonment in the state prison.

Sec. 120. Venue.-Venue in divorce suits, unlike most other suits, must be brought in the county of the plaintiff's residence, whereas the general rule is that suits be brought in the county of the defendant's residence. 

One reason for this variation is perhaps that the plaintiff is better known at the place of his or her residence, and whether or not he or she is entitled to a divorce perhaps can there be best established. 

Our Statutes require that the plaintiff must have been an actual bona fide inhabitant of the state in which he or she sues for twelve months next preceding the hearing of the suit, and shall have resided in the county for six months next before the institution of suit.

Sec. 121. Laws in Other States.-The laws of the different states on the subject of divorce vary to such a radical degree that there is a great deal of confusion. 

In South Carolina there is no such thing as a divorce, the same not being allowed by law; while in Nevada it may be procured without trouble upon a few weeks' residence only.

The grounds upon which a divorce may be had, as well as the effect of the decree when entered, vary in the statutes of the different states. 

In Texas a divorce granted one of the parties sets both parties at liberty and either is permitted to remarry; if, however, the divorce was obtained upon the ground of cruel treatment, the parties cannot marry for one year. In some of the other states only the party who obtains the divorce is set at liberty, and the same decree granting the divorce may provide that the other mem-

DIVORCE 

85 ber of the marital relation be not granted a divorce and be not allowed to remarry.

There is, therefore, a great deal of confusion upon the subject, and under the decisions one state may recognize the decree of divorce granted in another state or may refuse to recognize it, as it suits the whims of the courts of that state. An individual may be a single person in one state and a married one in a neighboring state. 

Sec. 122. Default.-In most legal actions where the defendant does not answer, the plaintiff is entitled to judgment by default without the introduction of proof, but in divorce proceedings he must offer his witnesses and introduce his testimony whether there be any answer filed or not. 

Sec. 123. Condoning.-If one of the parties to the marriage contract is guilty of acts that would entitle the other to a divorce, and this couple knowingly continue to live together, the act or conduct is said to be condoned or forgiven and cannot of itself be the basis of a suit for divorce. Upon a renewal of the conduct, however, causing a final separation of the parties, the former bad conduct may be shown as an illustration of the general behavior of the party.

Sec. 124. Division of the Property.-The property acquired during marriage is community, or partnership property, and if there be no mention of property in the suit and judgment granting the divorce, the parties will each hold an undivided one-half interest in all the community property, which property may be partitioned at some future date. The better plan, perhaps, and the one

more generally practiced, is to divide the property at the time of the granting of the divorce. 

In this division, the separate property goes to the party originally owning same, and the community property is divided equally. 

Upon the filing of the suit for divorce, the wife may, by injunction duly issued from the court, if she deems it necessary, enjoin the husband from in any manner disposing of, encumbering, or making away with any of the property in which she has an interest, and the court will, in such cases, require him to file an inventory under oath of all the property possessed by him in which the wife may be interested.

Sec. 125. Homestead Rights.-The court cannot divest either the husband or the wife of any portion of their estate, but he can, within his discretion and if the facts so warrant, set aside the use of the home and other property to the wife and children-or perhaps in some instances, to the husband and children-and enjoin the other spouse from interfering with the same in any manner.

He may also set aside the husband's interest in the community property to aid in maintaining and educating the children, but the title to this property in such a case would remain in the husband and the income from same only would pass as directed by the orders of the court. 

Sec. 126. Alimony.-There is no such thing in our law as permanent and continuous alimony, but if, after the institution of the suit, and pending the trial of the case, the wife should report to the court that she is destitute, or has no income sufficient to provide for her wants,

DIVORCE 

87 the court can order the husband to pay into the court, for her use and benefit, a sum of money sufficient for her needs, and may continue such order until the case is finally heard upon its merits. Should the husband refuse to pay such alimony, after being so ordered by the court, he may be adjudged as guilty of contempt, and be imprisoned in the county jail until he purge himself of such contempt by complying with the orders of the court.

Sec. 127. Custody of the Children.-In granting the decree of divorce the court will award the custody of the children to either the husband or wife, not in accordance to the love and affection that may be borne by such parent, but with reference solely to the future welfare of the children. If the court be of the opinion that the husband is so situated and constituted as to better provide and care for the minor children, their custody will be awarded to the husband; for similar reasons, they may be awarded to the wife.

Sec. 128. The Evils of Divorce. Many of our best and most religious citizens look upon divorce as a great and growing evil: as the destruction of families, and as legalizing sin in the eyes of God by an act of the law. 

The theory of our law, however, is that the parties do not come into court seeking a divorce until the home life and the domestic relationship have already been wrecked and destroyed, and that the social and moral obligations existing between the parties have been so breached and violated that no social benefit can arise or accrue to the said parties or to society by having the parties lawfully bound together, when their presence is only a source of dissension and trouble, inflicting un-

bearable burdens upon them and often leading to crime; that under such a state of affairs, it is better that the parties should be released from their civil contracts with each other, and be left in the management of their business affairs.

The action, however, of any person in putting away a husband or wife for the purpose of contracting other marriage relations cannot be too strongly condemned. 

It is not only wicked in the sight of God, disgraceful and disreputable in the eyes of society, but criminal and demoralizing in the eyes of the law. To better safeguard our courts and our social system against divorces of this character, it seems that it would be proper to place certain restrictions around the granting of divorces. For instance, after the filing of the suit, and the entering of preliminary orders, for the purpose of protecting the parties in their lawful rights, it might be well that each case remain on the docket undisposed of for a period of one year. It has, however, been provided that if the divorce be granted on the ground of cruel and abusive treatment, remarriage will not be allowed for one year thereafter, but there is no sufficient penalty to enforce the observance of this provision.

CHAPTER 16 ANCILLARY PROCEEDINGS 

Sec. 129. Generally.-In many suits, in order to better protect the rights of the parties, and to maintain the status of the property until final judgment can be entered, it is necessary to have some auxiliary process in addition to the regular procedure. 

This auxiliary process is not a separate and independent suit, but is designed, by the use of the court's powers, to preserve intact the rights of the parties as near as possible until the suit has been settled, or until the court has finally adjudicated the matter in controversy.

These ancillary writs are used in emergency cases, and are drastic measures, and where the party elects to ask for them, he is usually required to give bond that he will not injure the defendant, and will pay to the defendant all such damages as may be adjudged against him for wrongfully taking out such writ.

There is much of a sameness in the manner in which they are procured, and the objects which they subserve. 

They must be obtained upon affidavits, or sworn pleadings only, and bond must be given in order to procure them. 89

The principal writs referred to in this section include Injunctions, Writs of Mandamus, Attachments, Sequestrations, Garnishments, Distress Warrants, and Receiverships.

Sec. 130. Injunctions.-An injunction is a writ, issued by the court and auxiliary to the main suit, prohibiting the defendant, under the penalty of the law, from doing anything pending the litigation, that would affect the rights of the parties.

To illustrate: In a suit for divorce against the husband, the wife is entitled to a writ of injunction to enjoin the husband from disposing of any of his property, or in any manner encumbering it during the pendency of the suit.

Sec. 131. Writs of Mandamus.-A writ of man damus (mandate) is a mandatory injunction, directing the party to do certain things; as for instance, a public officer may be compelled and directed by writ of mandamus to perform a certain duty required by law, which he refuses or neglects to do.

Sec. 132. Attachments.-A writ of attachment is a writ directed to an officer, directing him to take into possession certain property and hold the same subject to the orders of the court, pending the determination of a suit between the parties.

Sec. 133. Sequestration.—A writ of sequestration is very similar to a writ of attachment, and is obtained by the same process, but is levied upon property upon which the plaintiff asserts a lien or right of ownership, while a writ of attachment is levied upon property of the defendant, upon which the plaintiff seeks to create a lien.

ANCILLARY PROCEEDINGS 

91 

Sec. 134. Distress Warrants.-A distress warrant is a writ very similar to a writ of sequestration, but it is a remedy applied only in proceedings between landlord and tenant, and is the landlord's remedy where the tenant seeks to make away with property upon which the landlord would have a lien. And if the landlord sees fit to sequester or attach this property, the writ issued is designated in law as a "distress warrant."

Sec. 135. Receivers.-A receiver is a party appointed by the court to take charge of certain property where it is not deemed wise to leave it in the hands and management of the defendant pending the suit, and where it may be necessary to use and operate the property during the pendency of the litigation. 

An illustration may be given in the case of a railroad. It could not be conveniently attached—it is a public necessity—and its principal value to either plaintiff or defendant depends upon its continuous operation. 

The court will, therefore, not shut the property down, but pending the litigation, will appoint a receiver as an officer of the court, to hold the property in custody. 

This would be done for the protection of creditors generally, until the matters in controversy be finally determined.

CHAPTER 17 INJUNCTIONS Sec. 136. Definition.-One of the ancillary writs named in the last chapter was the Writ of Injunction and, as explained, it is a preliminary interlocutory order of the court that may be issued before the case can be heard upon its merits, directing the defendant to refrain from doing anything affecting the status of the property that would tend to defeat the purpose of the litigation.

Sec. 137. Prohibitory and Mandatory Injunctions. 

-By the term "injunction" as generally used, is meant a prohibitory injunction, that is, one that enjoins a defendant from doing certain things. But, in certain instances, the terms of the injunction granted may be mandatory; that is, directing the defendant to do certain things. It may direct the custodian of books or records to deliver them to the applicant for the injunction; or it may direct the officers of the city to levy a tax to meet a judgment that has been obtained against the city. 

Sec. 138. By Whom Granted.-Judges of the Dis trict and County Courts may issue writs of injunction, when properly applied for, to maintain the rights of the parties with respect to the subject matter of the 92

INJUNCTIONS 

93 litigation, during the pendency of the suits in their respective courts. The County Court has the right to issue writs of injunction where the amount in controversy comes within the jurisdiction of that court, while the District Court may issue writs in all other cases, large and small. Most of the injunctions issued in this state are granted by judges of the District Court. 

Sec. 139. How Issued.-After citation has been issued, or, as is the case in most suits, at the time that citation is issued, the clerk of the court may issue an instrument called a "writ of injunction," directed to the defendant and wherein he is plainly told what he must or must not do.

Sec. 140. When Issued.-When the applicant for an injunction files with the clerk and presents to the judge of the court, his sworn petition for an injunction, he sets up the reason and the grounds upon which he is entitled to an injunction, and it must appear from said sworn pleading that the relief asked for requires immediate action on the part of the court, and that to wait until the issues of the suit are finally determined would be ruinous or detrimental to the rights of the applicant.

One means of showing this is to allege and show that the defendant is insolvent, or incapable of responding in damages to the plaintiff for the injury that he is about to inflict. An example of this would be: Where a poor man, or an insolvent concern, was about to take and appropriate a tract of valuable timber, and cut and carry it away from the premises and from the control and custody of the applicant, and thereby deprive the applicant of his property and make worthless any judg-

ment he might obtain upon final hearing in court. If it appears to the court, upon the hearing of this sworn petition, that immediate action is required to prevent an injury being done, for which the defendant will be unable to recompense the party, that is, will be incapable of responding in money for the damage done, then the court will endorse on said petition his fiat. 

By this is meant that he will write, at the conclusion of said petition, an order stating the terms upon which the injunction is granted. The clerk will then issue the writ, wherein he usually copies the fiat of the court, stating the terms and conditions of the injunction. He then directs the defendant to refrain from doing the things indicated by the judge's fiat or order granting such injunction. The writ, when signed and sealed by the clerk, is taken by the sheriff and delivered to the defendant. 

Sec. 141. Bond.-When the judge endorses his fiat on said petition for an injunction, prescribing the terms and conditions thereof, he also names the amount of bond that the plaintiff is required to give before the clerk will issue the injunction. The applicant, or plaintiff, must file with the clerk a bond in the amount named by the judge, signed by two or more good and solvent sureties, which bond must be conditioned that the plaintiff, or applicant for the said writ of injunction, will pay to the defendant all costs of suit and damages that may be adjudged against the plaintiff in favor of the defendant for wrongfully suing out this writ of injunction. And if, at a subsequent period, upon the hearing of the case, it should appear that the injunction was wrongfully issued and was without proper cause, or was sued out to annoy and harass the defendant, the court

INJUNCTIONS 

95 or jury trying the case upon its final hearing may allow the defendant as damages against the plaintiff and his bondsmen such sum of money as will recompense said defendant for all of the losses that he has sustained by reason of the issuance of such writ of injunction. And the said defendant may, at that time, introduce proof to show what his loss has been by reason of the execution of such writ. 

A writ is styled: 

"STATE OF TEXAS. 

TO JOHN DOE, GREETINGS:" 

Then follows a recitation reciting the filing of the petition, and the action of the judge thereon, and commands the defendant to do, or not to do, the particular act complained of. 

Sec. 142. Dissolution of Injunction.-If, upon filing of the application for an injunction, the judge should be of the opinion that the parties will not suffer any immediate loss, he may suspend action upon the application, and require the defendant to have notice and permit a hearing from both parties before granting the writ. This is fair and proper, and should always be done, except in cases of emergency.

In event, however, that notice cannot be or is not given, the defendant, upon being served with the injunction, has the right to file his motion in court to dissolve said injunction, that is, to have the court rescind the order granting same. Upon this motion the defendant will be entitled to a hearing on his side of the controversy; and if the court is of the opinion that the injunction has been improperly issued, it will then be dissolved.

The reader should not overlook the fact that an injunction is usually only an ancillary proceeding. That is to say, the main suit which will probably be tried before a jury, will come up in regular manner and be finally disposed of, while the injunction is only a temporary proceeding to prevent the defendant from placing the property, or the subject of litigation, beyond the control of the court, or in such an attitude or condition as to render the final judgment of the court ineffective.

There are, however, some suits filed where the relief desired upon final hearing is a permanent injunction, forever restraining the party from interfering with work, or trespassing upon premises. In this variety of cases the writ of injunction becomes the main issue of the case, and it is not so much an ancillary procedure as in the instances above given.

Sec. 143. Disobedience-Contempt.-After the writ has been served upon the defendant, enjoining him from interfering with the things and matters therein mentioned, he becomes in contempt of court if he disobeys such order, and the court may punish him for such contempt by fine or by placing him in jail. Here he may be kept until the court shall believe that he has purged himself of such contempt, or is willing to obey the orders of the court.

CHAPTER 18 ATTACHMENTS 

Sec. 144. Definition.-An attachment is a writ issued by the court directing the sheriff or constable to take into his custody certain property of the defendant, pending the litigation.

Sec. 145. The Object and Purpose of An Attach ment is to hold property where the court may be able to reach it and dispose of it according to the judgment finally rendered in the trial of the case, and the writ should properly and justly issue only where the defendant is about to deprive the plaintiff of the opportunity to realize upon his judgment by making away with his property, or doing some other thing that would make the judgment ineffectual when obtained. 

Sec. 146. Grounds.-The Statutes prescribe several grounds upon which writs of attachments may issue:

That the defendant is justly indebted to the plaintiff in the amount of the demand; and 

That the defendant is not a resident of the state, or is a foreign corporation, or is acting as such; or 

That he is about to remove permanently out of the state, and has refused to pay or secure the debt due the plaintiff; or 97

That he secretes himself so that the ordinary process of law cannot be served on him; or 

That he has secreted his property for the purpose of defrauding his creditors; or

That he is about to remove his property out of the state, without leaving sufficient remaining for the payment of his debts; or

That he is about to remove his property, or a part thereof, out of the county where the suit is brought, with intent to defraud his creditors; or 

That he has disposed of his property, in whole or in part, with intent to defraud his creditors; or 

That he is about to dispose of his property with intent to defraud his creditors; or

That he is about to convert his property, or a part thereof, into money, for the purpose of placing it beyond the reach of his creditors; or 

That the debt is due for property obtained under false pretenses.

Sec. 147. The Affidavit.-The party desiring to have a writ of attachment issued must file with the court an affidavit signed by himself, his agent or attorney, stating that the writ is not sued out to injure or harass the defendant. The affidavit must be positive and certain in its provisions, that is, the plaintiff or his agent cannot swear in said affidavit that he believes that the defendant has disposed of his property, or that the defendant is about to remove the same out of the county, as the use of the word "or" would show uncertainty on the part of the affiant. The affidavit should be based upon one of the grounds, and all uncertain terms and conditions should be omitted.

ATTACHMENTS 

99 

Sec. 148. Upon Liquidated Demand Only.-A writ of attachment will not issue in a suit for damages, or where the amount in controversy is an unknown sum to be thereafter fixed or estimated by the court. Therefore, a writ of attachment will not issue in any suit for damages growing out of a tort, as for personal injuries, but will issue only upon liquidated demands, that is, where the debt sued for is a known sum, as is the case with a promissory note. 

Sec. 149. Procedure.-If A owed B $1000.00, and

A's property consisted of a stock of merchandise, or a herd of cattle and A had no other property subject to execution of the court, and it became known to B that A was about to remove this property out of the county, or dispose of it so as to leave nothing with which to pay B's debt or out of which B could make his money, then B would be entitled to a writ of attachment. 

He would file his suit in the usual way: That is, he would sue A in the court for $1000.00; he would file his written petition and have the clerk issue citation giving him due notice of the suit, stating the time when the court would convene, and summon him to appear and answer.

But now it occurs to B that before the court meets and the trial can be had, A may have his cattle, or his stock of goods, out of the reach of the court, perhaps sold and converted into money, therefore, if A does anything that will show to B, with reasonable certainty, that such is his intention, to protect himself B will go back to court and ask for a writ of attachment. He files his affidavit, gives bond and has the writ issued; then the sheriff goes out and takes the cattle or stock of goods into his possession.

Sec. 150. Bond.-Before the issuance of a writ of attachment, B, the applicant, must give bond in double the amount of the debt, which bond must be signed by two good and sufficient sureties, and be conditioned that the plaintiff will prosecute his suit to effect and pay all such damages and cost which may be adjudged against him upon the final trial of said cause for wrongfully suing out such writ of attachment. 

Sec. 151. Levied by the Officer.-The officer takes this writ, which directs him to make such attachment, goes to the defendant or to the place where his property is situated, and takes such property into his possession. He then files an inventory of same, and makes his return or report to the court. All of the property thus attached is held by the officer subject to the disposition of the court, unless replevied by the defendant. 

If the attachment be upon real estate, the sheriff cannot, of course, take it into his possession, nor is it necessary in this case for him even to go upon the land -he simply takes the writ, makes a levy upon the land described and records the levy in the county clerk's office.

This fixes a lien upon the land, and its record gives notice to all parties dealing with such property. 

Sec. 152. Replevy Bond.-If, when the officer makes his levy and takes possession of the property, the defendant desires to retain possession of the property himself, and not permit the officer to take it into the custody of the court, he may do so by giving what is known as a "replevy bond," that is, a bond in double the amount of the debt or property, conditioned that he will pay such judgment as may be awarded against

ATTACHMENTS 

101 him by the court, to the payment of which his property might be subjected. 

After the officer has taken the property in his custody and held the same for a period of ten days, if the defendant has not replevied the same, then the plaintiff may do so by giving bond for its safekeeping. 

Sec. 153. Indemnity Bond.-An attachment is a dangerous, stringent and drastic remedy. To protect himself, the sheriff or officer, before levying the same, may require the plaintiff to give him an indemnity bond. 

The object and purpose of this bond is to protect the sheriff from any suit for damages that the defendant may claim by reason of a wrongful attachment. 

Sec. 154. When It Is Safe to Attach, in many instances is a doubtful question, as the plaintiff may be reasonably certain that the defendant is doing one of the things provided for in the Statutes that entitle him to a writ of attachment, and yet he may have no way of proving the same. The right should always be exercised with care, as a wrongful writ might be issued against a good, upright, honorable man, entirely solvent, and one who is trying to meet his obligations; one who would, in fact, be able to do so had not the writ of attachment been issued and thus demoralized his business, closed his doors, destroyed his credit, and damaged his business reputation generally.

In a case of this kind the defendant is undoubtedly damaged, and if the plaintiff has not exercised the proper care and precaution to determine and ascertain that there were just and lawful grounds before issuing the writ of attachment, the injured party should have

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law damages in such sum as will compensate him for the loss that he has sustained.

On the other hand the party may procure a loan of money, a bill of merchandise, or other things of value, upon fair representations and promises, and after procuring the same, set about to deliberately defraud his beneficiary, or the party from whom same was obtained. 

He may be just in the act of leaving the country with his only property; or of transferring it out of the country; or of converting it into money so that the party who let him have the goods, etc., would have no means of enforcing the payment of his debt. 

In this case a writ of attachment is a very just and proper remedy, for if the parties were left to abide final judgment of the court upon a trial before judge or jury at some future time, the judgment granted would probably be of but very little value, as the only property upon which execution would be effective would have long since disappeared, or been carried out of the state or county.

CHAPTER 19 SEQUESTRATION AND DISTRESS WARRANTS Sec. 155. Distinguished From Attachments.-Se questration Writs and Distress Warrants are very similar to writs of attachment, but are issued under different circumstances, and only in certain specific cases, and are known by these special names as distinguished from the general writ of attachment. 

An attachment, for instance, is issued to secure property and on which to fix a lien, while a writ of sequestration is issued against property upon which the plaintiff already has a lien, or some right of ownership. 

Sec. 156. Jurisdiction.-The jurisdiction of the court is determined by the location of the parties and the amount in controversy, just as in the case of other suits.

The writ is issued by the Justice of the Peace, or the clerk of the court in which the case is to be tried. 

There are seven statutory grounds named upon which a writ of sequestration may issue:

Ist. In connection with divorce proceedings, instead of writ of injunction. 103

2nd. In a suit for the possession of personal property where the plaintiff swears that he believes the defendant will illtreat the same during the pendency of the trial. 

3rd. In a suit to foreclose a mortgage on personal property, and where the plaintiff makes affidavit that he fears the property will be illtreated. 

4th. In a suit for possession of real estate where the plaintiff makes oath that he fears that the defendant will waste or convert the proceeds of said real estate to his own use and benefit, or otherwise damage the same during the pendency of suit. 

5th. In a suit for the possession of any property where the plaintiff makes oath that he has been violently ejected from the possession thereof. 

6th. Where suit is brought to foreclose a lien or mortgage on real estate and the plaintiff makes affidavit that he fears the defendant will waste and convert the same, or the proceeds thereof, during the pendency of the suit. 

7th. Suit affecting real property where the defendant is a non-resident of the state.

The affidavit must be certain and must be based upon some one of the statutory grounds, and must not be in the alternative. This latter provision, you will remember, also applies to affidavit for writ of attachment. 

Sec. 157. Bond. Before taking out a writ of sequestration, bond must be made by two or more good and sufficient sureties in double the amount of the property in controversy. Such bond must be conditioned that

SEQUESTRATION AND DISTRESS WARRANTS 

105 the plaintiff will pay all damages that may be adjudged against him for wrongfully suing out such writ. 

Sec. 158. Custody of the Property.-Upon receipt of the writ the sheriff will, as in the case with attachment, proceed to take into his possession the property so sequestered, and hold the same subject to the final judgment of the court.

The defendant, however, may replevy the property by giving bond in double its value. If the plaintiff recovers his judgment, the defendant and his sureties will be liable upon this replevy bond. If the defendant does not replevy the property within ten days after the levy, the plaintiff may replevy and hold same. 

Sec. 159. Damages.-Damages may be allowed the defendant for wrongfully suing out a writ of sequestration, or distress warrant, just as in the case of an attachment.

Sec. 160. Distress Warrants.-A distress warrant is a writ peculiar to dealings between landlord and tenant. 

The landlord has a lien upon certain property of his tenant (which is defined in the chapter entitled, "Landlord and Tenant"), and if the tenant seeks to make away with this property so as to defeat the landlord in his right of recovery in the collection of his rents, the landlord may sue out a writ of attachment, which in this specific instance is styled "a distress warrant." It can be issued only by the Justice of the Peace, and if the amount of the rent in controversy, or property distrained, is more than $200.00, the Justice must make the writ returnable to the County Court; if the amount of the property distrained exceeds $1000.00, he must make it returnable to the District Court.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law 

This subject is also treated in the chapter on Landlord and Tenant. 

Sec. 161. Ancillary Writs Reviewed.-It will be seen that these several writs are all auxiliary to the main suit. They are used to hold the property until the issues between the parties are regularly tried and disposed of. 

A writ of attachment is one where the plaintiff has no claim upon the property, but by levy of the writ acquires a lien upon the same superior to any subsequent attachment, for in cases of this kind the first party making the attachment procures an advantage. 

With writs of sequestration, however, this is not the case, as this writ is based upon a right of ownership, or a lien that already exists by virtue of a mortgage or contract, or on some statutory ground. The purpose of the writ of sequestration as distinguished from a writ of attachment is not to create a lien alone, but to preserve the property intact and prevent its deportation during the pendency of the suit. 

These things are also true of the distress warrant, the landlord having a lien by virtue of his contract as well as by statute.

CHAPTER 20 RECEIVERS 

Sec. 162. Receivers.-A receiver is ordinarily some disinterested person appointed by the court to receive and possibly operate such properties as the court may have occasion to take into its possession. 

Sec. 163. Purpose. The purpose is to aid the jurisdiction of the court in protecting the property, collecting its rents and income, and continuing the operation of the same pending the suit.

An illustration of a receivership might be given in the case of a railroad. Most all railroads are mortgaged to some trust company, or other person or concern, as trustee, to secure its bonded indebtedness. These bonds are issued against the road and are protected by a mortgage on the property, and if the owner and holder of these bonds is not paid, he has a right to have the mortgage foreclosed upon the railroad and may bring suit for that purpose and have the road put up and sold to satisfy his mortgage, and the proceeds applied to the payment of the bonded debt secured by said mortgage. 

When this suit is brought there are usually a great many other creditors, some of whom, particularly those who have furnished ties, building material, etc., and the 107

laborers, have a lien, and their debts are superior, in point of payment, to the bondholders. Although it is necessary, in order to keep up the value of railroad property, to continue the operation of the road, yet for obvious reasons it would usually be unfair and unsatisfactory to the mortgagee and the other creditors to allow the company itself, pending the litigation, to continue in charge of things. Under these conditions the court will appoint some competent person as receiver, who takes the property out of the hands of the company and proceeds to manage it as an officer of the court during pendency of the suit.

Another illustration of where receiverships may be had, is in cases of partners disagreeing in the management of a business, and one forcibly ejecting the other from the management; or where one partner may sell his interest to an objectionable party and the other partner refuses to receive him into the business. In this case either party has an equal right to the management of the business, and if they cannot agree upon the management, the court will, upon application of either party, appoint a receiver to manage the same until the property can be lawfully divided in the main suit filed for the partition of same.

Sec. 164. Duties of the Receiver.-It is his duty, upon taking charge of the property, to make out and file a correct inventory of all of the property of the defendant or other parties that comes into his hands, and report the same to the court; also to handle the property in a manner that will preserve the same to the best interests of the owner and the several creditors. 

Sec. 165. Classification of Claims.-When an estate of this character has been placed in the hands of a re-

RECEIVERS 

109 ceiver and suit brought to foreclose a lien and have the same sold, the court will generally provide that all other persons having debts against the property may intervene in the suit and prove up their debts and have the same passed to judgment. There are frequently a great variety of those debts: Liens prior to mortgage, as in the case of taxes and labor claims; liens such as the mortgage bonds; and plain debts without security.

The court will classify the different claims and those having liens will be paid ahead of those without liens. 

Taxes usually come first; labor liens second; mortgage liens third, etc.

If the owner of the property desires to prevent a sale, he may, by paying off the indebtedness, take the same back into his possession, and continue its operation; otherwise, the same will be sold at public sale and the money applied to the payment of the debts in the order above indicated, and the remainder, if any, will be paid to the original owner.

One of the duties of the receiver, in such cases, is the operation of the property. Receivers of railroads are subject to all of the laws governing the management of such roads as the company was subject to. Persons injured while the receiver has charge may recover damages and have the property applied to payment of same just as they could against the old management. 

The debts and obligations of the receiver are superior to the debts and obligations of the company, and must be paid first.

Sec. 166. Bond of Receiver.-The bond of the receiver may be given by some surety company, or by two or more good and solvent sureties in the amount fixed

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law by the court, conditioned ordinarily for the faithful performance of his duty, and the accounting for the funds that come into his hands.

Sec. 167. Liability of the Receiver.-In his personal capacity the receiver is liable only for his gross mismanagement or fraudulent application of the funds and property that come into his hands. For a court to attempt the operation of a business by a receiver is often a disastrous experiment resulting in dissipating the estate and eating it up in court cost.

CHAPTER 21 GARNISHMENTS 

Sec. 168. Nature. A garnishment proceeding is one of an inquisitorial character to locate the effects of the defendant, and is not of service against a defendant of financial standing in a thoroughly solvent condition, but is serviceable to the plaintiff in those cases where the defendant may have his property secreted or hid, or where it may not be visible so as to be subject to the ordinary writs of attachment or execution. 

An example would be: Where a defendant owned stock in some corporation; or where he had money on deposit in some bank; or where some third person owed the defendant a debt. The law provides, in such cases, for an inquisitorial proceeding, whereby the third person (or officer of the corporation) may be called upon to answer in court, under oath, if such defendant is credited on the books as the owner of stock in the corporation, or if such third person has any property of the defendant in his possession or is indebted to the defendant. 

Sec. 169. Procedure. Two Suits on the Docket.- 

The garnishment proceedings differ from the other ancillary proceedings in that there are two suits upon the docket-one is the plaintiff against the defendant, 111

and the other the plaintiff against the garnishee. In the first suit, the plaintiff is entitled, if his suit is well founded, to judgment for his debt; and if the garnishee reports into court that he has property of the defendant in his possession, the plaintiff will also obtain judgment against him for the amount of the debt, if the property of the defendant held by the garnishee equals in value the judgment obtained against the defendant in the other suit. 

This judgment against the garnishee, when settled, satisfies the judgment in both cases. That is, the third party, who has some of the defendant's money, or effects, pays the defendant's debt into court and the plaintiff recovers in this manner.

Sec. 170. Grounds for Garnishment.-Grounds for garnishment are statutory only, and the writ of garnishment will issue under three conditions:

Ist. If in the original suit a writ of attachment has issued and an attachment bond been filed, a writ of garnishment also may issue to any third person believed to have in his possession effects belonging to the defendant, without the giving of any additional bond other than that which was given in the attachment proceedings. 

2nd. When the debt is just, due and unpaid, and the plaintiff makes an affidavit to such fact, stating that the defendant has not, within his knowledge, property sufficient to pay such debt, and that the writ of garnishment prayed for is not sued out to injure or harass the garnishee.

In this second ground the plaintiff must give bond with two or more good and sufficient sureties for the protection of the defendant in event it should appear to the court that the writ was wrongfully sued out.

GARNISHMENTS 

113 3rd. Where the plaintiff has already procured a valid judgment, which has not been satisfied by the defendant. 

This is the safest ground for the plaintiff, and he runs less risk of being called upon for damages to the defendant for wrongfully suing out the garnishment. 

Sec. 171. Application for Writ of Garnishment.-If 

John Doe has a debt against Richard Roe, and the latter does not appear to have any property out of which such debt can be made, but John Doe learns that he has some money in the First National Bank, then John Doe, after having sued Richard Roe for his debt, will apply for a writ of garnishment, making the affidavit above stated, and give the bond required, and the Justice of the Peace or the clerk of the court in which such suit is brought, will issue a writ to the bank, and it is required to answer whether or not Richard Roe has any money in its possession. 

Sec. 172. Bond.-Under the second statutory ground, in which the plaintiff sues out the writ before obtaining judgment, or without having filed an attachment bond, he is required to give a bond in double the sum of the debt or amount in controversy, with two or more good and sufficient sureties for the protection of the defendant. 

If it afterward appears that the garnishment was improperly and wrongfully sued out, or without lawful reason or cause, the defendant may show that he has been injured in a business or social way, or that his reputation has suffered, and he may thereby recover damages of the plaintiff and his bondsmen.

Persons suing out a writ of garnishment should be very careful that the debt is just and due, and should be careful to ascertain that the other lawful conditions exist

before resorting to extreme measures. It is always better to obtain judgment first and then have the writ of garnishment issue, but in some cases the defendant would hide his property if delay of this kind were allowed, and in these instances it is the duty of the plaintiff's attorney to judge of the situation and take the risk. 

Sec. 173. Answer of the Garnishee.—The garnishee is not liable for the defendant's debt-he is merely a third party of whom the court, at the instance of the plaintiff, has made inquiry concerning the effects of the defendant, and for the protection of the garnishee the law allows him reasonable attorney's fees for answering in the case, which are taxed up as costs. Neither this charge nor any part of the court costs is paid by the garnishee, but he must answer or judgment will be taken against him by default.

The garnishee, when he files this answer, must swear to it, and it must truthfully show what property of the defendant is in the possession of the person or corporation garnisheed.

Sec. 174. When the Writ Will Issue.-A writ of garnishment, like a writ of attachment, cannot be issued in every suit, but only in those cases where the debt is just, due and unpaid, and is for some known stipulated amount. It cannot issue in a suit for tort, as for damages for personal injuries, or injuries to property where the amount in controversy is unknown and will be left to the discretion of the jury to determine. 

Sec. 175. Exempt Property.-It is not all debts, sums or funds that are subject to garnishment. The wages of a laborer are exempt and cannot be garnisheed, nor can funds in the hands of the court or a receiver of the court

GARNISHMENTS 

115 be levied upon. However, if the funds, in passing from the court to some agent of the defendant, can be located by the plaintiff before reaching defendant's hands, a writ of garnishment will apply, but the law will not permit writs of garnishment to issue to the courts and the officers thereof, nor to cities, counties or municipal corporations.

CHAPTER 22 EVIDENCE Sec. 176. Definition.-The term "evidence" includes all the means by which any disputed matter of fact is established or disproved: By oral testimony from witnesses, by records, books, documents, letters, guns, knives, or anything that would tend to throw light upon the matter under investigation.

The greater amount of evidence may be divided under the two principal heads of oral testimony and written evidence.
