Sec. 177. Oral Testimony.—The statement made by the witness from the witness stand to the court or the jury having the matter under investigation, is called oral testimony. The witness either comes voluntarily, at the request of one of the parties, or else is brought into court by processes issued by the clerk. The party desiring the attendance of a witness applies to the clerk of the court, or to the Justice of the Peace for a summons, or subpoena as it is called, and this is issued and signed officially, delivered to the sheriff or constable, who summons the party to appear in court on the day of trial. 

Being brought into open court, the witness is sworn to tell the truth concerning such matters as may be under 116

EVIDENCE 

117 investigation. He is first examined by the party offering him, and is then turned over to the other party for further inquiry, which is called cross-examination. After both parties have inquired about all the matters under investigation the witness is supposed to know of, he is dismissed and another witness is called, until the facts upon which the party relies are established. 

Sec. 178. Depositions.-The witness may not be able physically to come to court, or may be out of the reach of the court; and in all civil cases his depositions may be taken in writing.

The manner ordinarily of taking these depositions is to propound written questions; file these in the court that is to hear the case; give the opposite party or his lawyer five days notice that these questions have been propounded. The opposite party then, within these five days, propounds an additional set of questions, called cross-interrogatories. When the cross-interrogatories have been filed, or the five days have expired, the party can go to the clerk or the Justice of the Peace and obtain from him a commission, which is an official permit authorizing some officer to take the answers of the witness to the questions that have been propounded. The officer, usually a notary public, justice of the peace, or clerk of court, has the witness come before him, and writes his answers as numbered in the interrogatories. When all the questions have been answered, the witness signs his name and swears to them, and the notary or officer seals them up in an envelope and delivers them to the court in which the case is pending, either in person or through the mail.

Depositions of a witness cannot be taken without notice to the opposite party, therefore, the mere written

or sworn statement of the party cannot ordinarily be used as testimony, because the law requires that the opposite party must have notice and an opportunity to cross-examine the witness and inquire about such facts as would be of value to him.

Sec. 179. Documentary Evidence.-Written testi mony includes deeds, contracts, sometimes letters, or any other writings that prove or tend to prove or disprove, the matters in controversy. In a land suit, deeds or documentary records often constitute the principal portion of the evidence.

Sec. 180. Rules of Evidence.-In order that the matter under consideration be limited to the facts in issue and the investigation not cover too wide a range, the courts have established certain rules of evidence which must be adhered to. For example: If John Doe should sue Richard Roe on a note and Richard Roe should object to the payment of the note, it would be improper to prove that John Doe was a wild, disreputable character, because that would not prove the matter in dispute, and would be prejudicial to his rights, and immaterial. 

Sec. 181. The Best Evidence.-One of the elementary rules of evidence is, that the best evidence by which the contents of a written instrument can be proved must always be offered, rather than secondary evidence. For example: A witness cannot testify about the contents of a deed, because the deed itself is better evidence. Secondary evidence cannot be introduced until it is shown. that the original evidence has been lost or destroyed and cannot be produced. 

Sec. 182. Hearsay.-Hearsay is information that comes to the knowledge of the witness second or third

EVIDENCE 

119 hand, as rumors or reports. It is not admissible in evidence; it is not the best evidence; it is uncertain; it is unreliable; neither the original party, who made the statement nor those through whom it came were sworn, and testimony to be admissible must be given under oath. 

Sec. 183. Hearsay: Exceptions to the Rule.-There are certain facts, however, which can be established by hearsay testimony. In these particular instances hearsay testimony becomes the only available and direct testimony by which a thing may be proven or disproven. An example of this kind would be: In matters of pedigree one could testify who one's grandfather was, and what his name was, and who his father was. Although the person testifying would not know these facts, and it would be a matter of hearsay, yet the fact that this information has been accepted in the family and passed down from generation to generation, makes it acceptable to the court as competent testimony.

Dying Declarations, another exception, are admissible in original cases where the injured party is confronted with death and is cognizant of the fact that his life on this earth is at its end, and believes that he is soon to be in the Great Beyond. Here the solemnity of the situation is such that his statements as to who inflicted the injuries upon him, made at this time, may be treated as testimony and introduced in court. Such declarations will not, however, be accepted as to outside matters affecting anything further than the particular crime that produced the death or injury.

Another exception is known as res gesta, a thing happening in the same transaction, a spontaneous, contemporaneous exclamation, or utterance; some declara-

tion or act of the party superinduced and brought forth by the immediate transaction under investigation. For example: The exclamation of a person at the very moment that a murder is committed, is admissible as a part of the res gesta, that is, a part of the same transaction and occurrence. If the declaration is made after a sufficient lapse of time to allow thought or reflection, it is not res gesta and is not admissible. 

Another exception is reputation, which can be proved only by hearsay testimony. That is, what the rest of the community think of a person. The question is: Do you know Doe's reputation in the community in which he lives "for peace and violence?" Or for "truth and veracity?" Or for the particular matter under inquiry?

Market value of a piece of property, where it is sought to show such value, is another exception. The valuation is that which other people place upon it, and what it is supposed to bring on the market in cash, if offered at that time.

A further exception to the rule excluding hearsay, is that of expert testimony. For instance, a physician after qualifying as an expert, that is, stating that he is or has been a practicing physician, and knows of the symptoms of a certain disease, is permitted to say whether or not the patient was suffering with such a disease. So also a banker, who observes many and varied handwritings, may be offered as an expert witness to prove handwriting.

The conclusions of these expert witnesses are admissible, but the conclusion of an ordinary witness is not admissible, because the jury is supposed to draw the conclusion and make its own deductions. Opinions of expert witnesses are perhaps not properly classed as hearsay.

EVIDENCE 

121 

Sec. 184. Immaterial Testimony.-A very frequent objection to testimony is that "it is immaterial," by which is meant that even though the testimony was given it would not throw any light upon the matter in issue sought to be established or disproved. For example: In a suit for a horse, it would be immaterial to prove that the plaintiff was wealthy, as that would have nothing to do with the case. 

Sec. 185. Admissions.-The admissions of a party to a suit are often very important testimony. For example: 

Where A sues B for a tract of land and B claims the land by limitation, that is, that he has had it in possession for five or ten years, and has been claiming it during this period as his own. In such a case, if A produces a witness who offers to testify that he heard B say that he did not own the land and that it was not his own land, testimony of such declaration would be admissible. Any testimony including his own declaration to that effect, showing or tending to show that such party has not claimed the land as his own during the period in controversy, is admissible in evidence against him. 

Sec. 186. Confessions.-Admissions of guilt made by a defendant in a criminal case are not admissible against him unless made freely and voluntarily.

Persons accused of crime have been known, through fear, or persuasion, to confess their guilt, when in truth and in fact they were not guilty. The law, recognizing this, will not admit a confession in evidence against a defendant unless it appears that it was freely and voluntarily made. If made to an officer having defendant under an arrest, the effect of such confession must be explained to the defendant, and he must be warned that the statement may be used against him on his trial.

Sec. 187. Direct Evidence.-Evidence may also be subdivided into two other great divisions known as 

Direct Evidence and Circumstantial Evidence. 

Direct evidence is that which comes first hand, such as an eyewitness to a transaction.

Sec. 188. Circumstantial Evidence.--That variety of evidence which does not come from an eyewitness or some person present at the transaction, is called circumstantial evidence; such as seeing a person going in the direction of the place where a murder was committed; such as finding him shortly afterwards with blood on his clothing; such as finding shot in the body of the deceased similar to those in possession of the accused; and such as finding on the accused a bloody knife, or sharp instrument. 

Circumstantial evidence should be received with great care as many circumstances may link themselves together and appear to connect with crime a person who is entirely innocent. Well-linked circumstantial evidence, sometimes, however, makes a case as strong as direct testimony.

Sec. 189. Prima-Facie Evidence.- By the term 

"prima-facie evidence" is meant that degree or amount of testimony which, when offered, makes out a primafacie case; that is, that amount of facts, which, if not contradicted, would entitle one to a judgment at the hands of the court. The introduction of a note, duly signed, without any credits or markings on it, is prima-facie evidence of a debt; an insurance policy when offered with proof that all premiums have been paid, and that the insured is dead, makes out a prima-facie case against the insurance company, showing that the amount of the policy is due from the company.

EVIDENCE 

123 This prima-facie case may, however, be rebutted by the insurance company. They may say that the man is not dead, or that before his death, he did something that vitiated or invalidated his policy. It then becomes the duty of the plaintiff, if he can, to introduce other testimony to explain away, or rebut, the testimony given by the defendant, which he must do in support of the primafacie case he made out in the first instance.

Sec. 190. Privileged Communications.-Privileged communications are those confidential statements made between an attorney and his client, between a physician and his patient, and between a minister or a priest and his parishioner.

The court respects confidential statements and will not permit them to be inquired into. Thus, an attorney may hear the statement of his client, and the client may make it with the knowledge that if the attorney does not remain in his employ the attorney cannot betray him.

Sec. 191. The Burden of Proof.-By burden of proof is meant that the party who seeks relief of the court must prove the facts entitling him to such relief. 

The plaintiff usually has the burden of proof. In a criminal case, the state takes the burden of proof. When the plaintiff has produced enough facts to make out a prima-facie case, then the defendant must introduce testimony, and until the prima-facie case is made the defendant is not required to offer any proof whatever.

CHAPTER 23 OFFICERS CHARGED WITH THE ADMINIS- TRATION OF THE LAW 

Sec. 192. Groups.-The officers charged with the duty of administering the law may be divided into two groups: 2nd.

Ist. Those engaged directly in administering the law, consisting of Judges, Sheriffs, Clerks and Attorneys.

Those who are incident to the administration of government, consisting of the Treasurer, Assessor and Collector of Taxes, Superintendent of Public Instruction, and County Surveyor.

Sec. 193. The First Group.-Officers named in the first division are frequently called Administrative, or Ministerial Officers. The judge presides at the hearing of any case over which his court may have jurisdiction, and where necessary, after the conclusion of the hearing, makes the proper orders and judgments. 

Sec. 194. Clerks.-The clerk records the judgments rendered by the court, and under his direction and by authority of law, and by the direction of the attorneys of the court, issues the various processes to carry out orders and decrees of the judge. These writs, when so 124

OFFICERS CHARGED WITH ADMINISTRATION OF LAW 125 

issued, are placed in the hands of the sheriff, whose duty is to see that the writs so issued by the clerk are served and enforced. For instance, if it is a citation notifying a defendant that a suit has been filed against him, it is the duty of the sheriff to forthwith serve the defendant with a copy of the citation. If it is an execution issued upon some judgment directing the sheriff to enforce some decree of the court, or to collect money, he takes the execution and if necessary levies upon the defendant's property, disposes of it at public sale, and turns the proceeds into court for settlement of the judgment. 

Sec. 195. Attorneys.-Attorneys-at-law are classed as officers of the court because they are supposed to be skilled in the knowledge of the law, and by this means properly present the claims of the contending parties, that the court may intelligently act. The necessity for their service in this particular is all the more apparent when we stop to consider that many cases are so complicated in their nature that it would be impossible for the court itself to have time to properly understand the law and facts of the matter in controversy if not first prepared and systematically stated by the lawyer. It is made a crime by statute of the state for a lawyer to be unfaithful to his client by taking a fee from the opposite side or in any manner accepting a bribe to betray his interests, and all communications between the lawyer and client are confidential and the lawyer cannot be compelled to divulge them on the witness stand, or in the grand jury room.

Sec. 196. Officers of the County.-The officers of a county in Texas, from the lowest to the highest, consist of the following: In each precinct of the county there is a Justice of the Peace and a Constable. In

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law each county there are Commissioners, a County Treasurer, Assessor and Collector of Taxes, Superintendent of Public Instruction, Sheriff, County Attorney, County Clerk, District Clerk, County Judge, and in some counties with sufficient population, one or more District Judges.

Sec. 197. District Officers and State Officers.-The state is divided into a number of judicial districts according to population and volume of business. These districts are created by the Legislature and ordinarily consist of two or more counties. Some larger counties embrace two or three districts. In each of these districts, there is elected by the people a District Attorney for a term of two years, and a District Judge for a term of four years. The duties of the District Attorney are to aid the grand jury when they call upon him, in the investigation of a crime and to prosecute those who are indicted and endeavor to secure their conviction. The District Judge presides over this court, and in addition to the criminal matter presented by the District Attorney, hears the civil litigation that may be brought and presented by the lawyers of the bar. 

The District Clerk is elected for each county, and, unlike the District Judge and District Attorney, remains at the county seat of the particular county, and keeps the records and minutes of the District Court. 

Sec. 198. Judges of the Appellate Courts.-There are eleven Courts of Civil Appeal in the State of Texas. 

Each of said courts consists of three Judges, a Chief and two Associates. These judges are elected by the people of each of the districts into which the state is divided, the duties of these courts being not to try cases

OFFICERS CHARGED WITH ADMINISTRATION OF LAW 127 

originally, but to hear and determine appeals taken from the District and County Courts. These appeals, as elsewhere explained, consist of a record of the trial that was had in the lower court, and no original testimony is introduced. When the courts have examined these records and read the briefs of the lawyers filed in each case, they determine whether or not there were any errors committed by the lower court. If they find none, the case is affirmed. If they find that an error was committed, the case will be reversed and remanded for another trial in the lower court. 

Sec. 199. The Judges of the Supreme Court and the 

Court of Criminal Appeals.-There is one Court of 

Criminal Appeals in the State of Texas to which all appeals in criminal cases are carried and the appeal is heard upon the record only. This court consists of three judges elected by the entire state. Two Commissioners are selected to aid this court. 

The Supreme Court.-The Supreme Court also consists of three Judges elected by the state at large, and who compose a court of last resort in the state. This court is supplemented by six commissioners as set forth in Sec. 37. If any litigant is dissatisfied with a judgment rendered by any Court of Civil Appeals, he may ask for a writ of error to this Supreme Court, which will review the case and determine whether or not the Court of Civil Appeals has erred in its judgment. 

Sec. 200. Legislative Officers.-The state is divided into thirty-one Senatorial Districts, and from each of these districts there is elected one Senator for a term of four years. These thirty-one members compose the 

Upper House at Austin known as the State Senate. The

state is also divided into one hundred twenty-seven Representative Districts, and from each are elected one or more Representatives, who represent the people in the Lower House at Austin, called the House of Representatives, and which consists of 150 members. These two bodies constitute the Legislature of Texas and make the laws.

Sec. 201. The Chief Executive.-The Governor of the state is its Chief Executive. It is his duty to approve all laws passed by the Legislature, or to veto same, if they do not meet his approval. He has charge of the militia of the state, and is the Chief Commander of the state forces, unless they be called into federal service. He has the appointment of numerous officers and heads of various departments of the state. 

Sec. 202. Further State Officers. In addition to the

Governor and the officers named, we have the Attorney General, a very important officer, then the Comptroller of the Public Accounts, and many other public officials who are not so immediately connected with the administration of the law. 

Sec. 203. Jury Commissioners.-Returning to the county and its administrative affairs, it is the duty of the District Judge, at each term of his court, to appoint three of the county's best citizens as Jury Commissioners, to select from a list of the qualified voters of the county a sufficient number of jurors for each week of the succeeding term. This list of jurors is kept secret, placed in a sealed envelope, and left in the custody of the district clerk, who opens it before the convening of the next court, and has the sheriff to summon the jurors thus selected to appear to perform jury service.

OFFICERS CHARGED WITH ADMINISTRATION OF LAW 129 

Sec. 204. County Commissioners.-In each county there are elected four County Commissioners, who, with the County Judge, comprise the Commissioners Court. 

These Commissioners are the financial and legislative representatives of the county. All salaries and expenses to be paid out of the treasury must be approved by them. All the public buildings are built under their direction. All public roads and bridges are creatures of their work.

Notaries.-The Governor, by and with the consent of the State Senate, appoints a convenient number of notaries public. The term of office begins on the 1st of June following the Governor's election, and continues for two years. The principal functions of the notary are to administer oaths and take acknowledgments to instruments like deeds, etc. A man or woman twenty-one years of age may be appointed. A Justice of the Peace is a notary public by virtue of his office.

CHAPTER 24  Sec. 205. Scope and Purpose of the Chapter.-In order that the reader, or student, may be more familiar with the procedure of the District Court, where the principal portion of the litigation is conducted, I give in detail an abbreviated record of a land suit in the 

District Court.

Sec. 206. The Origin of the Suit.-D. C. Cross and 

W. H. Ross were neighbors and friends and lived on adjoining farms. There was a tract of land lying between their places, a small portion of which, about three acres in one corner, had been in Ross' possession for many years. So far as Cross or the general public knew, this three acres belonged to Ross' own tract, and was not a portion of the tract in controversy. Not knowing that Ross had any claim on this land, Cross bought this entire tract from the reputed owner, who, it seems, did not have good title. Cross took possession of a portion of the land and fenced it, put his deed on record, and paid taxes for about eleven years, when he learned, for the first time, that his neighbor, Mr. Ross, was also claiming the land. The latter forbade his cutting any 130

timber from the same, or in any other way using the premises. Cross applied to his attorneys, and being advised of his rights, filed a petition in the District Court.

Sec. 207. Petition. 

In The District Court of 

THE STATE OF TEXAS, 

County of Harrison. 

Harrison County, Texas. 

September Term, A. D., 1913.

To The Honorable R. B. Levy, Judge of Said Court: 

Now comes D. C. Cross, who will hereinafter be styled Plaintiff, and complaining of W. H. Ross, of

Harrison County, Texas, who will hereinafter be styled Defendant, respectfully shows to the Court: Ist.

That the Plaintiff is the owner in fee simple of the 

A. B. Collins Survey of land, situated in Harrison 

County, Texas, about 15 miles N. 47 W. from Marshall, on the Waters of Little Cypress, by virtue of Certificate 

No. 223, issued to A. B. Collins by the Board of Land 

Commissioners of Harrison County, March 4, 1844. Beginning at a stake in the S. E. corner of Manuel Gutterez Survey, from which a Post Oak bears N. 37 W. 

II vrs., a Black Jack brs. N. 29 E. 2-4/10 vrs., both mkd "J W." Thence North 160 vrs. a stake in S. W. corner of J. H. Murry's Survey. Thence East 581 vrs. to a stake W. N. W. corner of John Beaty's Survey. Thence 

S. 809 vrs. to a stake in the E. N. E. corner of James 

A. L. Baker Survey. Thence West 753 vrs. to a stake in the E. S. E. corner of J. R. McCarty's Survey. 

Thence North 649 vrs. to a stake in N. E. corner of

said McCarty's Survey, and in South Line of said Gutterez Survey from which a Pine brs. S. 5 E. 7 vrs. mkd. "X." Thence East 172 vrs. to the beginning, containing 103 acres of land, more or less. 2nd.

Plaintiff avers, if same be necessary, that he, and those under whom he holds, have been in actual, peaceable and adverse possession of the above described tract of land, holding the same under deeds duly registered, paying all taxes thereon, cultivating, using and enjoying the same for a period of more than five years next before the institution of this suit. 3rd.

Plaintiff shows that on or about the 1st day of March, 1913, while he was in peaceable possession, and in full enjoyment of the aforementioned premises, the Defendant, W. H. Ross, entered upon said land and with force of arms did violently dispossess and eject the Plaintiff therefrom, and now withholds the possession of said premises from this Plaintiff. 

Wherefore, premises considered, Plaintiff prays that citation issue in the terms of the law, and that the Defendant, W. H. Ross, be cited to appear herein; and upon final hearing of this cause, Plaintiff have judgment for the title and possession of the aforementioned premises, for damages in the sum of $100.00 as the value of the timber taken and appropriated by the said Defendant; and he prays for general relief.

HARRIS & DAVENPORT, 

Attorneys for Plaintiff, D. C. Cross.

Sec. 209. The Answer.-Mr. Ross, receiving the citation from the sheriff, went to consult a lawyer, whom he employed, and who filed for him an answer:

D. C. CROSS 

VS. 

W. H. Ross.

Number 961 

In The District Court of 

Harrison County, Texas. 

September Term, A. D., 1913. I. 

Now comes the Defendant, W. H. Ross, for answer herein. He demurs to the Plaintiff's petition, and says that same is insufficient in law, and of this he prays judgment of the court. II.

Further answering herein, the Defendant says that he is not guilty of the wrongs therein charged against him, and puts himself upon the country. 

III. 

Further answering herein, the Defendant says that he, and those under whom he holds, have been in actual, peaceable and adverse possession of the property described in Plaintiff's petition, cultivating, using and enjoying the same for a period of more than ten years next before the institution of this suit, all of which he is ready to verify.

IV. 

Said Defendant would further show that by reason of such possession and use, he is the owner of the land so described in Plaintiff's petition, and that Plaintiff's claim to said property casts a cloud over his title thereto. 

Wherefore, he prays that upon hearing hereof Plaintiff take nothing by this suit, but that Defendant re-

cover of and from the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross, title and possession of the land described in Plaintiff's petition. 

S. P. JOHNSON, Attorney for Defendant.

This was all done more than ten days prior to the convening of the District Court, all of which must be done within that period, except filing of the answer by Mr. Ross, which must be done by Tuesday, the second day of court.

Sec. 210. The Trial.-After the convening of the court, the judge began the calling of his docket, whereupon were listed by the clerk all the cases that were to be tried. Reaching the case of Cross vs. Ross, the attorney for the Plaintiff, Cross, announced ready for trial. 

The Defendant called a list of his witnesses, and finding them present, also announced ready for trial. Thereupon the judge instructed the clerk to prepare a duplicate list of the jurors present who had been selected and who were in attendance upon the court, and to furnish the attorneys for each of the litigants a copy of this list. This being done, the counsel for Mr. 

Cross inquired of the jurors if they were related within the third degree-as close as second cousins-to either of the parties to the suit; or if they had any fixed opinion about what should be done with the case, or any bias or prejudice towards the parties. 

After interrogating the jurors in this manner, each party retired into a private place with his lawyer and friends and there discussed the list of jurors to decide upon those most objectionable, each party being allowed to strike six names from the list. After each party had struck from this list the names that were to him most undesirable, the lists were then returned to the clerk,

who made up the jury of the first twelve men who remained on the list and whose names had not been scratched. These twelve were then placed in rows of chairs, called the jury box, and were sworn to try the case according to the law and evidence. 

Sec. 211. The Pleading. After the jurors were sworn, the Plaintiff's attorney stood before them and told them in a short, simple way, what the suit was about, and read to them the petition that he had filed in the court, which we have just seen in this chapter. 

After the reading of the petition, the Defendant's attorney arose, and read his answer, wherein he denied all of the matters that had been claimed and set up by the Plaintiff.

Sec. 212. The Testimony.-All of the witnesses in the case, both for the Plaintiff and for the Defendant, were now called around and sworn; whereupon the Defendant asked the "rule," by which is meant that the witnesses would not be permitted to stay in the court room so as to hear any other witness testify, and would not be permitted to talk to each other or to anyone else about their testimony or about the case, except that they might talk to an attorney in the presence of an officer. When the witnesses were placed under the rule, a sheriff conducted them to a waiting room outside of the court.

The Plaintiff then called his first witness, Jim Dawson, who testified that he knew the location of land in controversy, and knew that the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross, had built a pasture on this land prior to the year of 1905, and that he, himself, had used this in 1904 and 1905. 

The pasture was then two or three years old. Witness was then cross-examined by the Defendant.

The next witness called was A. J. Mings, who testified that he knew the pasture and while engaged in the cattle business, had leased it for two years from Mr. 

Cross, and that it was sufficient to hold the cattle; and that he knew of its being there for a number of years afterwards; and that on one occasion he heard the Defendant, Mr. Ross, complain that Cross was fencing up the grass on the range, but made no claim to the land in question; that he had known Mr. Ross intimately for many years, and they were indirectly related to each other, and had lived neighbors for a long period, and had never heard of Mr. Ross setting up any claim to this land, until about the time this suit was filed. 

J. S. Mings, Sid Johnson, Charley Stone and the

Plaintiff, D. C. Cross, all testified for Plaintiff substantially to the same facts already given. The Plaintiff then offered in evidence his deed to the land which had been duly recorded, also tax receipts for eleven years, and announced that he would rest. By this he meant that he had introduced enough testimony to entitle him to judgment in the event the other party did not introduce enough to overturn it. 

Defendant's Testimony.-The Defendant offered his witnesses, who consisted of some of the old family servants of the Ross family and his brothers and brothersin-law, all of whom testified that Mr. Ross claimed the land in controversy and had a small portion of it under fence. He offered no title, but claimed it purely under the ten-year limitation. After the conclusion of the testimony, the attorneys on each side presented their respective views of the law to the Judge. 

Sec. 213. Charge.--The court then delivered his charge, as follows:

D. C. CROSS 

VS. 

W. H. Ross

Number 961 Gentlemen of the Jury: 

In The District Court of 

Harrison County, Texas. Ist.

You are the judges of the facts in evidence, and of the weight to be given to the testimony of the witnesses. 

But, as to the law, you must be governed by the written instructions given you in the Charge of the Court. 

The burden of proof is upon the Plaintiff to establish his case by a preponderance of the evidence, and if not so shown, you will find for the Defendant. 2nd. "Peaceable possession" means such as is continuous, and not interrupted by adverse suit to recover the estate. "Adverse possession" is an actual and visible appropriation of the land commenced and continued under a claim of right inconsistent with and hostile to the claim of another. 3rd.

If the jury shall find from the evidence that the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross, has had five years peaceable and adverse possession of the premises in controversy, cultivating, or using and enjoying the same, and paying all taxes thereon, and claiming under a deed duly registered for five years before the date of the institution of this suit, then return a verdict for the Plaintiff for the land.

4th.

But if the jury shall find from the evidence that the 

Defendant, W. H. Ross, and his father before him, were in actual, peaceable, and adverse possession of the premises, using and enjoying the same for ten years before the institution of this suit, and further find that at the time of the entry of Plaintiff, Cross, upon the land, and during Plaintiff's possession, if any, of the premises, the Defendant, Ross, continued the adverse possession of the premises, then, if you so find, you will return a verdict for the Defendant, Ross, for all the land except the portion enclosed by a fence by Plaintiff, Cross.

RICHARD B. LEVY, 

Judge 4th District.

Sec. 214. Verdict.-After receiving the charge, the lawyers made arguments to the jury, who then retired to consider the case, and returned in court the following verdict: "We, the jury, find for the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross. 

ARCH NOBLE, Foreman.”

Sec. 215. Judgment.-Thereupon, the court entered judgment, a copy of which was as follows:

Number 961 D. C. CROSS 

VS.

In The District Court of 

W. H. Ross. 

Harrison County, Texas. 

September Term, A. D., 1913. 

On this, the 24th day of September, 1913, came on to be heard in the District Court of Harrison County, Texas, the above numbered and entitled cause, and came the Plaintiff and the Defendant into open court, and

announced ready for trial. Thereupon came a jury of twelve good and lawful men, to-wit: Arch Noble, and eleven others duly impanelled and sworn, and who, after hearing the pleading, evidence and argument of counsel, and after having received the Charge of the Court, retired to consider of their verdict. After duly considering the same, did, on the 24th day of September, 1913, return into court the following verdict: "We, the jury, find for the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross. (Signed) ARCH NOBLE, Foreman."

It is therefore ordered, adjudged and decreed by the Court that the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross, do have and recover of and from the Defendant, W. H. Ross, title and possession of the following described land, to-wit: 

Situated in Harrison County, Texas, on the waters of Little Cypress, about 15 miles N. 47 W. from Marshall, located by virtue of Certificate No. 223, issued to 

A. B. Collins by the Board of Land Commissioners of Harrison County, March 4th, 1844. (Here follows par ticular description of land as set out in Section 207.) 

Together with all cost of suit in this behalf expended; and that plaintiff have his writ of possession and execution for costs.

Sec. 216. Motion For A New Trial.-Within two days after the rendition of this verdict, the attorneys for the Defendant, Ross, filed a motion for a new trial, which was as follows:

D. C. CROSS 

VS. 

W. H. Ross.

Number 961 In The District Court of 

Harrison County, Texas. 

September Term, A. D., 1913.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law 

Now comes the Defendant, W. H. Ross, and moves the Court to grant him a new trial for the following good and sufficient reasons, to-wit: Ist.

The verdict of the jury is contrary to the evidence in this: The Plaintiff, Cross, showed no legal or record title to the land in suit, and all the testimony considered as a whole fails to establish any right of recovery under the statute of five years limitation. 2nd.

The Court erred in refusing to give special charge requested by the Defendant, W. H. Ross, which was as follows: 

"Gentlemen of the Jury: 

If you believe that W. H. Ross, and his father before him, B. C.

Ross, held actual possession of the land in controversy for ten years at any one period of time, then you cannot return a verdict for the Plaintiff, but your verdict will be for the Defendant, W. H. Ross.

Special Charge No. 8, Requested by Defendant. 

S. P. JOHNSON, Attorney."

After hearing this motion, the court overruled the same, to which action of the court the Defendant gave notice of appeal to the Court of Civil Appeals of the 

Sixth Supreme Judicial District of Texas, at Texar kana, and within twenty days filed an appeal bond.

CHAPTER 25 SUITS IN COURT OF APPEALS Sec. 217. Appeals, How Taken.-When either party is dissatisfied with the judgment of the trial court, he may appeal his case to the Court of Civil Appeals for his particular district. The manner of taking this appeal is by giving notice in open court of such intention, and within twenty days filing an appeal bond in such sum as will, in the judgment of the clerk, cover the costs of the case. The appeal thus taken is one purely of record, and no testimony is taken in the upper court; which court reviews the case entirely on the 

Statement of Facts and the Transcript of the Record. 

Sec. 218. Statement of Facts.-The Statement of 

Facts in the Cross-Ross case, as in all other cases, consists of a statement prepared by the parties and approved by the trial judge. In event the parties fail to agree on a statement, then the trial judge himself prepares it. The following would constitute an abbreviated 

Statement of Facts: 

143

D. C. CROSS 

VS. 

W. H. Ross.

Number 961 

In The District Court of 

Harrison County, Texas.

September Term, A. D., 1913. 

Be it remembered, that in the trial of the above numbered and entitled cause, the following facts were proven and testimony offered: James Dawson testified for the Plaintiff, D. C. Cross:

That he had known this land for a number of years and knew when Mr. Cross built his pasture on it, and that he had rented this pasture for two years from Mr. 

Cross. That he had never heard of any other person making claim to the land, except Mr. Cross. 

D. C. Cross, Plaintiff, testified for himself:

That he bought this land in July, 1894, and paid a valuable consideration therefor in cash. That he immediately placed the deed upon record and took possession of a portion of the tract of land by enclosing it within a pasture. That he has continuously claimed the land and paid all taxes on it for more than ten years since the date of purchase, and on up to the time of the suit. That he never heard of the defendant,

Ross', claim to this property until more than ten years after he had purchased it and had possession of a portion of it. That he had the land run out by a surveyor, and found for the first time that at the northeast corner of the tract, a narrow wedge-shaped strip of about three acres of the land was enclosed in Ross' field, who lived upon an adjoining tract to the north. That Ross laid claim to the timber that he had made upon the land and forbade him to use it, whereupon he brought this suit. That he had lived in close proximity to the land

SUITS IN COURTS OF APPEALS 

145 for more than twenty years, and never heard of Ross claiming it. (Several other witnesses testified to substantially the same facts as did Plaintiff and the witness, Dawson. In this abbreviated statement their testimony is not here produced.) 

The Plaintiff closed. 

W. H. Ross, Defendant, testified for himself:

That his father, B. C. Ross, had lived upon the W. H. 

Morris headright of land, situated to the north of the tract in controversy, and that after the death of his father he, himself, had lived upon it. That their joint possession extended back for more than thirty years, during all of which time he had had the small strip of three or four acres enclosed under the fence of the father's plantation, which lay to the north of the land in controversy. That he had repeatedly cut timber from this land, both for repairing fences and for saw logs, and that he and his father before him had claimed this land as their own. 

Nelse Field testified:

That he had been a family servant of the Ross family for many years, and heard Mr. B. C. Ross tell Judge 

Hendricks, many years before, that he, Ross, claimed the land across the field down to Bear Creek. He never knew of Mr. Ross having the land run out. He did not know where any of the lines were. There were about seven hundred acres of unenclosed land in this body, and if this particular tract of 103 acres had ever been separated from the rest of it, he did not know it, and if any portion of it lay to the south of Bear Creek, he

did not know it. He had, himself, cut timber from this land under Mr. Ross' instructions.

It further appeared in the testimony that there were several hundred acres of unenclosed land made up of three or four different headrights south of the Ross field, and that Mr. Ross had never had the portion that he claimed surveyed, nor had he paid any taxes on it except for the past three years, and that he had not paid upon the Collins head right, which was the tract in controversy, but he had, by mistake, paid upon the Baker headright, which lay to the west of the tract in controversy. (A number of other witnesses testified for Defendant, Ross, that the land in controversy had been claimed by Mr. Ross and timber had been cut from it, but none of them testified to the exact limit.) 

It further appeared that upon the death of B. C. 

Ross, who owned a great number of tracts of land, his children had divided his estate by partition deed, in which no mention was made of the Collins tract, the one now in controversy. The Plaintiff, Ross, however, contended that it was orally understood that he was to continue the claim of his father over this property. 

The Defendant closed.

We, the parties to the above numbered and entitled cause, agree that the foregoing is a true and correct statement of the facts proved and testimony introduced upon the trial of this case.

T. W. DAVENPORT, 

Attorney for Plaintiff. 

S. P. JOHNSON, Attorney for Defendant.

Approved: 

R. B. LEVY, Judge of the Fourth Judicial District of Texas. 

Sec. 219. Transcript.-In the above section was set out a Statement of Facts. This constitutes one portion of the record on which the Appellate Court considers the case. The other portion of the record is called the 

Transcript, which consists, usually, of a typewritten copy of all the pleadings of the parties in the order in which they were filed, and of certain other papers, and usually appears in the following order: 

I. Plaintiff's Original Petition. 2. 

Defendant's Original Answer. 

3. Charge of the Court. 

4. Verdict of the Jury. 

5. The Judgment of the Court upon the verdict. 

Motion for a New Trial. 

6. 

7. Order overruling this Motion, and Notice of

Appeal. 

8. Appeal Bond. 

9. Assignments of Error. 

10. Copy of the Bill of Costs. 

The above, when copied in full, generally constitutes the Transcript. The Pleadings, which are an important part of the Transcript, appear in Chapter 24. 

Upon these two records, the court acts in rendering its opinion.

Sec. 220. Assignments of Error.-The Assignments of Error, embodied in the Transcript above, consist of a succinct and carefully presented statement by the party taking the appeal, of the errors made by the court upon

the trial of the case, and by reason of which, he should have a reversal in the Appellate Court. 

The number of these Assignments depends entirely upon the nature of the case, and the view of counsel as to what the errors were. They may range from one to a hundred, or more, and usually start in the following order:

Now comes the Appellant, W. H. Ross, and assigns error as follows: etc.

Sec. 221. Hearing in The Appellate Court.-When the Statement of Facts and Transcript had been duly prepared and certified to by the clerk, they were forwarded to the clerk of the Appellate Court, who, in this case, resided at Texarkana, Texas, the Sixth Supreme

Judicial District of Texas. 

The case was placed upon the docket and in due order was reached by the court. The attorneys for the respective parties were notified of the day that the same was set down for hearing and given an opportunity to be present. The case was presented by oral argument to the court. In addition to the oral argument, the counsel filed briefs supporting their contentions, and citing authorities, that is, other cases and statements from books in support of their contentions. 

Sec. 222. Briefs.-The brief is a written or printed document usually argumentative in form, in which each of the Assignments of Error is taken up and treated. An effort is made to show the court why the same is good or bad, as a matter of law, and why it should not be sustained.

Sec. 223. Opinion of the Court.-After hearing the argument of the counsel, and considering the briefs, the

SUITS IN COURTS OF APPEALS 

149 court then examines the record, that is, the Statement of Facts and the Transcript, and decides what is right and lawful. If it finds no error has been made, the case is affirmed; if it finds that some error has been committed, it is reversed and remanded for a new trial; and if the court should believe that a new trial would not change the status of the case, it would reverse and render, that is, it would render judgment finally disposing of the case without sending it back for another trial.

These opinions are collected, printed in book form, and are to be found in the library of all lawyers under the title, "Southwestern Reporter," "Texas Supreme 

Court Reports," etc. 

Sec. 224. Writ of Error.-When the case has been finally affirmed, reversed, or rendered, the losing party may apply to the Supreme Court of the State for a Writ of Error. His application consists of a written petition to the court stating wherein, in his judgment, the Court of Appeals has erred. If the Supreme Court believes that his contention has merit, it will grant the Writ of Error, by which is meant, that the case is brought before that court for a hearing of the same sort as was had before the Court of Appeals, and should they be of the opinion that the Court of Appeals was in error, they will reverse the case and either send it back to the trial court, or finally dispose of the case. If they should believe that there is no real merit in the application for the writ, they will refuse the application, and the case is never presented in the Supreme Court. 

In the case before us, Cross vs. Ross, the judgment of the trial court was affirmed by the Court of Appeals, and the Supreme Court refused a Writ of Error, which finally ended the litigation.

CHAPTER 26 HISTORY OF A DAMAGE SUIT Sec. 225. Parties.-About the 1st of August, 1907, 

The Green Lumber Company, a Missouri corporation, was operating a large sawmill, manufacturing lumber in the timber belt of Marion County, Texas. B. C. Quitman was, at this time, in the employ of the said company, filling the position of edgerman. His duty was to receive the boards as they fell from the saw, and pass them on to a small set of saws where the bark edges of the planks or boards were trimmed off.

On the day in question, however, the regular sawyer, Berry Steel, was absent, and Quitman was directed to perform Steel's duty as a sawyer, this duty being to stand upon a large carrier on which the log was placed, and which played back and forth in front of an immense circle saw. A part of his duty was to adjust what was called the guide pin. This pin was where the ends of two wooden pegs protruded through the guide and fitted as a clamp on the saw just back of the teeth, which held the saw in position in order that the boards might be cut of a uniform thickness. 

This guide pin would be tightened, when it would become loose, by the sawyer twisting the threads of the 150

HISTORY OF A DAMAGE SUIT 

151 metallic portion of the pin, where it passed through the guide. In the absence of Quitman, the pin had been taken from the guide, and cotton waste or packing of some kind had been placed in the guide around the threads, so as to tighten the pin. Quitman did not know of this, and in attempting to adjust the pin, when it became worn at the end, found that the pin refused to move under the pressure ordinarily applied, and a wrench gave way on the head of the pin and precipitated his hand into the saw, cutting, mangling and permanently injuring and disabling the member.

Quitman says that he was exercising ordinary care and caution, and was doing his work in the usual way, and he would not have been hurt if the pin had been in proper repair and the cotton waste had not been placed therein. Feeling that he was hurt by reason of the company's negligence, Quitman demanded remuneration for his damages, and brought suit in the Marion County 

District Court. 

Sec. 226. Plaintiff's Petition.

THE STATE OF TEXAS, 

County of Marion.

In the District Court of 

Marion County, Texas,

April Term, A. D. 1908. 

To the Honorable Judge of Said Court: 

Now comes B. C. Quitman, a resident citizen of Harrison County, Texas, and hereinafter called Plaintiff, complaining of the Green Lumber Company, hereinafter called Defendant, a corporation duly incorporated and doing business in the State of Texas, and in Marion County, and having as its manager in Marion County,

D. D. Todd, upon whom service may be had, and for cause of action, Plaintiff represents to the Court: Ist.

That heretofore, to-wit, on August 7, 1907, as now and for long prior thereto, the Defendant was operating a large saw mill in Marion County, Texas, operating railroads and tramways by means of which an extensive lumber business was carried on and conducted by the said Defendant in the manufacture of trees as raw material taken from the forest and cut into lumber. 2nd.

Plaintiff avers and shows to the Court that on or about the said date, to-wit, August 7, 1907, he was regularly employed in the service of the said Defendant, Green Lumber Company, the position held by him being that of edgerman, whose duties are to receive the broad boards from the saw, and by means of smaller saws, divide them into proper widths and take off the bark edges of the planks. 3rd.

That on said date, however, the Plaintiff was by the direction of the Defendant removed from his regular place of employment, with the duties of which he was familiar, and transferred to another more hazardous and dangerous place of work, i. e., that of sawyer. 4th.

Plaintiff shows that the duty of the sawyer is to control and direct the movements of the carriage upon which the large logs are placed to be sawed into boards, and that said carriage plays back and forth in front of a large circle saw, which cuts the boards from the logs.

HISTORY OF A Damage SUIT 

153 5th.

That said saw, in its revolutions, is held in position by what is known as a guide and guide-pin. That the end of said guide-pin nearest to the saw is made of wood securely fastened in the metallic part of said pin, which is screwed into the guide by means of threads, and as the wood in said pin, which holds the same in place, wears away, the said pin is, by means of the threads on the metallic part, screwed up tighter to hold the same in place. That in the regular operation of the saw mill machinery it becomes necessary to frequently tighten this pin to correctly gauge the saw so that the lumber cut may be of proper widths and thickness. 6th.

That the metallic end of said pin is square, and by means of a wrench fitting over these square corners, the pin is turned and made to tighten upon the saw, and that this operation is ordinarily and usually done while the saw is in motion, and that when the pin is of the proper length, and the wrench and implements by which the same is operated, are in good condition, the work may be done without danger and with comparative ease. 7th.

Plaintiff shows that the guide-pin used by the Defendant on this occasion, was entirely too short, being only a few inches in length, making it necessary for Plaintiff to place himself very near the front of the saw in operating the guide. That the metallic end of the pin, on which the wrench is placed to turn the same, had been worn smaller, and the square corners of same had been partially worn away, and that the wrench furnished Plaintiff, and which was used to operate this guide pin,

was not adjustable, but was made out of one piece of iron or metal, and that the same had been worn larger so as to loosely fit around the end of the guide-pin. 

That the Defendant had, without knowledge of the Plaintiff, tightened the threads and made the guide-pin harder to turn, so that it required more pressure and greater effort on the part of the Plaintiff to move the pin on this particular occasion than formerly, and that when Plaintiff attempted to adjust the said guide, as was his duty to do, the wrench turned and slipped off the guide and threw his hand in front of the saw while same was in a state of operation, and that as a result his thumb was cut off, and his two first fingers cut to pieces by the teeth of the saw as it revolved, before Plaintiff could remove his hand, and that said hand has been permanently stiffened and rendered almost useless to Plaintiff. That the thumb is gone; that the first finger, although partially healed, remains perfectly stiff, and in a useless condition, and is a constant source of pain and irritation; and that the third finger has been rendered stiff and unwieldy by the said injury. 

That as a result of said injury sustained by Plaintiff, he has suffered great mental and physical pain. That he could not rest or sleep for many nights following the accident.

That he lost three months time from his work, and that his services were worth to himself and family the reasonable sum of $100.00 per month.

That he paid the physician to dress his wounds and attend him during his illness the sum of $50.00, which is a reasonable charge. That he expended for medicine and liniments in treating said wounds, the sum of $10.00, which is a reasonable charge.

HISTORY OF A DAMAGE SUIT 

155 

That the loss of his thumb and the stiffness of his fingers incapacitates him from laboring as he formerly could and did. That he has not the strength in the hand and cannot use it as conveniently as formerly on account of the absence of said thumb, and the stiffness of said fingers.

Plaintiff shows that he is a man in the prime of life, and up to the time of his injury was in good health, and is thirty-eight years of age and has a wife and children to support.

Plaintiff shows that he has no profession by which he could earn support for livelihood, and that he depends upon manual labor for a support for himself and family, and that his ability to earn a living has been greatly impaired by said injuries, which are permanent, and from which he will never recover so long as he lives. 

Plaintiff further shows that the condition of his hand, thumb and fingers is such that they are constantly subject to hurts and injuries because of their sensitive, stiffened condition, and from which he will suffer so long as he lives. 8th.

Plaintiff shows that his said injuries were caused by negligence and carelessness on the part of the Defendant, its agents and servants, and but for such negligence and carelessness the Plaintiff would not have been hurt and injured and damaged as he was.

Plaintiff shows that the Defendant was negligent in not supplying him with reasonably safe tools with which to work said machinery.

That it was negligent in not supplying a guide-pin of greater length for the safety and protection of its employees.

That it was negligent in permitting and allowing the said guide-pin to remain in its worn and defective condition.

That it was negligent in supplying Plaintiff with an old wrench too large for the top or end of guide-pin on which it was to be used. 

That it was negligent in tightening the pin where the threads of same were to turn in the metallic part of the guide without the knowledge of the Plaintiff. 

That it was negligent in placing the Plaintiff in a position of great hazard and danger without first having warned him of the extra hazard and condition of the machinery, this not being his regular place of employment. 9th.

That it was the duty of the Defendant to have furnished the Plaintiff with reasonably safe tools and machinery with which to work, and it was its duty to warn the Plaintiff of the extra hazard of the work that he was required to perform in the new place of service where they had now placed him, and that Defendant was negligent in not performing said duties, and that by reason of said negligence, Plaintiff was injured, and would not have been injured had it not been for the negligence and carelessness of said Defendant, its agents and servants; and that by reason of the said negligence, the Plaintiff was injured as above set out, and was made to suffer and will continue to suffer throughout the remainder of his life to his damage in the sum of Eight Thousand Dollars.

The premises considered, Plaintiff prays that Defendant be cited to appear and answer herein; that he

HISTORY OF A DAMAGE SUIT 

157 have judgment against the Defendant for the sum of 

Eight Thousand Dollars; for all costs of suit, and for general relief, and he will ever pray.

T. W. DAVENPORT, 

Attorney for Plaintiff.

Sec. 227. Defendant's Answer. 

B. C. QUITMAN 

VS. 

GREEN LUMBER COMPANY. 

Ist.

In the District Court of 

Marion County, Texas,

April Term, A. D. 1908. 

Now comes the Defendant, Green Lumber Company, and demurs to the Plaintiff's petition filed herein, and says same is insufficient in law, and of this it prays judgment of the Court. 2nd.

Further answering herein, the Defendant denies each and every allegation in Plaintiff's petition contained, and demands strict proof of the same. 3rd.

Further answering, the Defendant says that the Plaintiff, B. C. Quitman, was acting in the original scope of his employment, and the wrench and guide-pin were in full, open view, and that if Plaintiff was injured, he assumed the risk and cannot recover. 4th.

Further answering herein, Defendant says that at the time of the injury, Plaintiff was using his left hand instead of his right, and that he was, himself, negligent in the manner in which he attempted to adjust said guidepin, and was hurt by reason of his own negligence, and cannot recover.

Wherefore, Defendant prays that Plaintiff take nothing by this suit, and that he go hence, and that the Defendant recover his costs. 

ARNOLD & BENEDICT, Attorneys for Defendant.

On the trial of the case, the testimony of Quitman and other witnesses showed the case to be substantially as stated in his petition. 

Sec. 228. Result of Trial.-After hearing the testi mony, the court, the Honorable P. A. Turner, Judge, believing that the Plaintiff himself was guilty of negligence, or that he would not have been hurt, instructed the jury to return a verdict for the defendant company. 

Sec. 229. Hearing in the Court of Appeals.—Upon hearing of this case in the Court of Appeals at Texarkana, the court there was of the same opinion as the trial court, and affirmed the verdict. The Plaintiff, Quitman, then applied to the Supreme Court at Austin for a Writ of Error, which was granted.

The case was duly submitted to the Supreme Court by brief and oral argument, as it had been in the Court of Civil Appeals, which court overruled the judgment of the Court of Appeals and the trial court, and held that the action of the defendant's agent in not having the guide-pin properly repaired and in attempting to repair it by putting waste therein was the act of the company itself. A corporation can act only through its duly constituted agents. It was the duty of the servant, Quitman, to operate the machinery, but it was the master's duty to keep it in repair so that it could be operated with reasonable safety.

HISTORY OF a Damage SUIT 

159 In obedience to the mandate of the Supreme Court, the case was tried over again at Jefferson, Texas, in Marion County, with judgment in favor of the Plaintiff, Quitman.¹

Sec. 230. Workmen's Compensation. 

Since the trial of the Quinn case, 118 S. W. 733, the

Legislature has enacted what is known as the Workmen's Compensation law, which provides for some compensation to a workman injured in the scope of his employment regardless of whether the injury was the result of the employer's negligence or not. 

Farmers, railroads and domestic servants are not covered by the provisions of this Act, but all factories, mills and construction work of every kind employing labor are embraced within its terms. 

Subscribers.-Every corporation or person above outlined may take out compensation insurance with some company authorized by the laws of Texas to write such. insurance, and in event an employee sustains an injury while on duty he receives sixty per cent of his wages during the time of his disability, provided, however, that such compensation shall not be less than $7.50 per week, nor more than $20.00 a week during disability for a term not exceeding four hundred weeks. The loss of a hand, foot or eye is covered by specific items within the law fixing the amount of the recovery.

Industrial Accident Board.-When any employer of labor who has become a subscriber has an employee 1 For a history of a case in most respects similar to the Quitman case see: 

118 S. W., 733, B. C. Quinn vs. Glenn Lumber Co. 

126 S. W., 3, B. C. Quinn vs. Glenn Lumber Co. 

140 S. W., 863, B. C. Quinn vs. Glenn Lumber Co. 

149 S. W., 285, B. C. Quinn vs. Glenn Lumber Co.

injured or where any employee sustains an injury, the facts shall be reported to the Industrial Accident Board. 

It is the duty of this Board to hear all controversies between the corporation subscriber and the workman, and when the Board has made its award, if either party is dissatisfied therewith, an appeal may be made to the courts.

When the award of the Industrial Accident Board is unacceptable to either party, a suit may be filed in the proper court having jurisdiction of the matter and may be tried by the court or jury and the award of the Industrial Accident Board may be lowered or raised or set aside entirely, as may be found by the judgment of the court.

One of the primary differences between a suit for negligent injury and a suit under the Workmen's Compensation law is that under the latter the workman is entitled to some compensation, regardless of the negligence of the employee, though the compensation may be smaller than would have been allowed by a jury in the regular damage suit case.

PART THREE SUBSTANTIVE LAW 

OR 

THE LAW AS IT IS THE RIGHTS AND DUTIES OF PERSONS 

Having, in the preceding chapters of Part Two, illustrated the practice of law, we will now more fully treat the subject in a substantive way, and will only incidentally refer to the manner of its administration already discussed.

Substantive law is to the average student a dry subject, but when the practical application is suggested to 161

the mind, much of the supposed dryness disappears; and for this reason, we have first introduced Legal Procedure so that in the succeeding chapters the student may readily make the application of each subject as presented. 

No attempt will be made to present all the law in these chapters, but only those portions with which the average man comes in contact.

CHAPTER 27 CONTRACTS Sec. 231. Definition.-A contract is an agreement reached between two or more parties to do some lawful act. It may be either oral or written. 

Sec. 232. Oral Contracts.-An oral or verbal con tract is one that is not reduced to writing. In most instances such contracts are just as valid and binding as written contracts, but their proof is much more difficult, and they are much more susceptible of giving rise to misunderstandings and disagreements. 

Sec. 233. Exceptions.-There are a few instances where oral contracts or agreements are not binding, as for example: A promise to pay a debt of another is of no force and effect unless in writing. Also a promise to lease or transfer an interest in real estate for a longer period than one year is void unless in writing.

Sec. 234. Written Contracts.-Written contracts em brace a wide range of subjects and forms, and perhaps the instances where the parties actually enter into writ ing, calling the instrument a contract, represent but a small portion of the many written contracts. A letter, making a proposition and the acceptance of the propo- 163

sition by the other party, constitutes a contract; so with a telegram and the answer accepting. Deeds, notes, bills of lading, insurance policies, and a vast number and variety of similar documents, come within the general term of contracts.

Sec. 235. Essentials of a Contract.-The essentials of a contract, either oral or written, are, that the minds of the parties must meet; that is, A must offer to do certain things in consideration of B doing other things, and when B accepts A's proposition and agrees to do his part of the undertaking, the minds of the parties have met and the contract is completed. The writing is merely the evidence of the contract.

There must be a consideration in every contract. It may be a sum of money, or service to be performed, or it may be love and affection.

When a party fails to deliver the consideration, such as the service or money promised, the contract is thereby broken and fails for want of consideration. 

Sec. 236. Illustrations.-The illustrations here given will be hereinafter more fully treated under their particular heads, but will be here briefly treated to illustrate the present subject.

Sec. 237. Landlord and Tenant.-In cases of renting or leasing lands or buildings, contracts are always made, and very frequently entered into in writing in a formal manner. The owner is styled the Landlord, and the renter or lessee is styled the Tenant. 

The first element of this contract is: The landlord has a farm or building subject to lease, and the tenant has a need or use for the property. A price, or the terms

CONTRACTS 

165 of the lease are stated and adjusted between the parties, and if agreed to the same become a contract. If it is for a longer period than one year it must be put in writing and should be witnessed by one or more disinterested persons, or acknowledged before a notary public. To become a matter of record, it must be acknowledged before a notary public. 

Sec. 238. The Use of Money.-Another very frequent basis of contracts is the use of money, and the security for its payment, such as mortgages, notes, bonds and like obligations. 

Sec. 239. Bonds.-When a nation, state, county or city desires to borrow money for the purpose of making some public improvements, such as building courthouses, public roads, or for any other lawful subject, it authorizes, by proper ordinances, or statutes, the issuance of its obligations, called bonds, payable at some time in the future, frequently forty years, the interest on which ranges from two to six per cent, very frequently about five per cent.

This interest is payable either annually or semi-annually, as will be shown by a small coupon attached to the bond, there being as many coupons as there may be installments of interest, and when the last coupon is clipped the principal is due and is to be paid. 

An essential of these bonds is that there must be a tax levied at the time they are issued, or means provided whereby the interest will be paid, and the principal taken care of at final maturity. 

The issuance of these bonds is a means of borrowing money, the bonds being placed upon the market and sold to the highest bidder. They sometimes bring more than

par, and most states and cities have laws providing that they shall not be sold for less than par. 

The purchaser of these bonds holds the obligation of the city to repay his money, with the interest therein provided, and when he has purchased the same a contract has been entered into between him and the state, city or county issuing said bonds. 

Railway companies and large corporations also issue bonds for the purpose of borrowing money. Their bonds, instead of having a tax levied, are secured by a mortgage on their property. This mortgage is placed in the hands. of a trustee, who holds the same for the benefit of all parties who may become the owners of the bonds issued. 

When the bonds have been issued and sold, the purchaser and the corporation have made a contract, whereby the corporation has agreed to repay the purchaser his money with interest, and the trustee has agreed to hold the mortgage on the company's property and see that the same is paid and the contract complied with. 

Sec. 240. Notes and Mortgages.-A more simple form of contract, and one most generally employed by private individuals, as well as by banks and corporations, is by the giving of notes instead of bonds. 

Thus: If A borrows of B $1,000.00 and gives his note with 6% interest, payable at some time in the future, B thus loans A a sum of money upon the note so given and this constitutes a contract wherein A has promised to pay B a sum of money at a fixed period. 

Sec. 241. Insurance.-There are many varieties of insurance, the principal ones being fire insurance and

CONTRACTS 

167 life insurance, both of which have also a great many different forms.

The insurer is practically always a corporation, or an association, that undertakes for the payment of so much money to insure the life or property of the other, called the insured, in a specific sum, which sum is the amount of the policy, and the consideration paid for such policy is called the premium.

When a company issues its policy and undertakes and agrees to pay so much upon the death of the party, or the destruction of his property, and the insured accepts such policy and pays the premium therefor, they have entered into a contract.

The details of this subject of insurance will be treated in a chapter of its own at the proper place in this work. 

It is here given to illustrate the subject of contracts. 

Sec. 242. Breach of Contract.-When either party has contracted to perform any undertaking, as above illustrated, and the other party relies upon such contract, he has a right to expect that the same will be carried out, and if the other party neglects, fails or refuses to perform his portion of said contract, such failure constitutes a breach for which a suit may be maintained to either enforce the contract and require the party to perform same, or to collect the damages sustained by reason of party's failure.

Sec. 243. Sacredness of Contracts.-A contract should not be lightly considered, or made with undue haste, as it may mean the doing or undoing of the parties, and when once made, regardless of the consequences, each party should live up to it, until the word of the other is

given, or the consent had, to terminate same. A man's word should be his bond. A verbal contract is as sacred as is a written obligation, and the man who lives up to his contract without evasion, is the man who is esteemed by the business world, and whose financial standing is good.

CHAPTER 28 LANDLORDS AND TENANTS 

Sec. 244. Definition.-Any contract, either express or implied, wherein the parties occupy the relationship of owner and tenant, or lessor and lessee, is referred to in our Statutes under the general subject of Landlord and Tenant.

It is not always necessary that the landlord should be the owner, as the lessee may become a landlord by subletting the premises, or a portion thereof. 

These contracts between the landlord and the tenant are frequently in writing, and where they extend for more than a year should always be in writing, and a copy kept by each party. 

An express contract is one the terms of which are stated in words. For example, where A, on a certain date, enters into a verbal or written agreement with B to lease B's house or farm for a period of ten months. from said date at a rental of $50.00 per month, this would be an express contract because the terms are stated (expressed) in words.

An implied contract is one, the existence and terms of which are manifested by conduct. For example, where 

A occupied B's house for the period of ten months and 169

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law continues to occupy it after that period, nothing being said as to what the future rent is to be, there is an implied contract that A shall continue to pay the same rental per month that he has been paying, until some other contract or agreement is made with B. 

Sec. 245. Tenancies-Terms.—A tenancy may be for a term agreed upon in a contract, as for a fixed number of months, or years; it may be from year to year, from month to month, at will or at sufferance. Tenancies for the term are those where the parties expressly agree when the lease shall terminate, and may be for a number of years or any shorter period. Tenancies from year to year are most frequent in the rural or farming districts, where the nature of the employment, namely, the producing and harvesting of the crop, requires the entire season. 

These tenancies are frequently made orally and the tenant tills the land for a certain interest in the growing crop. 

Month to month tenancies are most frequent in cities and towns, and occur where houses or buildings are rented at a fixed sum per month, and may, at the option of either party, terminate at the end of any month. 

Tenancies at will occur where the tenant occupies the premises during the pleasure of the landlord, and such tenancies are subject to his orders and demands for possession at any time upon reasonable notice.

Tenancies at sufferance occur usually in cases where the tenant, without any agreement with the landlord, continues to hold the premises for a further period of time, as for another month or another crop. In such cases, as we have seen, there exists an implied contract that the tenant will pay the same rental as for the last succeeding period.

LANDLORDS AND TENANTS 

171 

Sec. 246. The Landlord's Lien.-In tenancies upon the farm, whether in writing or not, the landlord has a lien, superior to other liens, on the crop and on all animals, tools and other property furnished the tenant with which to make a crop, and this lien exists on said crop and property while on the premises and for thirty days thereafter. This lien, however, applies only to such animals as shall be furnished the tenant to make a crop, and does not include those brought by him to the premises. 

In cases where houses are rented the landlord has a lien on all property in the house for the payment of his rent for the entire term thereof, not to exceed a period, however, of one year. If the tenancy, however, is from month to month, the landlord's lien, except for the immediate month, is inferior to a prior mortgage given upon the property. But, if the landlord has a rent contract, for a fixed period, then the lien is superior to the mortgage for the amount of rent that has accrued, or would accrue during said period, not exceeding twelve months. 

Sec. 247. Distress Warrants.-When the tenant re fuses to pay his rent, and the landlord has reasonable grounds to believe that the tenant intends to defraud him of its payment, he may go before a Justice of the Peace. and make oath of such fact, stating that the amount sued for is for rent and advances; and also that such warrant is not sued out for the purpose of vexing and harassing the defendant, but for the protection of the landlord. A writ will then issue directing the officer to take into his custody the property upon which the lien is claimed. 

This oath, or affidavit, may be made by the landlord, or by his agent or attorney, before a Justice of the Peace, and if the amount of property distrained, or taken into custody, does not exceed $200.00, then the justice shall,

after citation has been duly issued and returned, try and dispose of the case, foreclose the landlord's lien, and sell the property to pay his claims and demands. 

If, however, the value of the property distrained exceeds $200.00 in valuation, then the warrant shall be made returnable to the County Court, where the case will be tried; if it exceeds $500.00 and is not more than $1,000.00 it may be tried in either the County or District 

Court; but if the value of the property distrained exceeds $1,000.00, then the justice shall make the warrant returnable to the District Court, where the parties will have their rights adjudicated as in other suits. 

The remedy of the landlord against the defaulting tenant, seeking to make way with his crops or with property from his house, is by distress warrant, which is always issued out of the Justice Court, but made returnable to the court having jurisdiction of the amount. 

The proceedings are very similar to those of sequestration.

Sec. 248. Exemptions.-The household goods, tools and implements, books and other exempt property of the family are not subject to the landlord's lien, but his stock of merchandise or other property stored, as well as the crops and material furnished to grow same, are subject to this lien.

Sec. 249. Rights of the Tenant.-The tenant has the right to stipulate the terms of the tenancy in the rent contract, and to require the landlord to live up to same, and in case of a breach, to sue the landlord to recover the damages occasioned thereby.

Should the landlord distrain his property, the tenant has the right to replevy it by giving bond with two or

LANDLORDS AND TENANTS 

173 more good and sufficient sureties, conditioned that he will pay whatever sum the court shall determine is due from him to his landlord. When this replevy bond is filed, he has the right to take the property back again into his possession, but he and his sureties will be bound to pay, in cash, the amount of the judgment, if any, that the landlord may recover. The tenant, if he so elects, can refuse to replevy his property and sue the landlord for conversion.

Tenants desiring to sublet property should have the right to do so given them in the contract of lease, otherwise the landlord is not bound to respect the rights of the subtenant, as he may be a party to whom the landlord would not, in the first instance, have rented his premises.

CHAPTER 29 COMMERCIAL PAPER Sec. 250. Definition.-The term "Commercial Paper" 

refers to an obligation or promise in writing of some person to pay a stipulated sum of money to the holder of the instrument, as in the case of a promissory note.

One of the distinguishing features of this paper is its negotiability, by which is meant, the right of the holder to transfer it to another party, or to several succeeding parties in turn, the holder of which becomes the owner of the original obligation, and to whom the maker of the paper is bound in said sum of money.

Some of the most common forms of negotiable paper are promissory notes, drafts, checks and bonds. 

Sec. 251. Obligor, or Maker, Payee and Holder. The original obligor, or the person who signs or draws the paper, and who is personally liable for the amount named, is usually called the Maker, and the party to whom the same is payable and who becomes the first holder or owner of said paper is ordinarily known as the Payee.
