If this Payee desires to sell the obligation, he may transfer the same to a third party, who then becomes the holder of the paper, and this third party has all of 174

COMMERCIAL PAPER 

175 the rights of the original owner, and may maintain suit against the original maker in his own name to enforce the payment.

Sec. 252. Endorser. When the holder of any negotiable paper, whether he be the original payee or some subsequent owner, desires to transfer the same, and for the purpose of so doing, writes his name upon the back thereof, he becomes an endorser. Here a new feature is added to the contract as between the original parties, because by reason of the endorsement the endorser also becomes liable to the last holder of said paper, and by reason of his endorsement promises to pay the same in the event that the maker does not, which promise constitutes a new contract between this endorser and the subsequent owners of the paper. So on with each endorsement, so that, in cases where the negotiable paper has been transferred several times and the original maker refuses to pay the same, the holder may not only sue the original maker, but also each of the endorsers thereon. 

Any of the endorsers sued on, or included in the suit, have the right to have all endorsers prior to them brought into the suit, Sec. 253. Assignments Without Recourse.-Any person desiring to transfer paper without becoming liable to the subsequent holders, should, after signing his name for the purpose of endorsement, write thereunder the words, "without recourse," thus: "John Doe, without recourse." In such case the purchaser or holder of the paper takes notice of the fact that he must look to the original maker of the paper and not to the endorser. 

If, however, the person transferring the paper has no doubt about its genuineness, or of the ability of the

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law maker to pay it, he generally signs it in the ordinary way and does not reserve to himself the protection of signing without recourse, as this has the tendency to cast suspicion, or a cloud, over the paper. In cases where the paper has been endorsed without recourse, the purchaser should inform himself before paying out his money, as to the responsibility and financial standing of the original maker. 

Sec. 254. Innocent Purchaser.-If the original maker of a note or an obligation does not receive value for it when he parts with it, or if at the time of its execution he has some outside agreement or contract with the payee that is not performed, he may generally urge this against the payment of the note. But, if the holder of this note, at any date before its maturity, sells the same, or transfers it in any way, for a valuable consideration, then the holder becomes an Innocent Purchaser. The latter is not a party to any of the previous understandings and agreements between the original parties, and can enforce the payment of the note upon the terms specified thereon. 

If, however, this purchaser should receive knowledge, or notice, in any form or manner, of the fact that the original owner repudiated the note, or that there was a private agreement or understanding concerning its payment, then he cannot become an innocent purchaser, and he but stands in the place and stead of the original holder, with only his rights under the contract.

Sec. 255. Purchaser After Maturity. The buyer of negotiable paper, after maturity thereof, cannot become an innocent purchaser. He is required to take notice of the fact that the paper is past due, and he may, for this reason, take into consideration the fact that the maker

COMMERCIAL PAPER 

177 would have paid it at maturity if there had been no objection to same. The purchaser after maturity acquires the same rights as the original holder of the paper, and no more.

Sec. 256. Orders and Drafts.-In many instances in commercial dealings one party draws upon another, or writes an order directing another to pay a certain sum of money to a third person named in the paper, and to charge the same to his account. This draft holds the person drawing it liable for the amount thereof, as a note would. If the party upon whom it is drawn honors the draft, he may either pay the same or write the word "accepted" across the same, in which instance he then becomes responsible and liable for the amount of the paper, as he thereby honors the draft and promises to pay same. 

Sec. 257. Protest.-If, upon presentation of the draft or order to the third party (often a distant bank) this party refuses to honor same and neither pays nor accepts it, then the holder of this draft or order must go back to the original drawer or writer for his money. In order to do this he should have the paper protested before a notary public, wherein he makes affidavit that he did, at a certain time and place, stating it, present the said paper to the person upon whom it was drawn, and that the party refused to pay or honor the same.

When it is thus protested and notice given to the original drawer, the several endorsers thereon, if any, become each in turn responsible to the other to refund the said money, with the protest fee. 

Sec. 258. Suits.-In Texas it is not necessary to protest a paper in order to fix liability upon the original

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law drawer, if the party holding the same prefers to institute suit.

If suit is brought, it is not necessary to protest said paper, but the general practice prevailing in many banking institutions is to protest. Of course, after the paper has been duly protested, if the original drawer should then refuse to pay same, suit will still become necessary, but this rarely occurs as the protested draft is as valid an obligation of the original drawer as his promissory note would be. 

Sec. 259. Actions Upon.-Suit to enforce the payment of any negotiable paper must be brought in the court having jurisdiction of the amount and venue as to the parties.

As before explained, the maker is entitled to be sued in his own county and precinct, unless the paper is made payable at some other point, in which instance he may be sued at that point. As, for instance: If John Doe of 

Marshall, Texas, gives his note to Richard Roe, payable at Dallas County, then Richard Roe may sue upon this note either in Marshall or Dallas. 

Suit should not be brought upon a promissory note, or other negotiable paper, until the same is due and payable. A suit brought before maturity cannot be maintained, but will be dismissed at the plaintiff's cost. There are, in rare instances, cases where suit may be maintained before the paper is due, as where it is made to appear to the court that some article of property or merchandise is subject to a particular debt, and that the same will be made away with or disposed of and placed beyond the plaintiff's reach before the maturity of the paper.

COMMERCIAL PAPER 

179 

Sec. 260. Evidence.-Upon suit for the collection of a negotiable instrument it is not necessary to prove by oral testimony or otherwise, that the note was actually executed and delivered. The instrument itself, when offered in court, is all of the evidence required, unless the defendant, before going into trial, files a written denial under oath denying the execution of said paper. Under such circumstances testimony may be offered by both sides upon the issue thus raised.

Sec. 261. Defenses.-One of the principal grounds of defense to an action upon a negotiable instrument is the failure of consideration, i.e., a failure or refusal to do the thing or deliver the property for which the draft or note was given. This defense, however, is not available against an innocent purchaser without notice. 

Another defense is that of limitation. 

Sec. 262. Limitation.-Open accounts are barred and cannot be collected after two years from the date on which they become due and payable. 

Notes and other written evidence of indebtedness that have been duly executed, or accepted, are barred four years after the date of maturity. 

By being "barred by limitation" is not meant that the debt is extinguished, but that the defendant may, if he so elects, plead the statute of limitation. If he pleads this statute, he cannot be compelled by law to pay the debt, nor can a judgment be rendered against him thereon, regardless of how just the obligation may be. 

It is, therefore, incumbent upon the holder and owner of negotiable paper, regardless of how it is secured, to see that it does not become more than four years old from the date of maturity. And before this period arrives the

DAVIDSON S SIMPLIFIED LAW 

debtor should be required to give his written renewal of said obligation, and if he refuses, then the holder of said paper, in order to protect himself, must file suit before the four years shall have been completed.

If the note is secured by lien upon land it is necessary to have this renewal acknowledged before a notary public, and recorded in the deed records, as land notes may be barred by limitation just as other paper.

Sec. 263. Righteousness of the Plea.-The statute of limitation against personal actions here referred to was not enacted for the purpose of enabling a man to defeat any just obligation, but rather to protect parties against stale and unjust claims. Many obligations, after four years have elapsed, may be partially forgotten; the receipts and cancelled checks, by which the same were discharged, may have been destroyed; or the party to whom money was paid may be dead. After this period of time has thus elapsed, the law presumes that the obligation has been paid, or in some way satisfied. However, if the debtor knows that the same has not been paid, and that it would have, before the expiration of four years, been a lawful demand upon him, and then refuses to pay the same, he is guilty of dishonorable conduct in refusing to pay a just debt, and in most instances, is hiding behind and taking advantage of the very leniency and clemency that the creditor has shown him in permitting the paper to become of so great an age.

An honest man should never plead the statute of limitation to a debt that he knows has not been paid. 

A very beautiful illustration of this is given in the life of Sir Walter Scott, who failed in a business enterprise and became involved to the extent of about $700,-

COMMERCIAL PAPER 

181 000.00, which was, in his day, a very great sum; measured by the wealth of men at that time, equal to several millions of dollars at this day. Scott was, perhaps, not offered the advantage of the plea of limitation, but he made no effort whatever to compromise his indebtedness with his creditors, but applied himself diligently to work and wrote that beautiful series of books-Ivanhoe, and other volumes that bear his name as the author-and from the proceeds of this labor succeeded in paying the last dollar of his indebtedness.

CHAPTER 30 PARTNERSHIP 

Sec. 264. Definition.-A partnership is an association of two or more persons in a business enterprise, agreeing to share the losses and profits. 

It may consist of a large number of partners, or it may consist of only two. The business may require capital, to which each party may contribute an equal share, or an unequal share, agreeing to prorate the profits accordingly; or, one partner may place his capital in the business against the skill and labor of the other partner, and share the profits; or, a partnership may exist where skill or labor are the only capital or elements of partnership, which may be true of a firm of physicians, attorneys or contractors.

Sec. 265. Management of the Business.-The sev eral partners may agree among themselves as to the method of conduct and management of the business. 

One partner may be delegated to do the purchasing, another the selling, or each may do and perform any and all of the work of the partnership, as it becomes convenient for him to do so. 

The acts of either partner will be binding upon the partnership, unless otherwise expressly provided and 182

PARTNERSHIPS 

183 notice of this fact be conveyed to the third party dealing with such firm. However, to bind the partnership in all cases, a transaction must be within the scope of the partnership business and not in some business foreign to the line of business conducted by the firm. 

Sec. 266. Liabilities of the Partnership.-A partner in the management of the business may make contracts for which the entire assets of the business may become responsible. He may make debts to which the entire partnership property may be subjected and sold, but such debts must be made in a transaction affecting, and within the scope of, the partnership business, and must not be a matter relating only to the private business of the partners.

Sec. 267. Suits Against a Partnership.-In cases of suits against a firm of this character citation may be served upon any one of the active partners of the business, and judgment obtained in such suit will be binding against all of the partnership property and against the individual property of the partner so served. 

Where it is desired that the several members of a partnership be personally bound by the judgment, each member of said partnership should be served with the citation, and the name and residence of each member should be alleged in plaintiff's petition. 

Sec. 268. Who Are Partners.-A partnership may exist between parties engaged in a business enterprise. 

In cases where such partnership has not been announced to the world, and the interested parties do not actually know each other by the name of partners, if the elements of partnership exist they become such in law, regardless of whether they intentionally designate, or

DAVIDSON'S Simplified Law do not designate each other as such. If their business. relations are such that each contributes something to the common enterprise, and each shares in the profits of said business, then there exists in law a partnership, for the obligations of which either party may become responsible.

Sec. 269. Partnerships Distinguished From Corpo rations. We not infrequently see the business card of "John Doe & Co.," which may mean an incorporated concern, or it may mean that John Doe is in partnership with one or more gentlemen doing business under the name of "John Doe & Co."

In suing a corporation, you serve its officers with citation, and the entire property of the corporation is bound by the judgment that you obtain, but the individual property of the several stockholders is in no way affected. However, as above stated, with a partnership the individual property of each partner may be subject to the partnership debts.

A corporation is a creature of the law. Where several parties contribute a certain amount of funds, which is ordinarily done by buying stock, these stockholders may elect a board of directors, which board of directors selects a president, secretary and business manager, and applies to the Secretary of State for a charter. This charter, when granted, authorizes them to conduct one specific line of business, which is not done in the name of any one of the stockholders or directors, but in the corporate name that has been selected, and this corporation stands as an individual. It can sue and be sued as a natural person.

When the stock subscribed by the several stockholders has been paid in and the funds thus subscribed

PARTNERSHIPS 

185 are spent in any bad investment, the corporation becomes insolvent, and each stockholder loses only the stock that he subscribed in the first instance, and cannot be made liable for any other debts howsoever numerous. As we have seen, with a partnership the case is different.

In transferring real estate, each member of a partnership should sign the deed and acknowledge it before a notary public, but a corporation may convey by its officers authorized so to do by its board of directors. 

Sec. 270. Desirability of the Partnership.-Parties contemplating forming a partnership, more particularly in a commercial enterprise, should be careful in the selection of a partner, not only as to honesty and integrity, but also as to business ability. From long acquaintance, or other reliable information, you should feel so well assured of this contemplated partner's ability and competency that you would be willing to give him the management of the concern; or, your knowledge of him should be such that you believe him to be a man who will acquiesce in your management of the business without interfering or interrupting the plans and objects undertaken.

The same may be said of taking stock in a corporation. If you feel absolutely satisfied with the integrity and business ability of the stockholders and directors, you may, with propriety, own a portion of the stock in the concern; otherwise you should be able to own at least 51 per cent (a controlling interest) of the stock, or leave the business entirely alone.

CHAPTER 31 INSURANCE Sec. 271. Definition.-Insurance may be divided into several departments or heads, the principal of which are life, fire, accident, health, tornado, indemnity and marine.

Life insurance is an undertaking, on the part of the company or association, to insure the life of the policyholder for the stipulated amount. It assures him that in event of his death the company will pay said amount to his estate, or to the beneficiaries named in the policy. 

The amount of life insurance that may be carried is practically unlimited-it may be only a few dollars, or it may be a million—the insured paying so much per hundred or per thousand to the company for such protection.

Sec. 272. Old Line and Mutual.-Life insurance companies are divided into three classes designated as 

Old Line, Fraternal and Mutual. 

The Old Line Company is a corporation organized for profit for the benefit of its stockholders. Old Line 

Companies, which succeed in business, acquire and handle vast and almost unlimited sums of money, and the 186

INSURANCE 

187 stock in such companies comes to be worth many times its original face value. Many Old Line Companies have a profit-sharing mutual feature, whereby the policyholder is in a measure the recipient of some of the company's profits, which he receives in addition to the protection of his policy.

Sec. 273. Mutual Fraternal Life Insurance.—Mu tual Life Insurance in its fraternal form, is an association of the members in a fraternal body, where the members are subject to assessments for the benefit of each other member of the association, such as the Woodmen of the World, Praetorians, and kindred orders. 

Sec. 274. The Policy. The contract issued to the insured, generally called the "Policy" or "Certificate," is a written instrument insuring the life of the party for the benefit of his estate or some beneficiary named, subject to numerous warranties and printed stipulations, embodied in the policy. 

Sec. 275. Premiums.-The premiums paid are the monthly or yearly assessments made in payment for a policy so issued, and a failure to pay any of these premiums will permit the policy to lapse, and the company to escape its liability in event of death or disaster. The policy will ordinarily lapse immediately upon the failure of the party insured to pay the premiums when due. 

Sec. 276. Fire Insurance.-Fire insurance business is principally done by incorporated concerns, and we find comparatively little mutual fire insurance. 

It is unlike life insurance in that the amount of insurance is always limited, and should not exceed the value of the property insured.

Sec. 277. Insurance Upon Houses and Real Estate. 

-The Statutes have provided that in cases of total loss by fire to any house or other real property, the amount of the policy, fixed by the contract of insurance on said house, shall be a Liquidated Demand, by which it is meant that the amount of the policy becomes due and payable, and the company cannot escape liability, or reduce the amount of insurance, by showing that the property insured was of less value than the amount of the policy upon same. 

Sec. 278. Payment. Where houses are burned, the company must either pay the entire amount of the policy, or prove that the loss was in some manner caused by the insured for the purpose of collecting the policy, which would be the case if the insured should burn his own property.

Sec. 279. Insurance on Merchandise.-A policy of insurance issued upon a stock of merchandise exposed for sale and constantly fluctuating in amount cannot become a fixed or liquidated demand as is the case with houses and real property.

It is therefore provided in most all of such insurance that the insured shall take an inventory of his stock of goods and keep a reasonably accurate set of books showing the amount of goods purchased and sales made from that date on; and that these books and inventories shall be kept in an iron safe, or at some other place not subject to the fire that would destroy the building in which the merchandise was situated. 

Sec. 280. Warranties.-There are numerous and sundry clauses incorporated in these insurance policies which are rarely read and understood by the party tak-

INSURANCE 

189 ing out insurance, and many just and deserving claims for insurance have been defeated by failure to observe and carefully comply with some of these conditions designed as "Warranties," one of which is known as the "Iron Safe Clause." This provides that the party will keep in an iron safe his books and inventory. Another is that he will take out no other insurance without the knowledge or consent of the first company; or that the property insured is free from debt; or that it is not encumbered; and numerous other like provisions. 

Sec. 281. Actions Against Insurance Companies.- 

Suits may be maintained in the county where the loss occurred, or where the insured resided at the time of his death, or in the county where the principal offices of such company are situated.

Service for such suit may be had by serving any agent in the state with citation, or by serving same upon the Commissioner of Insurance at Austin, Texas. Fra ternal companies may be sued only by service upon the 

Insurance Commissioner. 

Any company failing and refusing to pay any judgment or execution issued thereon may have its permit to do business in this state cancelled.

CHAPTER 32 ESTATES Sec. 282. Definition.-An Estate consists of real and personal property which includes lands, money, securities, live stock, and all other belongings known as property; but, the term in many instances has special reference to real estate. 

It may consist of property in fee where the title is held absolutely; it may be in a life estate only where the then owner has no right to dispose of it after his natural life; it may be in expectancy-in the nature of a remainder where a party is to receive it after the death of some other person; or it may be in a lease only for a term of years.

Sec. 283. In Fee Simple.-Title in fee simple means absolute and unconditional ownership with the right to give, sell or dispose of same in any manner that may please the owner. Most of the real property in the 

United States is held in this manner. 

Sec. 284. Life Estates.-Life estates consist of prop erty held by the owner during his natural life only. 

This variety of property exists generally by virtue of some will or devise that has been made by some ancestor 190

ESTATES 

191 or other person leaving property to certain people during their natural lives, and at their death to some other party or parties.

An instance of this character would be: If A should will to his son, B, a tract of land during his life and at B's death to his daughter, C, then B would become the holder of a life estate and C would become invested with a remainder.

Sec. 285. Remainders.-A remainder is title to property where the owner has no right to immediate possession, or management, but at some future time is to come into possession thereof, upon the happening of some other event, as in the example above given. 

Remainders and life estates exist in most instances with reference to real property, as it is a more difficult matter to provide for a remainder in money or perishable property. This may be done, however, after a fashion, by the donor, or devisee, providing that the principal sum shall be invested, and that the income therefrom may pass to one person, but that the principal, at such person's death, should pass to some other person. Such arrangements are sometimes effected by means of bonds or longtime securities.

Sec. 286. Community Property.-In addition to the above, estates may be divided into three other classes of different variety, which grow principally out of the relationship of husband and wife, and are, respectively: community property, the wife's separate property, and the husband's separate property.

By the term "Community Property," is meant the property acquired by the husband or wife during their

marriage, except that which is acquired by gift, devise or descent.

Sec. 287. The Wife's Separate Property Is: (a) 

The property acquired by her before marriage; (b) 

That which she receives after marriage by gift, devise or descent.

This property is now subject to the wife's management. She has the right to collect the rents and income from it, but cannot dispose of her real estate and stocks and bonds except by her husband's joining in the deed of conveyance, or by the District Court granting her authority to do so.

A further exception would be where the husband deserts her and goes to another state and takes up his abode there and she is left in management and control of her property. In this case, it has been held that she has a right to dispose of her property without being joined by him.

She can also dispose of her property by will to take effect at her death without being joined by her husband, or in any way consulting him about it. 

Sec. 288. The Husband's Separate Property.-The husband's separate property is the property acquired by him as a single man, and that which he receives by gift, devise or descent, as in the case with the wife. 

He has absolute control of this property during his life and sanity, and may dispose of any of such property, except the homestead, by deed without being joined by his wife. In case of the homestead the wife must join, regardless of whether the same be separate or commu-

ESTATES 

193 nity property, and no deed is valid to the home unless acknowledged by the wife, and her consent must be voluntarily given and not obtained by threats or coercion.

The husband also has the right to dispose of all of the community property except the homestead, as above provided.

Sec. 289. Justice of Provisions. The justice of these provisions has been often questioned by many, and especially by women who deem themselves capable of managing at least their separate estates. 

This theory of the law is one that has been handed down from past generations, and it assumes that the husband, by reason of his outdoor life and business responsibilities, knows better the necessity of making dispositions of the property than the wife, and that management of the property by both members of the family would tend to create dissension and confusion, destroy concert of action, and prevent attaining the same results that a single and constantly pursued course would achieve under one management.

There is, perhaps, no law that does not work a hardship on some one, and in many instances this law imposes such hardship upon married women. It was, however, during the Thirty-Third Legislature (1913) so amended as to permit them to manage their separate property, which certainly is not unfair. The law giving this permission is, of course, not compulsory in requiring the wife to manage her property, and this right can by her be delegated to her husband to be resumed by her at any time. Her husband must join, however, in any valid deed of conveyance.

Sec. 290. How Estates Pass.-Estates pass from the hands of one owner to another by Deeds, Devise and Inheritance, each of which subjects will be fully treated in the following chapters.

CHAPTER 33 

DEEDS 

Sec. 291. Definition.-A deed is a written instru ment transferring the title to real property. The maker is called the Grantor, and the taker, or beneficiary, is called the Grantee. It recites the names of the parties; the consideration of the transfer; the description of the property transferred; a warranty of the title so transferred; and closes with an acknowledgment of the same before a notary public, or some officer authorized to administer oaths.

Sec. 292. Parties To a Deed.-The grantor must be capable of contracting. That is, must be sane, twentyone years of age, and if a married woman, must be joined in the contract by her husband. It is not necessary that the grantee, or the recipient of the property so conveyed, be capable of entering into such a contract, as property may be deeded to a minor, who is incapable of reconveying it. 

Sec. 293. Consideration.-A deed is a contract made for some valid consideration as the inducing motive or cause that brings about the transfer. 

This consideration is usually a sum of money, or its equivalent as the exchange of other property. The con- 195

sideration, where the relation of the parties is very close, may be the love and affection borne by one party toward the other; it may be valuable services, or professional services rendered; but some consideration, as an inducing or moving cause, must be, or exist, to pass the title. 

Sec. 294. Description. In order to pass the title to property it is necessary so to describe the thing sold or conveyed, as to enable the purchaser to identify and distinguish it from other property. This is very essential in the transfer of real estate.

In cities and towns where the property has been platted out in streets, lots and blocks, and the plats made. a matter of record, it is a sufficient description to refer to it by lot and block number of the town, or the addition to the town.

If it be rural property that has been regularly divided into sections, it may be described by referring to the number of section or quarter section. If it has not been sectionized, as is the case with most all East Texas property, then the description should recite the headright on which the land is located. It should begin at some known corner of the land and there describe that corner by natural objects, if possible, and then go east, south, west, and north to the point of beginning. The lines may not run east and west; they may not be parallel to each other; the land may have many corners and offsets; it may be bounded by a running stream or a public road with numerous crooks in the boundary; but, it should be seen that the successive calls of course and distance finally bring you back to the place of beginning so as to inclose the land described.

DEEDS 

197 In making these descriptions, it is proper that you begin at some known corner, or at a certain distance and direction from some known corner of some original survey as patented by the state. It should then be stated that the land conveyed is all, or a part of a certain original headright, as originally surveyed and patented. 

Sec. 295. Warranty.-After the land has been described, the party selling the land usually incorporates a clause whereby he guarantees the title, and in which he agrees to warrant and defend forever the title to the said property to the grantee, his heirs and assigns. 

This warranty clause is sometimes misunderstood. It means that the grantor will return the purchase money paid for the property, with 6 per cent interest from date of purchase, in event the title proves defective and to have been so at the date of the transfer. It does not mean that the grantor must, nor does it obligate him to, defend the title against a mere trespasser. It becomes the duty of the purchaser, without reference to his warrantor, to protect his premises from trespassers, who may be seeking to acquire his property by the Statute of Limitation, or to otherwise infringe upon his rights. 

The warrantor, however, is usually made a party to the suits affecting the title, and he enters the contest and aids the owner in defending the suit, and in event the owner loses, he takes judgment over and against the warrantor by virtue of the warranty in his deed.

The warranty is of considerable value where the warrantor is a man of property and means; but, where the grantor is a man of little financial worth, the warranty is of but small value.

Sec. 296. Special Warranty.-There are two kinds of warranty clauses in deeds: The general warranty, which has been described in the foregoing section; and a special warranty, which obligates the grantor to warrant and defend his title, or some particular title. 

A special warranty is little better than a quitclaim deed, as the warrantor only intends to defend such title as he may have in the property, and if the warrantor had good title he will hardly be called upon to defend it; if he had no title, he has not obligated himself to defend any, because he has contracted to defend only such as he had. 

Sec. 297. A Quitclaim Deed. - A quitclaim deed is one without any warranty clause whatever whereby the grantor transfers the property, or his interest in same, without recourse, so to speak, and agrees to quitclaim, that is, to cease to claim the property himself, and releases whatever right he has, small or great, to the grantee.

This kind of a deed is sufficient color of title, under which to take possession of land and acquire good title by five years limitation. If no other person is in possession of the land, except the man who made the quitclaim deed, it is sufficient to vest the purchaser with title to the property. 

Quitclaim deeds are always looked upon with suspicion, and it puts the purchaser on notice of the fact that the owner will not guarantee the title, nor in any way be responsible for his claim. These deeds are frequently given to the owner of property to remove a cloud from the title, the person executing the deed making no claim to the property himself, although the public

DEEDS 

199 records show that he has or might have an interest or claim therein.

Sec. 298. Restrictions and Limitations.-A deed conveying a piece of property, without specifying what particular interest therein is conveyed, will convey all the interest that the grantor has in said property. But the grantor can convey no more title than he has. If he holds only a half interest in the property and deeds it all, his deed will convey to the purchaser only a half interest.

If he owns all the property, he can limit or restrict his conveyance to any interest therein, by saying that the property conveyed is an undivided one-half interest, or an undivided one-fourth interest, etc.

He may limit the interest conveyed by other restrictions. For example: He may write in the deed a declaration that it conveys the interest inherited by him from his father's estate. This interest would, of course, depend on the mother's rights, and on the number of children holding shares in said estate.

A conveyance of one's interest in the estate derived from his father will not convey any portion of the same estate derived from his mother.

Another restriction would be that of a life estate, as the deed might contain a restriction that the estate conveyed was for life only, or for a number of years, taking the nature of a lease. 

Sec. 299. Signature. The deed should be signed by the party making the conveyance, and should be in his own name, but if he should sign a fictitious name, or the name of some other person, and receive the consideration, the title to the property would still vest in the

purchaser, although a cloud would thus be cast on the title. 

If the property conveyed is that of a married woman, as before stated, the signature of the husband should also be had. 

If the grantor be a corporation, the corporate name should be written, beneath which should follow the name of the particular official of the corporation making the signature. It is proper that there should be a recitation in the deed in this case stating the authority of the officer or agent to make such conveyance. 

Sec. 300. Authentication. By authentication is meant the acknowledgment of the grantor or grantors before a notary public, county clerk, or other proper officer, that they executed the instrument for the purposes and consideration therein stated. 

In the case of the wife's acknowledgment, it must be taken privately and apart from her husband. That is, the husband should acknowledge, and then, in his absence, the wife should have the deed or instrument read over or explained to her, and then she must acknowledge that she willingly signed the same, and that she does not wish to retract it. 

The officer taking this acknowledgment must write his name clearly, and his official title in full just beneath his name, and to the left of which he should affix the impression of his seal. 

Sec. 301. Registration.-After the deed has been duly signed and acknowledged it should be immediately carried to the county clerk of the county in which the land is situated and there recorded.

DEEDS 

201 After it has been recorded, which the clerk does in books kept for this purpose, the original is returned to the owner and the copy remains in the books in the clerk's office for the notice and inspection of the public. 

Any man dealing with the particular property described in said deed must take notice of its existence and he is charged with notice whether he examines the record or not.

Sec. 302. Suits.-In all suits affecting the title to land, the deeds affecting the property from its earliest history to date may be brought into court and offered in evidence to show title. Copies from the clerk's office may be used if the originals are lost. 

An imperfect acknowledgment is often disastrous to title. The Legislature has sought to correct this by providing that although the acknowledgment may be defective, if the deed has been on record for ten years, it is still admissible in evidence. 

Another defect which may grow more serious with time is in the description of the property. The description should start from a well-known object or point that has become fixed as a part of the history or geography of the locality; otherwise, in the course of time it may be impossible for one to tell where the land was really located.

Sec. 303. Abstracts.-An abstract of the land title is simply a history of the title, or copies of all the deeds. and instruments affecting the particular piece of property copied and arranged in regular form. 

They are taken from the deed records, the probate records and judgment records, and sometimes the marriage records of the county in which the land is situated.

By inspecting all the several items that go to make up this record, one is enabled to know whether or not the transfers have been regular; and whether or not there are any missing links; and that the title, so far as the record shows, has actually passed to the claimant and is clear of liens.

If the abstract shows missing links and fails to connect the last claimant with the original patentee, or first owner, the title is defective, and the claimant could not dispossess a trespasser in an action in the District Court in trespass to try title because he would first have to show title in himself. He must do this by a consecutive chain of deeds from the state to himself. If he was not in possession of these deeds, he would then have to rely on the Statutes of Limitation; and if the property has not been actually reduced to possession for the proper period, or the proof cannot be had on this point, a trespasser holds the property and remains in possession because the plaintiff is unable to show any lawful right to dispossess him.

It is therefore proper that every land deed should be recorded; and that every purchaser of real estate should have an abstract made of the title, and have that abstract examined by a competent attorney. Guaranty titles by solvent guaranty title companies are to a very considerable extent taking the place of abstracts and opinions thereon.

CHAPTER 34 

WILLS

Sec. 304. Definition.-A will is a written instru ment made by any person over 21 years of age, and of sound mind, making a disposition of property to become effective at the death of such person. Any person who is or has been lawfully married, even though not 

21 years of age, can make a will. 

Sec. 305. Who Can Make a Will.-A will is unlike a deed in that it may be revoked at any time before the death of the testator; it may be made by a married woman without the knowledge or consent of her husband, and the same is effective to convey her interest in the property of herself and husband. It is not acknowledged before a notary public, but may be written entirely in the handwriting of the testator, or signed in the presence of two subscribing witnesses. It cannot be recorded in the deed records as an ordinary deed conveying property, but an application to probate same must be duly made in the court, and the validity and genuineness of the will established. In which case, the County 

Judge enters an order in the minutes of the court directing that the will be recorded. 203

Sec. 306. How Made.-If the will be not in the handwriting of the testator, it must be signed by two subscribing witnesses over fourteen years of age, and these two witnesses should not be interested parties, or parties to whom any bequests are made. The mere fact that the subscribing witnesses may be beneficiaries under the will will not, however, invalidate the will, but if the will is proved up by their testimony, they can receive no portion of the estate devised to them, unless the witnesses would have been entitled to a share of the estate of the testator had there been no will, in which case they will be entitled to so much of such share as shall not exceed the value of the bequests to them in the will.

Sec. 307. What May Be Willed.-The entire estate of the testator may be devised to any person whomsoever it may please the testator to remember.

The law assumes that the estate or property is that of the testator and no other person has a right to say what he shall do with it. He may will it all to one child if he chooses; he may will it all to a stranger and leave none to his relatives.

There is a prevalent idea that it is necessary for each child or lawful heir to be named in the will, if left only a pittance, but this is an erroneous idea. Under the

Texas law, the entire estate may be willed to any person, or for charitable purposes, or in any other manner, to the exclusion of the direct heirs. The mere fact that those who are near, or who should be near and dear to the testator, are not mentioned in the will, may or may not be a circumstance to show that some undue influence was exercised over the mind of the testator to induce

WILLS 

205 him to make the will as it was made. However, it is always the better practice at least to mention the relatives.

Sec. 308. How Disinherited.-Should the testator desire to cut off any direct heir from receiving a portion of his estate, he cannot do so by merely saying: “I will that my son, Richard, take no portion of my estate." In order to prevent Richard from getting a child's inheritance in the estate, the estate must be otherwise disposed of so that there will be nothing left for Richard. 

Therefore, the proper method to disinherit a child is to devise all the property to such parties and purposes as will consume the entire estate without any of it being left for the heir whom it is desired to disinherit. 

Again, should some portion of the estate not be disposed of by the will, that portion of the estate will pass by inheritance, or the law of descent and distribution, just as though there had been no will at all. For example: If the deceased owned five tracts of land and should specify how the first four tracts should pass, and who should receive them, but make no provision to cover the fifth tract, that tract will pass by the law of descent and distribution to his heirs as though there had been no will.

Sec. 309. Objections To a Will.-When a will has been offered for probate, any person who would be interested in said estate may contest the probate of said will. are: 

The principal grounds upon which a contest is based, First: That the will is a forgery, and was not made or signed by the testator.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED LAw 

Second: That the deceased, at the time of making said will, was mentally incapacitated to contract or convey property, or make any valid transactions, which condition may have been produced by diseases causing weakness of the body and mind to such an extent as to deprive the testator or testatrix of his or her mental faculties at the time of making said will. 

Third: A ground of contest practically the same as the above, is, that the testator was unduly influenced by other parties, who gained such an ascendency over the mind of the deceased as to be able to dictate the provisions of the will; that the will, as made, is not the will of the deceased free from outside influence, but is the will of some designing party, who, by the exercise. of some undue influence over the mind of the testator, procured the making of the will in its present form; and that the same is not the last will and testament of the deceased according to his individual wishes and inclinations.

The strength of such a contest would, of course, depend largely upon the circumstances surrounding the making of the will, and upon the subscribing witnesses. 

To prevent a successful contest the witnesses should be persons of intelligence and removed as far as possible from any suspicion of selfish interest. 

Sec. 310. A Verbal or Nuncupative Will.-Is an oral disposition of one's estate. It is usually made upon the deathbed where the deceased directs in the presence of witnesses, in clear and simple language, the disposition of his property. These directions must be witnessed by three persons, and should be committed to writing within six days, and the matters thus written down by

WILLS the witness must be sworn to by him, probated and made a matter of record, to effect the disposition of the property mentioned in the manner directed by the deceased. 

Such will, however, is more subject to objections, and more prolific of litigation than the written form. 

The memory of the witnesses is often defective as to certain points.

Sec. 311. Form of the Will.-There are no prescribed forms by which a will must be written. It may consist of only one sentence, or it may be several pages, but it must clearly express the intention of the testator. 

A very short will would be as follows: 

Marshall, Texas, June 1, 1937.

At death I leave all of my property to my son, Henry Green.

J. H. GREEN. 

The above style of a will must be clearly in the handwriting of the deceased, and parties who are acquainted with the handwriting must be able to testify to it. be:

Another, and more generally approved form would 

THE STATE OF TEXAS, COUNTY OF HARRISON, 

In the name of God, Amen:

I, John Doe, being of sound and disposing mind, and realizing the uncertainty of life and the certainty of death, make and ordain this as my last will and testament.

I give and bequeath to my beloved wife, Ruth, the sum of $10,000 in money and the home upon which she now lives. 

I give to my nephew, Joseph Brown, the sum of $1,000.

I give to the Board of Trustees of the Charity Hospital the sum of $1,000, for their use in said institution.

I also desire that a sufficient portion of my estate be set apart for the education and support during minority of my grandson, Jonas Smith.

I direct that the remainder of my estate be divided equally, share and share alike, among my three surviving children, namely, Francis, Henry and William.

I appoint my beloved wife, Ruth, as executrix of my last will and testament without bond, and direct that the County Court take no further action with reference to my estate than to probate this will and file an inventory and list of claims. 

I here call upon my friends, R. L. Lawrence and

J. P. Robertson to witness this last will and testament, I signing in their presence and they signing in my presence. (Signed) John Doe

Witnesses: 

J. P. Robertson, R. L. Lawrence. 

Sec. 312. Executors.-A will may name some person to take charge of the estate and see that the provisions of the will are carried out. This person is called the executor.

WILLS 

209 Some wills do not contain any provision for such, and in these cases the court appoints an administrator with the will annexed, whose duty it is to carry out the terms of the will.

If the will names an executor the court will, in probating the same, recognize such executor as the person to carry out its provisions, and it becomes his duty, as in the case of an administrator with the will annexed, to make bond in a sum of money equal to twice the value of the estate.

Independent executors are those not required to give bond, and are allowed to manage the estate free from the control of the court, a much wider latitude than other executors or administrators. An independent executor can, upon his own motion, do anything without the order of the court that an executor under bond could do with the order of the court. 

The testator is supposed to have such confidence in the party named as independent executor, as to be willing to relieve him of the necessity of making bond, and of the inconvenience of going to court for his orders. 

The law provides, however, that should it appear that an independent executor is making away with the property in a manner so as to defeat the purpose of the will, any devisee or creditor may, by complaining to the court and upon sufficient cause, require him to give bond and place himself under orders of the court. 

Sec. 313. Title Under Will.-The will, when duly probated, passes title to the property, according to its provisions, just as effectually as a deed, or as the law of descent and distribution, but it should be borne in

mind that the will, like a deed, can pass no more property than was owned by the testator at the time of his death. For example: He has no power to will the entire community estate of himself and wife, and his will becomes effective only as to his half interest in such community estate.

CHAPTER 35 THE LAW OF INHERITANCE Sec. 314. The Law of Descent and Distribution.—It is hardly possible to cover this subject completely in one short chapter; however, the following eight sections will explain or state those cases which most frequently arise. 

In case of doubt a lawyer should always be consulted and the Statutes examined. The rule of inheritance is fixed in this state by statute; under the Feudal system and in England, the rule being entirely different from that now in force in Texas and in the United States generally.

Under the Feudal system and under the old English rule, the eldest son inherited practically all of the property, and especially the landed estate. 

This harsh and unnatural procedure was made necessary to maintain and continue the operation of the Feudal system, which has been discussed in Chapter 3 of this work.

Had the estate been divided, as with us, among the several children, the great estates of the Feudal lords would have degenerated into a number of petty farms, whereas the Feudal policy was to divide the kingdom into so many earldoms or large subdivisions. The head of each would bear the name of the territory rather than his own name. Thus, the Duke of Norfolk's real 211-

name may have been Montgomery, yet upon the inheritance of this estate, he becomes known as the Duke of Norfolk. No portion of this estate could be subdivided and appropriated among his other children. 

This rule prevailed in the Colonial history of Virginia, where the aristocratic English families settled, but was abolished under the regime of Thomas Jefferson, before the tidal wave of the American doctrine of liberty and equal rights. 

The American doctrine is that there shall be no titles of nobility; that all men are created free and equal; and that the youngest child inherits as much of his father's estate as the eldest. 

Sec. 315. Parent and Child.-Upon the death of the parent without will, and without surviving husband or wife, the estate will pass in equal portions to his or her children, or to their descendants. 

Sec. 316. Husband and Wife.-Upon the death of the husband or wife the survivor inherits of the community one-half; or, in fact, if it be community property, one-half really belongs to the survivor and the other half passes to the child or children of the deceased, whether they be of that marriage or some other. 

If there be no child or children the entire community estate passes to the surviving husband or wife, without the necessity of an administration upon the estate. 

Sec. 317. Separate Property to Survivor.-In case of separate property, which has been elsewhere defined, the survivor inherits one-third interest in all of the personal property, and one-third interest in the income (known as a Life Estate) in all the real property, and the balance passes to the heirs of the deceased, that is, to his children. If there be no children or other descend-

THE LAW OF INHERITANCE 

213 ants, then next to father and mother, and brothers and sisters. If there be none of these, then the surviving wife or husband inherits the entire property. 

Sec. 318. Child and Parent.-Upon the death of a child without descendants of his own, or surviving spouse, the property passes in equal portions to the father and mother, if they be living. If only one be living, then one-half of such property passes to the father or mother and the other one-half is divided among the brothers and sisters and their descendants. 

If there be neither mother nor father, brothers nor sisters, or their descendants, the estate passes to the grandparents.

If there be no grandparents, then the estate is divided equally into two moieties or portions, and is divided equally among the relatives of the father and mother, according to the nearness of kin. 

Sec. 319. Brother and Sister.-Upon the death of a brother or sister without heirs above specified, the estate of the deceased is divided equally among the surviving brothers and sisters.

Sec. 320. Homestead Rights.-The title to the prop erty, constituting the homestead, passes by the law of descent and distribution, as other property, but if the surviving wife or husband shall reside upon the property or claim it and use it as a homestead, or if any of the minor children, with a duly appointed guardian, shall remain upon such homestead, the homestead shall remain intact and is not subject to division until this homestead right shall have ceased by the minors becoming of age, or the surviving wife or husband dying or abandoning the home. In such event the homestead will descend as other property.

Sec. 321. Half Blood and Full Blood.-In case there be half brothers and sisters the full blood will inherit an entire share of the property and the half blood will inherit only one-half as much, unless, however, the property is descending from that parent or relative to which all of the children bear the same relation. For instance: 

If the father shall have married twice and there be children by each marriage, in the division of his property all of his children will take an equal portion, and so with the mother; but, if one of the brothers and sisters should die leaving an estate, his own brothers and sisters would get double the amount of his half brothers and sisters. 

Sec. 322. The Second Marriage.—In event of a sec ond marriage, the second wife or husband does not acquire any interest in that part of the estate owned by the children of a former marriage; that is to say, if the father should die, the wife would get one-half of the community estate and the child, or children, the other half. 

If the wife should remarry, this does not affect the children's half derived from their father. 

If she should then die, the children will get the remaining half of the real estate, subject to a one-third life estate therein and homestead right in the survivor of the second marriage. The children also receive twothirds of the personal property out of the one-half of the personal property owned as her part of the community estate of the first marriage by the last surviving parent. 

The other one-third of such one-half of the personal property goes to the survivor of the second marriage. 

Any estate acquired after the second marriage is community property by that marriage, and same passes as in the first instance.

CHAPTER 36 ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATES 

Sec. 323. Administrators.-A person named in a will to administer an estate is called an executor; when named by the court he is called an administrator. 

Sec. 324. Necessity of Administration.-Upon the death of a person owning any considerable estate, or of one conducting any business, it is generally necessary to have the estate wound up in a lawful manner, which makes an administration necessary. 

If there be debts due the estate of the deceased the debtor desires to know, and has a lawful right to know, to whom he should pay his money. And to prevent a multiplicity of orders coming from the different parties at interest, creating general confusion to the detriment of the estate and the danger of the creditors and parties. at interest, it is necessary to have some lawful head to have charge of the property until the same can be distributed according to law or to will. 

This party may be named in the will as executor; or if the will does not name an executor, then an "administrator with the will annexed" will be appointed to carry out the terms of the will. 215

In many instances, where the party dies intestate (without any will at all) it is necessary to have an administrator for the same reasons above stated. In this case the court appoints an administrator, who takes charge of the property, collects the debts or bills, and distributes the property finally according to the law of descent and distribution. 

Sec. 325. Creditors. Before the several devisees in the will, or the heirs at law, can come in for their share of the estate, the debts against the estate must be satisfied, and if none of the heirs of the deceased apply for administration, and make bond to lawfully administer the estate, any one of the creditors may do so. 

Sec. 326. Administrators: Who May Be.-The right to appointment is fixed in the following order: First, the party named in the will, called the executor; second, the surviving husband or wife; third, child or children, father or mother, brother or sister, or any creditor or any other person whom the court may appoint, if those entitled to the place waive their right to such appointment.

The administrator is allowed reasonable compensation for his services, which consists of five per cent commission on all moneys collected or disbursed for the estate. 

Sec. 327. The Widow's Allowance.-Where there is no will, and no children, and the estate is all community property, there is no administration necessary as all the property vests in the surviving wife or husband, who is responsible to the creditors for their debts, to the extent of the value of property received. But where administration is necessary, the exempt property and the homestead

ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATES 

217 are not subject thereto, and are exempt from the indebtedness of the estate.

The court shall also, following the appointment of such administrator, make an allowance or appropriation from the estate sufficient to support the widow and minor children for the term of one year, which sum should be fixed by the court at an amount not exceeding $1,000.00. 

This sum is superior in point of payment to all debts of the estate, save taxes and the vendor's lien. This allowance is made only where the widow and children have no property adequate for their maintenance and support. 

The amount of the allowance, of course, is determined by the value of the estate and by the size, state of dependency, and financial and social standing of the family. 

Sec. 328. Report to the Court.-The administrator 

(except the independent executor) must report the condition of the estate, together with receipts and disbursements, to the court each year. Upon the winding up of the said estate, he must file his final account, showing how he has managed and disposed of said property. 

When the final account has been filed the estate is then closed and the property passes as directed by the will; and if there be no will, then by the law of descent and distribution.

Sec. 329. Order to Sell Land.-The independent ex ecutor may sell land without the approval of the court, but any other executor or administrator must first make an application to the court setting up the particular property desired to be sold, and if the court grants the order the property may be sold according to direction of the court, at either private or public sale, and the proceeds. thereof applied to the payment of debts, or to the pay-

ment of any other lawful purposes, designated by the court, and made necessary by the indebtedness of the estate, or the provisions of the will. 

Sec. 330. Community Administration.-Upon the death of the husband or wife, and where the estate consists of community property, the survivor may qualify as administrator or administratrix, giving bond for the proper management of the estate and for paying over that portion of the estate owned by the deceased to his heirs, upon their becoming of age. 

The survivor, under this type of administration, has unlimited powers to manage the property independent of any action of the court, subject only to the liability of the bondsmen should he or she squander the estate and deprive the heirs of the deceased of their lawful rights. 

Should the wife qualify as survivor she may continue such administration until the children of the deceased become of age, or until she remarries, in which event she must qualify as administratrix in the regular manner, and give bond, or close the administration.

CHAPTER 37 GUARDIAN AND WARD Sec. 331. Guardianship.-Guardianship is another means of handling in the courts certain estates of which there are no legal owners capable of managing. 

Sec. 332. Who May Have Guardians.-Minors, lunatics or persons of unsound mind, and habitual drunkards, may have guardians of their persons, or their persons and estates, as the case may be, appointed by the County Court. If a child should come into any considerable estate the court will appoint a guardian, even though the parents are living.

Sec. 333. Who May Be Guardians.-The person thus appointed is styled the "guardian," and the person for whom the guardian is appointed is called the "ward." 

Any person over the age of 21 years may be appointed as guardian. 

Sec. 334. Who Are Entitled to Be Appointed Guardians. If either the father or mother survive, such survivor shall be first entitled to the guardianship of the child; next after which come brothers and sisters, uncles and aunts, and so on with the nearest of kin. 

When applicants are equally entitled to the appointment by degrees of relationship, the court will take into 219

consideration the welfare of the ward and appoint the one who is best prepared and qualified to look after the ward's interest.

Sec. 335. Duties of Guardian.-It is the duty of the guardian to take possession of the ward's estate, if he be guardian of such, and if there be any perishable property to preserve it, and by order of the court, convert it into money; to collect the rents and revenues, if there be any; to maintain and care for the property; to support and educate the ward; and to do whatsoever things may be necessary for the best interest of the estate and the moral and intellectual developments of the ward.

Sec. 336. Bonds and Restrictions of the Guardian.- 

The guardian of the estate must make bond to the court in double the sum of the estimated value of the property of the ward. If the guardian, while under his bond, misappropriates the property, the friends of the ward or the judge of the court may have the guardian removed and another appointed in his place, and have him and his sureties reimburse the estate for the funds so misappropriated.

So also may the ward, upon becoming of age, maintain an action against his guardian for funds so wrongfully used.

To prevent such misappropriation, however, and to require the guardian to keep himself and the court in touch with the affairs of the ward, he must file an annual report with the court showing the condition of the estate, and the amount of receipts and disbursements for the year. Each item of disbursement, where possible, must be represented by receipt or voucher, showing the purpose for which and the parties to whom the money was

GUARDIAN AND WARD 

221 paid.

This report is known in law as the Guardian's 

Annual Report. 

Sec. 337. Sale of Ward's Property.-As in case of administration, the guardian may sell some portion of the estate to pay the debts of the ward, or to procure funds to support, maintain and educate the said ward. 

This can be done, however, only under the orders of the court, and it must first appear that the ward's estate does not embrace any money or personal property that may be applied to such purposes.

Sec. 338. Orders-How Obtained.-No sale of the ward's real estate can take place until an order has first been made by the court. To obtain this the guardian must file an application with the County Court, sitting as a probate court, asking for an order to sell a specific piece or parcel of real estate. The applicant must show the necessity of such sale.

When this application is filed, the clerk of the court will issue notices to all parties interested in the estate of said ward and cause such notices to be posted at the courthouse door of the county, and at two other places in said county, for a period of twenty days, stating in substance that an application has been made to sell such property, or publish said notice in the local paper. 

If any person knows any reason why this sale should not be made for the purposes named, he may report the same to the court so as to prevent or contest the sale. 

Sec. 339. Estate and Person. The parents of the child are its natural guardians, but if the child, during its minority, becomes possessed of an estate, it is the duty of the parents, or some other person, to qualify as the lawful guardian of the estate. There may be,

therefore, at the same time two guardians—one may be guardian of the person and the other guardian of the estate. The guardian of the person is not required to give bond, as is the guardian of the estate. 

Sec. 340. As Distinguished From Administration.— 

The procedure and management of an estate by a guardian is, in many respects, very similar to that of an administration of the estate of a deceased person by an administrator. Both proceedings are conducted in the probate court, and the guardian and administrator are each required to give bond, file inventories, make reports to the court, apply for orders to sell property, and do and manage the estate in many particulars in a very similar manner. A distinction between the two proceedings lies in the fact that administration is for the purpose of closing, or winding up, an estate of a deceased person, which should be done in the course of a year or more, while guardianship is for the purpose of looking after an estate during the incapacity of a minor, lunatic or other person in need of a guardian. 

To illustrate: If an administrator pays all of the debts due out of the estate of a deceased party and divides the remainder of the property among the parties who are entitled to same, the administration is closed. 

One of these parties, however, may be a minor of tender years, who can make no valid contract. He is supposed, in law, to be incapable of taking care of, or managing his property, which may be, and often is, of such a character as to require some person of responsibility to manage it and care for it. This person, when duly appointed by the court, is called the guardian. 

Sec. 341. Wards Over 14 Years of Age May Select 

Their Own Guardian.-Any minor over 14 years, be-

GUARDIAN AND WARD 

223 coming possessed of an estate, may make choice of some person for his guardian, which person shall be appointed by the court, and although the minor may have had in his more tender years another guardian, yet, upon arriving at the age of 14 years, he is privileged to change guardians and choose another, whom the court will appoint and recognize instead of the first one.

Sec. 342. Guardians By Will.-The surviving par ent may appoint by will a person to be guardian of his minor children.

Sec. 343. Drunkards and Lunatics.-Guardians of habitual drunkards and persons of unsound mind conduct the affairs of their wards' estates just as if such wards were children of immature years, and look after and collect the revenues of the estates and prevent their wards from squandering their property in an improper and wasteful manner. 

Sec. 344. Termination of Guardianship.-If the ward be a minor, the guardianship is terminated upon his becoming twenty-one years of age. If the minor be a girl, and she should marry before reaching her twentyfirst year, the law then recognizes her in her marriage relation as being of age and the guardianship terminates. So with a person of unsound mind and the habitual drunkard, if it be shown to the court by sufficient evidence, that such person is capable of managing the affairs of his estate in a sane and prudent manner, the guardianship will be terminated by order of the court.

CHAPTER 38 PROBATE COURT¹ Sec. 345. Probate Docket.-Probate means to prove and establish for record. 

A County Judge is also styled a Probate Judge, and he is required to have what is called a "Probate Docket," that is, a large, well-bound book, wherein all probate matters are docketed and looked after by him, sitting as Probate Judge.

There are in County Courts three dockets--one is called the "Civil Docket," another the "Criminal 

Docket," and a third the "Probate Docket." With this last we will treat in this chapter. 

Sec. 346. Subjects For Probate Docket.-All ap plications to probate wills must be filed in the County Court and entered upon the Probate Docket; all applications for letters of administration and for letters of guardianship must be filed and entered upon this docket; all papers of every kind and character, applica- 

1This chapter might be more properly classified with Part Two, under Administrative Law, but the procedure in probate matters, being so essential to the understanding of the present group of chapters on Wills and Estates, it was thought proper to treat it here. 224

PROBATE COURT 

225 tions, citations, and orders, inventories, the oath and bond, annual reports, and every other paper from the Probate Court, must be recorded at length in the office of the County Clerk in the minutes of the Probate Court. 

This is not true on the Civil or Criminal Docket. 

Sec. 347. Notices.-When there is filed any annual account, or any application for the probate of a will, for letters of administration or of guardianship, or for the sale of any real property being administered in 

Probate Court, the clerk of the Probate Court must issue citation and post or publish the same for the proper number of days before the hearing is had, giving notice to the public of the action of the court desired by such application. 

Sec. 348. Contests.-These notices are required to be given in order that things may not be stealthily and secretly done by interested or designing parties. Other persons who feel an interest in the matter, upon seeing these notices, may go into court, examine the application before hearing, and object to the court granting such application. And if they have any lawful interest or right in the matter, they may file a contest and by the introduction of testimony, show the court why such application should be refused. This occurs very often over the probate of a will.

A will, in due form, may be probated with but little difficulty if there be no contests filed; but, if some person, claiming an interest, files a contest alleging, for example, that the will was a forgery, or that it was obtained by undue influence, by fraudulent representations, or by some other questionable means, a question of fact is raised and a long and bitter fight may ensue.

DAVIDSON'S Simplified LaW Sec. 349. Appeals in Probate.-Either party to a contest, being dissatisfied with the final decision of the County Probate Court, may take an appeal to the District Court of such county, and there have the same tried de novo (anew).

There is another distinction between procedure in the County Probate Court and in the ordinary civil suits in the County Court. In ordinary suits between individuals, as well as in criminal cases, there is no appeal from the County Court to the District Court, but appeals are taken direct to the higher Appellate Courts, and tried upon the record only. On the Probate Court, however, appeals are taken from the County Court to the District Court just as appeals are taken in small civil suits from the Justice Court to the County Court. 

Sec. 350. The County Judge.-In order that the mind of the student may not be confused with the several duties of the County Judge, it may be explained here that when he is hearing matters from the Probate 

Docket, he may be called the "Probate Judge," and his court the "Probate Court." As soon as the probate business has been heard or disposed of, the same judge may immediately try a criminal or a civil case, the former by removing the Probate Docket and having another book called the "Criminal Docket" brought in, wherein appear the names of all those cases to be tried for offenses against the law. The cases in this docket may be disposed of and the Civil Docket brought in. In this docket appear those cases representing suits concerning rights between people. All of these several dockets are handled by the same judge and clerk, but are kept separate as different phases of the law to be administered by the County Judge.

PROBATE COurt 

227 Insanity. If it shall come to the knowledge of the 

County Judge that any person is a lunatic and that the welfare of himself, or others, requires that he be placed under restraint, he shall have such person brought into the Probate Court where he shall be tried before a jury for lunacy. The jury will be required to find whether he is of unsound mind, and if his condition is such as to require restraint or confinement, the condition also of his estate, etc. If the jury finds the party to be insane, the court will order the unfortunate sent to an insane asylum for restraint and treatment.

CHAPTER 39 HUSBAND AND WIFE Sec. 351. Marriage Relation. The celebration of marriage under our social system, is a moral and religious rite imposing upon the parties many social, moral and religious duties; but in the eyes of the law, as to its effect upon estates, it is a contract and life partnership between the parties.

In this contract the husband is supposed to be the managing partner and all of the property acquired during the marriage relation is owned jointly by the husband and wife, and each has an equal interest therein. 

If the marriage relation is terminated by death, or divorce, the wife or husband receives one-half of all the property acquired by this partnership, and cannot be divested of the same by any legal procedure by the opposing spouse.

Sec. 352. Marriage Contracts.-The husband has the right to manage the community property jointly acquired, but not the right to collect the rents and revenues from his wife's separate property should she desire otherwise. 228

HUSBAND AND WIFE 

229 

Sec. 353. The Husband's Right to Manage.—The theory of the law is that a single, well-directed management is more beneficial to the estate of the husband and wife, or partnership welfare and interest, than would be the dual or conflicting management of both parties, and that should the wife have equal management with the husband, it would be an additional source of trouble and disagreement to disrupt and disturb the marriage relations.

This view, however, is being very strenuously objected to by many of the more progressive, intelligent women of the present day, and it is perhaps but right that the wife should at least have the privilege of managing, controlling and disposing of her separate property at will, without the consent of her husband. Such a change was finally made in our law to the extent of allowing the wife to manage her separate property, but not the exclusive and independent right of sale, unless authorized by order of the court.

Sec. 354. Wife May Complain to Court.-Under the old law, if the husband neglected or failed to pay over to the wife the proceeds or income from her estate, she could complain to the County Judge, and obtain an order from the court directing the payment of such income into her hands. She now has the right to collect her rents directly from the tenant without the interference of her husband.

Sec. 355. Wife's Capacity to Contract.-The law assumes that the husband understands better when and how to sell land and the wife is deemed incapable of conveying, unless she be joined by her husband in such conveyance. If she should sell property, even though it

be her separate property, without being joined by her husband in the deed, the conveyance or contract is void, and neither she nor her husband is bound thereby, and no title passes unless authorized by the court. 

In cases, however, where the husband and wife are living apart, or he is out of the state and she is managing the business affairs without his help or presence, the law, in many instances, recognizes her right to contract and manage the property without being joined by him, and does not inflict upon her the inconvenience of her incapacity of contracting. 

So also the husband is bound for debts incurred by the wife for wearing apparel, food, board and lodging, medical services, and all other necessities of life; the size and amount of such contracts, of course, are governed in a measure, by the financial and social standing of the couple.

Sec. 356. When A Woman Becomes of Age.—A 

woman, like a man, is not of lawful age until she is 21 years old. She may, however, marry without the consent of her parents after she is 18 years of age. Should she marry before she is 21, her minority ceases, and she is thereafter recognized as being of full age. 

The law recognizes the right of a married woman to change her mind in sale of land until the deed is signed, acknowledged and delivered. sent.

The husband cannot sell the home without her con- During the pendency of divorce proceedings, the wife will be allowed alimony.

HUSBAND AND WIFE 

231 Upon death of the husband, the widow's allowance for one year's support will be paid in preference to other debts.

The homestead, household goods and wearing apparel are exempt property of the wife and family and are not subject to the husband's debts.

CHAPTER 40 HOMESTEAD 

Sec. 357. Definition.-The homestead is the dwelling and land occupied and used by the family as and for a home.
