In the rural districts it may include not exceeding 200 acres of land; in the cities it may include one or more lots or blocks, if the same be used as a part of the home.

At the time of its designation, however, its value, to be exempt, should not exceed $5000.00, after which time it may be improved and its value may increase until it is worth hundreds of thousands of dollars, and still it is a home, and entitled to all of the exemptions of a homestead.

Sec. 358. History of the Homestead.-Texas is the pioneer in the homestead law. It was the first state in the Union to provide that the home where the wife resided and where the children were supposed to be reared, should be exempt from the debts of the head of the family, incurred in business dealings and transactions. Many believe that the time will come when the home will be entirely exempt from taxation. It is now 232

HOMESTEAD 

233 exempt from state taxes but not from county and school district taxes.

Sec. 359. The Object of the Law. The theory of our American institutions is to protect the home and fireside of the citizen; that the basis of every government rests upon the unencumbered and untrammelled citizen, who is supposed in return to protect and love his government, and fight for his home and fireside. His home is his castle and cannot be taken from him, except by his consent and by that of the wife, who must do it freely and voluntarily, without coercion or force. 

All creditors or persons in business dealings must take notice of the fact that the home on which the parties. reside, cannot be subjected to the payment of debts. 

The Constitution of Texas provides that the home shall be exempt from forced sale, except for its purchase money and for taxes. 

Sec. 360. Cannot Be Mortgaged. The home cannot be pledged or mortgaged as security for any debt, except the money for which same was purchased, or improved.

Sec. 361. Mechanic's Lien. -The law provides, however, that by the wife's consent, freely given and acknowledged before a notary public, she and her husband may grant a mechanic's lien for the purpose of making improvements upon said home, and said lien must be executed and signed before any of the material is placed upon the ground, or any of the labor performed.

So jealously does the law guard the home from being mortgaged to raise sums of money for other purposes,

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law that even a deed to the home may, by outside testimony, be shown to be only a mortgage, and thereby declared null and void as affecting the home.

Sec. 362. The Homestead Right. The homestead right does not destroy or abrogate property rights, but the property constituting the home, when it ceases to be the homestead, will pass by descent and distribution to the heirs just as other property, but it cannot be divided or descend as other property so long as the family continues to use it as such. 

If the husband and father dies, the widow has the right to retain the exclusive and entire use of it for herself and minor children during the remainder of her life, and it cannot be divided or partitioned during such time.

So with the husband in event of the death of the wife. 

And, in case of the death of both father and mother, if there be minor children with a duly appointed guardian, who are occupying the home, the homestead rights still remain, and it cannot descend by the law of descent and distribution, nor be partitioned among the heirs, to whom it will go upon the dissolution of the family. 

When the home has been abandoned, and is not used as a homestead any more, it may be subject to debts, and it may then pass by descent and distribution, and be partitioned as other property.

CHAPTER 41 LAW OF ADOPTION 

Sec. 363. An Adopted Heir.-The children born to parents are, in this country, by law, made their natural heirs, and in the absence of a will inherit, by the law of descent and distribution, their property of every kind and character. In the absence of children the brothers and sisters, parents and grandparents, or other kin, inherit. The law provides that any person desiring to adopt a child as his own may do so and make such child his heir at law.

The idea is current that a guardianship constitutes an adoption, and that the ward of the guardian becomes his heir. This is in no sense true. Guardianship is an entirely different procedure from that of adoption. In the former case the guardian undertakes to care for the person and estate of the ward without agreeing to make the ward his heir, or give him any portion of his estate. 

Sec. 364. How Adopted.-No action of court is re quired to adopt a child. All that is necessary is to prepare a written instrument reciting one's intention, and to sign it and acknowledge such signature as a deed would be acknowledged. This statement, when so acknowledged, is to be filed with the county clerk of the 235

DAVIDSON'S Simplified Law county and recorded. In such case the child becomes a lawful heir of the person so adopting him. 

Sec. 365. Right of an Adopted Child.-A child thus adopted is entitled to all the rights of one born to the family. However, in case there should be a child born to the parents, then the adopted child cannot inherit more than one-fourth of the parents' estate, although, if the parents so desire, they may will him a larger share. 

Sec. 366. Transfer by Parents. Should the parents of any child, or the surviving parent, consent that a child be taken and adopted by another person, the parent or parents may give their written consent to release all claim upon such child, and this written instrument must be signed and acknowledged before a notary public. 

This does not give the parties into whose custody the child is given the right to again "transfer" such child.

CHAPTER 42 ARBITRATION 

Sec. 367. Generally.-Men may, whenever and wherever they please, settle their disputes by arbitration, without the control or knowledge of law. This is the simplest and most inexpensive way.

Should they, however, desire to have this arbitration conducted in a manner prescribed by law, they may do so and thus have their disputes adjudicated by law, but by arbitrators rather than by a court. 

Sec. 368. A Written Agreement.-A written agree ment should be prepared, setting out the matter to be arbitrated, and naming two arbitrators, neither of whom should be related to either party, and each of whom should possess all the qualifications of a juror. 

Sec. 369. Agreement Filed in Court.-The agree ment thus prepared will be filed in the proper court having jurisdiction of the subject matter, or the amount in controversy.

Sec. 370. Day of Trial.-Upon such agreement being filed the Justice of the Peace, or the clerk of the court, as the case may be, will name a day for the hearing of such case, not less than two days from the filing 237

of such agreement, and issue subpoenas for any witnesses that may be desired by either party to the arbitration. 

Sec. 371. Oath of the Arbitrators.-Upon the day set for trial the arbitrators will be sworn by the clerk or Justice of the Peace, and the taking of the testimony and the hearing of the statements will begin. They may grant continuances or postponements if sufficient cause be shown. The plaintiff, or person seeking relief, will offer the first testimony, and will be entitled to an opening and closing of the argument. 

Sec. 372. The Award.-The hearing being over, the arbitrators will write out their findings and the same will be filed as a judgment. 

Sec. 373. Umpire.-In case of disagreement between the two arbitrators, as to what should be done, they shall agree upon a third man, to be called in to aid in reaching a decision. If they cannot agree upon a third man, then the Justice of the Peace or clerk will appoint one, who is called the "Umpire." This umpire should be selected before hearing starts.

Sec. 374. Right of Appeal.-The decision of the ar bitrators is final, and the matter in controversy is settled, unless the right of appeal to the courts is expressly reserved in the agreement to arbitrate. 

In case of an appeal to the court the opposite party is served with citation and the case is tried as an ordinary suit.

CHAPTER 43 ASSIGNMENT FOR CREDITORS 

Sec. 375. Defined.-An assignment for the benefit of one's creditors consists of a deed of conveyance of all the property of every kind and character owned by the insolvent person, except his home and other exempt property, to some person named as trustee. 

The trustee holds the same for the benefit of the creditors, and for the purpose of distributing the same among them that they may all receive a just and proportionate share. 

Sec. 376. Inventory.-At the time of making such assignment, it is the duty of the party to make out an inventory with schedules annexed, showing the name and residence and the amount of the debt due each creditor; also the origin and consideration of such debt and the security held to secure its payment. The schedules must further show the total assets of the insolvent party, including all real and personal property, which inventory must be duly sworn to. 239

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law Sec. 377. Accepting Creditors.-This deed of as signment to such trustee may be made for the benefit only of such creditors as shall accept. The insolvent estate, however, must pay as much as 33 1/3 per cent of the indebtedness, or the debtor will not be released from further liability to such debtors. 

If, however, the creditor accepts the assignment and agrees to take his proportionate share of the assets in the hands of such trustee, and these assets pay an amount not less than one-third of his indebtedness, the debtor becomes absolved from any further liability, and the creditors cannot again call upon the debtor for the payment of the balance due.

Sec. 378. Non-Accepting Creditors.-Any creditor, who may feel that the insolvent is not acting in good faith, or may, for any other cause or reason, refuse to accept the terms of the assignment, may demand the payment of his debt in full and proceed to reduce the same to judgment and undertake to subject any property that he believes to be withheld by the defendant to the payment of the judgment, or he may apply to the United States Court for bankruptcy proceedings and have the insolvent declared a bankrupt, and his estate, less the exemptions, prorated among the creditors through this court.

Sec. 379. Notice to Creditors.-Within 30 days after making such assignment, the creditors should all be notified by mail of such action, giving the name of the trustee and such other data as will inform the creditors of the condition of affairs. These notices should be mailed to each creditor and should also be published in some paper. Any creditor receiving such notice is

ASSIGNMENT FOR CREDITORS 

241 entitled to four months within which to accept the terms of the assignment.

The assignee, or trustee, must give bond, which shall be filed in the court, to protect the creditors and to account for all of the funds of the estate that come into his hands.

CHAPTER 44 BANKRUPTCY-A FEDERAL PROCEDURE FOR INSOLVENTS Sec. 380. Federal Bankrupt Laws.-The Congress of the United States has enacted a uniform bankruptcy law which applies alike in all of the states, and may be availed of by any person voluntarily who desires to obtain relief from his debts, or may be availed of by the creditors of any insolvent person for the purpose of equally distributing his estate among the several creditors. 

Sec. 381. Who Are Bankrupts.-Any person who makes a deed of assignment, or who attempts in a state of insolvency to prefer one creditor over another, or attempts to conceal or hide his property while in such insolvent condition, is termed a bankrupt by the provisions of the law, and may be, upon application of the creditors, adjudged a bankrupt and have his estate administered under the bankrupt law. 

Sec. 382. The Defense of the Bankrupt.—Should the creditors be in error about the insolvency of the supposed bankrupt, he has the right of answering and showing such facts as would establish his solvency. In which 242

BANKRUPTCY-A FEDERAL PROCEDURE FOR INSOLVENTS 243 

event, the petitioning creditors should pay the cost, and in some cases, the damage, if any, that the supposed bankrupt may have sustained.

Sec. 383. Petition For.-The petition for bank ruptcy may be voluntary or involuntary. When filed by the bankrupt himself, it is said to be voluntary; when filed by his creditors, it is said to be involuntary. 

The reason for voluntary bankruptcy is where the bankrupt feels himself so hopelessly involved in debt that he is unable to pay, and desires to wipe off the slate, surrender what he has to his creditors, and start over in life without having his future accumulations encumbered and covered by old debts and judgments; for after a man is adjudged a bankrupt, all preexisting indebtedness is at an end and can never thereafter be collected, except such portion as shall be paid from the estate of the bankrupt then in existence. 

Sec. 384. Who May Become Bankrupts.-Persons who owe more than $1000.00 may be adjudged bankrupts, but no wage earner, railroads, buildings, road associations or state banks, can avail themselves of such law. 

Sec. 385. Duty of Bankrupts.-The duty of bankrupts is to attend the creditors' meeting, to obey orders of the court, and to file a schedule showing the condition of his estate.

Sec. 386. Referee.-The judges of the several Federal District Courts appoint referees to whom matters of bankruptcy may be referred for hearing. This referee acts something as a deputy to the judge. He causes notices to be issued to the creditors, and declares the

dividends from the estate, prorating the money going to the different creditors.

Sec. 387. Trustee. The creditors of the bankrupt meet and discuss the condition of the estate and elect a trustee, or upon their failure to elect, the court will appoint one. This trustee is custodian of all of the property and effects of the bankrupt. 

Sec. 388. Exemptions.-The bankrupt law allows to the bankrupt the benefit of all exemptions allowed by the laws of the state in which he lives, his homestead, his wages, his library, and a limited amount of live stock and other articles enumerated in the state law of exempt property; and these articles cannot be applied by the creditors upon the payment of his debts; and the bankrupt will exercise full control over them. 

Sec. 389. Liens Dissolved.-All attachments levied and mortgages or liens given for the four months preceding bankruptcy, except in good faith with no intention to defraud or secure priority of payment, are dissolved and rendered void and without effect by the institution of bankruptcy proceedings.

This enables one creditor to place himself on equal footing with another and to have the estate prorated equally among them all. 

Remarks.-Bankruptcy proceedings are generally very expensive and the dividends from the insolvent estate are often eaten up and consumed by the expense of the litigation. It is ordinarily better for the creditors and the debtor to make an honest effort to prorate the assets of the insolvent among the creditors themselves, rather than that the estate be put through bankruptcy.

BANKRUPTCY-A FEDERAL PROCEDURE FOR INSOLVENTS 245 

One great value, however, of the bankruptcy law in cases of small and medium sized estates, is that it may hold in check all debtors or creditors who seek undue advantage over other creditors, and forces them to share the losses equally. And too, the fear, on the part of creditors, of expensive bankruptcy proceedings often enables the bankrupt to effect a more equitable settlement with his creditors. This gives the reader only an idea of the purpose of the bankruptcy act. It is altered and extended both by interpretation and by act of Congress. 

A competent lawyer should be consulted before any action is had.

CHAPTER 45 EXEMPTIONS 

Sec. 390. Imprisonment for Debt.-In the earlier ages of the English Jurisprudence, and even down to the adoption of the Constitution of the United States, men were imprisoned for debt; that is, when they refused to pay, or were unable to pay a financial obligation, they were placed in prison until the debt was paid. 

This was a harsh treatment, and if the debtor had really failed in a financial way, it even became impossible for him, although a good man, to procure his liberty. Thus numbers of good citizens were made to share the felon's cell with the thief, the murderer and the ordinary criminal.

It was of these unfortunate people that James Oglethorpe founded the colony in Georgia. 

Sec. 391. Property Subject to Debt.-As democratic ideas took hold of the minds of the people, the world became more lenient upon this subject and imprisonment for debt was abolished and is not now permitted in the United States, nor in many other countries. At one time all of the property of the debtor was subject to his obligations and might be sold and applied to the 246

EXEMPTIONS 

247 satisfaction of the debt; even the wearing apparel upon his person, the home in which he lived, and all that he and his family possessed might be taken and sold. 

Sec. 392. Modern Policy More Humane.—The more recent enactments of law, as well as state constitutions, have made rapid strides in the direction of protecting and caring for the homes of the unfortunate and oppressed.

In a free government, every citizen is a sovereign. 

He is supposed to be patriotic and to fight for his home and fireside, and for a government that protects him, and the government has thrown its protecting arm around this home, and certain necessary effects are designated as exempt property. These cannot be subjected to the debts of the owner, nor sold at forced sale for any purpose. 

It might be said that this is a harsh rule behind which bad men may hide and avoid the payment of their just debts. But it might be observed in this same connection that no rule or regulation or doctrine, howsoever good or religious, has ever been propagated or established, but that at times it may have been used as a cloak for the lawless; there is no counterfeit without the genuine.

With the exemption laws known, the people in dealing with the irresponsible citizen must take notice of the fact that his home is exempt and not subject to his obligations, just as they would take notice of the fact in any case where the man owned no home or property at all.

Sec. 393. Property Exempt to the Family.-The exempt property in Texas consists of:

Ist. 

The homestead. 

2nd. Household and kitchen furniture. 

3rd. A lot in the cemetery. 

4th. Farming implements. 

5th. Tools, books, and every appurtenance of a trade or profession. 

6th. Library and family portraits. 

7th. Five milk cows and calves. 

8th. Two yoke of oxen. 

9th. Two horses or mules and a wagon. 

10th. One carriage or buggy. 

11th. One gun. 

12th. Twenty hogs. 

13th. Twenty sheep. 

14th. Saddle, bridle and harness used by family. 

15th. Provisions and forage for home consumption. 

16th. Current wages.

Sec. 394. Exemptions to Persons Other Than Fam ilies.-A single person is entitled to the following exempt property: 

Ist. Lot in the cemetery. 

2nd. Wearing apparel. 

3rd. Tools, books and any appurtenances belonging to any trade or profession. 4th. 

Horse, bridle and saddle. 

5th. Current wages. 

Sec. 395. The Exempt Homestead. The exempt homestead above referred to is the residence used by the family as a home, and improvements connected therewith. If in the country the homestead cannot exceed 

200 acres; if in town the home may be composed of one or more lots, if at the time of the selection of the same

EXEMPTIONS 

249 as a home, the value does not exceed $5,000.00, exclusive of improvements.

Property once a part of the home, but no longer used in such connection, may, under certain circumstances, lose its homestead nature and rights and become subject to the debts of the owner.

In addition to the residence, the homestead may include a farm not even adjacent to the residence lot, but from which the family support is drawn, and which is used and cultivated by the family. It may include a business house entirely separate from the residence, where the support of the family is earned. This business house is known in law as the Business Homestead. 

Sec. 396. A Home Cannot Be Mortgaged.-So sacred does the law guard the homestead that it not only cannot be subjected to forced sale, but as elsewhere explained, it cannot even be voluntarily mortgaged or encumbered to secure a debt. All other exempt property, such as horses, cattle and other personal property, may be bound or mortgaged to secure a debt, and after failure to pay same, the mortgage may be foreclosed and the property taken and applied upon the debt, but this must be done by a contract voluntarily made. So, also, may a single person mortgage his or her homestead, but a married person cannot, not even by his wife's consent.

Sec. 397. Public Property Exempt.-In addition to the property exempted to families and individuals, the law has also exempted the public buildings of the community, which are held and used for the benefit of the municipality. Hence, the courthouse, the city hall, public library, and all property belonging to the city or

county and used for the public benefit cannot be seized and sold for debt.

If, however, a city or county, or other public institution holds property for profit, that is not used for the public benefit, this may be subject to the law the same. as the property of an individual. 

Sec. 398. Exceptions.-The rents due upon farms and advances made to enable the tenant to make crops are covered by the statute of the landlord's lien, and these crops, as well as the stock, tools and implements furnished to raise the same, are not protected by the law of exemptions, but are subject to the landlord's lien, who took the risk and made the necessary advances to enable the tenant to grow the crops.

Summary: From the foregoing it appears that the homestead of a family cannot be taken for debt-it cannot even be mortgaged to secure a debt; that the homestead of a single person, and all other exempt property of any person, may be mortgaged to secure a debt, but cannot be taken to pay debts, except it be first voluntarily mortgaged by contract; but that all property of persons over and above exemptions may, at any time, be subjected by legal process to forced sale for the payment of debts.

CHAPTER 46 LIENS 

Sec. 399. Liens Defined.-A lien may be defined as a right against property of a debtor whereby the payment of a debt is secured in having pledged for its payment a specific piece of property. 

As for example: John Doe may give his note to

Richard Roe for $100.00. In this case the debt exists, but there is no lien; but, if John Doe also gives a written mortgage upon a horse and delivers it to Richard Roe to secure the payment of said note, then Richard Roe has a debt, and in addition thereto, a lien upon a specific piece of property. This lien stands ahead of some other prior or subsequent creditor without a lien; it is ahead of any creditor whose debt may be subsequently made, in so far as the same may be secured by the horse.

If this note should be collected by law, the mortgage would be foreclosed and the horse sold. If this horse brought only $90.00, this $90.00 would be applied upon the debt evidenced by the note, and the remaining $10.00 would still be due from John Doe, although Richard Roe would have no lien to secure the payment of this 251

balance of $10.00; if there should be any other property above exemptions, it could be, by execution, subjected to the payment of the same. If, however, the horse brought $125.00, or any sum more than the $100.00, the excess, after payment of the debt and costs would be paid back to the owner of the horse. 

There are two kinds of liens: Statutory Liens and 

Contract Liens. 

Sec. 400. Statutory Liens.-Liens created by law are called Statutory Liens. The following are several of the most common classes: 

Ist. Taxes. Taxes are a lien upon all of the property of the party owing such taxes. The tax against a homestead is a lien against it, but, taxes due on other property cannot be collected out of the homestead. 

2nd. Judgment Liens.-When a judgment is ob tained in any suit against a party and an abstract or synopsis of the same is filed and recorded in the county clerk's office, this becomes a lien upon all of the real property of the defendant in the county, and acts as an encumbrance upon said property just as a mortgage, and remains in force for a period of ten years. At any time during this period the lands, above the homestead exemptions, may be subjected to the payment of such judgment. 

3rd. The Laborer's or Mechanic's Lien. - This lien can be fastened upon a homestead only by written contract, made in advance, as has been elsewhere explained, but the mechanic and laborer, who performs work upon any other property, whether by written contract or not, may file his contract or a sworn statement of his claim, at any time within three months after his

LIENS 

253 debt is due, with the county clerk, and the filing of this statement or contract creates a lien upon the property named in favor of the laborer or mechanic, which may be enforced by suit. 

4th. Clerks, Accountants, etc.-Clerks, bookkeepers, laborers, farm hands and accountants likewise have a lien upon the goods, merchandise or farm products and other property or materials connected with the service performed to secure their wages. This lien must be fixed by filing a sworn statement of the amount due and labor performed with the county clerk. 

5th. Railroad Laborers.-Those who labor upon any railroad with teams, shovels or otherwise, have a lien to secure their wages against the railroad, its franchise and equipment for a period of twelve months after such wages become due, and it is not necessary to file this lien to fix, or in any manner secure it-it remains in force for a period of twelve months, and before the expiration of the twelve months, if not paid, suit should be filed, which keeps the lien alive. 

Only those who perform manual labor are protected by this lien; the clerks and accountants in the offices are not included. 

6th. Hotels.-Hotels and boarding houses have a lien upon the baggage of their guests to secure the payment of board bills, and to protect their lien they should not permit the baggage to be removed until the bills are paid. 

7th. Livery Stables.-The owners of livery stables and pastures also have a lien to secure their charges, upon the live stock placed therein.

8th. Blacksmiths.-A carpenter, blacksmith, or mechanic, who repairs any article or vehicle of whatever kind has a lien upon the article repaired to secure his charge, and he may retain possession of the same until such charges are paid. 

9th. Landlord.—A landlord has a lien on the crops produced by his tenant to secure his rents and advances. 

So also has the owner of a building a lien upon the property of the tenant placed therein, which lien remains in force while such property is in said building, or on such farm, and for thirty days after its removal. 

If the debt is not paid within thirty days suit should be brought, or the lien is lost. This lien does not cover household goods and exempt property. 

10th. Attachment Liens.-An attachment lien does not exist by reason of law, but the law provides that the lien may be created without the person's consent by means of attachment. 

For example: If A owes B a debt, and the lawful grounds for an attachment exist, as explained in Chapter 18, B may issue a writ of attachment and levy upon A's property, and this property, if not exempt or mortgaged, becomes subject to B's debt and B is entitled to have his debt satisfied out of such property before it can be subjected to any other person's debt. 

11th. Stallions.-The owners of stallions, bulls, etc., have a lien upon the offspring or progeny. It is not necessary to record any instrument to fix this lien, and it remains in force for a period of ten months from the birth of such offspring or progeny, but such lien cannot be enforced until five months after birth.

LIENS 

255 

Sec. 401. Contract Liens.-In addition to the Statu tory Liens, which are made to exist as a matter of law for the protection of certain parties, the parties may, by mutual contract, give and deliver their written instrument whereby liens are created. In this class may be enumerated:

Ist. Mortgages. A contract by which one party gives to another a written instrument conveying property to secure a debt, thus creating a lien on the property, is called a mortgage.

This mortgage may be on crops, live stock, or other personal property, and should be filed with the county clerk immediately after its execution. The clerk enters a brief sketch of the names of the parties and the property mortgaged in a book kept for that purpose styled, "Chattel Mortgages." This record is kept for the information of the public, and is at all times open for inspection. 

2nd. Pledges. Where property by which a debt is secured, consists of jewelry, notes, bonds or valuable papers, the possession of same may be delivered to the party whom it is desired to secure, but by this delivery such personals do not become the property of the person with whom they are placed, but are held by him to secure the payment of his debt. 

3rd. Deeds of Trust. Where the conveyance given is to real property it is not called a chattel mortgage, but is known as a "deed of trust," by which one party deeds land to another not to pass the title, but that the title may be held in trust to secure the payment of a debt. If the debt is not paid at maturity, the property may be sold by the trustee, or foreclosed upon and sold under order of

court, and the proceeds applied to the payment of the debt.

A deed of trust, unlike a chattel mortgage, must be recorded at length by the county clerk in a book kept for that purpose. 

4th. Mechanic's Lien. A mechanic's lien is one created by contract to secure the erection ordinarily of a building where a portion of the money and cost of material is not to be paid in cash. If this building is to be a homestead, or to be situated upon the homestead, the signature of the wife must be had to the contract and the same must be fully explained to her, and she must acknowledge it privately and apart from her husband as she would a deed to her home, and this must be done before any of the material is placed upon the premises, or any of the labor done.

A mechanic's lien must be recorded at length like a deed of trust. 

5th. Vendor's Liens. When in the sale of real estate, a part or all of the purchase price is represented by notes, a lien may be retained by the seller to secure the payment of the notes. This lien should be reserved in the deed and mentioned in the notes. It is superior to all other claims against the property.

The foregoing enumeration is sufficient to illustrate liens created by contracts and liens created by the statutes, and comprises those that most frequently come within the ordinary affairs of life.

CHAPTER 47 EMINENT DOMAIN Sec. 402. Definition.-The right of eminent domain. 

is the superior right of the public to subject the property of the individual to the public good under certain conditions. The Constitution and Statutes of the state, however, guarantee to the individual that his property shall not be taken and applied to public use without due compensation being first made.

Sec. 403. The Right: By Whom Exercised.-The state, county, city, or public carriers may exercise this right. It is, perhaps, more generally exercised by railroad companies in procuring their rights of way across private property. It is exercised by cities in building aqueducts, laying sewer pipes and water mains, and other like public utilities.

Sec. 404. Damages Assessed.—Where the parties can not agree upon the damage sustained, or compensation to be paid, the law provides that the County Judge may appoint three disinterested citizens to act as commissioners to estimate and determine the amount that shall be paid. If the parties can agree upon these commissioners, the court in appointing them will give preference to the 257

persons agreed upon, otherwise he will appoint them himself.

Upon the appointment of the commissioners they should view the property. They set a day for hearing and have witnesses appear before them, and hear such testimony and facts as will enable them to determine the claims of the parties, and assess the amount of damages that the landowner will sustain. 

Sec. 405. Right of Appeal.-If the landowner, or either party, is dissatisfied with the award made, he may at any time within ten days file his objections and have the other party cited, and the case heard in court in regular form as an ordinary suit. 

Sec. 406. The Purpose of This Right.-The aim and object in the administration of our laws should be to secure the greatest good for the greatest number, and were it not for the right of eminent domain, any selfish and unreasonable citizen might prevent many thousands of others from receiving a vast and substantial comfort. 

It may be necessary to open a street for the comfort and benefit of the citizens of a community, or it may be necessary to supply the inhabitants of a large city with pure fresh water from some out of town district, and this supply may have to be piped across the private premises of contrary and disinterested parties, who, were it not for the law of eminent domain, might deprive all the people of the city of this necessity. 

The right of eminent domain is given to railroads, interurban lines, telegraph and telephone companies, because the law recognizes the services performed by these corporations as of public or quasi public character, as the operation of railroads, the rapid transportation of

EMINENT DOMAIN 

259 freight, and the carrying of mail and freight by railway trains is now a public necessity. 

The Government does not own the railroads, but in consideration of its giving the corporation the benefit of this right of eminent domain, it exercises the right to control and regulate these public carriers in the management of their business, and which it properly should do to a degree far more than with that of a private business establishment.

CHAPTER 48 MUNICIPAL LAW Sec. 407. City Laws.-Acts of Congress are known as Federal Laws; the Acts of the Legislature are known as the Statutes of the State; the Ordinances of a City 

Council, or City Commission, are Laws of the Municipality, and apply only to the particular town or city. 

Sec. 408. City Charters.-The Constitution of the state provides that all cities having more than 5,000 population may have a special charter. This charter is to the city government what the Constitution is to the state; that is, it is a basic law that the city council has no power to change.

This charter lays down the plan of city government, defines its powers and authorizes the city to legislate and enact ordinances within certain bounds so as not to conflict with any of the laws of the state. 

A charter is now formulated by a chosen committee and adopted by a vote of the people of the particular city. Formerly, it was by act of the Legislature. It is supposed to cover every subject of city government, such as streets, water, light, fire, sanitation, public schools, and any other subject that may be desirable to place under the control of the city. 260

MUNICIPAL LAW 

261 By virtue of this charter so adopted, the city has power to administer its affairs and to make and exact necessary ordinances and regulations for its citizens. 

Sec. 409. The City Council.-Formerly all of our cities were governed by a mayor and the city council. 

The mayor was the head of the government, and the council was a body of men elected from the several wards of the city, who acted as a legislative board, and were presided over by the mayor. They possessed the power to make laws for the city; to levy taxes, make public improvements and appropriate the funds to the several departments.

Sec. 410. The Commission Form of Government.— 

Since the Galveston flood, another form of city government has become very popular with people, known as the Commission Form. And more lately the city manager form has become popular. 

Instead of having a city council composed of a great number of aldermen, the city is governed by a board of commissioners, usually from three to five men, as may be provided in the charter. The head of the commission is called the mayor, or the chairman of the commission, as may be provided. This commission has all of the power of the old city council, and is much less unwieldy; it acts with more directness and more precision. The fiery debates that once took place at the city council meetings are rarely met with before a board of commissioners, who, in a quiet way, agree upon the best course and act in most cases by unanimous vote without a division.

Sec. 411. Initiative, Referendum and Recall. These terms are not confined to municipal law; they may apply to the state, or even a nation. Many of the city charters

of the state embody them as a safeguard to the people in the government of cities. They are, perhaps, better adapted to municipal government than to larger bodies. 

By Initiative is meant the right of the people to initiate an ordinance; that is, by a petition of a certain percentage-usually twenty-five per cent of people they may present an ordinance to the city commission or council asking that it be passed. It is the duty of the council to pass such ordinance, or to order an election whereby the people of the town shall vote upon the proposition, and if the majority vote in favor of it, it becomes a law. 

By Referendum is meant that the people have a right to have the propositions that have been acted upon by the city government referred back to them (the people). 

For instance: If an ordinance enacted by the city council or commission is unsatisfactory, a petition, duly presented, containing the proper percentage of voters, requires the commission to order an election whereby the people may approve or disapprove the same. If the majority vote against such ordinance it is repealed and is of no further force or effect. 

By Recall is meant the right of the people to recall any city official elected by them. If his conduct has been unsatisfactory or in any way reprehensible, the people may, by petition duly signed and presented, have an election ordered for such office. In such election new candidates may run, and if the then occupant of such office is not reelected his term expires by virtue of such election, and the new man takes his place. 

These provisions should not be used on frivolous occasions, and in fact, are rarely ever used by any city,

MUNICIPAL LAW 

263 but they are a safeguard for the protection of the rights of the people against unwise and unscrupulous officers.. 

Sec. 412. Police Court.-The charter in addition to providing for the city council, also provides for a police court, usually known as the Recorder's Court. 

In this court cases of arrest made by the city police on charges of ordinary misdemeanors are tried. This court has no civil jurisdiction that would enable it to try suits between parties, but has the criminal jurisdiction of a Justice of the Peace, and in some charters the criminal jurisdiction of a County Court. In this court are tried cases of disturbing the peace, intoxication, abusive language, obstructing the public streets, creating a public nuisance, and in fact all other misdemeanors of this rank and class.

Sec. 413. The School Board.-Each city is usually, by virtue of its charter, created into an independent school district, managed and controlled by a Board of School Trustees. These trustees are usually appointed by the city commission or council. It has been found most satisfactory to have them appointed, as the election of a school trustee so often breeds dissatisfaction in his future administration of the public school. 

This Board of Trustees elects a City School Superintendent and employs all the teachers necessary for the several public schools of the city; provides for buildings and their furnishing and equipment, and does all things necessary to operate the city schools.

The charter usually authorizes the city to vote a special tax for school purposes. This tax, when collected, is with the other school funds paid over to this board to be used for the benefit of the schools.

Sec. 414. Public Utilities.—Many of our cities now have the right under their charters to own all the public utilities and are the owners of, and operate, the water works, the electric light plant and the sewer system. 

Sec. 415. Other City Officers. In addition to the 

Mayor or Chairman of the Commission and the City Council or Commissioners, and the Recorder, who presides over the Recorder's Court, there are several other city officers: The City Attorney, being the legal adviser of the city government, and all its departments, and the representative of the city in all suits; the Chief of Police, who has charge of the police force and the preservation of order and quiet; the Sanitary Officer, whose duty is to keep the town clean and report any unsanitary premises, to look into back yards, even of private residences, and see that there are no cesspools or rain barrels to breed mosquitoes, or other unsanitary conditions to breed flies and spread disease to the public; the City Secretary, who keeps the minutes of the city commission; the City 

Treasurer, or Auditor, who keeps the accounts of the city's finances and cares for the moneys and several funds; and the Assessor and Collector of Taxes. These latter offices are very frequently combined, and so are the offices of City Secretary and Treasurer, and in some instances, all four of the offices are combined, deputies and assistants being appointed as may be found necessary. 

A good city government is run upon a business basis, and any good citizen should never become so engrossed in his private affairs as to prevent him from taking a keen interest in the management of his municipal administration and seeing that the best possible men are had for the public places. Next after the management

MUNICIPAL Law 

265 of his own business affairs, the citizen of a city should be interested in his city government. 

Sec. 416. Charters for Small Towns.-Towns of less than 5,000 inhabitants may not have special charters, but they may accept certain general laws provided for governing cities and towns, and incorporate and have a Mayor and City Council, very similar to the larger cities, but they have no special charter and their powers are much more limited. Upon the whole, however, the administration of the affairs of a small town is upon the general plan of that indicated above for larger cities.

CHAPTER 49 PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

Sec. 417. History.-Public free schools are an outgrowth of and a necessary counterpart to democratic government.

The United States of America was the pioneer in this great movement a boon to civilization, and a safeguard of personal liberty and free government. Man cannot protect his rights or successfully defend them until he knows what they are. In a monarchy, where the mass of people are supposed to have but little or no voice in public affairs, an education may not be so essential to the welfare of the state; it may suffice to educate rulers only. 

Under the republican form of government, however, every citizen is supposed to be a voter and every voter a sovereign, a part of the governing power, and in order to exercise this power intelligently, a certain amount of education and general information is indispensable. 

It was well said by one of the founders of the Nation: "An ignorant voter with a ballot in his hand, is more to be dreaded by the commonwealth than the muskets of foreign lands."

Thomas Jefferson, the author of the Declaration of Independence, the great democratic statesman and schol- 266

PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

267 ar, was one of the chief exponents of the idea of universal education, and while some progress was made during his period, it was many years later before anything like the present magnificent system of public schools that we now enjoy had been established in all the states of the Union; but the seed sown by Jefferson and his copatriots have been constantly bearing fruit until almost every child has an opportunity to obtain an education at public expense.

An educated people being necessary to the preservation of the republican form of government, the state should not only give every child an opportunity to attend the free public school, but should, to a certain extent, compel its attendance, not alone for the child's benefit, but for the benefit of the state in which he will later become a sovereign voter. 

Sec. 418. The Texas Public Schools.-The Constitu tion of the old Republic of Texas directed that Congress make provisions for a system of public free schools. The 

Constitution adopted in 1876—the one under which we now live-declares: "A general diffusion of knowledge being essential to the preservation of the liberties and rights of the people, it shall be the duty of the Legislature of the state to establish and make suitable provisions for the support and maintenance of an efficient system of 

Public Free Schools."

Sec. 419. Permanent School Funds.-The Legisla ture of Texas, in compliance with the Constitution, set apart a vast amount of the public domain as a part of the public school fund. All of the unsold public lands of 

Texas not otherwise appropriated, now belong to the public free schools. This permanent fund now includes

many million dollars and cannot be used, or in any way spent or appropriated, but must be invested in bonds or approved interest-bearing securities, and the interest so received becomes a part of the available school funds. 

Sec. 420. The Available School Funds.-By avail able school funds is meant the funds to be distributed annually for the support of the schools for the scholastic year. This fund is composed of the interest received from investments of the permanent school fund, a poll tax of $1.00 per head upon all persons from 21 to 60 years of age, one-fourth of all the occupation taxes collected, including gasoline, and an ad valorem tax not to exceed 20 cents on the $100.00 valuation of all property in the state. This fund has now become sufficient so that when distributed there is from $15.00 to $30.00 per capita for each scholastic, so that the school district with one hundred scholastics would receive from $1,500.00 to $3,000.00 of the public school money from the state. 

In addition to this fund provided by the state, the law allows cities and towns and school districts to vote a further tax within the particular district which may be added to the general fund, or used to improve the school property.

Sec. 421. White and Colored.-The Constitution of the state requires that the white and colored children be taught in separate schools, and no child of either race will be permitted to enter the schools of the other. 

Sec. 422. State Teachers Colleges.-As a part of the great school system that our state has been gradually developing, there have been established colleges at 

Huntsville (The Sam Houston Normal), Denton, San 

Marcos, Alpine, Commerce, Nacogdoches and Canyon

PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

269 City. In these schools teachers are specially prepared and trained for the work of teaching in the public schools. 

These institutions are maintained at public expense. The teachers equipped here are supposed to be better prepared for the work of teaching than those who have not attended such institutions, and for this reason the public free schools of the state have grown in favor with all classes until even the wealthiest citizens feel that their children can get better training in the public free schools than in most private institutions. Any one preparing to teach will do well to take training in a teachers college. 

Sec. 423. In addition to the State Teachers Colleges above mentioned, the state maintains a number of higher institutions of learning: (a) University of Texas at Austin, with an enrollment of many thousand students, the permanent fund of which has been greatly enriched by the discovery of oil upon its allotment of the state public land. 

It has a medical branch at Galveston and a School of Mines at El Paso.

The University is controlled by a board of nine regents appointed by the Governor of Texas. 

(b) Agricultural and Mechanical College at College 

Station, near Bryan. While it is nominally a branch of the University of Texas, no detailed control is exercised. 

The two institutions, to all practical purposes of operation, are independent of each other.

The "A & M," as it is popularly called, gives military training and specializes in agricultural and engineering courses.

It is controlled by a board of nine directors appointed by the Governor of Texas.

(c) North Texas Junior Agricultural College at Arlington and John Tarleton Agricultural College at 

Stephenville. Each of these junior colleges is under the control and direction of the Agricultural and Mechanical College. Each has a splendid student body, and not being so large, the younger student comes more directly in touch with the faculty and enjoys the benefits of this contact. 

(d) Texas Technological College at Lubbock. This is a newer institution and a school of the first class, governed and controlled by a board of nine directors appointed by the Governor of the State. As its name indicates, it features instruction in technology and textile engineering, but also carries the usual college courses. 

(e) Texas State College for Women at Denton. This is the Texas school for women. It is controlled by a board of nine regents appointed by the Governor, four of whom shall be women. In addition to the usual college courses which are of a high order, special training is given in the domestic arts, including dressmaking, a chemical study of food and cooking, practical housekeeping and nursing, and also courses in business. 

(f) Texas College of Arts and Industries at Kings ville. This school, younger than the others, is winning for itself a place among them worthy of the patronage it receives. 

(g) Prairie View State Normal and Industrial Col lege. This is a school for the training of colored teachers. It is under the control of the board of directors of the Agricultural and Mechanical College. It performs a valuable service to the Negro race and incidentally to the whole state.

PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

271 The state has other school interests but the principal institutions have been enumerated. In a work of this kind no attempt is made to go into details. 

Sec. 424. State Board of Education.-The State 

Board of Education consists of nine members appointed by the Governor, by and with the consent of the Senate. 

The term of three of its members expires every two years, so that a full term is six years. This board looks after the general school interests of the state, including the selection of textbooks. Its activities, however, do not conflict with those of the State Superintendent of Public Instruction, or with those of the board of regents, or directors, of the various institutions of learning. 

Sec. 425. State Superintendent.-The State Superin tendent of Public Instruction is elected by the people every two years. He has an office at the State Capitol and takes general supervision of all of the school work in the state. He superintends the preparation of all examinations to be conducted for teachers. He hears all appeals that are taken from the rulings of County Superintendents.

Sec. 426. County School Superintendents.-All coun ties having a scholastic population of more than 3,000 have a County Superintendent of Public Instruction. 

This superintendent has an office at the county seat and conducts examinations of applicants to teach; approves all contracts made by trustees; holds Teachers' Institutes, which all the teachers in the county are supposed to attend, and there discuss matters of common interest for the general advancement of the school work. It is also his duty to visit in person the public schools and observe the work done by the teachers and see the gen-

eral conditions of the schools, and advise and aid the teachers in their work. 

Most cities have a City Superintendent. In counties having less than 3,000 scholastic population, the County Judge is ex officio County Superintendent. 

Sec. 427. Census.-During the month of March, one of the trustees, or some other capable person appointed by the superintendent, will, by making a house to house canvass, take the scholastic census, which includes every child that will be six years old and under eighteen years old on the first of September following.

The trustees of any school district may admit free of tuition any pupil from the district not over twenty-one years of age.

Sec. 428. Teachers.-No person can teach in the public free schools without being qualified and equipped for the work. The state requires that this equipment be ascertained and a certificate issued of such fact. No board of trustees can lawfully contract with a teacher until the teacher has first exhibited his or her certificate. It is made a penal offense for a trustee to do so until such certificate is produced.

Teachers must be of good moral character and be able to speak intelligently the English language, and must keep a daily register showing the daily attendance of pupils and make a monthly report from this register. 

No one carries heavier responsibilities than the teacher. 

The final destiny of the children is affected and largely molded by the impressions made while under the teacher's care, and when compared with the good done and service rendered, there are perhaps few people in the world so poorly paid in dollars and cents as the teachers.

PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

273 They never grow rich, few have a bank account, and in most cases, their greatest treasure is the grateful remembrance of the hundreds of pupils who have grown into mature manhood and womanhood. 

Sec. 429. Board of Examiners.-The State Superin tendent appoints a State Board of Examiners, consisting of three competent teachers living within the state, to serve during his pleasure.

County Board of Examiners. The County Superin tendent shall appoint two persons as a County Board of Examiners, each of whom shall hold a state teacher's certificate of the first grade or higher. 

The County Board will hold an examination on the first Friday and Saturday in the months of April, June,

July, September and December. The County Board must use the questions prescribed by the State Department of Education.

All teachers applying for state certificates will have their papers forwarded directly to the State Superintendent and graded by the State Board of Examiners. 

Teachers' Certificates.-Teachers' certificates are of two kinds or groups: (1) Elementary certificates. (2) High School certificates. (3) Special certificates. 

Elementary certificates are of the following classes: 

1. Elementary certificates of the first class. 

2. Elementary certificates of the second class. 

3. Elementary certificates of a permanent class. 

High school certificates are similarly divided and grouped. Special certificates are granted in kindergarten

work and special branches of study. Certificates are granted upon the completion of certain specified college work which may be extended and built upon. A permanent high school certificate will be granted a student who has satisfactorily completed a four-year course in any State Teachers College, The University, or other senior college.

Anyone may obtain from the State Department of Education a bulletin giving in detail the law applicable to these matters. 

Teachers holding diplomas from the University, or any of the State Teachers Colleges, do not have to stand an examination, but their diplomas entitle them to the rank of a teacher holding a permanent certificate. 

Sec. 430. Trustees.-Each school district has a board of three trustees, to be elected by the people at an election held for that purpose on the first Saturday in April. 

The trustees so elected hold office for a term of two years. 

The term of two of the trustees expires one year and the term of the third trustee expires the following year, so that there are always one or two old members on the board. In case the trustees are not elected, it is the duty of the superintendent to appoint them, and while it is a prerogative of the people to elect them, it is in many cases better for the schools to have them appointed by the superintendent. He is, in many cases, a better judge of the qualifications of the applicants, and is often successful in inducing a good person to take the appointment who could not be persuaded to make the race at an election. The greatest disadvantage of all, however, in electing school trustees, is that in small villages and some rural districts, considerable partisan feeling is

PUBLIC SCHOOLS 

275 aroused that enters into the employment of teachers, and in many cases, militates against the successful management of the school. The community is generally fortunate where it permits the superintendent to name its 

Board of Trustees.

In nearly all of the cities, a Board of Trustees is appointed by the city government. The Board of Trustees determines how many schools shall be taught in the district; how many teachers to each school; the amount of salary to be paid; and the amount to be expended, if any, for fuel and necessary supplies.

The high schools of the county, not within independent districts, are controlled by five trustees elected by the entire county. The several schools of the county may be, by the superintendent, classified into: Primary, intermediate and high schools.

The Board of Trustees constitute a body politic and may sue and be sued; they may contract and be contracted with; they employ the teachers; purchase necessary supplies, and assist in the management and control of the schools; they cannot include in the teacher's salary any sum of money except for the teacher's wages; they cannot arrange with the teacher to apply any portion of his or her salary to the payment of fuel or for other expenses. Under the nepotism law, they cannot employ any relative to teach, nor issue any voucher for pay to any person whomsoever that is related within the third degree (as close as the second cousin) to any member of the board. They sign the teachers' contracts and the vouchers for monthly payments.

The trustees of a school should be of the best citizenship in their respective communities; they should be

thoughtful of the general good of the school and careful that their individual likes and dislikes do not enter into the discharge of their duties to the public. Any trust is sacred, but a trust for all the children of a community is doubly so.

CHAPTER 50 CORPORATIONS 

Sec. 431. Definition.-A corporation is an artificial being a name given to an association of people who unite, in a certain manner provided by law, for the development or execution of any particular enterprise, or for forwarding any purpose.

There are private corporations, public corporations, and quasi public corporations. 

Sec. 432. Private Corporations.-A private corpora tion is one created for some religious, charitable or profitable purpose. If incorporated for profit, all of the proposed capital must be subscribed, and fifty per cent actually paid in. The charter and written instrument setting out the purpose of the corporation and names of its directors must be filed with the Secretary of State at Austin.

A corporation can be formed for conducting but one business, except in a few cases in the performance of kindred or associated enterprises, as in the manufacture of electricity and power and ice, but the same corporation could not run a bank and a dry goods store. 277

If the charter is not prepared according to law, or is for more purposes than is permitted by law, the Secretary of State will not approve or file the same. 

Corporations incorporated in other states may obtain a permit to do business in Texas by filing a copy of their charters, and by otherwise complying with the Texas Statutes.

Sec. 433. The Charter.-The charter procured in 

Texas must contain: Ist. 

Name of the corporation. 

2nd. Purpose for which it is chartered. 

3rd. Place or places at which it is proposed to do business. 

4th. Term of years, or life of corporation, which in no case shall exceed fifty years. 

5th. The number of directors. 

6th. The amount of capital and the number of shares into which the same is divided. 

7th. If a ferry is to be operated and maintained by the corporation, the name of the stream crossed. 

8th. If a road is to be built, it must state the kind of a road, the places it will pass, and the counties into which it will extend.

The charter must be subscribed to by at least three of the directors, two of whom must reside in this state, and they must acknowledge these signatures in the same manner as in the acknowledgment of deeds. 

Sec. 434. Powers.-A corporation may be incorporated for fifty years and shall have similar rights and powers in the management of its affairs and conducting of its business as an individual, it being an artificial per-

CORPORATIONS 

279 son with many of the lawful rights of a natural man. It must have not less than three directors. The directors are chosen at a meeting of stockholders duly fixed and called. 

Sec. 435. Stock.-A corporation has a fixed capital stock and this is divided into a number of shares usually of $100.00 each. These shares are paid for by those desiring to become interested in the corporation. If the company prospers and makes large profits, a share may be worth many times its face value; if the company does not prosper, the shares may become worthless, which occurs when the corporation becomes insolvent. 

The stock may be transferred only on the books of the company, together with the delivery of the certificates of stock, so that the books will at all times show who owns the stock of the company. 

The purchasers and holders of the stock in a corporation may be called upon at any time to pay in any unpaid portion of their subscription, and if any judgment is rendered against the corporation before they have paid in all the amount of stock subscribed for by them, the individual stockholder becomes liable for the payment of this judgment to an amount equal to the unpaid stock subscribed by him; but if he has paid into the treasury of the corporation for all the stock subscribed by him, he is not liable for any of the debts of the corporation. 

For instance: John Doe might be worth $100,000.00, and he might hold $1,000.00 of stock in the Citizens 

Lumber Company. The Citizens Lumber Company may fail and not be able to pay its debts. All of the property of the company would be subject to its debts and it would be insolvent. John Doe would, therefore, lose his

$1,000.00 stock, but would not be personally liable for any of the company's debts, as he and the company would, in the eyes of the law, be two separate and distinct persons. 

Sec. 436. Dividends.-If a corporation prospers and money accumulates in its treasury, the directors declare dividends on the stock. These dividends are a distribution of the profits among the stockholders and may be paid annually, semi-annually, or at such times as the board. of directors may decide upon, or at such times as may be fixed by the by-laws of the company. No member of the company is entitled to receive individually one dollar or one cent of the company's money except that it be paid to him as a dividend, or that he be hired as an employee of the company. 

Sec. 437. Conveyances.-Corporations, under their charter, may buy such land and real property as may be necessary in the discharge and performance of their business, and in addition to this may also take and receive real property in settlement of debts due the company.

In selling this real property, all of the directors and stockholders do not have to sign the deed, but the deed is signed usually by the president of the company, or the presiding member of the corporation. The name of the corporation is written thus: First National Bank, by 

John Doe, President. If the deed is executed by any other member of the corporation other than the president, cashier, or presiding member, it is proper to recite in the deed the section of the by-laws of the company, or the resolution of the board of directors directing the sale and authorizing the particular party to consummate the same on behalf of the corporation. The party exe-

CORPORATIONS 

281 cuting the deed for the corporation acknowledges his signature for the execution of the instrument. 

Sec. 438. Dissolution.-A corporation may cease to exist and be dissolved by the expiration of the time limit in its charter. It may be dissolved by a judgment of the court so decreeing and the appointing of a receiver or trustee to wind up its business. 

If no receiver be appointed for a defunct or insolvent corporation, the directors hold the assets of the company in trust for the benefit of the creditors and stockholders. 

Sec. 439. Public Corporations.-Public corporations are not those organized for profit, but are usually created by law for the administration of public affairs, as a city or county government. They are not required to file a charter with the Secretary of State, nor is stock taken or subscribed for; they exist by virtue of law. 

Sec. 440. Quasi Public Corporations. Quasi public corporations are those organized to conduct business of a public character for private profit and gain, such as railroads, telephone and telegraph companies. These corporations are given the right of eminent domain and superior rights to other profit-making concerns, because the state and the entire public are concerned in the performance of their duties.

The Government retains the right to control and regulate these quasi public concerns, and they are known as "public service corporations."

Sec. 441. Religious, Educational and Charitable.- 

Religious, educational and charitable corporations are not organized for matters of profit, nor is it necessary for

them to state the capital stock in their charters, which they file with the Secretary of State.

The directors of these corporations must not contract a debt beyond the means of the corporation to pay, and where they do, the directors making such a debt become individually liable for same.

Sec. 442. Benefits and Abuses.-Corporations allow the employment of a greater amount of capital in many cases, in the development and forwarding of an enterprise than any one individual could or would supply, and in this and other particulars, they become great developers of the country.

In its dealings with the rest of the public, a corporation may not be found always as considerate as an individual, or able to make the concessions that an individual would make under the same circumstances. Its agents act by an inflexible rule or by-law, and cannot often exercise any discretion in the particular case.

To cure the evils that grow out of the system, sometimes described as corporate greed, the law endeavors to hedge about corporations such restrictions as will give individual citizens, as weaker members in the commercial world, an equal opportunity to succeed.

CHAPTER 51 BANKS 

Sec. 443. Definition.-A bank is a corporation, organized for profit, which receives deposits, makes loans and otherwise deals in money and credit. Banks are, to a large degree, public service corporations. 

They are divided into several classes: Regional 

Banks, National Banks, State Banks, Trust Companies, Private Banks, and Savings Banks. They are all organized on the same general principle. The capital is made up of a number of shares, called stock, and the stockholders elect a board of directors for the management and operation of the bank. A cashier is employed, usually from among the stockholders.

The officers of the bank consist generally of the president, vice president and cashier. Some banks have several vice presidents, and other officers.

Sec. 444. Stock.-The stock of the bank is repre sented by certificates issued under seal of the corporation, attested by its officers, showing that the holder or owner has so many shares of stock of certain face valuation in the particular bank.

Parties desiring to organize a bank determine the amount of capital stock, and proceed to open a subscrip- 283

tion list for such stock. The parties subscribing for the stock pay the value of same into the hands of the officers of the corporation and receive their certificates therefor. 

Sec. 445. Nature of the Business.-The bank must not enter the field of speculation; it deals in money and giltedge securities, and is not permitted, in its corporate capacity, to buy land, stock or merchandise, as an investment. It may take the same in settlement of a debt, but should dispose of same for money as soon as proper opportunity affords.

It is not to be supposed that a bank should make any great showing or declare any considerable dividend by the operation upon its own capital stock. This capital is often only a very small part of the funds at its disposal. 

As soon as the bank is organized, the business public, having more or less confidence in the institution, depending largely upon the individual standing of the officers and directors, begin to deposit their money with the bank for safekeeping. Here they may check it out at pleasure and not run the risk of keeping large sums of money on their persons or in their homes to invite the robber or run the risk of fire. These deposits from the general public reach many times the amount of the capital stock. 

Sec. 446. Deposits.-The money deposited in the bank by its customers is loaned to other customers, the bank keeping a certain reserve on hand, usually from fifteen to twenty-five per cent, to protect such depositors. 

Banks, in a prosperous condition, rarely have as much. cash in their vaults as is represented by the deposits, hence if all the depositors should call for their money on the same day, a bank would, perhaps, not be able to pay then, although entirely solvent. But it can be safely estimated,

BANKS 

285 under normal business conditions, that there will always be new deposits coming to fill up and take the place of the sums withdrawn, and, besides, the money is loaned, or a great deal of it, on short-time loans so that the bank could, on short notice, call in large sums of its money to satisfy its depositors in case of a run being made. 

Sec. 447. Loans and Discounts. From the capital stock, deposits, and accumulated funds, the bank is not permitted to make general investments in property, but will loan this money on proper security. 

Where the borrower is a person of wealth and unquestioned solvency, his individual note is usually sufficient to make the loan, but where the individual desiring to procure such loan is, in any way, involved, or a person of limited means, then the bank may require sureties to sign the note along with the principal guaranteeing its payment.

These loans may also be secured by pledges; the owner and holder of land notes, bonds, or other good paper, may deposit the same with the bank as collateral security and obtain a loan in this manner. 

The notes thus given usually bear a rate of interest anywhere from six to ten per cent, and this interest, when paid, becomes profits of the bank. 

The owner of a note may sell to the bank, and if the bank considers the note a good piece of paper, it may purchase the same by discounting it five or ten per cent, or a sufficient sum to represent the desired profit. 

Sec. 448. Checks and Drafts.-After a customer has deposited money in the bank, he can withdraw the same all at once or in such quantities as may meet his convenience. This is done by giving checks against his account.

Checks are simply written orders to the bank instructing it to pay a certain person a certain sum of money. 

The following is a form of the ordinary check: No. 

Marshall, Texas, July 3, 1912.

THE FIRST NATIONAL BANK OF MARSHALL, TEXAS 

Pay to...... ...JOHN DOE………………………….. 

TEN and 00/100........... or bearer, $10.00 

.DOLLARS 

R. ROE. 

If any other person holds a claim against you, or you have authorized another person to collect a sum of money of you at your bank, he will draw upon you by what is called a "draft," which is a written order to the bank to pay him so much money and charge it to your account. 

Before the bank will pay this draft, it will present it to you for acceptance. On the following page is a usual form of draft.

Drafts are a very convenient form of transferring money where deeds, notes or other valuable papers are to be delivered or are to pass between people at some distance; as for instance, John Doe in Washington City might sell a piece of land to Richard Roe in Dallas, Texas. John would execute his deed and draw on Richard for the sum to be paid for the property, attach the draft to the deed and send it to one of the banks in Dallas, and the bank would notify Richard that the deed and

BANKS 

287 draft were there, and Richard, by going to the bank and paying the draft, could get the deed. If the draft is not paid, it may go to protest as explained in Sec. 259.

THE DALLAS NATIONAL BANK 

$1,000.00

At Sight 

Pay to the Order of

CUSTOMER'S 

DRAFT 

Dallas, Texas, January 1, 1934. 

With Exchange

THE FIRST NATIONAL BANK OF MARSHALL ONE THOUSAND DOLLARS 

Value received and charge to account of

To......... JOHN DOE….............. 

RICHARD ROB 

Sec. 449. Dividends.-By loans and discounts, and the doing of a general banking business, there is accumulated in the hands of the bank considerable profits that eventually go to the stockholders. It is the custom of most banks to declare a semi-annual dividend. These dividends are the stockholders' share in that portion of the profits which it is desired to divide among the shareholders. It is not often that the bank divides all of its profits at any one time, but generally keeps an item among its accounts known as "undivided profits," which sometimes amounts to a large sum of money. This item of

"undivided profits" belongs to the shareholders and goes to making the stock more valuable. In many instances the stock becomes worth three or four times its face value on account of the earning capacity of the bank and on account of the large sums of undivided profits, and in this way the stockholders are benefited, not only by the dividends and receipts, but by the increased value of their stock. 

Sec. 450. National Banks.-National banks are corporations chartered under the laws of the United States, and are different in many particulars from other banks. 

The United States Government does not guarantee that a national bank will not fail, for such a bank is a private corporation for private profits. The Government does, however, guarantee that the holder of one of the notes issued by such bank shall not sustain any loss by having the note become worthless on his hands, as this note is secured by the Government bonds kept in the 

Treasury Department at Washington.

In the earlier days of private banks notes were issued and placed in circulation, and were not guaranteed by the Government; and no one was really able to tell whether a bank note was good or bad, and he never knew when the bank might fail. But to all intents and purposes national bank notes are as good as any other money, being guaranteed by the Government.

After issuing these notes and placing them in circulation, they are treated by the bank, as well as the rest of the public, as so much money. It is in contemplation that the twelve Regional Banks, through the direct supervision of the Government, will henceforth issue all circulating notes.

BANKS 

289 Deposits in national banks up to a stated amount are guaranteed. 

Sec. 451. State Banks.-State banks are corporations organized under the laws of the State of Texas. They are not banks of issue, but use their capital and funds in making loans and discounts.

In order, however, to further safeguard these institutions against failure, a number of bank examiners go from bank to bank and from place to place. They examine the books and accounts, inquire as to the quantity of securities, the volume of business and the business methods of each and every bank, and require said banks to operate within safe business practices. 

Sec. 452. Private Banks.-Private banks formerly did a flourishing business. This was due to public confidence in the management, but with the passing of the generation the management would change, so that the law looks upon government control and supervision as more conducive to safety. The private bank is now practically a matter of history. 

Banking and Trust Companies cannot capitalize for less than $50,000.00 nor for more than $10,000,000.00.

Such concerns act as trustees in large transactions and may also do a banking business, but are different from all other banks in their capacity to act in a trust, guaranty or fiduciary capacity.

Sec. 453. Regional Banks.-Under President Wil son's Administration, the Federal Reserve Banks were authorized, there being one each located at Dallas, Atlanta, Richmond, Philadelphia, New York, Boston, Cleveland, Cincinnati, Chicago, St. Louis, Kansas City,

and San Francisco. These really comprise one great Government bank divided into branches for convenience. The 

Government deposits its funds in these banks, supplies them with circulating notes upon proper security and appoints a board to supervise them. 

These great institutions do more of a wholesale than a retail business. They do not deal directly with the individual, but with the Government and with the many banks which become subscribers to, or members of, the Regional System. These banks are supposed to be of such strength as to protect the country against panics and under Government supervision are supposed to curb and keep down combinations of capital sometimes referred to as a money trust.

CHAPTER 52 TAXATION 

Sec. 454. Taxes.-Taxes are the prorated proportion of the expenses of operating the Government assessed against each particular individual. Ad valorem taxes are assessed in proportion to the value of the property held; poll taxes are assessed at so much per head; occupation taxes are levied against occupations, and are in the form of a permit or license.

Under the Constitution taxes must be uniform upon all people and all property. 

Sec. 455. Direct Tax.-Direct taxes are those ordinarily assessed and collected in cash and known by the specific name of taxes. They include the poll tax, occupation tax and ad valorem tax, and are assessed by states, cities and counties.

Sec. 456. Indirect Tax.-An indirect tax is a tax collected in an indirect manner, such as duties, commonly known as tariff, internal revenue upon distilled spirits and tobacco, etc. Much of the revenues by which the United States Government is supported is collected by indirect taxation. The tariff is an indirect tax, because 291
