it is not collected direct from the man who finally pays it, but is a duty, or a charge, that any person must pay upon imported goods brought from a foreign country. 

To illustrate: If there were a tariff of 40 per cent on silks, a silk merchant importing a shipment of silk valued at $1,000.00, would have to pay the United States revenue officer, at the customhouse, one being situated at all ports, a sum of $400.00. This $400.00 goes into the 

United States treasury as so much tax. The silk merchant adds it to the price of his silk, and the silk that was formerly worth $1,000.00 must now be valued at $1,400.00, and the purchaser, who finally buys this silk, pays the tax levied for the support of the general government. He pays it in such an indirect way that he is scarcely aware that he does it. So it is with whiskey, tobacco, and the collection of internal revenues. The income tax is direct. 

Sec. 457. Ad Valorem Tax.-The ad valorem tax is a direct tax levied upon property in proportion to its appraised valuation, and is paid direct to the tax collector. 

Sec. 458. Poll Tax.-The poll tax, or tax per head, or per capita, is levied upon the theory that every person with or without property should pay a small tax to support the Government that affords him protection. 

Sec. 459. Occupation Tax.-The occupation tax is a tax levied upon a profession, business or calling. Many occupations are not taxed at all; others are taxed very heavily. For example: The licensed saloon was formerly taxed $375.00 per year by the state, and a further sum by the cities and counties. The city or county could each levy a tax equal to one-half of the state tax. Two-horse

TAXATION 

293 peddlers are taxed $30.00 per annum; foot peddlers are taxed $5.00.

Sec. 460. Tax Rate.-The Constitution of the state provides that the tax rate for state purposes, exclusive of the amount levied for the public free schools and for the public debt, shall never exceed 35 cents on the $100.00 valuation of property.

In cities, where many vast municipal improvements are made by special charter, a tax of $2.50 on the $100.00 valuation is permitted, which shall include the city's revenues for all purposes.

The particular rate for the year is determined in the following manner:

First, all the property in the state is assessed, the valuation determined and the total found; then all the amount of money that the state or city or county will need for the incoming year is carefully estimated, and when this amount of money is determined, it is then seen what rate of taxation is necessary to levy upon all the property in order to raise this sum. A tax of $1.00 on every $100.00 valuation becomes the rate if this assessment will produce the necessary income. It may become higher or lower according to the needs of the Government, provided the constitutional limit be not exceeded. The 35 cent tax does not yield enough to support the state government, and larger levies are made on gas, etc. 

Sec. 461. How Assessed and Collected. During the first part of the year, each property owner upon oath makes out an itemized list of all the property owned by him on January 1st, and the tax assessor then assesses such property holder, and in this manner each party in

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law the city or county, as the case may be, is assessed for his property. This assessment is copied on rolls kept in the tax assessor's office, copies of which are forwarded to the 

Comptroller's office at Austin.

After the rate has been determined, the amount of the property rendered by each individual is multiplied by this rate, and the result is the amount of taxes due by such taxpayer, which tax becomes due on the first day of October of each year, and must be paid before the first day of February following. If it is not paid to the tax collector by the first day of February, it becomes delinquent and a penalty of ten per cent is assessed against the taxpayer as a penalty for delaying such payment. Taxes may be paid semi-annually.

Sec. 462. Tax Liens.-The amount of the tax due by each party is a lien upon his property to secure its payment. The tax lien is superior to all other debts or liens of every character, and can be collected by foreclosing such lien in the courts, or by the actual seizure of personal property by the tax collector. The homestead, however, may be sold for the tax due upon it, but not for the tax due upon other property; but the tax due upon the homestead may be made out of any other property owned by the party. 

Sec. 463. Tax Sales.-Where a person refuses to pay his tax, any personal property belonging to him may be seized by the tax collector and converted into cash and applied to the payment of the debt; or suit may be brought to foreclose the tax lien upon real property, and a sufficient portion of the real estate be put up and sold under a judgment of the court at public outcry to pay said tax.

TAXATION 

295 

In the case of the sale of real estate, the owner has two years in which to redeem same by paying such penalties and costs as shall have been accrued, and if not redeemed within said two years, the land becomes absolutely the property of the purchaser.

CHAPTER 53 RAILROADS 

Sec. 464. Railroads.-Railroads are classed as "public service corporations." They have the right of eminent domain, and they serve the public in such a manner as to give them privileges over and above ordinary private corporations. Being of a quasi public character, they are subject to regulation by the laws of the land; hence we find many laws on our books regulating railways that do not apply to ordinary private corporations. 

Sec. 465. How Organized.—A railway corporation, when organized under the laws of the State of Texas, must be composed of not less than ten men. It must have a board of directors of not less than seven. This board of directors is elected by the stockholders. The stockholders hold an annual meeting. 

Sec. 466. Right of Way. The public is interested in having quick mail service, and through transportation for freight and passengers; hence railways are given the right of eminent domain; that is, the right, as elsewhere explained, of taking property whether the owner is willing to sell or not, by paying a fair compensation. 

Sec. 467. Public Crossings.-A railroad has a right to cross any stream, watercourse, public highway, or other 296

RAILROADS 

297 road by restoring the same to as good condition as found, keeping the same in repair, so as not to impair its usefulness. Thus, where a railroad crosses the road or a public street, it is in the power of the state, or the county, or the city, ordinarily to compel such railroad to restore the street or road to its former condition and keep the crossing in repair for the safety of the traveling public. The road overseer, or street commissioner, can compel such repairs.

Sec. 468. Right to Cross Fences.-The said railroad has a right to cross the field or enclosure of any person. 

At the point of entrance to said enclosure, it shall install a stock gap, that is, a gap under and on each side of the track in such a manner as to allow the cars to pass, but prevent the stock from going in or out. If any new fence is built, or enclosure extended, the railway company shall also build additional stock gaps for each crossing into an enclosure. 

Sec. 469. Restrictions.—Many other limitations are placed upon railroads, some of which we will here enumerate.

In constructing a line of railroad, if the same passes within three miles of a county seat, the road must be built through said county seat.

It must treat all parties alike; it cannot refuse the freight of one person and accept the freight of another. 

It is required to furnish separate coaches for the white and black races.

It is required to have a schedule time on which its passenger trains shall be run; and to keep the passenger depots open, lighted, and in cold weather, warm, for one hour before and one hour after the departure of all trains.

Sec. 470. Connecting Lines.-Each line of railroad connecting at any point with another line of railroad is required to receive the freight from the connecting line and transport the same over its own line to any point upon the same, the roads dividing profits according to the service rendered, or as fixed by agreement between those roads. 

Sec. 471. Passengers.-Passengers are those who ride upon the trains of the road, after buying tickets, or obtaining passes, or other permits to ride.

The company owes the passenger the highest degree of care not to injure him, or permit him to suffer any injury, or indignity, while on said train. It owes the passenger a higher degree of care than it does its servants or employees, and must respond in damages for any injury done him or his property. 

Sec. 472. Employees.-A railway company, as any other master, is required to furnish the servant, its employee, with reasonably safe tools and appliances with which to work, and a failure to exercise ordinary care to secure such tools and to furnish the same is negligence toward the said employee; and if the employee is injured on account of such negligence, he is entitled to recover damages against the company. Upon discharge it must give the employee a written statement showing cause for its action. A refusal to give such statement subjects the company to heavy penalties for blacklisting. 

Sec. 473. Indebtedness of Railroads.-Railway com panies generally find it expedient to mortgage their lines in order to raise money. In executing a mortgage, they give the same to some person as trustee and issue bonds against the road secured by said mortgage. These bonds

RAILROADS 

299 are sold in the market to any person seeking an investment, and they constitute a lien upon the railroad which must be paid ahead of the claim of the owners represented by the stockholders.

Sec. 474. Liens.-The bondholders have a mortgage lien against the railroad property and equipment, but the laborer who labors with teams, tools, or otherwise, in the construction, repair or maintenance of said road, has a statutory lien given him by the statutes to secure payment for his labor, which lien is good for one year from the time that said labor is performed and is superior to the mortgage lien.

Sec. 475. Materialman's and Mechanic's Lien.—Any person furnishing material, such as crossties, rails, or bridge timbers, out of which a railroad is constructed or repaired, has a lien that is superior to the bonds, and he may, at any time within four months after making such sale, file a copy of this claim in the office of the county clerk, and thereby secure a lien against the company, which is superior also to the mortgage lien. 

Sec. 476. Receivers.-In cases where railroads become insolvent, or are unable to meet their obligations, the court in foreclosing the mortgage, or other liens, will not stop ordinarily the running of the road, but will appoint some suitable person as receiver to continue the operation of the road under the direction of the court. 

While the receivership is pending and while the mortgage is being foreclosed, this receiver has all the powers necessary to operate the road, and purchase supplies and material to continue its operation, and his contracts and obligations are superior to the indebtedness of the company, of any character, made before his appointment.

Sec. 477. Railway Commission of Texas.-For the purpose of adjusting rates and having a general railway supervision, the state has created a Railway Commission, composed of three men, who are elected by the people of the state, and who have offices at Austin, Texas, who supervise the management of the railways of Texas. They have the power of fixing rates and adjusting differences between roads, and compelling necessary improvements. 

Sec. 478. Street Railroads.-Within all of our cities, and between many of them, electric lines have been established known as street car lines, and interurban lines. 

These lines carry passengers for short distances, and sometimes a light quality of freight. They, like steam railroads, are common carriers, and are required to transfer passengers from one system to the other where they connect, or where there are two companies operating within the limits of the same city. 

Sec. 479. Offenses Against Railroads.-It is unlawful to throw rocks or stones or anything against a moving train, to steal a ride upon a moving train, or to wreck or in any way injure the property of a railroad company. 

Sec. 480. Mail Service.-The railroads have mail contracts with the Government which materially aid in the rapid distribution of the mails from one portion of the country to another, and regular mail trains are operated between nearly all of our principal cities.

Sec. 481. Government Control.-Railroads are nec essary and very essential to the development of a community, but must be under the control and direction of the Government to prevent extortion and arbitrary rates as well as preferential treatment of shippers. Without

RAILROADS 

301 Government control and regulation, one shipper could be made rich and the other ruined. Two factories in the same town, or two cotton buyers in the same market, could work with equal opportunities, thrift and industry, yet the one who secured the favor of the railroad could be made rich, the other a bankrupt. Within the power of the railroads, one city may be built while another is ruined. 

Government supervision of railroads must be rigid, but practical and just.

Truck Lines.-With the construction of hard-surface roads and the perfection of the gasoline motor, large trucks for the carrying of freight, and busses for the carrying of passengers, have appeared upon the public roads and highways. They are now competitors with the railroads as common carriers. They, too, must be subject to state control.

Air Transportation. - Airplanes, because of their speed, are carrying the mail and a great many passengers, a trip from Washington to Texas being only a matter of a few hours.

CHAPTER 54 PUBLIC ROADS 

Sec. 482. Defined.-Public roads are those roads established and maintained by the county and state. 

Sec. 483. How Established and Maintained.-The 

Commissioners' Court, composed of the County Commissioners and the County Judge, usually upon a petition of citizens of that section of the county affected, establish and lay out public roads to be maintained under county supervision. They are divided into three classes: 

First, second and third. A first class road should be not less than forty, and may be sixty feet in width, and should be clear from all obstructions. A second class road is thirty feet in width and may contain stumps and other obstructions, not more than six inches in height. A third class road is not less than twenty feet in width and may contain stumps and other obstructions, not more than six inches in height.

Sec. 484. Overseers.-These public roads are main tained under the old road law system, which provides 302

PUBLIC ROADS 

303 for the appointment of an overseer from among the residents along said public road. He is required to warn or notify the hands to appear and perform duties upon the road to the extent of five days during each calendar year. The court, in appointing this overseer, designates the hands under his charge, and where all hands in his locality are not enumerated, or set apart to him, he is to have the hands within his territory, as defined by the Commissioners. Although still the law, most counties now maintain their roads by hired crews and machinery. 

Sec. 485. Persons Liable for Road Duty.—All per sons from twenty-one to forty-five years of age, who have resided in the county for as much as fifteen days are subject to road duty, and may be warned by the road overseer to appear and perform service upon the road. In most counties this law has fallen into disuse. 

Sec. 486. Substitutes.-Any person not desiring to perform such service may pay the road overseer $1.00 per day for each day that he is notified to work, which shall not be more than five days in the year. The said overseer shall divide the said five days so as to work the road twice each year.

The system of working roads by the adjoining residents has proven very unsatisfactory in many counties, and the roads remain in a very poor and unworked condition. In some counties, road superintendents are appointed to take the place of the old overseers. 

Sec. 487. Special Road Laws.-In many instances, the Legislature has enacted, upon request of particular counties, special road laws that differ very materially from the general road laws. Here the road may be

worked by convicts, or the work may be let out by contracts, or be cared for by various other means; bonds may be issued to build macadam roads, and improve the general road conditions. 

Sec. 488. Highway Department.-At the inception of our Government it was provided in the Constitution that Congress should have the power to create and establish post roads. Soon after this came the advent of railroads, and travel in the middle and latter part of the last century was usually made by railroad. Local travel consisted in going from the farm to market and all roads led to nearby towns which were often the county seats. 

All road laws were written with the idea that the roads should be laid out by the county and maintained by the County Commissioners' Court. Such a thing as the state highway had not existed except in so far as the roads of one county would, perhaps, meet those coming from another county.

Automobiles.-With the advent of the automobile, the railroad had a competitor in long-distance travel and a State Highway Department sprang into existence which called for the establishment of roads running from one side of the state to the other. The Federal Government joined in the establishing of these highways and contributed toward their construction. A tax has been levied upon the sale of gasoline and upon the operation of automobiles on the highway, which yields many millions of dollars annually. The Highway Department has laid out approximately 20,000 miles in state highways in Texas, much of which has been built into concrete or other hard-surface roads, and all of which is under systematic maintenance of the Highway Department.

ELEEMOSYNARY INSTITUTIONS 

305 

Sec. 489. Eleemosynary Institutions.-The State of 

Texas has endeavored to provide asylums, hospitals, and other institutions for the insane, feeble-minded, the deaf, dumb and blind, Confederate soldiers, and others. A list of these institutions is given here. (a) Rusk State Hospital at Rusk, for the insane. (b) Wichita Falls Hospital in Wichita Falls, for the insane. (c) Terrell State Hospital at Terrell, for the insane. (d) San Antonio Hospital at San Antonio, for the insane. (e) Austin State Hospital at Austin, for the feebleminded. (f) Austin State School at Austin, for the feebleminded. (g) Abilene State Hospital at Abilene, for epileptics. (h) Deaf and Dumb Asylum at Austin, where inmates are taught printing and other useful occupations. (i) School for the Blind at Austin. 

(j) State Orphans' Home at Corsicana. 

(k) Confederate Home at Austin. In this home the rapidly disappearing survivors of the Confederate Army are received and cared for. 

(1) Confederate Woman's Home at Austin. Wives and widows of Confederates over sixty years of age may be received into this home and cared for. (m) Deaf, Dumb and Blind Asylum for colored youths at Austin.

(n) Austin State School for Feeble-Minded, designed for feeble-minded children. 

(0) State Tubercular Sanatorium, for the treatment of tuberculosis, located near San Angelo. The approval of the County Judge is necessary for admission. The inmates are divided into three grades: 

1. Indigent public patients, 2. Non-indigent public patients, 3. Private patients. (p) State Home for Neglected and Dependent Children at Waco. This institution is designed for neglected children who are not suited for the State Orphanages. 

Unruly children can be confined here by order of the juvenile judge. 

(q) Colored Girls' Training School at Jacksonville. 

This institution for colored girls is of similar character as that for neglected and dependent white children. (r) State Hospital for Crippled and Deformed Children. This institution was a gift to the State of Texas by the Texas Public Health Association, and is an adjunct of the John Sealy Hospital at the Medical Department of the State University at Galveston.

The number of these hospitals and institutions has grown from time to time until their inmates number many thousands and are constantly increasing. A detailed statement of requisites for admission, and the care and instruction could hardly be given in a work of this character. Persons interested should examine the statute or apply to their county judge, or in some cases to the Board of Control at Austin.

PART FOUR CRIMINAL LAW

CHAPTER 55 CRIMINAL LAW 

Sec. 490. Crime in General.—Crime is the doing of an act forbidden by law.

Sec. 491. History of Criminal Law.-Criminal law is perhaps older than civil law. Certainly many hundreds of years ago it was in a written form. We have the Ten 

Commandments, and the Laws of Moses, which were statutory enactments forbidding crime, and in many instances, assigning the punishment, some four thousand years old.

Under the Common Law of England, from which our laws were originally derived, crime was not always defined by law; it was left as an unwritten law, and a man was punished for doing any wrong against his fellowman, or against the public in general, which was calculated to provoke a breach of the public peace, or which in good conscience the society of all the land would not sanction. This is now the law to a large measure of some states and still the law of England today. It has many good points, but there is always a border upon the realm of what is a crime and what is not a crime, and some things are considered morally wrong that are not considered as crimes at law. Under these circumstances, 309

it is deemed preferable to have written laws in order that a man may have an opportunity to know what the law is before he violates it. So, in the wisdom of the lawmakers of Texas, all crimes are defined by the statutes in that portion of the same known as the Penal Code, and any offense or act not covered by that code cannot be punished by law. 

Sec. 492. Divisions of Crime.-Crime may be divided into grave and minor offenses, the former usually denominated felonies and the latter misdemeanors, thus: 

CRIME FELONIES MISDEMEANORS Capital Offenses 

Ordinary Felonies

Offenses Punishable by Fine 

Only 

Offenses Punishable by Fine and Imprisonment 

Sec. 493. Felony.-A felony is an offense punishable by death, or confinement in the penitentiary. Felonies are subdivided into capital and less than capital cases, or capital and ordinary felonies. 

Sec. 494. Capital Case.-A capital case is one that may be punishable by death. Murder, treason, rape and robbery by firearms are classed as capital offenses. Ordinary felonies, such as theft and perjury, are punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for a number of years. 

In capital cases, a court may deny the defendant bail if he thinks it probable that the accused would be capitally punished by a jury. In all other cases, the defendant is entitled to bail.

CRIMINAL Law 

311 

Sec. 495. Misdemeanors.-All those petty offenses less than felony which are punishable by fine or by short jail sentences are classed as misdemeanors. 

Sec. 496. Innocence Presumed. In the United States and in all English-speaking countries, the innocence of the accused is presumed until his guilt is established beyond a reasonable doubt, and until he is finally convicted by a court of competent jurisdiction. The rule is different, however, in the Latin countries: France, Spain and 

Italy, for instance. In these countries, when a man has been once accused, he stands guilty until he can prove himself innocent. The theory of the American and English doctrine is that it is better that a guilty man should occasionally escape than that the innocent should be unjustly punished. 

Sec. 497. Manner of Punishment.-There are five methods of punishment provided for under the Texas codes, namely, death, confinement in the penitentiary, imprisonment in the county jail, fine, and depriving of civil rights. It is believed by many that corporal punishment should be inflicted in certain petty offenses like drunkenness, rather than the imposition of a fine. 

Sec. 498. Principals, Accomplices and Accessories. 

Principals in the commission of a crime are all those who act together in the perpetration of the offense, including those who may stand and watch, or otherwise aid in the actual commission of the offense. Principals are punished for the particular offense as defined by the statute. 

Two men may be charged with murder of one. The shooting may have been done by one of the two, but if the other was aiding and assisting, he is in law just as guilty as the one who actually did the shooting.

Accomplices are those who, though not present at the commission of the offense, give advice, aid and comfort toward the perpetration of a crime. Accomplices are punished as severely as the principal.

Accessories are those who participate in the crime after knowing of its commission by the way of aiding the criminal to escape or concealing him from arrest. 

This, however, under the laws of Texas, does not include the immediate members of the criminal's family, but under United States law it does include them. 

Sec. 499. Classes of Crime.-Crime, according to the nature of the offense, with reference to its effect upon the state, society or individuals, is divided into fifteen great classes, each of which will be treated separately in the next succeeding chapters. These classes are enumerated in the statutes in the following order: (1) Offenses against the state, such as treason and misapplication of public money. (2) Offenses against the executive or other branches of the government, such as bribery. (3) Offenses against the right of suffrage, such as intimidating a voter. (4) Offenses against the right of religious worship, such as disturbance of public worship. (5) Offenses against public justice, such as perjury. (6) Offenses against the public peace, such as affrays. (7) Offenses against public morals, such as bigamy. (8) Offenses against public policy, such as lotteries and gambling. (9) Offenses against public health, such as a nuisance.

CRIMINAL LAW 

313 (10) Offenses against public property designated for the use of the public, such as injury to roads and bridges. (11) Offenses against trade and commerce, such as forgery. (12) Offenses against persons, such as assault to murder. (13) Offenses against reputation, such as libel and slander. (14) Offenses against property, such as arson and theft. (15) Miscellaneous offenses, such as robbing orchards, speeding autos, etc.

CHAPTER 56 OFFENSES AGAINST THE STATE 

Sec. 500. Treason.-Treason consists in levying war against the state, or adhering to its enemies and giving them aid and comfort. The penalty is death or life imprisonment.

Sec. 501. Misprision of Treason. The failure on the part of any person, who knows of a treasonable act of some other person, to communicate the same to the authorities within five days, is termed Misprision of Treason. This offense is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary from two to seven years. 

Sec. 502. Misapplication of Public Money.-Any treasurer, receiver, or depository of the public money, who shall use the same as his private means and apply the same in any private enterprise, or in any way use it other than to keep and transmit it to the treasurer or proper custodian for the state, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to ten years. 314

OFFENSES AGAINST THE STATE 

315 

Sec. 503. Occupation Tax: Failure to Pay.-There are a great many occupations in the state that are taxed, and before a person can pursue same, he must pay the occupation tax, sometimes called a permit or license. Selling whiskey, for instance, without a license, or certain peddling without license, is punishable by law.

CHAPTER 57 OFFENSES AGAINST THE EXECUTIVE, LEG- ISLATIVE AND JUDICIAL BRANCHES OF THE GOVERNMENT Sec. 504. Bribery of Public Officers.-Any public officer holding any position to which he has been elected by the people, or to which he has been appointed, who shall accept a bribe to do, or not to do any public act, or to cast a vote for or against any public measure, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two, nor more than ten years. 

Sec. 505. The Same.-Any person who shall bribe any public officer as above provided, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two, nor more than five years. 

Sec. 506. Bribing a Clerk of Record. Any person who shall pay a clerk of the County or District Court, or the keeper of any of the public records to make an improper entry, or to mutilate, change or destroy any part of same, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary not less than two, nor more than five years, and the officer accepting the bribe shall receive the same punishment. 316

OFFENSES AGAINST BRANCHES OF GOVERNMENT 

317 

Sec. 507. Attorneys at Law.-Any lawyer guilty of accepting money from others than his client to divulge the client's secrets and neglect his business or litigation, is deemed guilty of bribery and may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for two years. 

Sec. 508. Drunkenness in Office.-Any state or district officer who shall get drunk and be found in a state of intoxication, shall be subject to removal from office, and to a fine from $10.00 to $200.00. Any county or municipal officer who shall get drunk shall be fined for the first offense from $5.00 to $50.00; for the second offense from $50.00 to $100.00; for the third offense from $100.00 to $300.00, and shall be subject to removal from office. 

Sec. 509. Drunkenness in a Public Place.-Any per son who shall get drunk and be found in a state of intoxication in a public place, shall be deemed guilty of a misdemeanor, and be punished in any sum not more than $100.00. It will be seen that the gist of this offense is being in a public place while drunk; where one retires to his room, as Noah did, there is no penalty attached. 

The statute forbids drinking upon passenger trains.

CHAPTER 58 OFFENSES AGAINST SUFFRAGE Sec. 510. Privileges From Arrest.-Every voter shall on election day be privileged from arrest, except for treason, breach of the peace, or felony. Any one arresting a voter for anything other than for one of these reasons, or otherwise preventing him from exercising the right of suffrage, shall be subject to a fine not to exceed $300.00. 

Sec. 511. Illegal Voting.-Any one who shall knowingly cast an illegal ballot, or who shall be guilty of repeating, that is, voting more than once, at the same election, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years. 

Sec. 512. Folding Ballots.-Any one who shall in depositing his ballot knowingly fold two or more ballots, shall be fined not exceeding $100.00. 

Sec. 513. False Swearing by Voter.-Any voter whose right of suffrage shall be challenged, and who shall make oath to his right to vote in the particular election, and shall swear falsely with reference to such right of suffrage, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years. 

Sec. 514. Procuring to Swear Falsely.-Any person who shall procure or induce a voter to swear falsely with 318

OFFENSES AGAINST SUFFRAGE 

319 reference to his right of suffrage, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary not to exceed three years, or by fine not to exceed $3,000.00. 

Sec. 515. Altering or Suppressing Ballots.-Any person who shall alter, destroy, or otherwise suppress a ballot after the same shall have been cast, shall be confined in the penitentiary from three to five years, and any officer of the election having in charge the returns of same who shall fail to deliver and make his return to the proper officers, shall be fined not exceeding $1,000.00.

Sec. 516. Preventing Returns.-Any person who shall by force, threats, or otherwise prevent the returns of election being made, shall be punished by fine not exceeding $2,000.00. 

Sec. 517. Secret Ballot.-The manner in which a man shall vote is intended by law to be private and unknown to any other person than himself, and any officer of the election or other person who shall fail to keep such information secure, or who shall give out any such information, shall be fined not less than $50.00, nor more than $500.00, and in addition thereto, may be imprisoned in the county jail for six months. It shall be the duty of the county clerk to keep the ballots for one year, and at the expiration of that time, he shall destroy the same, and for failure to do so, he may be fined from $50.00 to $500.00 and imprisoned in the county jail for six months. 

Sec. 518. Voting Machines.-Paper ballots are being replaced by voting machines in some counties. This is not mandatory.

CHAPTER 59 OFFENSES AGAINST RELIGIOUS WORSHIP Sec. 519. Religious Freedom.-One of the things that the fathers prized most highly was the privilege of worshipping God according to the dictates of their own consciences. For this reason, the Pilgrims settled New England, the Quakers Pennsylvania, the Catholics Maryland, and the Huguenots South Carolina. In the formation of the Union, not one of these creeds, nor the established Church of England, which was so strong in Virginia, could be made the religion of the country by law. It was, therefore, very wisely agreed to protect all worship without preference or partiality and leave it to a man's individual conscience as to the body, if any, with which he would affiliate. 

Sec. 520. Belief in the Divine.-It is considered that every citizen, however, as a basis of good morals, should believe in a Supreme Being. The test is applied in administering oaths where the witness is required to tell the truth, "so help me God."

Sec. 521. Disturbance of Religious Worship.-Any person, who shall by loud, vociferous swearing and indecent or unseemly conduct, disturb religious worship, shall be fined not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00. 320

OFFENSES AGAINST RELIGIOUS WORSHIP 

321 P 

Sec. 522. Working on Sunday.-Any person, who shall labor, or compel his servant to labor on Sunday, or who shall hunt game within a half mile of any church, schoolhouse or residence, on Sunday, shall be fined not less than $10.00 nor more than $15.00. There are some exceptions to this rule, such as the doing of household duties; urgent necessities of the farm; the operation of boats, cars, foundries, hotels, livery stables, telegraph and telephone offices, and the handling of the United States mail. 

Sec. 523. Gambling on the Sabbath.-Horse racing within any city or town, or betting upon the same, or otherwise gambling and betting on the Sabbath, is punishable by fine of from $20.00 to $50.00.

Sec. 524. Selling Goods on Sunday.-Any person who shall keep open on Sunday before 1 p. m., a place of public amusement to which an admission fee is charged, or shall operate on the Sabbath any store for the purpose of selling or disposing of goods, shall be punished by fine of not less than $20.00, nor more than $50.00. The sale of newspapers, burial material, ice, ice cream, milk, gasoline and drugs, is permitted, and markets may remain open until 9 o'clock a. m. 

Sec. 525. A Day of Rest.-It is not the purpose of the law to compel people to believe in a Christian Sabbath, but rather to protect those who do believe in it. 

The law assumes that it being a Christian country, the majority of people believe in the Christian Sabbath, and its established observance not only affords protection to those who devoutly observe the day, but it is a very wholesome regulation for society in general in affording a day of rest from the heavy and constant labor of the week.

CHAPTER 60 OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC JUSTICE Sec. 526. Scope of Chapter.-An offense against the administration of public justice means an offense in any manner interfering with, or hampering, the administration of the law, as will be seen by the following specially enumerated offenses.

Sec. 527. Perjury.-Perjury consists in deliberately making under oath a false statement, as in the trial of a case. This does not include a mistake innocently made, or made under fear or agitation, or a statement of any wholly immaterial circumstance. It is the wilful and deliberate falsehood, made to mislead and deceive, which is criminal. If the perjury be committed upon the trial of a capital case, and if the death penalty was inflicted upon such perjured testimony, then the punishment for such perjury shall be death. If perjury is committed in a civil trial or in that of an ordinary felony, the punishment is confinement in the penitentiary from two to ten years. 

Sec. 528. False Swearing.-The voluntarily making under oath of a false statement or affidavit not required to be made in any trial, or judicial proceeding, is false 322

OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC JUSTICE 

323 swearing. It is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years. An example of false swearing would be if one made a false affidavit to satisfy a quarantine regulation; or where, in proving heirship to property as to how many children there were in a certain family, John Doe should swear there were only two when in fact he knew there were more. 

Sec. 529. Subornation of Witnesses.-Procuring or inducing a witness to swear falsely or commit perjury, is punishable the same as perjury. An attempt to suborn or bribe a witness is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years. 

Sec. 530. Officers Wilfully Permitting Prisoners to 

Escape. Any sheriff, constable, jailor, or any officer in charge of any prisoner who shall wilfully and knowingly permit the said prisoner to escape, shall be deemed guilty of an offense against the laws of the state, and may be punished according to the seriousness of the crime with which the escaping prisoner was charged. For the escape of a prisoner in a capital case, from two to ten years in prison; in an ordinary felony, from two to five years; in case of a misdemeanor, by fine not exceeding $1,000.00. 

Sec. 531. To Negligently Allow an Escape.-If such officer shall by negligence and carelessness, without any wilful intent, allow a prisoner to escape, he shall be punished by fine not exceeding $2,000.00 if the escaped prisoner be charged with a capital offense; if charged with ordinary felony, then by fine not exceeding $1,000.00; if charged with misdemeanor, then by fine not exceeding $500.00. It is also an offense for an officer to neglect to arrest a criminal, or to allow arms to be conveyed into prison.

Sec. 532. Resisting Officers.-If any person shall re sist an officer who has a warrant for his arrest, or any writ to be served upon him, or shall resist any officer who has a right to make an arrest without a warrant, then he shall be fined in any sum not exceeding $500.00, and if the party making the resistance shall use arms, the penalty shall be higher according to the offense. 

Sec. 533. False Certificate of Officer.-It is unlawful for any officer, notary public, clerk, or other person, to make a false certificate to any deed or other written instrument, and for so doing, he may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years. 

Sec. 534. Conversion of Public Money.-The appro priation by an officer to his own private use of public money is an offense of a kindred nature to embezzlement, and the person so offending may be punished as in case of theft.

Sec. 535. Peculation.-It shall be unlawful for a public officer to purchase claims against the state, such as county script, or witness fees. It is unlawful for a public officer to sell supplies to the county, to the city, or state, or to become interested in any contract with the city, county or state. A penalty for such violation shall be by fine of not more than $1,000.00, and in some instances not less than ten nor more than twenty times the amount of claim or debt.

The object of this statute is to prevent public officials such as County Commissioners, County Judges, and civil officers from controlling the buying by furnishing supplies made from their own purchases, and reaping a profit to the exclusion of legitimate trade, and perhaps at unreasonable prices. It also removes the temptation

OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC JUSTICE 

325 3 from an officer of withholding any money in order that the holder of the indebtedness against the county or state might sell his paper at discount.

Sec. 536. Nepotism.-Nepotism is the appointment to any office or position of profit of a relative of the officer making such appointment. The relative in question must be within the second degree to constitute the offense.

The inhibition not only extends to the appointment, but includes each and every payment of salary. The penalty for such violation is not less than $100.00 nor more than $1,000.00. 

Sec. 537. Compounding Crime.-Agreeing with a criminal for something of value that he shall not be prosecuted, and that his crime shall be kept secret, is in law compounding crime. Thus: If A steals B's hat, and 

B then agrees with A that if he will return the hat, he will see that he is not prosecuted for it, then B is bribed to conceal a crime for the value of his hat, and is guilty of compounding crime with A and may be punished.

CHAPTER 61 

OFFENSES AGAINST THE PUBLIC PEACE 

Sec. 538. Unlawful Assemblies.-Unlawful assemblies are the gathering together of a number of persons for the purpose of committing an offense, or for the purpose of illegally depriving some one of his legal rights, or disturbing him in the enjoyment thereof, or to rescue a felon from the custody of the officer, or to frighten persons by mask and disguise. 

Sec. 539. Whitecapping.-This offense consists of threats, either written or by signs, in the way of giving one notice to leave a community, or by painting a skeleton or crossbones, and posting same in a threatening manner. It is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary not less than two nor more than five years. 

Sec. 540. Riots.-Riots are similar in nature to unlawful assembly as defined above, except taking on a more serious form. The punishment is confinement in the penitentiary for a term of years according to intent of the parties and seriousness of the offense. 

Sec. 541. Affrays.-Where two or more people shall fight together in a public place, they are deemed guilty 326

OFFENSES AGAINST THE PUBLIC PEACE 

327 of an affray and may be fined in any sum not exceeding $100.00. The reader will note the distinction between an affray and an assault, elsewhere defined. The gist of the offense in case of an affray is willingly fighting together in a public place which is calculated to disturb others and provoke a breach of the peace. It is therefore an offense against the public, while an assault is an offense against a person, and is committed by one person striking or threatening to strike another.

Sec. 542. Disturbance of the Peace.-Cursing, swear ing, and the use of loud, vociferous, vulgar or indecent language, publicly displaying a deadly weapon, or making an indecent exposure of the person in a manner calculated to provoke a breach of the peace, either at some public place or private residence, is called a disturbance of the peace and is punishable by fine not exceeding $100.00.

Sec. 543. Shooting in a Public Place. It is unlaw ful to discharge a firearm across any public street or highway, and is punishable by fine of from $25.00 to $100.00. 

The firing of torpedoes or cannon crackers when done on the streets of a town or city, comes within the provisions of this Article, and is punishable by fine not exceeding $100.00.

Sec. 544. Horse Racing in a Public Road. It is a misdemeanor to race horses along any public highway, and is punishable by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00. The reason for this law can be readily seen in the danger that might ensue to others using the highway in case of an animated race along the same road. 

The same is true of automobile races.

Sec. 545. Unlawful Carrying of Arms.-It is un lawful to carry about the person any pistol, dirk, dagger, slung shot, spear, sword cane, or knuckles, and the person guilty of offending shall be fined in any sum not less than $100.00 nor more than $500.00, or confined in jail not less than thirty days, nor more than twelve months. 

There are certain exceptions to this rule, notably sheriffs, constables, policemen and private citizens who are traveling away from home, and other persons who may be thrown in such immediate and close proximity to serious danger that they feel the necessity of being prepared to defend themselves upon short notice before they can secure the protection of the law; also a person carrying to his home or place of business a deadly weapon, but this will not allow him to habitually carry a deadly weapon to and from his place of business. 

Sec. 546. Carrying Arms to Church.-It is unlawful to carry arms to church or to a public gathering. The penalty for such an offense is the same as provided in 

Section 545. 

Sec. 547. Selling to Minors.-Any person who shall sell any minor without the written consent of his parent or guardian, any arms defined in the preceding sections, shall be punished by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $200.00, or by confinement in the county jail, not less than ten nor more than thirty days. Nor can minors buy tobacco when under sixteen years of age.

CHAPTER 62 OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC MORALS Sec. 548. Scope of the Chapter. This subdivision of the law includes all those nameless, immoral and indecent offenses of that order of crime that places the offender in the category and upon a level with the lower order of animals. Some of these offenses will be treated under this chapter.

Sec. 549. Bigamy.-Bigamy is the offense of either a man or woman having more than one living wife or husband, which occurs in cases where the parties shall remarry without obtaining a lawful divorce from the former spouse. This offense is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two, nor more than five years.

Sec. 550. Minister Unlawfully Performing Gere monies. Any minister, magistrate, or other person authorized by law to perform the marriage rites, who shall marry, or pretend to marry, any couple without at the time having license duly issued by a county clerk, shall be punished by fine of not less than $50.00, nor more than $500.00. 329

Sec. 551. Marriage Between Whites and Blacks.- 

It shall be unlawful for any person of the Caucasian race to intermarry with a Negro or person of Negro descent, and such person so offending shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary not less than two, nor more than five years. This not only extends to persons married in Texas, but to those who may have married elsewhere and removed here to live.

Sec. 552. Forbidden Marriages.-People of close kin are forbidden to marry. Example: Uncle and niece, or any other degree of kin closer than that of first cousin. 

In many states, first cousins are forbidden to marry, but the statute of Texas does not extend to that limit. This offense is defined in law as incest and extends to all cases of carnal knowledge between such parties, irrespective of marriage. 

Sec. 553. Indecent Publication. 

Any person who shall print or in any manner publish any indecent article, picture, or scene calculated to corrupt the morals of the youth or public, shall be punished by fine of not less than $100.00, and any person who shall engage in the business of selling and distributing impure literature of such variety shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not exceeding five years.

Sec. 554. Desecration of Graves.-Any person who shall deface any tomb, injure or destroy any nearby trees, or otherwise desecrate, injure, or interfere with any grave or tomb, shall be punished by fine not exceeding $500.00, or by imprisonment in jail not to exceed six months. To unlawfully remove the dead body from a grave is an in the penitentiary for a maximum of twenty-five years. offense subject to severe penalty, even up to confinement

CHAPTER 63 OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC POLICY Sec. 555. Issuing Due Bills as Money.-Any person who shall issue, endorse, or pass any bill or other paper intended to circulate as money, shall be punished by fine. of not less than $10.00 nor more than $50.00. It will be seen that the effect of this statute is not to reach counterfeiters who are punished by federal law, but is to prevent persons or corporations from issuing a form of a due bill to be intended as a circulating currency among the people of the vicinity.

Sec. 556. Passing the Notes of a Broken Bank.—If any person shall knowingly pass as money the note of any broken bank, he shall be confined in the penitentiary for not less than two, nor more than five years. The protection insured by this law has become by recent legislation almost obsolete or useless, as the only banks of issue have their circulation protected by the United States Government. 

Sec. 557. Lottery.-It shall be unlawful to dispose of any real estate by means of chance or lottery, and any person selling lottery tickets at any chance or game of chance, shall be punished by fine of not less than $10.00 nor more than $50.00. 331

Sec. 558. Raffles.-To raffle any real or personal estate of more than $500.00 in valuation, is punishable by fine of not less than $100.00, nor more than $1,000.00.

If the value of the property raffled be $500.00 or less the fine shall be not less than $5.00 nor more than $200.00. 

Sec. 559. Bucket Shop.-Any person who shall conduct the business known as a "bucket shop," where dealings in futures are had, with no real intention of an actual bona fide delivery of the property bought and sold, shall be punished by imprisonment in the penitentiary not exceeding two years. 

Sec. 560. Gambling.-It is unlawful for any person to bet at any game of cards at any place other than a private residence, and if a private residence be periodically or habitually resorted to for the purpose of gaming, then it shall not be subject to such exemption. To play at a game with cards in a public place is an offense regardless of whether there be anything bet or wagered on the result of the game. The penalty for so playing shall be a fine not exceeding $50.00.

Sec. 561. Betting at a Table.-Any person who shall play at any game of dice, dominoes, or similar game, at which anything of value is bet upon the result of the game, shall be punished by fine not exceeding $50.00.

Sec. 562. Keeping Gaming Table.-Any person who shall keep a gaming table or run what is called a game, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than four years, and any person who shall rent his premises with a knowledge that same shall be used for gambling, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for from two to four years; and any person in charge of any house or premises

OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC POLICY 

333 knowingly permitting any gaming paraphernalia to remain on the premises, shall be punished by confinement in the county jail for not less than thirty days nor more than one year.

Sec. 563. Remaining in Gambling Room.-Any per son who shall enter into and remain in any room where gambling is going on shall be punished by fine not exceeding $50.00.

Sec. 564. Officer Failing to Arrest for Gaming.-If it shall come to the knowledge of any officer that there is betting going on, and he shall, having a chance or opportunity, refuse or neglect to make an arrest of the parties so offending, he shall be punished by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00.

Sec. 565. Betting on Elections.-Any person who shall bet or wager anything upon the result of any public election, shall be punished by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $1,000.00, or he may be confined in jail from twenty to sixty days.

Sec. 566. Betting on Ball Game.-Any person who shall bet or wager any sum of money or thing of value upon the result of any ball game, shall be punished by fine of not less than $5.00 nor more than $100.00.

Sec. 567. Evils of Gambling. The evils of gambling would be very much minimized if there was no one injured other than those affected by the game, but in the great majority of instances, some one may sacrifice, and does lose, the results of hard-earned money that is needed for the support of a dependent family who are left in want, and oftentimes to suffer, as the result of a few moments' pastime. Gambling also has a very demoralizing

tendency, in that it appears to grow upon the individual; a desire to make money in some way other than by a legitimate enterprise and honest labor. So completely has the gaming propensity been able to fasten itself upon its victims that public officials, bank cashiers, and others in charge of trust money have been known to gamble away the funds of the state, of the county, of the bank, and even of the orphan.

No one's money is safe in the hands of a gambler although he be otherwise honest and respectable. 

Sec. 568. Cockfighting.-It is unlawful for any person or persons to match for purposes of fighting any cocks or other animals. The fine for such offense shall be not less than $10.00 nor more than $100.00. 

Sec. 569. Intoxicating Liquors.-The sale of intoxi cants is a subject that the law has attempted to regulate. 

Like opium, it has very dangerous and serious effects upon those who become addicted to the habit; like gambling, it does not stop in its baleful influence with the immediate user, but often extends to many that are dependent, and in no way responsible for its excessive use. 

The best way to control the liquor problem is a very serious question that has been much discussed by moralists and by the lawmakers of Texas. Many advocate absolute prohibition of liquor, while others favor a rigid regulation. We have regulation with prohibition in certain counties and localities, where the people have so voted. This is called local option.

Among some of the provisions regulating the sale of intoxicants, we find the following: 

Sec. 570. Sale to Minors.-If any person, his agent, or employee, shall sell to a minor intoxicating liquor, he

OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC POLICY 

335 shall be fined not less than $100.00 nor more than $1,000.00, and in addition may be confined in jail not more than one year. 

Sec. 571. Manufacture and Sale. It is unlawful for anyone to manufacture, sell or possess for purposes of sale, any intoxicating liquor, subject to certain exceptions. (a) One who obtains a permit or license to sell. (b) One who purchases in a lawful way whiskey for his own use and is transporting it into a place where its possession is lawful. (c) One who is bringing into the state for his own use.

Sec. 572. Liquor Control Board. The liquor traffic is regulated by a Board of Control consisting of three persons appointed by the Governor of Texas. The board selects an administrator who devotes his entire time to the service. All applications for permits to sell liquor must be made to this board.

Sec. 573. Permits the Board May Issue: 

Brewer permits for $1,000.00. 

Distillers' permits for $1,000.00. 

Winery permits, $50.00. If made from one's own grapes, then only $10.00.

Rectifier's permit to refine and purify spirits, $1,000.00. 

Wholesale liquor permit, $1,250.00. 

Wholesale beer and wine, $100.00. 

Package store permit (depending upon size of city), $50.00 up to $250.00.

Permits to store and like privileges may be granted by board for various activities in connection with the traffic. 

Sec. 574. Sale Regulations.-Beer and wine may be sold and consumed where bought. Whiskey is sold only in containers or packages not to be opened or used upon the premises. No sales between 12 p. m. and 7 a. m. 

No sales on election days or Sundays. 

Sec. 575. Local Option.-By local option is meant the right of any county or subdivision thereof to have an election ordered to determine whether or not intoxicating liquors may be sold within such county or subdivision. This arrangement was provided for by the Constitution of the state in 1876. The election is ordered by the Commissioners of the County Court, either by their own motion or upon a petition of two hundred and fifty voters from a county, or fifty voters for a justice's precinct. If a city is included within the precinct, then the petition shall equal one-tenth of the vote cast for governor at the last election. Upon receipt of such petition, it becomes the duty of the commissioners to order an election and post notices thereof. Whiskey, wine and beer may be prohibited or whiskey only. After the said election has been held, the commissioners meet, canvass the returns, and declare the result. If the majority of the votes cast on such election be for prohibition, then it shall be unlawful to sell such intoxicating liquor within such county or subdivision, and no license for its sale will be issued, and no other election on this question may be held within this county or subdivision until one year shall have expired, when another election may be held upon the same proposition.

OFFENSES AGAINST PUBLIC POLICY 

337 

Sec. 576. Selling in Local Option Territory.-If any person shall sell whiskey or any intoxicating liquor within local option territory, he shall be fined from $100.00 to $1,000.00, and may be imprisoned for not more than one year.

Sec. 577. Wine for Sacramental Purposes.-Any minister, priest, rabbi or religious organization may purchase wine for sacramental purposes only without being subject to the law regulating intoxicating liquors. 

Sec. 578. Desertion of Dependents.-Any man who shall wilfully desert and refuse to support his wife who may be in necessitous circumstances, or any parent who may desert and refuse to support his or her child under sixteen years of age, may be subject to fine and imprisonment in the penitentiary. 

Sec. 579. Vagrancy.-An idle person who makes no effort to earn a living by honest employment and who does not seek honest employment, and all suspicious characters, professional gamblers, prostitutes and people of professionally bad habits, are classed as vagrants, and are subject to a fine of not exceeding $200.00. 

Sec. 580. Insurance Agent Without Authority.-Any person who shall solicit insurance without first having obtained a permit for his company, and being duly authorized to solicit same, shall be punished by fine of not more than $100.

Sec. 581. Rebates.-No person shall accept any re bate in payment of premiums or other valuable consideration not specified in the policy of insurance. The penalty for such is a fine not exceeding $100.00 or a jail

sentence of not more than ninety days, or by both such penalties.

Sec. 582. False Statements by Physicians.- Any agent, or physician, who shall knowingly make any false statement with reference to an application for insurance, shall be fined not less than $100.00 and not more than $500.00.

d 

CHAPTER 64 HEALTH REGULATIONS Sec. 583. Public Health.--Public health consists of the physical well-being of the individual members of society. In law it is superior to all other rights of the individual with reference to his personal liberty and property rights. He may be required to remove his place of business, to correct his habits, or to do various and sundry things under the general laws protecting the public health.

Sec. 584. Public Nuisance.-A public nuisance is any trade or business injurious to the public health, such as allowing back yards or premises of any character to become foul or offensive so as in any way to endanger the public health. The ordinary fine for such is not less than $10.00 nor more than $100.00, and the imposition of same may be daily or constantly imposed until the nuisance is abated.

Sec. 585. Other Forms of a Nuisance.-It is unlaw ful to pollute or render impure or offensive any watercourse, lake, or pond. It is likewise unlawful for the owner to leave any dead animal, or to dump trash, within three hundred yards of any public highway, and for vio- 339

lation of this law he may be punished by fine of not less than $10.00 nor more than $200.00. 

Sec. 586. Selling Unwholesome and Adulterated

Food. No person shall sell for the purpose of human consumption any adulterated food or watered milk, or fail to freely label his products. For a violation of this law he shall be punished as provided by statutes applicable to each case.

Sec. 587. Adulteration.-By the term "adulteration" 

as here used, is meant an impure mixture of foods by which a cheaper article is mixed with one of superior grade so as to counterfeit a better quality. 

Sec. 588. Doctors and Dentists.-It shall be unlawful for any doctor or dentist to practice without being licensed therefor and having his license recorded with the clerk of the District Court of the county in which he expects to practice.

Sec. 589. Pharmacist.- No person shall practice pharmacy or fill prescriptions as such without first holding a certificate of such qualifications, which certificate must be posted in a conspicuous place in his place of business.

Sec. 590. Quarantine.-The state has enacted various regulations to restrict and prevent the spread of infectious and contagious diseases, the presence of which at a house is usually indicated by a yellow or some other highly colored flag or cardboard, and no person is allowed to go into or upon these premises and again mix and mingle with the public, unless it be a practicing physician, who shall thoroughly disinfect his clothing after leaving these premises.

HEALTH REGULATIONS 

341 The quarantine regulations are particularly extended to foreign vessels arriving at the ports of the state. Before the passengers or cargo are permitted to be unloaded, they first shall be carried through the quarantine station, and certain examinations and regulations complied with, under very heavy penalties for violation.

CHAPTER 65 OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY HELD IN COMMON FOR PUBLIC USE 

Sec. 591. Rights in Common.-Properties held for public use in which the public generally have a common interest, are navigable streams, public roads, bridges, public buildings, and other things incidental thereto. 

Sec. 592. Navigable Streams.-A navigable stream is one of sufficient size to be claimed and considered as one suitable for the purposes of navigation, upon which boats may be used for traffic and commerce. To obstruct such a stream is unlawful and the person so violating shall be subject to a fine of not less than $50.00 nor more than $200.00.

Sec. 593. Public Roads, Bridges and Streets.-It shall be unlawful for any person to obstruct public highways, or to interfere with any marker or signboard placed thereon, and for so obstructing or interfering, he may be subjected to a fine of not exceeding $500.00. 

Sec. 594. Road Overseers.-Every person appointed by the County Commissioners' Court to act as road overseer and who shall wilfully refuse to serve may be fined 342

OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY HELD IN COMMON 

343 not less than $10.00 nor more than $50.00. There may be some doubt about the constitutionality of this section, but it does not appear to have been tested. By the use of road gangs and county machinery the old-time road worker and overseer is almost obsolete. 

Sec. 595. Road Duty.-Every male citizen between the ages of twenty-one and forty-five, excepting ministers in the actual performance of duty, and cripples, shall be subject to road duty, which may be satisfied by the payment of either a road tax as provided by the particular county, or by one dollar per day in lieu of such service.

Any person failing to perform such service or pay such tax after due notice shall be subject to fine in any sum not exceeding twenty-five dollars.

Sec. 596. Boundary Line for Road.-The owner of land in a rural district must, when ordered by the Commissioners of the County, within one year after notice, give no less than fifteen feet upon the boundary line of his tract of land for the purpose of a road or outlet to other property in the rear thereof, and failing to do so, may be punished by a fine of not less than $20.00 for each month that he may persist in this refusal. 

Sec. 597. Leaving Gates Open.-It is unlawful for a person upon any third class road or upon a neighborhood road upon which gates are allowed, to fail to fasten them. 

Sec. 598. Ferries.-The crossing of streams where no bridges have been constructed, may be accomplished by ferries. Persons operating the same do so generally as a private enterprise, and must procure a license and maintain good boats for such purpose.

Sec. 599. Game, Birds and Fish.-Almost every Legislature has seen fit to amend or generally revise the game law.

At present (1938) we have a Fish and Game Commission composed of six members appointed by the Governor. There are selected numerous Game Wardens over the state for the protection of game and fish. 

Hunter's License.-All non residents, or out-of-state hunters, must procure a hunter's license from the Game, Fish and Oyster Commissioner, paying $25.00 therefor.

No license is required to hunt in the county of your residence. To hunt further from home, you must procure of your county clerk a license, paying $2.00 therefor. 

Birds. It is unlawful to kill any kind of a bird other than a game bird, the penalty being not less than $10.00. 

The same penalty will also attach for robbing nests. Birds depredating upon crops, however, may be killed; so may 

English sparrows, hawks, crows, buzzards, blackbirds and owls. 

Game Birds are named as wild turkeys, ducks, geese, grouse, prairie chickens, pheasants, quail, doves, pigeons, plover, snipe, jacksnipe, curlews, and robins.

The Open Season is that part of the year wherein protected game may be killed. Wild male deer may be killed from November 16th to December 31st, inclusive, blacktailed deer west of Pecos during November only. Females are not to be killed at any time. Wild turkeys may be killed from the 16th of November to the 31st of December in certain zones or counties. Quail may be killed from the 1st of December to the 16th of January, and not more than twelve shall be killed by any hunter dur-

OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY HELD IN COMMON 

345 ing one day. The lowest penalty for violating any of these last provisions is $10.00. 

Zones.-Dove shooting is regulated into zones and counties, the same being true as to squirrel and most other game. The hunter should consult the statute or local authorities in each county.

Fish must not be taken by poison or by dynamite or other explosives, the penalty for such being a fine of not less than $50.00 nor more than $100.00, and by confinement in jail for not less than sixty nor more than ninety days.

Nets and Seines must not be used in catching fish unless the meshes in the seine or dragnet be sufficiently large to allow small fish to escape. A minnow seine may be used, however, when not more than twenty feet in length. The penalty for unlawful seining or netting is not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00.

CHAPTER 66 OFFENSES AGAINST TRADE AND COMMERCE 

The present age of steam and electricity is also a great commercial age. The most remote parts of the earth have been brought close together by means of rapid transit. A consequent outgrowth of such vast volumes of business is that there should be frauds and crimes of various characters affecting the country's trade and commerce, to prevent which the state has enacted a series of laws designated to punish such offenses. 

Sec. 600. Forgery.-Forgery consists in the signing of another's name or affixing another's signature to an instrument without authority for the purpose, and with the intent to defraud him, or in the altering of a check. or written instrument so as to change the amount of the obligation. The offense is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary.

The gist of the offense in forgery is that there must be an intent to defraud. The mere signing of another's name to a paper does not constitute the offense, unless with the intent and design of creating some obligation on the part of the party whose name is signed, and for the purpose of injuring or defrauding. 346

OFFENSES AGAINST TRADE AND COMMERCE 

347 

Sec. 601. Altering Teacher's Certificate.-For any teacher in the public schools to wilfully raise the grade or character of the certificate held by him, is an offense punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than seven years. 

Sec. 602. Substituting Instruments.-Substituting instruments consists in the changing of one instrument for another so that the intention of a party executing the original instrument shall be changed and other conditions inserted. This is an offense and punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for from two to five years. 

Sec. 603. Personating Another.-If any person shall represent himself to be other than himself, or to be some other person, and as such other person affix a signature to any valuable paper or acknowledge any deed, he shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than seven years. 

Sec. 604. Land Titles.-Any person who shall alter any land certificate, field notes, patent, deed, or any record affecting the title of lands with an intent to make money or profit thereby, shall be confined in the penitentiary for not less than five nor more than twenty years. 

Sec. 605. False Certificate.-Any officer or other per son authorized to acknowledge deeds or legal papers, who shall falsely certify to the same or to any copy of same, or falsely certify to any fictitious signature to the original, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than five nor more than twenty years. 

Sec. 606. Uttering a Forged Instrument.-Any per son who shall knowingly pass any check or instrument of any kind, knowing the same to be a forgery, shall be

confined in the penitentiary for a period of not less than five nor more than twenty years. 

Sec. 607. Counterfeiting.-The making of an imitation or semblance of an original coin, or other money, or altering the same so as to increase its value, is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for not less. than five nor more than ten years. 

Sec. 608. Passing Counterfeit Coin. Any person knowing a coin to be a counterfeit and who shall pass the same for value to another person shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years. It is likewise a penitentiary offense to have in one's possession dies for the purpose of counterfeiting.

Sec. 609. False Weights and Measures.-No person shall use false balances or measures with an intent to defraud. The penalty for such is a fine of from $20.00 to $100.00 for the first conviction. Any public weigher intentionally giving out wrong weights, shall be fined not less than $25.00 nor more than $250.00 and may be imprisoned in jail for not less than thirty days nor more than six months.

Sec. 610. Notary's False Certificate.-Any notary who shall certify any check or give any certificate relating to commerce or navigation, knowing the certificate so made to be false, shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years.

CHAPTER 67 OFFENSES AGAINST THE PERSON Sec. 611. Scope of the Chapter. This subject in cludes all forms of assault, homicide, false imprisonment, kidnaping and kindred offenses, and is one of the largest divisions of crime. 

Sec. 612. Assault and Battery.-An assault and battery is any unlawful violence, however slight, upon the person of another with the intent to injure, and any attempt to commit a battery is an assault. Thus a threatening demonstration or gesture, if within such proximity of the other party as to enable one to carry it out, is an assault. To spit upon another is an assault. Assault and battery is punishable by fine of not less than $5.00 nor more than $25.00.

Sec. 613. Provocation for Assault.-No verbal provo cation, however provoking, shall be, or is a justification for an assault. Insulting language or any other provocation may be shown, however, to mitigate the punishment, and entitle the defendant to the lowest fine. 

The several degrees of assault are: Simple assault, aggravated assault, assault to murder, or an assault to commit some other offense. 349

Sec. 614. Aggravated Assault.-Aggravated assault is the next degree higher than simple assault, and a simple assault becomes an aggravated assault when committed upon an aged or infirm person, or committed by an adult upon a child, or by a male upon a female, or where committed in a manner to disgrace the party assaulted, or where serious bodily injuries are inflicted, or where the assault is committed in disguise. Punishment for aggravated assault is by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $1,000.00, and, in addition thereto, the party may be imprisoned in the county jail for not less than one month, nor more than two years. The punishment may be by either or both of these methods. 

Sec. 615. Assault to Murder.-An assault to murder is an attempt to commit murder which for some reason failed, as where the party was shot but did not die of the wound. The offense is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for a period of not less than two nor more than fifteen years.

Sec. 616. Assault to Commit Other Offenses.-An assault may be committed with an intent to rob, or an intent to rape, or an intent to commit burglary, and each of such assaults is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for various periods ranging from two to ten years. 

Sec. 617. Maiming.-Maiming consists in the wilful and malicious cutting off or depriving a person of some organ of the body, as a finger, a foot, or an eye. The offense is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary from two to ten years. 

Sec. 618. False Imprisonment.-Any person who shall wilfully detain another in his custody or deprive

OFFENSES AGAINST THE PERSON 

351 him of his liberty without lawful right to do so, shall be punished by fine of not exceeding $500.00, and by imprisonment in the county jail not exceeding one year, or by either or both of such means. 

Sec. 619. Enticing Minors.-Any person who shall entice or induce, or in any manner, decoy a minor to leave his parents or guardian, or any person acting instead of such parent or guardian, shall be punished by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $200.00. 

Sec. 620. Kidnaping.-The taking into custody of any person, either child or adult, and spiriting such person away without his or her consent, and depriving that person of his or her liberty and right of communication, is termed kidnaping, and is punishable by imprisonment in the penitentiary. Since the Lindbergh case severe penalties have been imposed by the national government. 

Sec. 621. Poisoning.-If any person shall wilfully and intentionally administer poison to another, from which death shall result, he shall be deemed guilty of murder in the first degree, and punished by confinement in the penitentiary for life, or by death, at the discretion. of the jury. Any attempt to poison another shall be punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years. 

Sec. 622. Homicide.-Homicide is the taking of life, and includes murder, manslaughter, negligent homicide, and accidents.

Sec. 623. Murder in the First Degree.-Murder committed with express malice was, under the old law, murder in the first degree. This express malice is usually evidenced by a formed design and scheme to commit

such offense, such as lying in wait, administering poison, and other circumstances showing premeditated designs to take life. Murder in the first degree was punishable by death or confinement in the penitentiary for life. 

Sec. 624. Murder in the Second Degree.-Murder committed with implied malice was murder in the second degree, under the old law. It was distinguished from murder in the first degree by the absence of any premeditated design to take life, as where two men quarrel without any former grudge, and one takes the life of the other, such offense ordinarily was murder in the second degree. It was something of a midway station between manslaughter and murder in the first degree, and was punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for any term of years not less than five. Degrees of murder, so long the law, have now been abolished and the defendant is simply tried for "murder." The jury has the discretion of giving the high or low penalty according to the nature of the crime. 

Sec. 625. Manslaughter.-The taking of a life in the heat of sudden passion produced by some mitigating cause is manslaughter. A very frequent illustration of this is found in the case where the female relatives of the defendant have been insulted by the deceased, and if the homicide occurs on the first meeting after the defendant learns of such insulting language or conduct, the offense will not be murder, but manslaughter. So also any other condition which with adequate cause renders the mind incapable of cool reflection, and where the homicide is committed before the party has had time to "cool off" or reflect, shall not be deemed murder, but manslaughter. 

This offense is now consolidated with, and is comprehended under, the general charge of murder so that the

OFFENSES AGAINST THE PERSON 

353 punishment for murder now includes penalties ranging from two years imprisonment up to death. 

Sec. 626. Negligent Homicide.-The taking of life by the party's carelessness or negligence, without malice, is termed negligent homicide, and is punishable by fine not exceeding $1,000.00, and by imprisonment in the county jail not exceeding one year. An illustration of negligent homicide would be the case of a person carelessly throwing a brick from a building, and thereby striking and killing a passerby on the walkway below. 

Sec. 627. Accidental Killing.-Where homicide occurs by pure accident and there is no failure to exercise care, and consequently no negligence and no malice or intention of committing the offense, then it is pure accident, and there is no penalty attached. 

Sec. 628. Self-Defense.-"Self-preservation is the first law of nature." No law has ever been enacted and, it may safely be said, never will be enacted, that will deprive one of the right of self-defense, and this right of self-defense is extended so as not only to defend one's self at the time of the immediate assault, but upon the appearance of danger. For instance, if the defendant was in an altercation with the deceased, and the deceased opened a grip and the defendant had reason to believe, or knew, that there was a pistol or deadly weapon in the grip, he had a right to shoot him in self-defense, even before the pistol was produced.
