So also if the deceased thrust his hand into his bosom, or hip pocket, or any other place where he is thought to have a weapon, the defendant may act in his self-defense, even though there was in fact no weapon there, if he believed that there was. The right of self-defense is further extended to include others, and

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law a person may act in the defense of his son, his family, or even his friend, where he believes that without such interference the party in whose behalf he acted would suffer death or serious bodily injury. Self-defense is one of the most frequently interposed defenses in murder cases, and like religion, personal liberty, and many other rights that men hold dear, is frequently much abused and counterfeited, and a very guilty man is sometimes able to escape on this plea. 

Sec. 629. Alibi.—An alibi is another defense frequently interposed in criminal cases. This defense consists in producing testimony to show that a man was not at the place at the time the offense was committed, but was at that particular time at some other place. 

Sec. 630. Insanity.-Another defense frequently interposed to shield crime is insanity. It is, of course, in some instances a genuine defense, but is undoubtedly much abused. It is unquestionably true that the state should prepare a home for the criminally insane, so if a defendant shall make the plea of temporary insanity as a defense for a crime, he may not be turned out upon the world to commit another crime, while under another fit of temporary insanity. 

Sec. 631. Threat.-Where any person shall seriously make threats against the life of another party, and these threats shall be communicated to that party, the threats may be used as a matter of defense, provided that at the time of the homicide the deceased was making some demonstration that caused the defendant to believe that he was going to put the threats into execution. 

Sec. 632. Dueling.-The fighting of duels in the early part of the last century was very common. Aaron Burr

OFFENSES AGAINST THE PERSON 

355 1 killed Alexander Hamilton in a duel. Andrew Jackson fought several duels. The occurrence of such was so common that those who framed the Constitution of Texas provided that every person who took the oath of office, small or great, should swear in the oath so taken that he had neither fought a duel at any time, nor acted as a second, nor in any way aided or abetted or carried a challenge for fighting a duel, and unless the party could take such oath, he was disqualified for the office. This offense has almost grown to be obsolete, and its commission is now very rare. The sending of a challenge to fight a duel is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years, and where the parties actually fight a duel, and one of the parties is killed, the other is guilty of murder in the first degree. 

Sec. 633. Prize Fighting.-Prize fighting is a fight between men or between a man and a bull or other animal, wherein things of value are wagered upon the result of the fight, or some championship is at stake, or an admission is charged to the arena or building where the fight takes place. Any person, engaging in such encounter, may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years. It is also unlawful to exhibit the same in a moving picture. 

Under Governor Charles Culberson a special term of the Legislature was convened to outlaw prize fighting. 

Since the World War, however, boxing has been reinstated to a restricted degree.

CHAPTER 68 OFFENSES AGAINST REPUTATION 

Sec. 634. Libel.-Libel consists in publishing or circulating any false statement with malicious design, affecting the reputation of the person about whom the statement is made. The offense is punishable by fine of not less than $100.00 nor more than $2,000.00, and by imprisonment not exceeding two years, or by either or both of these penalties. The malicious statement must be made with an intent to injure and it must be in print or writing. The gist of the offense consists in the malicious publishing or giving the statement notoriety. 

Sec. 635. Slander.-Slander, unlike libel, may be oral, and concerns females only. The offense is to orally or otherwise impute a want of chastity to any female, the penalty for which is a fine of not less than $100.00, nor more than $1,000.00, and the offender may be imprisoned for not exceeding one year. Civil actions may be maintained by the injured party for slander or libel, but this does not in any way affect the criminal prosecution herein set forth.

Sec. 636. The Evils of Gossip.-The trial of the of fense of slander or libel will very frequently illustrate 356

OFFENSES AGAINST REPUTATION 

357 the evil of gossip. A valid defense to the charge of slander or libel in certain cases is to be able to prove the truthfulness of the statement made affecting the injured party, but it is only in the rarest of cases that this can be shown. 

Every person should exercise judgment and caution in making libelous or slanderous statements, not only for the reason that he is laying himself liable to criminal prosecution as well as damages to the injured party, but for the more humane reason that in some instances at least, an entirely innocent party is injured, and perhaps ruined by the repetition of idle gossip. No good can come from such, and much evil may result. 

Sec. 637. Combination to Falsely Accuse.-If any persons shall conspire or agree among themselves to falsely charge or accuse one of crime so as to extort money or pecuniary advantage, or shall threaten to accuse another of crime for the purpose of extorting money, they shall be punished by fine of not exceeding $2,000.00, and by imprisonment in the county jail, and in certain instances, by imprisonment in the penitentiary for three years.

CHAPTER 69 OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY Sec. 638. Scope of the Chapter.-This division of our criminal law, like the offenses against the person, is one of the largest branches, and one from which a very large per cent of the criminal litigation of the country arises. 

Sec. 639. Arson.-Arson is the offense of wilfully burning a house. A house is any building consisting of as much as four walls and roof. The penalty for arson is confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than twenty years, and if it be one of the state capitol buildings, the imprisonment shall be for life. 

Sec. 640. House Burning.-The burning of one's own house is arson where there may be an insurance policy upon same, and the burning is for the purpose of collecting such policy.

Sec. 641. Attempt at Arson.-Any person who shall attempt to commit the crime of arson without the actual commission of same shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for two years. 

Sec. 642. Wilful Burning.-If any person shall burn or wilfully cause to be burned any other property than a house, such as stacks of corn or hay, or fences, he shall 358

Offenses AGAINST PROPERTY 

359 be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two years, and not more than five years, or by fine of not exceeding $2,000.00. 

Sec. 643. Ships and Bridges.-For the wilful burning of a ship, the party shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to seven years, and by fine of not exceeding $2,000.00. If it be a bridge, the penalty is from two to seven years in the penitentiary and by fine of not exceeding $5,000.00. 

Sec. 644. Burning of the Prairie or Forest.-If anyone wilfully fires, or causes to be set on fire, the prairies or forests, he shall be fined not less than $50.00 nor more than $300.00, and this offense extends not only to setting the forest or prairie of another afire, but it also includes a case where the fire is placed upon one's own land with the knowledge that it will spread to the property of another. 

Sec. 645. Wilfully Burning Grass.-If any person shall wilfully fire or cause to be burned any grass or pasture not his own, with an intent to injure the owner, he shall be punished by imprisonment for not less than one year nor more than three years. 

Sec. 646. Sinking a Vessel.—If any person shall scuttle, or in any manner wilfully and intentionally sink a vessel, the property of another, he may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years, or by fine of not less than $2,000.00; and if in the sinking of said vessel life is lost, then the offense is murder. 

Sec. 647. Telegraph or Telephone Line.—If any person shall destroy any telegraph or telephone line or obstruct the passage of messages thereon, he shall be pun-

ished by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years, or by fine of from $100.00 to $2,000.00. 

Sec. 648. Placing Obstructions on Railway Tracks. 

If any person shall wilfully place any obstruction upon any railway track so as to endanger life, he shall be punished by imprisonment in the penitentiary from two to seven years, and if life is lost, then the offense is murder. 

Sec. 649. Killing Animals to Injure Owner.-If any person shall wantonly, wilfully or maliciously kill any animal for the purpose of injuring the owner, he shall be fined not less than $10.00 nor more than $200.00, and the fine thus imposed will not prevent the owner from bringing a civil action to recover the value of the property.

Sec. 650. Cruelty to Animals.-If any person shall overwork, or cruelly or unmercifully beat or starve any domesticated animal or bird, he shall be punished by fine of not exceeding $200.00.

Sec. 651. Using Animal Without Consent of the 

Owner. If any person shall take, without the consent of the owner, possession of any horse or other animal and use the same, he shall be subject to fine of not less than $10.00 nor more than $100.00.

Sec. 652. Removing Buoy.-If any person shall wil fully or maliciously remove or cause to be moved any buoy or floating mark or object in any port or place where vessels enter, and same be done to deceive so that injury may result therefrom, the person thus offending shall be punished by imprisonment of not less than two nor more than five years, and by fine of not exceeding $2,000.00, and if death should result from such act, then the person

OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY 

361 shall be deemed guilty of murder and prosecuted accordingly. 

Sec. 653. Robbing Orchards.—Any person who shall enter upon the premises of another and take fruit, melons or any such products, without the consent of the owner, is subject to a fine of not exceeding $100.00. 

Sec. 654. Wilfully Destroying Crops or Fruit.—Any person who shall wilfully injure or destroy any growing crop, as fruit, corn or cotton, or shall injure any real or personal property, where the act so committed has not been included in some other provision of the law, shall be subject to a fine of not exceeding $1,000.00. If the value of the property destroyed be less than $50.00, then the fine shall not be more than $200.00. 

Sec. 655. Johnson Grass.-It is unlawful to sow, give away, or sell oats or other grain containing Johnson grass, or otherwise to bring the same knowingly and wilfully upon the premises of another, and penalty for so doing is by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $1,000.00. 

Sec. 656. Injuring Baggage.—If any carrier, or other person handling trunks or baggage of any description, shall wantonly, carelessly, or negligently destroy, or severely injure the same by reason of such carelessness or wanton handling, he shall be subject to fine of not exceeding $100.00. 

Sec. 657. Throwing Stones at Railway Train.—If any person shall throw a stone or missile, or fire a gun at, against, or into any train, boat, depot, building, residence, schoolhouse, church, or other building, public or private, he shall be subject to a fine of not less than $5.00 nor more than $1,000.00, or to ten days to two years impris-

onment; and if, as a result of such act, life is lost, the offense may be that of murder. 

Sec. 658. Injury to a Fence.-If any one shall wantonly and wilfully break down or destroy the fence of another, he may be punished by imprisonment of not less than one year nor more than five years; and if any two parties have a partition fence by mutual consent, neither shall withdraw such fence from the other without giving six months notice in writing. It is also unlawful to dog or shoot stock within the enclosure of a fence that is less than five feet in height or which has openings sufficiently large to allow the passage of animals through same.

Sec. 659. Hunting and Fishing.-Hunting or fishing on the enclosed land of another without consent is punishable by fine of not less than $10.00 nor more than $200.00. 

Sec. 660. Injuring or Altering Record.-If any person shall wilfully or maliciously change or alter any record, he shall be punished by fine of not more than $5,000.00, or by imprisonment of not less than one year nor more than five years. 

Sec. 661. Malicious Mischief.-There are a great many misdemeanors and small petty offenses grouped under the general term of malicious mischief, such as herding stock near a private residence, gathering pecans from the land of another, and many offenses that will not be mentioned here, not being of very general character or of frequent occurrence.

Sec. 662. Contagious Diseases Among Animals.-It is unlawful to keep any horse affected with the glanders

OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY 

363 or any infectious disease. It is likewise unlawful to permit any animal with a contagious disease to run at large, or to sell or ship the same. The violation of this section is punishable by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $200.00. 

Sec. 663. Destroying Timber.-If any person shall wilfully cut down or destroy any tree or any growing timber upon the land of another, he may be fined not less than $10.00 nor more than $5,000.00. 

Sec. 664. Burglary.—Burglary consists in entering a house at night by force, threats or fraud. If entry be in the daytime with the intent to commit the offense of theft or some other felony, it is likewise burglary. The punishment shall be by confinement in the penitentiary not less than two nor more than twelve years. 

Sec. 665. Robbery.-Any person who shall by force or threats rob another and take from him his money or other property of value, may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for life, or for a term of not less than five years; and if the robbery be committed by means of deadly weapons, then the punishment then the punishment may be by death. 

Sec. 666. Theft.-A theft consists in taking from the possession of another anything of value with the intent to appropriate it to one's own use and benefit. If the value of the stolen article be of $50.00 or more, then the punishment shall be by confinement in the penitentiary from two to ten years. If it be Petty Theft, that is, if the property stolen be of less value than $50.00, then the punishment shall be by imprisonment in the county jail not exceeding two years, or by fine not exceeding $500.00. 

If the value of the property is less than $5.00, the fine shall not exceed $200.00.

If any person shall be guilty of taking from the immediate possession of the person any property of whatever value, he may be punished by confinement in the penitentiary not less than two years, nor more than seven years. 

Sec. 667. Theft of an Animal.-He who steals an animal shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary; if the animal be a horse, for not less than two nor more than ten years. If the animal be a cow, sheep, hog or goat, then the punishment shall be from two to ten years, and this is true even though the value of the stolen animal may be less than $50.00.

Sec. 668. Bill of Sale to Animal.-Butchers and dro vers should provide themselves with a bill of sale to any animal or animals in their possession, and having an animal in their possession without such bill of sale, will subject them to a fine.

Sec. 669. Illegal Marking and Branding.-If any person shall mark or brand any animal not his own, or alter the mark or brand of such animal, he may be punished in the same manner as if for theft of such animal. 

Sec. 670. False Pedigree.-If any person in the sale of a blooded or highly bred animal shall furnish the purchaser with a false statement of the pedigree of such animal, he shall be punished by fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $500.00, or by imprisonment not exceeding the term of six months. 

Sec. 671. Estrays. If any person shall take up, sell, dispose of, or use any animal that is estray, or shall keep the same within his possession without giving public notice and having the same estrayed, according to law, he shall be subject to a fine of $250.00.

OFFENSES AGAINST PROPERTY 

365 

Sec. 672. Trade-Marks.-If any person shall coun terfeit or use the trade-mark of another, or refill any bottle, box or other receptacle whereon the trade-mark of another has been affixed, so as to deceive the purchaser, or to get the benefit of the other's trade-mark, he shall be fined not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00. 

Sec. 673. Embezzlement.-Embezzlement consists in taking and appropriating money held in trust by any party. For example, if the county treasurer should appropriate the county funds to private uses and deprive the county of same, he would be guilty of embezzlement. 

Sec. 674. Swindling.-Swindling is the acquiring of valuable rights or property by false, fraudulent and deceitful representations with the intent of obtaining and appropriating the same to one's own use. The crime is punishable in the same manner as theft. The giving of a check knowing that you have no funds in the bank may be swindling. 

Sec. 675. Disposing of Mortgaged Property.-If any person shall sell or move or cause to be moved any mortgaged property out of the state for the purpose of defrauding the mortgagee, or placing the security beyond his reach, he shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than five years. 

Sec. 676. Bringing Stolen Property Into the State.— 

If any person shall bring into the State of Texas any property secured elsewhere by theft, robbery or embezzlement, he shall be prosecuted for same and receive the same punishment as if the crime had been actually committed within the limits of this state.

CHAPTER 70 MISCELLANEOUS OFFENSES 

Sec. 677. Conspiracy.-Any agreement between two or more people to commit the crime of murder, robbery, arson, burglary, rape, or some other felony, or the entering into such agreement or conspiracy, even though not effected, is punishable by confinement in the penitentiary from two to five years, and if the conspiracy be to commit murder, from two to ten years. If the conspiracy in the latter case be carried into effect, it will be murder in the first degree. 

Sec. 678. Threats.-Any person who shall seriously threaten to take the life of another, or to do him serious bodily injury, shall be punished by fine of not less than $100.00 nor more than $2,000.00, and by confinement in the county jail for not less than one year. 

Sec. 679. Seduction.-If any person shall by promise of marriage induce an unmarried female under the age of twenty-five to depart from the life of virtue and chastity, he shall be punished by confinement in the penitentiary for not less than two nor more than ten years; provided, that if the defendant shall marry or in good faith offer to marry the prosecutrix before the trial of the case, the prosecution shall abate. 366

MISCELLANEOUS OFFENSES 

367 

Sec. 680. Employment of Children.-It is unlawful for any person to employ a child under fifteen years of age to labor about any factory or dangerous machinery or as a messenger in a city of more than 15,000 population, the penalty therefor being not less than $25.00 nor more than $200.00, and by imprisonment for not more than sixty days.

Sec. 681. Fences.-Parties jointly using a dividing or party fence must not cut loose from the other without giving six months notice; penalty for such is a fine of $10.00 to $50.00, each ten days constituting a new and separate offense.

Sec. 682. Anti-Trust Laws.-These laws are designed to protect the people from trusts, monopolies and conspiracies against trade so as to prevent a control and regulation of prices by the trusts at the expense of the public. 

Sec. 683. Trusts.-A trust is a combination of capital, or acts by two or more persons or corporations, to restrict trade or commerce; or to control prices and lessen competition, the object being to control the market and by buying at a depression and selling at a rise produced by the trust itself, to reap an unlawful and illegitimate profit at the expense of the consuming public. 

Sec. 684. Monopoly.-A monopoly is a combination of two or more corporations where they act under the same management or where one corporation acquires the stock of another so as to control its affairs, and thereby lessen the competition.

Sec. 685. Conspiracy in Restraint of Trade.-Where two or more persons or corporations agree to boycott or

refuse to buy from or sell to some person by prearranged agreement, it is a conspiracy in restraint of trade. 

Sec. 686. Penalty.-The punishment for violating the provisions of the Anti-Trust Law may consist, first, of forfeiture of the charter of the corporation, together with its right to do business in the state; and second, it cannot enforce any civil contracts or collect any outstanding debts or obligations incurred under such unlawful agreement; third, every person or corporation violating such Anti-Trust Laws shall forfeit to the state the sum of $50.00 a day for each day that such violation shall continue; fourth, the corporation's officers, agents or employees or the persons consummating such unlawful agreements and conspiracies may be imprisoned in the penitentiary for not less than two years. 

Sec. 687. Teachers' Certificates.-The trustees of the public schools and the department of public instruction of the several counties must not approve a teacher's contract or sign his vouchers until such teacher shall have first presented a valid certificate to teach in the public schools of the state; and any trustee or superintendent violating such provision, shall be subject to fine of not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00. It is also unlawful for any person to traffic in examination questions that are printed and sent out by the State Superintendent to be used in the examination of teachers for certificates, and any one who may be in possession of such lists of questions who shall sell the same or place the same in the hands of any applicant for a certificate before such examination shall have been conducted shall be subject to a fine of not less than $100.00 nor more than $500.00, and/ or imprisoned not less than twenty nor more than sixty days.

MISCELLANEOUS OFFENSES 

369 

Sec. 688. Offenses by Railroads. - The Railroad 

Commission of Texas has the right to inspect the books of any railway company and any company whose agent or employee shall refuse the commission or its representatives the right to inspect such books and accounts shall be subject to a penalty of not less than $125.00 nor more than $500.00 per day for each day that it shall persist in such refusal, and the agent personally making such refusal may be subjected to a fine in this amount. 

Sec. 689. False Billing and Weighing.-If any railway agent shall by false billing, weighing, or other device permit any person to obtain transportation for property at less than the regular rate, or by the same false device require him to pay more than the regular rate, he shall be fined not less than $100 nor more than $1,000.00. 

Sec. 690. Separate Coaches for White and Black.- 

It is the duty of every railway company operating a line of road into Texas to provide separate coaches or compartments for the use of the white and Negro passengers. 

The coaches or compartments shall be equipped alike as to comfort and convenience, and any company that shall refuse or neglect to provide such coaches or compartments shall be fined not less than $100.00 nor more than $1,000.00, and each day that such company shall persist in this neglect or refusal shall be a separate offense, and any passenger, white or black, riding in the wrong coach or apartment, who shall fail to move to the proper coach upon being asked by the conductor to do so, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor and shall be fined not less than $5.00 nor more than $25.00. This provision, however, does not apply to excursion trains run for some special excursion, or to mixed freight trains upon which passengers are permitted to ride.

Sec. 691. Bell and Whistle.-All locomotives must be equipped with a bell and whistle, and such bell or whistle shall be sounded within a distance of eighty rods of every crossing upon any public highway, and shall continue to ring or sound until such highway shall have been crossed, and upon the failure of the engineer to ring such bell or give such signal, he shall be subject to a fine of not less than $5.00 nor more than $100.00. 

Sec. 692. Ticket Agent's Certificate of Authority.— 

Every person acting as ticket agent or who shall sell or offer to sell any railway ticket shall be furnished by the company with a certificate authorizing him to make such sales, and any person selling a ticket without holding such certificate may be fined in any sum not exceeding $500.00. This provision, however, shall not apply to a person holding a ticket where it is not printed thereon that it is a penal offense to sell the same. 

Sec. 693. The Ticket Agent.-The ticket agent must present his certificate of authority to any person demanding to see it.

Sec. 694. The Redemption of Tickets. It is the duty of the railway company to redeem all unused railway tickets or any unused portion thereof presented to any agent of the company within ten days from their expiration, and any company failing or refusing to redeem such ticket at its full value for the unused portion thereof, shall forfeit to the holder of such ticket not less than $100.00 nor more than $500.00. 

Sec. 695. Unlawfully Riding Trains.-It is unlawful for any person to board a train without business thereon, with the intention of obtaining a free ride, and a person

MISCELLANEOUS OFFENSES 

371 so violating, may be fined not less than $5.00 nor more than $100.00. 

Sec. 696. Deposits in Insolvent Banks.-If any officer of a bank, trust company, or banking institution of any character, shall receive deposits or create any debts after knowledge of the bank's insolvency or failing condition, he shall be guilty of a felony, and may be imprisoned in a penitentiary for not less than two nor more than ten years.

Sec. 697. Public Officials Using Passes.-A public official may not use a pass on a railroad or bus line. Passes may be issued to employees, and there are other exceptions as provided by law. 

Sec. 698. Diverting Water or Gas, Etc., from the

Meter. Any person who shall divert the flow of water, gas, or electric current so the same may not be registered by a meter, or who shall by any such device obtain the use of gas, water, or light, without paying therefor, or allowing the same to go through the meter, and be properly registered, shall be subject to a fine of not less than $5.00 nor more than $100.00.

Sec. 699. Weights and Measures.-Flour shall weigh forty-eight pounds to the sack, or one hundred and ninetyeight pounds to the barrel; meal twenty-five pounds to the sack, and the name of the manufacturer and weight must be plainly marked on each bag or barrel or receptacle in which the same is placed. All adulterated or mixed cereals must be so marked diagonally across the package, and any person who shall give short weights or measurement as provided in this section, shall be punished by fine not less than $25.00 nor more than $100.00.

Sec. 700. Nepotism.-Any officer who shall appoint or vote for the appointment of any person related to him within a third degree, or shall vote for any relative where the salary is to be paid by the public, shall be subject to a fine of not less than $100.00 nor more than $1,000.00. 

Sec. 701. Lobbying. It is unlawful for any person interested in any proposed legislation to seek to influence the vote of any member of the Legislature in any manner except by reason and argument, and the fine for lobbying shall be not less than $200.00 nor more than $2,000.00, and imprisonment may be added. 

Sec. 702. Automobiles.-The owner of an automo bile before operating the same must register his name with the tax collector of the county wherein he lives, and shall place the number so registered upon his machine, and he shall not operate the said machine upon any of the public roads or highways at a greater rate of speed than forty-five miles an hour, nor shall he be guilty of racing upon any public road or highway, nor shall he at any time or place operate said machine at a greater rate of speed than is reasonably safe and proper, having regard for the use and traffic of the said street or highway, so as not to endanger the life or limb of other persons thereon. Within cities, the speed shall not exceed twenty miles per hour. The person operating such machine shall also carry a horn, bell or gong that can be heard a distance of three hundred feet, which he shall sound at crossings or upon approach of other persons or vehicles when necessary to apprise them of his approach, and shall also carry a headlight on the said machine between the period of one hour after sunset and one hour before sunrise. Every operator shall procure a driver's license.

MISCELLANEOUS OFFENSES 

373 No one shall drive while intoxicated. When striking another person stop and give aid. Any person operating an automobile or such vehicle, who shall violate any of the foregoing provisions, shall be punished as provided by statute, depending upon character of the offense. 

Sec. 703. Repetition of Offenses.—Where any person shall be convicted of a misdemeanor, and shall thereafter be guilty of a like offense, he may be punished for the second time double the ordinary penalty, and for the third offense, four times the ordinary penalty. 

In felony cases the punishment is likewise enhanced upon repetition of the offense.

PART FIVE CRIMINAL PROCEDURE

CHAPTER 71 CRIMINAL PROCEDURE Sec. 704. Scope of the Chapter.-In the last several chapters we have discussed and set forth the principal divisions of crime, and have enumerated most of the crimes that may be committed against the laws of the state, and have set forth the respective penalties assessed for such violations. We now call your attention to the practice or the machinery of court, by which these penalties are assessed, and the manner in which the criminal law is administered.

Sec. 705. Grand Jury.-The Grand Jury is a body of twelve men selected from the best citizenship of the county. Their names are selected by the jury commissioners at the same time that the petit jurors are chosen for each term of the court. This list of grand jurors is sealed and placed in the hands of the district clerk, who opens the same in sufficient time before the convening of the next term of the court to ascertain the names of the men, and have them summoned by the sheriff to be present and perform such service. 

The first work generally of the District Judge in each term of the court is to impanel this Grand Jury and discuss with them their duties and advise them of the im- 377

portance of the work that they are to undertake. This is called the Court's Charge to the Grand Jury. It is usually oral and partakes of the nature of a good moral lecture in which he points out the laws of the country that are probably being violated, and urges a careful investigation.

Sec. 706. Duty of the Grand Jury. After the jury have been impanelled and sworn and one of their number has been appointed as foreman by the District Judge, they retire to a room provided for their use and there elect a number of bailiffs. The duty of the bailiffs is to summon the witnesses to come before the Grand Jury that it may investigate crime. Any person knowing of any violation of the law may voluntarily go before the Grand Jury and tell what he knows. However, the greater number of witnesses who testify before such jury are those summoned by the bailiffs under the direction of the Grand Jury, and many of them are unwilling witnesses. 

Being brought before the Grand Jury in its secret deliberation, they are sworn to tell the truth concerning whatever violations of the law they may have knowledge of. 

All cases that have been bound over to the Grand Jury by the Justices' Courts, together with any other crime or violation of any character, of which the Grand Jury may be informed, are investigated. If the grand jurors are of the opinion that a party should be prosecuted for the commission of any offense, they find what is called a true bill, or what is more generally known as a bill of indictment. In finding these bills, nine of the twelve jurors must vote in the affirmative, or else no bill will be found. If, however, as many as nine of the jurors vote for the finding of the bill, then the District Attorney, or the County Attorney, one or both of whom being gen-

CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

379 erally in attendance upon the sessions of the Grand Jury, prepares the bill of indictment, setting out the crime of which the accused is supposed to be guilty. This indictment is then signed by the foreman of the Grand Jury and returned into open court, presented to the judge, and is then filed with the district clerk, who dockets the cases for trial and issues a warrant for arrest of the defendant and places same in the hands of the sheriff. The party, when arrested, must either go to jail or give bond. 

Sec. 707. Felonies, Prosecution of.-Felonies can be prosecuted only in the District Court and after true bills have been found; that is to say, an affidavit cannot be filed against a man for burglary, arson, murder, or some other felony for which he could be sent to the penitentiary, and a trial be had upon such affidavit and inforation filed thereon. The most that could be done upon such affidavit would be for the Justice of the Peace to bind the party over for the action of the Grand Jury, which may in turn find a bill; and when this bill is found, it constitutes the formal charge upon which the case will go to trial, and to which the defendant must plead guilty or not guilty. All the indictments that charge capital offenses are selected from the rest, and a special venire is drawn for each trial, if desired either by the state or the defendant. They are usually drawn even if no formal application is made therefor. The case is then set for some particular day of the court, and upon this day the witnesses, and the jurors whose names appear on the venire, are summoned to attend. 

Sec. 708. The Venire.-The venire is a list of names consisting usually of from forty to one hundred who are

selected and drawn by the clerk from a special list prepared by the jury commissioners, which includes the jurors who have been drawn for the several weeks of court, together with such additional names as the needs of the court may indicate. This venire is drawn for the trial in this particular case, and the jury, consisting of twelve men, are supposed to be selected from this venire. 

The whole number are first sworn, and then one by one they are taken in order as their names appear, and questioned. The jurors are asked as to any conscientious scruples about inflicting the death penalty for crime, and if any juror answers that he is opposed to capital punishment and that he would not inflict it, he is excused from further service.

The jurors are also questioned as to their knowledge of the case about to be tried and if they have any fixed opinions. If the answers show a fixed opinion, or if there be bias or prejudice existing in favor of or against the defendant, the party is disqualified. If neither these nor any other disqualifications arise, the juror is deemed qualified.

It is then for the State's Attorney to decide. If he believes the man will make a good juror, he announces to the court that the state will take him. The defendant then, if satisfied with him, will announce his decision, and this juror is sworn in. If, however, either the state or the defendant believes that the juror is not a proper man to sit on the particular case, either may excuse him without giving any reason. This is called a peremptory challenge, and either side may exercise fifteen peremptory challenges in the selection of a jury. After these fifteen challenges have been exhausted, every juror that qualifies himself must be taken, whether he meets the fancy and

CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

381 approval of the state and the defendant or not. In this manner a jury is made up to try a capital case. If the death penalty be waived by the state, or if it be an ordinary felony, then the defendant is not entitled to a special venire, and he will be tried before the jury for the week. 

The jury for the week consists usually of twenty-four to thirty-six men whose names are placed upon a sheet of paper and duplicate lists handed to the state and defendant, and the respective parties strike from this list six names considered by them to be most objectionable, as in a civil case, and then the trial proceeds in the same manner as a capital case.

How the jurors for the week are selected appears elsewhere under head of Jury Commissioners.

CHAPTER 72 TRIAL OF A CAPITAL CASE Sec. 709. Scope of the Chapter.-We believe the stu dent or the reader will better comprehend the procedure if given a concrete example wherein the several steps in a criminal case are enacted. Therefore, without taking up each of the different sections of the code of criminal procedure, we will give an example that will tend to illustrate the several sections. 

Sec. 710. Statement of the Case.-In the western por tion of one of the East Texas counties, there lived the defendant whom we will use for making the first illustration, namely, W. A. (Bill) Anderson. There also lived in the same community a man by the name of Jim 

Oliver and his brother-in-law, F. T. (Bud) Watkins.

Anderson and Oliver had not been very friendly for several years, but were on speaking terms, and would occasionally visit. However, the last of May, 1910, Oliver was seen by Anderson with a pistol, the result of which was that the former was indicted by the grand jury for unlawfully carrying arms. About the time of the finding of this bill of indictment, Anderson, while under the influence of whiskey, went to the home of Jim 

Oliver. He called to Jim Oliver, "Run and hide; Bill 382

Anderson is coming." Anderson had with him a case of whiskey and a Winchester rifle. He entered into some conversation with Oliver with reference to the finding of the true bill, and during such conversation, set his gun down by the fence near which they were talking. 

Oliver had come out of the field, with a hoe in his hand. 

Anderson, being intoxicated, and naturally feeling more than usual of the old grudge for Oliver, began to abuse him and called him a liar and reached for his Winchester. At this instant, Oliver struck him on the head with his hoe, knocking him back from the Winchester. Oliver himself seized the Winchester and pointed it in Anderson's face, who begged for his life. Oliver then handed the Winchester to his son, a boy of thirteen years, who extracted the shells and brought a pan of water and washed the blood from Anderson's face. Anderson took his empty gun and whiskey and walked away. After walking several steps, he turned and looked at Bud Watkins, who had just arrived on the scene, and cursed both him and Oliver, and told them that he would stay on their trail until he killed them. During the same day, late in the afternoon, some one came near Oliver's house and shot a number of times, killing Oliver's dog, and cursing and swearing very loudly. The voice was believed to be that of Bill Anderson.

For this trouble, Anderson was arrested and was to be tried before the County Court on July 5th, the same day on which Oliver's pistol case was to be tried. Early in the morning of the 5th of July, Oliver and his witnesses went to the county seat, the city of Marshall, to be present at court. Anderson did not go. The case did not come up for trial, so Oliver and his brother-in-law, Bud Watkins, riding in a wagon returned home, and

while passing through Cypress Bottom, on what is known as the "Rockway"-an elevated road-they were both shot to death.

R. A. Sedgwick was riding horseback at a short distance in the rear of the wagon, and saw Bill Anderson at the place from whence the shooting and smoke came. 

W. A. Anderson surrendered himself the next morning to the officers, and the Justice of the Peace, believing that there were some facts of self-defense in the case, granted him bail.

Sec. 711. Bill of Indictment.-Upon the convening of the grand jury, however, a bill of indictment was found against Anderson, as follows: 

"In the Name and by the Authority of the State of Texas: 

The Grand Jury, for the County of Harrison, State aforesaid, duly organized as such at the September term, A. D. 1910, of the District Court for said county, upon their oaths in said court present that W. A. Anderson, on or about the 5th day of July, A. D. One Thousand

Nine Hundred and Ten, and anterior to the presentment of this indictment, in the County of Harrison, State of Texas, did then and there unlawfully and with his malice aforethought, kill and murder F. T. Watkins by then and there shooting him, the said F. T. Watkins, with a gun against the peace and dignity of the state. (Signed)

N. A. GREEN, 

Foreman of the Grand Jury."

Sec. 712. Capias or Warrant for Arrest.-After the finding of this bill of indictment, a capias for his arrest was issued and placed in the hands of the sheriff, which was as follows:

TRIAL OF A CAPITAL CASE 

385 

The State of Texas: 

To Any Sheriff of the State of Texas, Greeting: 

You are hereby commanded to arrest W. A. Anderson, who is charged by indictment pending in the District Court of Harrison County, Texas, with the murder of F. T. Watkins, and that you have him before said

District Court at the courthouse of said county in the city of Marshall forthwith, then and there to answer said indictment. 

Herein Fail Not, But have you then and there this writ with your return thereon, showing how you have executed the same. 

Witness my hand with seal of office on this the 10th day of September, A. D. 1910. (Signed)

C. F. ARCHER, 

Clerk of the District Court, of Harrison County, Texas.

Sec. 713. Habeas Corpus.-Under this warrant and bill of indictment, the sheriff arrested W. A. Anderson and placed him in the county jail. Desiring to obtain bail, he applied to the District Court for a Writ of Habeas Corpus. This was an application by which he might be brought before the judge and there show that he was entitled to bail. Upon the hearing of this application, the court listened to the testimony of Mrs. Oliver, her son, Barney Oliver, Mr. Sedgwick, and some other witnesses, as to the details and nature of the killing. Anderson in his defense offered witnesses to show that there had been numerous threats made against his life by Oliver and Watkins, and that at the time he killed them, they were about to kill him. After hearing the testimony,

the court withheld its decision for some time, but finally allowed Anderson bail, which was readily made. 

Sec. 714. The Trial.-A venire was now ordered, who were duly summoned, and the state and the defendant each prepared a list of witnesses and had the clerk issue process to bring them to court. This process consisted of a written order to the sheriff directing him to summon each of the witnesses. The manner of the summons was had by the sheriff calling upon each witness, and reading the subpoena to him, and notifying him that he was commanded to be present on the day set for trial. 

In the same way the list of names constituting the venire were also summoned,

On the day set the state appeared and announced ready for trial. The defendant also announced ready for trial. Thereupon the selection of the jury was the first work of the court. The names on the list were each called and a number disqualified on the ground of scruples concerning capital punishment, others on account of having an opinion in the case. Finally twelve men were selected who were in every way qualified to try the case, and the trial proceeded. The examination of these men on the venire was conducted in the same manner as set out in 

Section 708. 

Sec. 715. The Pleading.-When the jury had been duly impaneled and sworn, the District Attorney read the bill of indictment to them, and to the defendant, Bill Anderson, and asked him if he pleaded guilty or not guilty. He pleaded "not guilty."

Sec. 716. Taking of the Testimony.-The state placed upon the witness stand Mrs. Oliver, the widow of Jim 

Oliver and the sister of Bud Watkins. She testified to

the occurrence at her house, and the disturbance that night; to the consequent threat made by the defendant, Anderson; to her husband and brother going to town on that particular day; and about their taking their guns with them. She said that about five o'clock in the afternoon of that day, she heard a great number of gun shots about two miles from her house in the direction of Marshall, somewhere near Cypress. That she soon afterwards learned that her husband had been shot, and she ran to the scene of the homicide. She found the bodies of her husband and brother lying in the bed of the wagon. 

Oliver's gun was lying beneath him under a cover of some kind; Watkins' gun was lying across him, grasped in his right hand. Their bodies were badly shot with many holes from what appeared to be buckshot. The wounds were in the left side of the face, shoulder, and back of the head. 

The state also offered Barney Oliver, thirteen years of age, who testified to substantially the same facts detailed by his mother, and then offered R. A. Sedgwick, who testified that he had been summoned as a witness to go to town on the date of the homicide and was returning home, riding horseback, and was about thirty-five steps behind the wagon in which Watkins and Oliver were riding at the time they were killed; that the road was straight and free from obstructions; that the woods extended up on both sides of the road; that Watkins had his gun across his lap with the stock under his left shoulder, and the muzzle pointed toward his toes; that he could not see Oliver's gun. That very suddenly he heard a gunshot which was followed by a fusillade, a great number of shots, he could not say how many, probably six or seven; that the shooting was very rapid; that the

horses started to run, and he saw both Oliver and Watkins fall backwards in the wagon; that the shooting came from the head of a pine tree which was the top of the pine that had recently been cut down for lumber and had fallen near and partially around a large oak; that he saw Anderson standing in this top by the side of the big oak, and that he could only see his head on account of the brush about his waistline. Anderson had his gun in his hand and when the witness rode up, Anderson squatted down in the brush; that the witness spurred his horse and rode away as fast as he could.

Dr. Aiken, the physician summoned, testified that the wounds in the heads and bodies of the dead men entered entirely from the left side; that Watkins had one shot that grazed the skin on the front of his body for some two or three inches, but did not penetrate; that another shot passed through his nose just above the lip, passing squarely through both nostrils; that they were large shot, what are ordinarily known as buckshot.

Luke Coleman and Ellis Jackson, the deputy sheriff and the constable, who went to the scene of the homicide on the evening of the killing, testified substantially that the pine top and oak were about twenty feet from the big road, and that by the side of the oak several switches had been cut and whittled upon; that the ground appeared as though some one had spit ambeer on it; that in the tree top, some fifteen feet away from the big oak, there was another place where the straw was mashed down and a limb had been pulled down from the top and a number of cigarette stubs lay upon the ground; that at this point were three empty shells from a shotgun recently fired; that at the foot of the large oak still standing, there were three other shells recently fired. These appeared to be

new and of a different make. At the foot of the pine top, some forty feet away about parallel with the road, there was another shell found. The heads and bodies of Oliver and Watkins had a great many shot holes in them. The 

State closed.

The defendant then offered his brother, Mason Anderson, his nephew, Dock Anderson, Jim Wilkins, Williard Wilkins, D. C. Crawford, Dooly Crawford, John 

Riley, Ike Clark, Fred Ruggles, and a great many other witnesses, all of whom testified to threats that had been made by Jim Oliver or Bud Watkins against the life of 

Bill Anderson. Witnesses said that they tried to make peace between Oliver and Anderson, and that Oliver and Watkins said they did not want Anderson's friendship, but were going to kill him, and wanted him to know it. 

Numerous other witnesses testified to the bad reputation of Jim Oliver, as to his fighting qualities. The defendant, Bill Anderson, testified for himself that he saw Oliver and Watkins pass down the road going towards Marshall early that morning; that he took his Winchester and went over to Craver's Mill, which was across Cypress towards Marshall, along the same road that Oliver and Watkins had traveled. That he returned home and in the afternoon decided he would go back to the mill, and on this occasion took an automatic shotgun loaded with buckshot, and an extra supply of shells in his pockets, and also a Winchester rifle in the magazine of which he carried a number of cartridges; that while on the 

Rockway, a short distance from the bridge, he saw Oliver and Watkins coming a distance perhaps of two hundred or three hundred yards; that he walked along towards them until they approached within fifty or sixty yards; that he then decided he would not pass them, as

he was afraid of them, but left the road and stood by the big oak in question and sat his Winchester down by the side of the oak and held his shotgun in his hand and stood there until Watkins and Oliver were even with him, and at that time, both of them raised their guns and pointed them at him. He shot first and killed them; that he went back then to the mill and undertook to 'phone the sheriff of what he had done; that while there he said he did tell 

Elmo Bass that he "had two bucks in the road down by 

Cypress." That he then went home, and on the following morning, surrendered himself to the Justice of the 

Peace, W. F. Crawford.

Sec. 717. Argument.-After the conclusion of the testimony, the attorneys made their several arguments to the jury. In these arguments, the state's attorney made the first speech, and the defendant replied. 

If there had been several attorneys on each side, then the state would have made another speech and the defense would have replied and so on with the state making the closing argument.

Before the argument, however, the court read to the jury his charge. The portion that related to murder we copy below, and also an abbreviated portion of the rest of the charge, which was as follows:

Sec. 718. Charge of the Court: 

THE STATE OF TEXAS 

VS. 

W. A. ANDERSON. 

Gentlemen of the Jury:

In the District Court of 

Harrison County, Texas.

The defendant, W. A. Anderson, is on trial before you, charged by indictment with the offense of murder, that is, the killing and murdering of F. T. Watkins; to this charge, the defendant pleads "not guilty."

You are the exclusive judges of the facts and circumstances in evidence, and of the weight to be given to them, and to the testimony of the witnesses, and of the credibility of the witnesses; but as to the law, you must be governed by the written instructions given you in charge of the court. 1. 

Murder is the unlawful killing of a reasonable creature in being in this state, by a person of sound memory and discretion with malice aforethought, either express or implied.

Murder is distinguishable from every other species of homicide by the absence of the circumstances which reduce the offense to negligent homicide, or manslaughter, or which excuse or justify the homicide. 2. 

Malice is a condition of the mind which shows a heart regardless of social duty and fatally bent on mischief, the existence of which is inferred from the acts done or words spoken. 

Malice in its legal sense means the intentional doing of a wrongful act towards another without legal justification or excuse. 

The legal signification of the term, "malice aforethought," extends beyond its popular signification. Its signification is more comprehensive than malevolent enmity, illwill or revenge; it includes all states of the mind under which the killing of a person takes place without any cause which in law will justify, excuse or extenuate the homicide. 

Malice is of two kinds, express and implied. 3. 

Express Malice 

Express malice is where one with a sedate and deliberate mind and formed design unlawfully kills another; which formed design is evidenced by external circumstances discovering that inward grudges and concerted schemes to do him some serious bodily injury, or other circumstances showing such sedate and deliberate mind and formed design unlawfully to kill, or to inflict serious bodily injury, which probably might end in the death of the person upon whom the same was inflicted.

4. 

Implied Malice 

Implied malice is that which the law infers from or imputes to certain acts; thus, when the fact of unlawful killing is established and there are no facts or circumstances in evidence which establish the existence of express malice and none which reduce the offense to manslaughter, and none which excuse or justify the accused. 

Now, if you believe from the evidence beyond a reasonable doubt, that at the time and place stated in the indictment, the defendant, W. A. 

Anderson, did unlawfully, with malice aforethought, shoot F. T.

Watkins with a gun and thereby kill him, and that said gun was a deadly weapon; and that said killing was not in self-defense, as hereinafter explained, and that said killing does not come within the meaning of manslaughter, as explained in this charge, then defendant would be guilty of murder; and if you so believe, you will find the defendant guilty and assess his punishment at death, or by confinement in the penitentiary for life, or for any term of years not less than five. 

If from the evidence you believe beyond a reasonable doubt that defendant is guilty of some grade of culpable homicide, but have a reasonable doubt whether the offense, if any, is murder or manslaughter, you must give him the benefit of the doubt, and if you find him guilty, it could not be of a higher grade of offense than manslaughter. 

A deadly weapon is one which in the manner used is likely to produce death or serious bodily injury. Manslaughter 5. 

Manslaughter is voluntary homicide committed under the immediate influence of sudden passion arising from adequate cause, but neither justified nor excused by law.

By the expression, "under the immediate influence of sudden passion," is meant: 

1st. The provocation must arise at the time of the commission of the offense and that the passion is not the result of a former provocation. 

2nd. The act must be directly caused by the passion arising out of the provocation. It is not enough that the mind is merely agitated by the passion arising from other provocation, or a provocation given by some other person than the party killed.

TRIAL OF A Capital CASE 

393 3rd. The passion indicated is any of the emotions of the mind known as anger, rage, sudden resentment or terror, rendering it incapable of cool reflection. 6. 

Adequate Cause

By the term "adequate cause," is meant such as would commonly produce a degree of anger, rage, sudden resentment or terror in a person of ordinary temper sufficient to render the mind incapable of cool reflection.

Any condition or circumstance which is capable of creating and does create sudden passion, such as anger, rage, sudden resentment or terror, rendering the mind incapable of cool reflection, whether accompanied by bodily pain or not, is adequate cause.

Where there are several causes to arouse passion although no one of them might constitute adequate cause, yet all the causes combined might be sufficient to do so. 

In this case as to whether the homicide was committed under the immediate influence of sudden passion, and as to whether there was adequate cause for such passion, are questions of fact for the decision of this jury. 7. Threats

Where a defendant accused of murder seeks to justify himself on the ground of threats against his own life, he may be permitted to introduce evidence of the threats made, but the same shall not be regarded as affording a justification for the offense unless it be shown that at the time of the homicide, the person or persons killed by some act there done, manifested an intention to execute the threats so made. 

In passing upon the issue of threats, the jury will consider and determine the same from the standpoint of the appearance to defendant and the effect upon his mind at the time of the killing and not in the light of subsequent events, if any. If the facts or circumstances suggested to defendant's mind that he was in danger of death or serious bodily injury, and he so believed, then in law he would not be guilty of the homicide but would be justifiable in the killing.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED Law 

8. 

Self-Defense 

You are further instructed that in addition to his plea of not guilty the defendant in this case interposes the plea of self-defense, and upon this issue, and as the law governing the same, you are now instructed. 

Homicide is permitted by law and subject to no punishment when inflicted for the purpose of preventing the offense of murder, or for the purpose of preventing the infliction of serious bodily injury when the killing takes place under the following circumstances: 

1st. It must reasonably appear by the acts, or the words coupled with the acts of the person killed that it was the purpose and intention of such person or persons to commit the offense of murder or inflict such injury. 

2nd. And the killing must take place while the person killed was in the act of committing said offense or inflicting such injury, or after some act or demonstration done by him showing evidently an intent to commit such offense or inflict injury. 

3rd. And it is not essential that there should be any actual or real danger to the life or person of the party killing if there be an appearance of danger caused by the acts or demonstration of the party killed, or those acting with him, or by words coupled with the acts or demonstrations of such party or parties, which produce in the mind of the defendant, viewed from his standpoint alone, a reasonable expectation or fear of death or some serious bodily injury to himself. 

However, the party whose person is unlawfully assailed is not bound to retreat to avoid the necessity of killing his assailant. 

Applying the foregoing instructions to the facts of this case upon the issue now submitted to you, you are charged that the defendant would be justified in killing the deceased if it is shown to have been done to prevent the deceased from murdering him, or from inflicting serious bodily injury on him, or if it is shown that at the very time of the killing or immediately preceding the killing, the deceased had made, or was in the act of making, some hostile demonstration toward the defendant such as would produce in his mind a reasonable fear or expectation of death or some serious bodily injury. 

Therefore, if you shall believe from the evidence that the defendant at the time of the homicide believed his life was in danger or of suffering some serious bodily injury, then you will acquit him.

TRIAL OF A CAPITAL CASE 

395 The defendant is presumed to be innocent until his guilt is established by legal evidence to the satisfaction of the minds of the jury beyond a reasonable doubt. The burden of this proof is upon the state, and if in case you have a reasonable doubt as to the guilt of the defendant, you will find him not guilty. 9. 

If you find the defendant guilty of murder, you will, in your verdict, assess the proper punishment therefor. 

If you find the defendant guilty of manslaughter, you will so say and assess the proper punishment therefor. 

If you find the defendant not guilty, you will simply so say in your verdict.

Your verdict will be in writing and signed by your foreman. 

W. C. BRADFORD, Judge of the Fourth Judicial District. 

Sec. 719. Verdict. The jury retired to consider the case, and upon the following day returned into court their verdict, as follows: "We, the jury, find the defendant guilty of murder in the first degree, and assess his punishment at confinement in the penitentiary for life. 

W. M. FRANKLIN, Foreman."

After this verdict, a judgment was entered on the minutes of the court decreeing that defendant be imprisoned in the state penitentiary for life. 

Sec. 720. Motion for New Trial.-The defendant's attorney now made a very strong motion for a new trial, setting up numerous alleged errors on the part of the court in giving his charge to the jury, and also in certain matters affecting the testimony.

DAVIDSON'S SIMPLIFIED LAw 

After hearing the motion for a new trial, the court refused the same, whereupon the defendant appealed to the Court of Criminal Appeals for the State of Texas, at Austin. When a defendant is found guilty of murder in the first degree, he is no longer entitled to bail, and so Anderson remained in jail pending his appeal. The case being reached finally on the docket of the Appellate Court at Austin, the same was duly considered and ably presented by the defendant's attorneys, but the court decided that the judgment of the trial court should be “affirmed," by which is meant that the judgment should stand and no new trial should be granted. The decision. was rendered by a divided court, two of the judges favoring the judgment, and the third, Chief Justice Davidson, rendering a dissenting opinion, not as to the merits of the case, but on the ground that the law had not been properly submitted to the jury.¹

At the time of the foregoing trial, the law provided for "Murder in the First Degree" and "Murder in the 

Second Degree." All murder committed upon express malice was in the first degree and was punishable by death or confinement in the penitentiary for life. "Murder in the Second Degree" was that committed upon implied malice and was punishable by confinement. in the penitentiary for any term of years not less than five. The degrees of murder were abolished in 1913 by the Thirty-Third Legislature, leaving it to the jury to fix a high or low penalty according to the heinousness of the crime.

The foregoing charge, under the former law, would have instructed the jury to find whether the murder was 

1A case similar to this one may be found in 138 S. W., p. 721.

TRIAL of a Capital Case 

397 of the "first" or "second" degree. Murder in the first degree and the second degree and manslaughter are all submitted now under the one head of "murder," which simplifies the court's charge and work of the jury in administering the law.

CHAPTER 73 MISDEMEANORS 

Sec. 721. Where and How Tried.-As we have seen one charged with a felony must be indicted by the grand jury and tried in the District Court by a jury of twelve men. Such is not true, however, of a smaller offense, classed as a misdemeanor. It is not necessary that the grand jury should indict, and it is not tried in the District Court, but is tried in the Justice's or County Court before a jury of six men. If the defendant is arrested on charge of some capital offense, or any other felony, he will not be tried until the grand jury convenes and finds a bill of indictment, but a bill of indictment is not necessary for the trial of a misdemeanor. A misdemeanor is tried upon an affidavit filed, or by affidavit and information filed thereon, and if filed in a Justice's Court, the case is called for trial at once. The defendant is entitled to two full days, if he claims it, in which to get ready. 

Sec. 722. Justice's Court; Criminal Jurisdiction.- 

The Justice's Court has jurisdiction over cases where the punishment is by fine only, and where the fine cannot exceed $200.00. 398

MISDEMEANORS 

399 

Sec. 723. County Court; Criminal Jurisdiction.- 

The County Court has original appellate jurisdiction over all misdemeanors except those for official misconduct. The Legislature has provided that in some counties the District Court shall exercise the criminal jurisdiction of the County Court. 

Sec. 724. Complaint in the Justice's Court.-If John

Doe has been guilty of disturbing the peace, fighting in a public place, intoxication in a public place, or some other misdemeanor, any person with knowledge of the fact, or upon belief and information, may go before the Justice of the Peace and make affidavit of that fact. The constable of the precinct generally does this, but other persons may do so, and frequently do where they feel aggrieved at the conduct of the defendant. When a complaint is written out, signed and sworn to, a warrant for the arrest is issued and placed in the hands of the constable, who makes an arrest of the defendant. His case is subject to immediate trial, but he may make bond for his appearance and claim two days in which to get ready. 

He is entitled to a trial before a jury of six men, or he may waive the jury and try it before the court. 

Sec. 725. Complaint in the County Court.-If the complaint be one over which the County Court exercises jurisdiction, it is usually made before the county attorney and then filed with the county clerk. The latter thereupon issues the warrant for the arrest, and places the case upon a criminal docket where it is brought to the attention of the County Judge at the next term of the County Court, which must be convened at least four times a year and for criminal business once each month unless otherwise provided for the particular county. Upon the call-

ing of a case, the state is first called upon to announce ready; then the defendant is called upon for an announcement, and if he is ready, or if the court finds that he has not sufficient excuse for continuance, the case will proceed to trial. A jury of six men will be impaneled unless the defendant waives the jury and tries his case before the court.

CHAPTER 74 TRIAL OF A MISDEMEANOR 

Sec. 726. Disturbing the Peace. In the village of 

Greensboro lived two neighbors, John Doe and Richard

Roe. They were very familiar characters in the community and were well known to the public by reputation and otherwise. During Christmas festivities and after taking a little more eggnog than a man of ordinary prudence would have done, John was a little boisterous and fully realized (in his own estimation) that he was the most important man in the village. In such frame of mind, he recalled the fact that his neighbor, Richard 

Roe, some few months before, had killed his favorite pointer dog, and upon the thought of the injustice that had been done him and upon the recollection of the many good points of the dead dog, he succeeded in working himself into something of a frenzy, and before stopping to consider anything, he found himself at Roe's gate. Roe and family were likewise celebrating the 

Christmas holidays, and several of the neighbors' children were enjoying a family Christmas tree, as it was yet in the forenoon. Without an invitation, and in a very unceremonious manner, Doe proceeded to the midst of the group, and undertook to demolish the Christmas tree, 401

much to the horror of the children, in a manner that seemed to him fully as satisfactory as the manner in which 

Roe had killed his pointer dog, and in the onslaught he proceeded to use profane, loud, boisterous and indecent language. The children and ladies present were frightened and retired to a distance, and trouble between Roe and his neighbor, John Doe, was narrowly averted by the timely arrival of the constable, Bony Aikin, who, seeing the disturbance, proceeded to arrest Mr. Doe without waiting for the issuance of a warrant, and carried him before the Justice of the Peace, the Hon. Stephen F. Perry, where a complaint was filed and the case was announced ready for trial. By this time, John had partially gained control of himself and asked for two days in which to get ready for trial, and offered to make appearance bond to appear in court at that time. 

Sec. 727. The Complaint.-The complaint filed in this case was in the following language: 

"In the Name and by the Authority of the State of Texas: 

I, Richard Roe, do solemnly swear that I have good reasons to believe, and do believe, that John Doe, on or about the 25th day of

December, 1929, and before making and filing of this complaint in the County of Brazoria and State aforesaid, did then and there unlawfully go into and within the private house of Richard Roe and did then and there unlawfully and wilfully use loud and vociferous language and swear and curse and loudly shriek in a manner calculated to disturb the inhabitants of said private house of Richard Roe against the peace and dignity of the state. 

RICHARD ROE, Affiant. 

Sworn To and Subscribed Before Me by Richard Roe, a credible person, on the 26th day of December, 1929.

STEPHEN F. PERRY, Justice of the Peace, Precinct No. 1, 

Brazoria County, Texas."

TRIAL OF A MISDEMEANOR 

403 Upon the foregoing complaint being duly sworn to, the Justice of the Peace issued a warrant for the arrest of the defendant, John Doe. This being placed in the hands of the constable, the said John Doe was duly arrested, but released upon bond. 

Sec. 728. Appearance Bond.-Not desiring to go to jail or remain in the custody of the constable, the defendant, John Doe, prevailed upon two of his neighbors to go on his bond, conditioned that he would appear in court on the day of trial. 

Sec. 729. Trial.-On the 28th day of December, two full days having elapsed, the justice convened his court and called the case of the State of Texas against John 

Doe, whereupon the state announced ready for trial.

There was no prosecuting attorney present, but the Justice of the Peace knew the witnesses and announced that he was ready to hear the case. The defendant likewise announced ready. He had not employed an attorney, but had conceived the idea that it was during the Christmas holidays, and that the court, or jury, would perhaps be more lenient with him, and decided that he would not plead guilty, but would fight the case on the ground that what he did was only in play, and that it was not intended or calculated to disturb the peace. The jury being waived, the defendant, John Doe, agreed to submit his case to the 

Justice of the Peace. 

Sec. 730. The Testimony. - Richard Roe, being called, testified: "That he was at home with his family on Christmas morning, December 25th, when his neighbor, John Doe, came down and conducted himself in such manner as to frighten and disturb the children, and interfered with

their Christmas tree and playthings; that he cursed and swore and used abusive language." 

Tommy Roe, the twelve-year-old son of Richard Roe, being called, testified substantially to the same facts as his father and enumerated some items and toys that were destroyed by Mr. Doe in his onslaught on the Christmas tree. 

Whereupon the state rested its case.

Mr. Roe then took the witness stand and stated that he had no intention of disturbing the peace, and that he did not swear and curse, that he merely talked in a loud tone to have some fun out of the children, and that if he had disturbed them, he was exceedingly sorry for it, but that he did nothing that would, in his judgment, disturb the peace under ordinary circumstances. He admitted, however, upon inquiry from the justice, that he had been drinking somewhat.

After his testimony, no further witnesses being offered, the defendant having offered to apologize, the court assessed a punishment of a fine of $10.00 and the cost of the prosecution, which all told amounted to $27.50.

Sec. 731. Trial in the County Court.-A trial in the 

County Court is similar to the above proceedings, except it is somewhat more formal, and both parties are usually represented by attorneys. The state is always represented by the County Attorney, or his assistant, and the defendant is usually expected to have an attorney. The cases tried in the County Court are frequently those that have been appealed from the Justice's Court. Any defendant who is convicted in the Justice's Court has a right to appeal his case to the County Court, where it is tried "de

TRIAL OF A MISDEMEANOR 

405 novo," that is, just as though it had never been tried before; the witnesses are all summoned and the case is completely tried over. In addition to the cases appealed from the Justice's Court, there is a class of cases a degree higher where heavier fines are laid and jail sentences imposed and over which cases the Justice's Court has no jurisdiction, including such offenses as aggravated assault, carrying pistols, and petty theft, that is, theft of any sum less than $50.00. A trial of one of these offenses can be very easily outlined from the above and foregoing trial of John Doe. Take, for instance, an aggravated assault where a man wilfully struck or assaulted a boy. You there have a case over which the County Court has original jurisdiction, and where the lowest fine is $25.00, and the maximum one $1,000.00, to which a jail sentence may be added. Another difference in this trial from the one had in the Justice's Court is, that the County Judge instructs the jury as to what is the law of the case, which the Justice of the Peace does not do.

CHAPTER 75 PRELIMINARY TRIALS 

Sec. 732. Preliminary Trials. We have seen that felony cases are tried by indictment found by the grand jury, and the trials are conducted in the District Court; that the smaller offenses known as misdemeanors in which no jail penalty can be annexed are tried in the Justice's Court; and that the larger misdemeanors in which a jail penalty may be assessed are tried in the County Court. 

Thus these three courts have jurisdiction of all classes of crime, but there will arise frequently in case of a murder, robbery, or other high crime, a necessity for an immediate arrest and legal action. Under these circumstances, the defendant although guilty perhaps of a felony or even a capital crime, is arrested and brought before a justice for hearing.

The defendant cannot be convicted nor have his case disposed of in this preliminary hearing, but the court will take the testimony and bind him over to the grand jury, by which action it becomes necessary for the defendant to give bond to await the action of the grand jury at the next term of court; and failing to do so, he is committed to jail. In the case of murder, or of capital offense, the Justice of the Peace must always bind the de- 406

PRELIMINARY TRIALS 

407 fendant over with or without bond as the facts in his judgment require, but in the case of ordinary felony, if the justice is of the opinion that there is not sufficient evidence against the defendant to find a bill of indictment, he will discharge him and let him go. This, however, will not prevent the grand jury from investigating the facts when convened, and they may procure additional information and find a bill of indictment; then the party will be arrested and tried before the District Court. 

Sec. 733. Bail or Commitment.-If at this time the justice is of the opinion that the prisoner is guilty of an offense whereby he would be capitally punished when tried by a jury, he may refuse him bail and remand him to jail without bail. If, however, he believes that although the defendant is guilty, yet he will probably not receive the death penalty, it is then the duty of the court to allow bail.

Sec. 734. Bail Bond.-An appearance bond is a written obligation signed by defendant and one or more good and sufficient sureties whereby they obligate themselves to pay to the State of Texas the sum of money called for by the bond as fixed by the Justice of the Peace, to be due and payable to the state in the event the defendant should fail to make his appearance before the court. 

Sec. 735. Sufficiency and Amount of Bond.-Two things usually enter into the court's estimate of the amount of bond that a defendant should give: First, the nature of the crime; and second, his ability to give bond. 

Thus, a poor man, without any rich friends, might be allowed bail on a charge of murder in the sum of $2,000.00, while a wealthy man's bond on the same state of facts might be fixed at $10,000.00. The reason for this

is that an unreasonable bond shall not be required, and if the case is bailable, the bond should not be made so high that it would have the effect of depriving the defendant of bail. Thus, a $2,000.00 bond might become much more difficult for a poor man to make than a $10,000.00 bond would be for one better situated from a financial point of view; and in the event that there is likelihood of the defendant desiring to flee and forfeit his bond, a man of much property under these circumstances should be required to give a larger bond. 

A Recognizance is a bail bond made in open court where the sureties need not sign, but where they acknowledge themselves bound for the appearance of the defendant, and the clerk then and there makes a record of their obligation. Ordinarily a bond should be signed by two or more good and sufficient sureties, but one good surety is sufficient. By sufficient surety is meant some person who owns property not mortgaged or encumbered, and in excess of the homestead and exemptions; that is, he must have enough property subject to the execution of the court to pay the amount of the bond. 

Sec. 736. Surrender of Prisoner by Surety.-In event a surety desires to come off the bond of a defendant, he may surrender him personally to the sheriff and notify the sheriff that he will not longer be bound by the terms of the bond, or he may, if he fears the prisoner might escape, cause a warrant to be issued and the defendant rearrested, and then release himself from the bond, under which circumstances, the defendant must make a new bond or be committed to jail. 

Sec. 737. The Accused May Waive an Examining

Trial. If a defendant desires, he may, on being brought

PRELIMINARY TRIALS 

409 before an examining court, waive the examining trial, and agree that the justice may place him under bond subject to the action of the grand jury, but the state's attorney may still, if he thinks necessary, take testimony for the information of the state.
