Sec. 738. Witnesses Under Bond.-If upon the preliminary hearing, the justice deems it necessary to secure their attendance, he may have any one or all of the witnesses placed on bond to appear before the District Court for the hearing of the grand jury.

CHAPTER 76 HABEAS CORPUS Sec. 739. History of the Writ.-The Writ of Habeas 

Corpus is an ancient English writ by which the people were protected against unlawful and unjust or continued imprisonment. Prior to the existence of this writ, any person might be arrested and confined in prison and languish there for years without a trial, or without even being informed as to the nature of his alleged offense. Now, by means of this writ, which is granted by a court of competent jurisdiction, an order is directed to the sheriff, jailor or other person detaining or holding the prisoner in custody, commanding such person to bring the prisoner into court and show lawful reason for detaining him. 

At this hearing, the officer may show that he holds the prisoner under warrant issued against him for a criminal offense. It is the duty of the court under such circumstances to see that a hearing is had at an early date, and if the sheriff or other person has no lawful reason for detaining the prisoner, the court orders him released. 

Sec. 740. Present Use of the Writ.-It is now a very rare case where it is necessary to resort to the writ of habeas corpus on account of unlawful imprisonment. It is more frequently used at this date as a means of obtain- 410

HABEAS CORPUS 

411 1 ing bail, in which case it is in the nature of an appeal from the action of the Justice of the Peace in refusing bail. For instance, if John Smith has been charged with murder before Justice Perry and refused bail, he may then apply to Judge Steadman, Judge of the Judicial 

District, for a writ of habeas corpus. A writ is granted and Smith is then brought before the District Judge, where the state is required to give testimony on the charge of murder just as it did in the examining trial before 

Justice Perry, and if Judge Steadman is of the opinion that John Smith, although guilty, will probably not be capitally punished, he will allow him bail. If Smith is a poor man, he may introduce testimony showing such fact to aid the court in determining the amount of bond. 

However, if the District Judge is of the opinion that Smith will probably be capitally punished by a jury, he may also refuse him bail, and the defendant may then appeal to the Court of Criminal Appeals of the State of Texas.

In hearings upon the writ of habeas corpus where a prisoner is using the writ to obtain bail, the County or District Attorney will appear for the state, and the prisoner employs an attorney to prepare his application for the writ and conduct the hearing, who is usually the same lawyer that defends him on final trial where he is finally acquitted or convicted of the offense.

CHAPTER 77 CRIMINAL COURTS Sec. 741. Administration and Enforcement.-The administration and enforcement of criminal law is similar to the civil practice so far as the clerical or ministerial work is concerned, but execution may issue not only against the property, but against the person as well. 

The defendant may in criminal cases be imprisoned and even put to death. The Justice's Court has criminal jurisdiction of petty cases and misdemeanors in which the penalty is by fine only, and which fine shall not exceed $200.00. 

Sec. 742. Police-Justice Court.-In most cities, there is what is ordinarily designated a Recorder's Court, in which small offenses are tried. This court usually has the same jurisdiction within the territorial limits of the city, as that of the Justice of the Peace. Neither of these courts can try any offense where the punishment for same may include a jail sentence, or a fine exceeding $200.00.

Recorder's Courts may, however, by act of the Legislature be given criminal jurisdiction concurrent with the 

County Court. 

Sec. 743. County Court.- The County Court has criminal jurisdiction of all misdemeanors committed in 412

CRIMINAL COURTS 

413 the county concurrently with the Justice's and Police Courts, and in addition thereto, it has jurisdiction of cases where the penalty may be that defendant be imprisoned in the county jail for various terms not exceeding two years.

Sec. 744. The District Court.-The District Court has jurisdiction of all felony cases, that is, cases where the punishment may be by confinement in the penitentiary or by death. The District Court is the highest criminal court of original jurisdiction. 

Charges of murder or high crimes may be brought before the Magistrate or Justice of the Peace for preliminary hearing for bail, but the question of innocence or guilt must be tried before a jury in the District Court and can be tried nowhere else.

Sec. 745. Court of Criminal Appeals.—In all cases where there is a conviction of crime in the County or the District Court, an appeal may be taken from either court directly to the Court of Criminal Appeals at Austin. 

In all criminal cases, this court is a court of last resort; it is the supreme court in criminal matters and its decisions cannot be reviewed by the Supreme Court itself.

In determining cases in the Court of Criminal Appeals, which is composed of three judges, nothing is considered except the record; no testimony of any kind is introduced except the record of that which was originally introduced in the court below. 

In passing upon this record, the court reviews it alone with an idea of determining if the defendant had a fair and impartial trial according to law. Whether the jury decided in a manner in which the court would

have decided, is given but little consideration, the question being: Were there any prejudicial errors committed during the trial?

Sec. 746. Right of Appeal.-The defendant has a right of appeal in all criminal cases wherein he has been convicted in prosecutions in the County or District Court, but the state has no right of appeal. If the defendant is once acquitted, that ends the litigation. 

Sec. 747. Punishment for Crime.-In petty crimes and misdemeanors the defendant is upon conviction fined or given a term in the county jail.

In a felony case, the convict is imprisoned in the state penitentiary or put to death.

In both state and federal courts the sentence may be suspended, and the defendant relieved of going to prison. 

In the federal courts this is in the nature of a probation and may be revoked for bad behavior.

Sec. 748. State Penitentiary.-In Huntsville is located the main penitentiary which is supplemented by a number of farms located in the valleys of the Trinity and Brazos Rivers in Southeast Texas. These farms are large and very fertile. 

Sec. 749. Death Sentence.-When a defendant was condemned to die he was formerly hanged by the sheriff. 

The hanging was usually attended by large crowds of the morbid and curious. At present the death penalty is by electrocution done at Huntsville under the direction of the warden of the penitentiary.

PART SIX LAW OF THE NATION

CHAPTER 78 LAW OF THE NATION 

Constitution of the United States of America.

The Constitution of the United States of America is not only the base but the origin of American Government and jurisprudence. Circumstances under which it came into being should be known to every student. It is necessary to a proper understanding of the value and sacredness of the document. 

A Compact.

As originally written the Constitution was a compact in the nature of an agreement among the several states whereby they entered into one common government subject to the terms of the agreement set forth, which they termed the Constitution. 

Congress Cannot Repeal or Modify.

Since the Constitution is an agreement and a compact among the states it can only be changed by consent of the states and Congress is without power to amend, alter or annul its provisions. 417

Unconstitutional Laws. 

Since Congress cannot amend or annul the Constitution then it follows as a necessary corollary that any law in conflict with the provisions of the Constitution is invalid and without force and effect. 

Sec. 750. History and Formation of the Constitution. -The charter granted a colony by the King of England was in a sense a constitution for the colony. The king bound himself not to violate the charter and the colony could not by its assembly violate the charter. It was its contract and guaranty of self-government. The people guarded these charters with jealous care. 

Sec. 751. Story of the Charter Oak.-The Connecti cut Colony prized its charter granting to it unusual freedom of government, and when the King of England on one occasion revoked this charter and sent his royal officers to take it up the citizens hid it in a great oak, which afterwards bore the name of Charter Oak. The English king did, however, breach or revoke nearly all of the charters and in each case the colony became a royal province subject to the government of the king and his appointed deputy or governor.

Sec. 752. Breach With England.-On the Fourth of 

July, 1776, the Colonies, by common consent, having revolted against England, declared themselves independent, adopted the famous Declaration of Independence and declared themselves self-governing. 

Sec. 753. No General Government.-Almost every one of the Thirteen Colonies had been founded under circumstances peculiar to itself. Oftentimes it had religion of a faith and creed wholly at variance and hostile

LAW OF THE NATION 

419 to that in the next colony. The laws and customs often differed radically.

The first Continental Congress was little more than a convention, having no power to act or to enforce its decrees other than to report back to the several states from whence the delegates or representatives came. No state was compelled to observe the act of Congress. The colonies were jealous of each other, especially the small colonies of the large ones. They were held together only by the presence of a common enemy-the army of Great Britain.

Sec. 754. Articles of Confederation.-The Colonies, having declared their independence of the mother country, were without any organized government of their own, and being conscious of the necessity of concerted action did on the 15th day of November, 1777, at a meeting of delegates from the several states draw up Articles of Confederation and perpetual union between the states. They styled the confederacy as the United States of America. 

This document was finally signed at Philadelphia on the 9th day of July, 1778, in the third year of the independence of America; but ratification by the states was not completed until 1781. It was little more than an alliance between thirteen independent nations, each state or nation reserving to itself the necessary functions of government. There was no president provided for and no means provided whereby the nation could act as a whole in a concerted manner. As Washington described it, "We are one nation today and thirteen tomorrow." 

Sec. 755. Jealousy and Chaos Among the States. 

Washington's View.-On October 7, 1785, less than two years before the Constitution was adopted, Washington

wrote James Warren: "The wheels of government are clogged and we are descending into a vale of confusion and darkness." On the 5th of November, 1786, he wrote to James Madison: "No day was ever darker and more clouded than the present ***. We are fast verging to anarchy and confusion. How melancholy is the reflection!" On December 26, 1786, Washington wrote Henry 

Knox: "I feel, my dear General, infinitely more than I can express to you, the disorders which have arisen in these states. Good God, who could have foreseen or predicted them?" 

Sec. 756. Events Leading to the Constitutional Con vention. In 1781 Pelatiah Webster proposed to Congress then sitting at Philadelphia the calling of a Constitutional Convention to draft a new Constitution. In furtherance of his idea, he did, on February 16, 1783, issue a pamphlet entitled "A dissertation on the political union and Constitution of the Thirteen States of North 

America." With a trenchant pen he reviewed the chaotic conditions. He proposed the division of the Federal 

Government into three independent coordinated departments-the Legislative, Executive and Judicial. This man was a pioneer, but Congress failed to act. There were, however, three young men who studied his proposal and in whose minds it bore fruit-Alexander Hamilton of New York, James Madison of Virginia and 

Charles Pinckney of South Carolina. 

Washington Again to the Rescue.-On March 28, 1785, joint commissioners of Virginia and Maryland met at Washington's home at Mt. Vernon to arrange an agreement between the two states touching the navigation of the Potomac River and Chesapeake Bay. The commis-

LAW OF THE NATION 

421 sioners completed this problem and took up the discussion of other commercial problems affecting the states. 

The Legislature of Maryland in ratifying this agreement sent out a proposal suggesting that commissioners representing all the states should meet for enacting trade regulations. Virginia joined in the invitation and a Trade 

Convention met in Annapolis in September, 1786. 

Sec. 757. Call for Constitutional Convention.-As a result of the Annapolis conference a convention was suggested as necessary to render the Constitution of the Federal Government adequate to the exigencies of the Union. 

This action was approved by the expiring Congress on 

February 21, 1787. To this conference all the states with the exception of Rhode Island appointed delegates. 

Sec. 758. The Constitutional Convention. The dele gates appointed to this convention were men of learning and of unquestioned probity and patriotism. Benjamin 

Franklin of Pennsylvania was the oldest, 81 years. The average age was 43 years. Many of the delegates had rendered distinguished service in the War of the Revolution. On the 14th of May, the day set for the convening of the convention, only two states were present, Virginia and Pennsylvania, Washington being among the 

Virginia delegation. On the 25th of May seven (7) states were present and the convention was organized. 

Sec. 759. Franklin Proposes Prayer.-After almost a month of effort nothing had been accomplished. Franklin arose and addressing Washington, the president of the convention, said, among other things: "Methinks it a melancholy proof of the imperfection of human understanding. We seem to feel our own want of political wis-

dom. We have gone back to ancient history for models of government. We have viewed modern states all around 

Europe, but find none of their constitutions suitable to our circumstances **. Sir, we have not hitherto once thought of humbly applying to the Father of Light to illuminate our understanding. I therefore beg leave to move that henceforth prayers imploring the assistance of heaven in these deliberations be held in this assembly every morning before we proceed to business." While the motion did not carry, it seemed to powerfully influence the convention and real progress was soon made in the convention.

Sec. 760. Compromises.-There were of necessity a number of compromises made. For instance, the small state was afraid of being absorbed by the large ones. To grant adequate protection to the small state it was provided that it should have equal representation in the Senate with the large and populous state. To prevent the small state from exercising undue power it was provided that in the lower house the state should be represented in proportion to population.

Sec. 761. Conclusion of Its Labors.-At the close of the convention the records were sealed and committed to Washington with the injunction that he retain the papers and the journal subject to the orders of Congress, if it were ever formed under the Constitution then drafted. 

Sec. 762. Adoption by the States.-It was provided that the Constitution should not go into effect until nine (9) of the states had assented thereto. The opposition to the adoption of the Constitution in several of the colonies was very severe and powerful. Especially was this true in Virginia. Patrick Henry and others assailed the docu-

LAW OF THE NATION 

423 ment as a surrender of liberty and portrayed dangerous conditions as flowing from its adoption. The most memorable and learned discussion perhaps ever conducted on the American continent was the debate at Richmond, Virginia, on the adoption by the State of Virginia of the Constitution of the United States. The influence of Washington, John Marshall and many others finally prevailed over Patrick Henry and his associates by a vote of 79 to 89.

Sec. 763. Preamble.-Preliminary to the drafting of the Constitution there were set forth its object and pur poses in six items called the Preamble, as follows: 

I. To form a more perfect Union 

2. To establish justice 

3. To insure domestic tranquility. 

4. To provide for common defense 

5. To promote the general welfare 

6. To secure the blessings of liberty to ourselves and posterity.

This document was written in 1787; 151 years have now transpired. It is as applicable today as it was then. 

William Gladstone of England pronounced the Constitution of the United States the most wonderful work ever struck off at a given time by the brain and purpose of man.

The Constitution of the United States deals with elementary and basic principles of government and does not overlap into the realms of legislation. Wherever the basic law attempts such, it is of necessity constantly being amended and is a handicap to the legislative branch of

government.

The Constitution is singularly left free from legislative provisions. The United States Constitution is a brief document, and though not given in this work, a review of its provisions and purposes is recommended to the student.

CHAPTER 79 UNITED STATES COURTS Sec. 764. Supreme Court.-The Constitution of the 

United States of America, Article 3, provides for a Supreme Court and such inferior courts as may be ordained and established by Congress. The Supreme Court at this time consists of nine members, a Chief Justice and eight Associates. Congress has the power to increase or decrease the members.

Sec. 765. Circuit Court of Appeals.-The United 

States is divided into ten circuits, in each of which has been established by Congress a Circuit Court of Appeals. 

The Fifth Circuit, of which Texas is a part, consists of all the Gulf States and the State of Georgia. This court has its headquarters in the city of New Orleans, Louisiana. It holds sessions, however, for the convenience of litigants, at Atlanta, Georgia, Montgomery, Alabama, and Fort Worth, Texas. These Circuit Courts now consist of three or more judges, as Congress may provide for the litigation. Their principal work is, as their name implies, to consider appeals from the District Court. 

Sec. 766. United States District Courts.-Each state is divided into one or more Federal Districts in which are located one or more United States judges. Texas is 425

divided into four districts, the Eastern, the Southern, the Western and the Northern. The Northern District is one of the largest and most populous districts and has three judges who divide the work among themselves. The 

Western District has two judges and one retired judge who at times presides and holds court. The Southern 

District has two judges. The Eastern District has one judge. 

District Judges, like Judges of the Supreme Court and Judges of the Circuit Court of Appeals, are not elected by the people but are appointed by the President and hold office during life or good behavior. Any District Judge upon reaching the age of seventy years, who has seen more than ten years active service, may voluntarily retire and the President will appoint a successor. 

A judge in retirement may still be called into service, however, to do such work as is compatible with his age and health.

Congress has lately passed a bill allowing Judges of the Supreme Court also to retire at the age of seventy. 

It does not necessarily follow because a man is past seventy years that he must retire. A number of our greatest jurists have remained on the bench until they were past eighty years of age. So long as their health holds up their accumulated knowledge and experience enables them oftentimes to pass upon a question more readily than a new man.

Sec. 767. Jurisdiction.- Federal or United States 

Courts are ordinarily careful not to encroach upon the jurisdiction of the State Courts. A State Court, for instance, has original jurisdiction in the enforcement of all laws of the particular state. A United States Court

UNITED STATES COURTS 

427 1 is limited to those offenses and to those questions and subjects covered by the Acts of the Federal Congress. 

Thus suits for personal injuries, suits for land, prosecutions for murder, arson and other crimes primarily belong to the State Courts. If an offense is committed upon federal property, as in a federal building, for instance, then the Federal Court may take jurisdiction and try the case. The United States District Courts take jurisdiction, for instance, of all matters bearing upon the post office and postal service; of all matters dealing with the collection of tariffs, imposts and internal revenue; also with counterfeiting and all other crimes against the United States. In a civil way the greater part of the litigation in the United States Courts is due to diversity of citizenship. If a citizen of Texas, for example, sues a citizen of another state, in the courts of Texas the citizen of the other state may remove the case to the Federal Court for trial, if the amount involved is of the value of $3,000.00 or more.

Sec. 768. Trials in United States Courts.-The con duct of a case in the United States Court is not unlike that in the State Court. A Federal judge is clothed with perhaps more discretion and power in the conduct of the litigation than the judge in the State Court. Fortunately, very few United States judges abuse this power but use it to administer justice with greater order and dispatch. In the Federal Court you stand when the judge enters. You are not ordinarily allowed to whisper or carry on any conversation in the court. You must wear a coat, and will not smoke while in the courtroom. One lawyer at a time only will address the court and he must stand while doing so. The court will not permit him to be interrupted until he has finished. In the United States

Courts the jury are the judges of the facts, just as they are in the State Courts, but the Federal judge is allowed much greater latitude than the State judge in commenting upon these facts to the jury and sometimes aids them. in reaching a verdict in this way. He must, however, tell them that they are the exclusive judges of the facts and that it is their privilege to disagree with him in the event he has made his views known upon the facts of the case. 

Sec. 769. United States Reports.-In most law libraries will be found what is called the United States Reports. These embrace decisions rendered by the United 

States Supreme Court. In the library is also found another set called Federal Reports in which are the decisions by the various Judges of the Circuit Court. The 

Federal Supplement is the name of another book in which United States District Judges may at times furnish opinions upon questions which they deem of special interest. This last one, however, is provided by private publication, usually by the West Publishing Company.

CHAPTER 80  (A TRIAL IN THE FEDERAL COURT) Sec. 770. A Trial in the Federal Court.-On the 26th day of March, 1807, Aaron Burr arrived in Richmond,

Virginia, under the military guard of Major Nicholas Perkins, having been sent therewith by order of General 

Wilkinson, stationed at New Orleans. The United States 

District Attorney, George Hay, and the United States 

Attorney General, Caesar Rodney, immediately moved the commitment of the prisoner to jail on a charge of treason against the United States. Thus began a trial which for more than six months held the entire Nation at attention, tensely awaiting the results. 

Sec. 771. To fully appreciate the moment of the occasion, it will be necessary, briefly, to review the politics of the Nation at that time. Aaron Burr had just retired from the Vice Presidency. He had lately killed Alexander Hamilton in a duel. He had almost defeated 

Thomas Jefferson for the Presidency. The whole weight of the Republican party was now behind Jefferson, and Burr found very few friends. He turned to the West for a new career. 429

The Ohio valley and the States of Tennessee and Kentucky, during the administration of John Adams, felt themselves neglected. There were no highways across the 

Allegheny mountains. The Mississippi river was their natural outlet to the sea. Spain, however, jealous of the rising power of the American Republic, had closed the port of New Orleans to these frontier states. Among them there was an insistent demand for war with Spain. They wanted New Orleans. They wanted the Floridas. The 

Adams administration manifested little interest in their wishes and there was open talk of secession in these western territories. 

Sec. 772. When Jefferson came into the Presidency the West felt that the time had arrived when they would have their war with Spain. A desire for this war was bred into the youth of the West. It was manifested at the Battle of San Jacinto, and Chapultepec, and finally at Santiago and Manila. Jefferson was of a pacific turn and negotiated successfully for the purchase of New Orleans, and not only of New Orleans, but of the entire Louisiana territory. This meant violent opposition in the Northeast, especially in New England. The 

West was now satisfied, but the East took up the cudgel and was about to secede. The New England Senators met at Washington to discuss secession and called upon 

Mr. Merry, the English Ambassador, to consult with him, and secure England's cooperation. The Nation was not closely knit together then, as now. Statesmen and jurists referred to the American Union as "these states." 

Marshall used the term in his opinion. 

Justice Chase had just been impeached by the United States House of Representatives. The Senate, over which

Aaron Burr as Vice President presided, had tried him and acquitted him. But the sentiment of the Government, and of the people, was greatly divided. There was much opposition to the power of the Supreme Court. 

There was talk of impeaching all of the Federalist members, which included John Marshall. Men not skilled in the science of government could see no reason why a few judges should set aside the act of a hundred Congressmen. They overlooked the fact that the Constitution as a basic law was a contract made between the thirteen colonies, under which guarantees were made that neither the President nor Congress had the right to violate it until the states themselves amended the document. 

Politically, Burr was an Ishmaelite. Neither the 

Republicans nor the Federalists took him to their bosom. 

He was a man of charming personality, and was banqueted, wined and dined almost as frequently as any man in American history. The people of Tennessee, under the auspices of Andrew Jackson, had given him three magnificent dinners. When he talked of war with Spain he was talking their language. 

Sec. 773. Venue of the Trial.-Burr went to the home of Harman Blennerhassett, who had built a magnificent estate, with flower gardens and driveways, on

Blennerhassett Island in the Ohio river. This island was a part of Virginia, because West Virginia had not then been severed from the original colony. Hence, venue lay in the State of Virginia and the trial was had at Richmond.

Sec. 774. The First Hearing.-Burr's counsel in cluded many of the great lawyers of Virginia and America, among them being Edmund Randolph, John Wick-

ham, Luther Martin of Maryland, and others. The Government was represented by George Hay, District Attorney, the Attorney General, Caesar Rodney, William Wirt and others. The weight of the administration, and public sentiment were squarely against Burr. "Of his guilt there can be no doubt" were the words of President Jefferson, uttered in advance of the trial. After the introduction of testimony for several days, Justice Marshall rendered his decision in part as follows: "On an application of this kind I certainly should not require that proof which would be necessary to convict a person to be committed on a trial in chief; nor should I even require that which should absolutely convince my own mind of the guilt of the accused; but I ought to require, and I should require, that probable cause be shown;" 

The court refused to commit Burr on a charge of treason, but did commit him on a charge of conspiring against a friendly power, the King of Spain. 

Sec. 775. The Grand Jury.-On the 22nd of May, 

1807, the United States Grand Jury for the District of Virginia was convened and started to investigate the charges against Aaron Burr. The grand jury is without a parallel in American history in the number of brilliant men thereon. It included five men who were either former Governors, or subsequently Governors of Virginia. 

It included several men who had been United States Senators. John Randolph was made foreman. John Randolph was, perhaps, eccentric, but he has been frequently described as the most brilliant man in the State of Virginia. Upon the impaneling of the grand jury the name of United States Senator Giles was called. Burr objected to Senator Giles on the ground that he had expressed an opinion on the floor of the United States Senate to the

effect that Burr was guilty. This was the first time in 

American procedure that a defendant had challenged a grand juror at the impaneling. Marshall ruled that such was the right of the defendant, and Senator Giles was excused. The next grand juror was William Carey 

Nicholas, also a Senator from Virginia, and afterwards a Governor of that state. Burr objected to him for the same reason, and that he was a personal enemy and had expressed his views upon Burr's guilt. He was also excused. Among those selected on the grand jury were Littleton Waller Tazewell and James Pleasants, both afterwards United States Senators; Joseph C. Cabell, joint founder of the University of Virginia with Thomas Jefferson; William Daniel, the father of Judge Daniel, and grandfather of the late Senator Daniel; Colonel James 

Barbour, afterwards Secretary of War under John 

Quincy Adams; John Randolph, and numerous others.

The investigation started and days and weeks were dragging by, but no bill. The district attorney, George Hay, was worried over the situation. And particularly so, since 

Aaron Burr, always fascinating in his manner, was being banqueted, wined and dined by the aristocratic residents of Richmond. He was gaining in popularity and building up behind himself a group of friends who were championing his innocence. The grand jury were waiting for 

General Wilkinson, who was to be the star witness of the Government. Wilkinson delayed coming. The district attorney, impatient at the prominence and favor which were being shown the defendant, now moved to commit him to jail on a charge of treason while the grand jury were sitting. This motion was resisted on the ground that the grand jury had the matter under investigation. 

Marshall ruled:

"The first contention is, that the grand jury being now in session ready to receive an indictment, the attorney for the United States ought to proceed by bill instead of applying to the court, since the only purpose of a commitment is to bring the accused before a grand jury. 

This statement contains an intrinsic error which destroys its operation. 

The commitment is not made for the sole purpose of bringing the accused before a grand jury; it is made for the purpose of subjecting him personally to the judgment of the law, and the grand jury is only the first step towards that judgment."

Evidence was necessary in support of the motion. 

Thus two parallel bodies were now investigating: The grand jury under John Randolph as foreman, and the court under United States Chief Justice John Marshall.

The same witnesses were testifying before each body; one open and the other secret, a situation perhaps without parallel.

Sec. 776. Arrival of General Wilkinson.-After this inquest had proceeded a great number of days and many tilts had occurred between counsel, General Wilkinson arrived. The general was stout in his build and bumptious in his manner and very extravagant in his use of superlatives in his English. He declared that future generations would write his name in history and do his name justice. He did not know that sometime the files of the viceroy of Mexico, then a province of Spain, would disclose that he was known as Code No. 13, and that he had presented a bill to the said viceroy for $86,000.00 in cash in connection with the arrest of Aaron Burr. General Wilkinson was carried before the grand jury. He made a bad impression. John Randolph said: "Wilkinson is the most finished scoundrel that ever lived * he is the only man I ever saw who was, from the bark to the very core, a villain." Although General Wilkin-

00 1 1 son had come as a witness, the grand jury proceeded to investigate his conduct, and it was only by a vote of seven to nine that he escaped indictment. Several days elapsed and the grand jury returned bills against Aaron Burr, Senator Smith, Senator Dayton of Ohio, Blennerhassett and several other persons. The District Attorney now had his inning and the motion to commit was granted and Burr was sent to jail.

Sec. 777. Subpoena Duces Tecum.-The people as a whole were backing the President. The President had said Burr was guilty. And they believed he had the evidence. A letter he had received from General Wilkinson was supposed to contain it. Burr and his counsel now moved the court for a subpoena duces tecum to Thomas Jefferson, President of the United States. This precipitated a bitter, acrimonious debate among counsel. Luther 

Martin of Maryland was particularly caustic in his criticism of the President. He was replied to by William

Wirt of Virginia. Court and counsel took part. This was one of the most momentous parts of the trial and it lasted for days. Finally Justice Marshall ruled in favor of the motion. Different passages of this ruling illustrate, even at that time, the tendencies that were being moulded by the American judiciary. They also indicated between the lines the political pressure that was being brought to bear by public sentiment demanding a conviction. We find the great judge delivering a great opinion in that simple language that the laity even must understand: "Upon immemorial usage then, and upon what is deemed a sound construction of the Constitution and law of the land, the court is of opinion that any person charged with a crime in the courts of the United States has a right, before as well as after indictment, to the process of the court to compel the attendance of his witnesses. *

"This question originally consisted of two parts. It was at first doubted whether a subpoena could issue in any case to the chief magistrate of the Nation; and if it could, whether that subpoena could do more than direct his personal attendance; and if so, whether it could direct him to bring with him a paper which was to constitute the gist of his testimony. "The single reservation alluded to, is the case of the king. Although he may perhaps give testimony, it is said to be incompatible with his dignity to appear under the process of the court. Of the many points of difference which exist between the first magistrate in England and the first magistrate of the United States, in respect of the personal dignity, conferred on them by the Constitution of their respective nations, the court will only select and mention two. It is a principle of the 

English constitution that the king can do no wrong, that no blame can be imputed to him, that he cannot be named in debate. "By the Constitution of the United States, the President as well as every other officer of the government, may be impeached, and may be removed from office on conviction of high crimes and misdemeanors. "By that of the United States, the President is elected from the mass of the people, and on the expiration of the time for which he is elected, returns to the mass of the people again. "How essentially this difference of circumstances must vary the policy of the laws of the two countries in reference to the personal dignity of the executive chief, will be perceived by every person." 

The language of Luther Martin and the issuance of the writ met with bitter resentment from the President.

He sent the papers called for by the subpoena but refused to go himself. Among other arguments advanced against the allowing of this motion was that the President might be summoned in criminal trials from one end of the country to the other, and that his duties would thus be interfered with. Marshall very correctly answered this by saying that this is no reason why the process should not be issued. It may be a very proper reason why the 

President may not attend, but if that reason exists then

1 it is for him to announce it and claim it, and not for the court to refuse the process.

Sec. 778. Treason Defined.-It became important in the progress of the trial to define treason. Under the 

English practice which we follow in America there were ten or fifteen different offenses that were classified as treason. All of this was eliminated by the American Constitution. No other important case of treason had been tried before the American court. Mr. Botts, of counsel, defined treason in a way that was acceptable to the court, which was as follows: "First, it must be proved that there was an actual war. A war consists wholly in acts, and not in intentions. The acts must be in themselves acts of war; and if they be not so intrinsically, words or intentions cannot make them so. In England, when conspiring the death of the king was treason, the quo animo formed the essence of the offense; but, in America, the national convention has confined treason to the act. We cannot have a constructive war within the meaning of the 

Constitution. An intention to levy war is not evidence that a war was levied. Intentions are always mutable and variable; the continuance of guilty intentions is not to be presumed. "Secondly, the war must not only have been levied, but Colonel Burr must be proved to have committed an overt act of treason in that war. A treasonable intention to cooperate is no evidence of an actual cooperation. The acts of others, even if in pursuance of his plan, would be no evidence against him. It might not be necessary that he should be present, perhaps; but he must be, at the time of levying the war, cooperating by acts, or, in the language of the Constitution, be committing overt acts. "Thirdly, the overt act by the accused, as an actual war, must not only be proved, but it must be proved to have been committed within this district. "Fourthly, the overt act must be proved by two witnesses."

At another time in the proceedings, discussing the subject of treason, Mr. Hay, the United States District 

Attorney, said: "In Great Britain there are no less than ten different species of treason; at least that was the number when Blackstone wrote, and it is possible that the number may have been increased since. But in this country, where the principle is established in the Constitution, there are only two descriptions of treason; and the number being fixed in the Constitution itself, can never be increased by the legislature, however important and necessary it should be, in their opinion, that the number should be augmented. By the third section, Article 3 of the 

Constitution of the United States, 'treason against the United States shall consist only in levying war against them, or in adhering to their enemies; giving them aid and comfort.' With respect to the latter description, there is no occasion to say anything, as the offense charged in the indictment is 'levying war against the United States;' but it adds that 'no person shall be convicted of treason, unless on the testimony of two witnesses to the same overt act, or on confession in open court'." 

Sec. 779. The Trial.-On August 3, 1807, Aaron 

Burr was put to trial on the charge of treason. There were present many celebrities from over the Nation. The 

Chief Justice was sitting on the bench along with the 

District Judge. The Attorney General and the District 

Attorney, and the most brilliant lawyers of the American Bar were arrayed for the trial. The first witness was 

General Eaton, who testified at length as to conversations he had had with Burr where he planned a treasonable separation of the Western States from the Union, including the seizure of New Orleans and the conquest of Mexico. This testimony was objected to in that it did not meet the constitutional definition of treason, that treason consisted of an overt act and that no overt act had been shown. The court permitted, however, this testimony to continue until the 20th of August. At that

time counsel for the defense moved to arrest the evidence on the ground that all of the testimony taken and being heard was irrelevant and immaterial until there was an overt act shown by two witnesses under the provi sions of the Constitution. By the Third Section, Article 

3, of the Constitution of the United States, it is provided: "Treason shall consist only in levying war against them, or adhering to their enemies, or giving them aid and comfort." Both sides realized that the fate of Aaron

Burr hung upon the court's action upon this motion. The most powerful display of legal talent and the most resplendent arguments perhaps ever adduced in an American court followed in the next nine days of the trial. The motion was made on the 20th of August, and on the 29th

Mr. Marshall delivered his opinion. The opinion was read with great care and deliberation and occupied a space of three hours. Among other pertinent portions appeared the following: "The question now to be decided has been argued in a manner worthy of its importance, and with an earnestness evincing the strong conviction felt by the counsel on each side that the law is with them. "A degree of eloquence seldom displayed on any occasion has embellished a solidity of argument and a depth of research by which the court has been greatly aided in forming the opinion it is about to deliver. "The testimony adduced on the part of the United States to prove the overt act laid in the indictment having shown, and the attorney for the United States having admitted, that the prisoner was not present when that act, whatever may be its character, was committed, and there being no reason to doubt but that he was at a great distance and in a different state, it is objected to the testimony offered on the part of the United States, to connect him with those who committed the overt act, that such testimony is totally irrelevant and must therefore be rejected.

"1st. That conformably to the Constitution of the United States, no man can be convicted of treason who was not present when the war was levied. "2nd. That if this construction be erroneous, no testimony can be received to charge one man with the overt acts of others until those overt acts, as laid in the indictment, be proved to the satisfaction of the court. "The question which arises on the construction of the Constitution, in every point of view in which it can be contemplated, is of infinite moment to the people of this country and to their government, and requires the most temperate and the most deliberate consideration. "Treason against the United States shall consist only in levying war against them. * "The law of the case being thus far settled; what ought to be the decision of the court on the present motion? Ought the court to sit and hear testimony which cannot affect the prisoner? Or ought the court to arrest that testimony? On this question much has been said: much that may perhaps be ascribed to a misconception of the point really under consideration. The motion has been treated as a motion confessedly made to stop irrelevant testimony; and, in the course of the argument, it has been repeatedly stated, by those who oppose the motion, that irrelevant testimony may and ought to be stopped. That this statement is perfectly correct is one of those fundamental principles in judicial proceedings which is acknowledged by all, and is founded in the absolute necessity of the thing. No person will contend that, in a civil or criminal case, either party is at liberty to introduce what testimony he pleases, legal or illegal, and to consume the whole term in details of facts unconnected with the particular case. Some tribunal then must decide on the admissibility of testimony. The parties cannot constitute this tribunal, for they do not agree. The jury cannot constitute it; for the question is whether they shall hear the testimony or not. Who then but the court can constitute it? It is of necessity the peculiar province of the court to judge of the admissibility of testimony. If the court admit improper or reject proper testimony, it is an error of judgment; but it is an error committed in the direct exercise of their judicial functions. * "That this court dares not usurp power is most true.

"That this court dares not shrink from its duty is not less true. "No man is desirous of placing himself in a disagreeable situation. 

No man is desirous of becoming the peculiar subject of calumny. No man, might he let the bitter cup pass from him without self-reproach, would drain it to the bottom. But if he have no choice in the case, if there be no alternative presented to him but a dereliction of duty or the opprobrium of those who are denominated the world, he merits the contempt as well as the indignation of his country, who can hesitate which to embrace. * * * "No testimony relative to the conduct or declarations of the prisoner elsewhere and subsequent to the transaction on Blennerhassett's Island can be admitted; because such testimony, being in its nature merely corroborative and incompetent to prove the overt act in itself, is irrelevant until there be proof of the overt act by two witnesses. "This opinion does not comprehend the proof by two witnesses that the meeting on Blennerhassett's Island was procured by the prisoner. 

On that point the court for the present withholds its opinion for reasons which have been already assigned; and as it is understood from the statements made on the part of the prosecution that no such testimony exists. If there be such let it be offered; and the court will decide upon it. The jury have now heard the opinion of the court on the law of the case. They will apply that law to the facts, and will find a verdict of guilty or not guilty as their own consciences may direct." 

Sec. 780. Verdict.-The jury were out about twentyfive minutes and reported through their foreman, Colonel 

Carrington, the following verdict endorsed on the indictment: "We, the jury, find that Aaron Burr is not proved to be guilty under the indictment by any evidence submitted to us. We therefore find him not guilty." 

This might be called a Scotch verdict. The words "not proved" were interlined, indicating that some of the jurors felt that although he might be guilty, his guilt had not been proved. Burr's counsel objected to the form of the verdict of the jury. The court ruled that it might

stand but that the record of the proceedings would simply show a verdict of not guilty. The following day Burr was released from prison after a trial that had lasted in intermittent hearings and sessions from the 26th day of 

March to the 14th of September.

Thus ended the most famous trial in the history of 

American criminal jurisprudence.

Usury-Charging an unlawful rate for the use of money. Six per cent is legal rate and as much as ten per cent is permitted when fixed by contract; more than this is usury. A party promising to pay usury may recover double the sum so paid.
